A NOVEL OF THE LINEAR ASCENT

THE HUNDRED
BANNERS

The tower was built so the world could never gather.
This is the story of the gathering.

The Ascent — one hundred stolen floors

Roothollow, at the foot

Prologue

They sent the boy down the valley at dusk with the last two sacks of barley, because the miller ground cheapest for whoever came latest and his mother would not pay a vain man’s price for the sake of an early night. He was seven. The sacks together outweighed him, the mill road ran a long way by the river in the dark, and he had been angry about all of it. Afterward he spent years trying to remember anything else of that evening — the color of the sky, whether she stood in the door to watch him go, whether the dog walked him to the gate — and found only the anger, and the weight of the barley, and the smell of the kitchen he had walked out of. His mother had set the loaves in before he left. She always baked on mill nights. Bread against his coming home.

The sound began while he was still short of the mill, and it came from under his feet, which was wrong. Thunder lives in the sky. This lay down in the roots of the valley and turned over, and the river jumped in its bed, and the barley hummed against his back like a hive.

He saw what came next in pieces, the way a child sees anything too big for him. A line of white light ran along the ridgetops where no road had ever been. The old boundary oaks tilted all together, courteous, like men taking off their hats. And the fields went up. Not thrown. Not falling the wrong way. Lifted — the whole valley leaving the ground in one piece, hedges and lanes and the stream still lying in its bed, rising as slow and level as a tray of loaves carried to table by something that did not mean to spill them.

The boy dropped the sacks and ran back the way he had come, under the shadow of his own country.

Above him hung the underside of home. Roots first: whole hanging forests of them, oak-root and hedge-root and the long pale roots of the pasture grass, combed downward and dripping. The well he had drunk from every day of his life passed over his head as a severed stone throat, raining. The duck-pond poured off its broken edge in a silver sheet that fell and fell and never seemed to strike anything. And in among the roots and the hanging stones there was lamplight — window after window still burning, calm and yellow, in the black belly of the risen ground, because nobody up there had learned yet to be afraid of the dark underneath them.

He found their kitchen by its light. He knew it the way you know your own door in a town of doors. He ran beneath it calling until his voice went, and the valley did not stop rising, and the little window climbed up among the other windows, and the windows climbed up among the stars, and then he could not tell them from the stars, and then it was quiet.

It rained soil for a while after. Clods, and slates off a shaved roof, and one bruised windfall apple, which he picked up and held in both hands and could not eat. High above him, small and growing smaller, he could hear the dog barking. In all its life that dog had never stopped barking before somebody came.

⁂

He did not leave the rim of the pit where the valley had been. Others gathered there over the next days: a drover caught out on the road with half his herd; the miller and his sons — the mill had stood a spear-throw outside the cut, and had ground on, stupidly, all that night; two hedgers; a wet-nurse from the far bank; a peddler who wept over nothing he could name. The left-behind. What the thief had not troubled to take. They made a camp of what had shaken loose from the underside as the land rose: half a barn, a length of good fence, a horse trough, and a cold aether-forge dropped whole out of somebody’s smithy, its coals scattered like a hand of black coins.

On the third night a clerk came walking the rim of the pit.

It was taller than a man and thinner, grey the way paper is grey, with a lantern in one hand and a book in the crook of its arm, and a fine chain at its throat worn the way a reeve wears a chain of office. It walked the whole rim with its lips moving, counting. It did not hurt anyone. That was the strangest thing about it, and the hardest to forgive. The drover threw a stone at it, and it noted the stone in the book.

When it reached the boy it stopped. He had not eaten since the barley went down with the light, and he would not leave the rim, and he was sitting with the apple he could not eat still in his hands.

“You are not in any of my columns,” the clerk said. Its voice was the finest thing about it — kind, even, reasonable, the voice of someone who has never once needed to raise it.

It asked him his name, and he gave it, because he was a child and grown things were asking. The clerk wrote the name low on a page already thick with names, and what it wrote there the boy could not read, and this account will not repeat it. Then it reached into its satchel and brought out a dark loaf, heavy tower bread, and broke the heel off, and held the heel out to him across the space between them.

“The tower feeds what it counts,” the clerk said. “Freely given. You may throw it in the pit if it pleases you. Some do.”

He was seven years old, and every loaf that had ever been baked for him hung a country over his head, straight up, going cold in a dead oven. He ate it. It was good — that was the shame he kept longest, longer than any of the rest of it: it was good bread, and he was glad of it, and it cost him nothing at all. Nothing is what everything costs, at first.

The clerk watched him eat with an expression he would spend the next fifty years learning to make himself — patient, kindly, keeping count — and then it closed the book and walked on along the rim, and its lantern went away into the dark, and he never told anyone. Not that night, and not any night after, through all the years when telling might still have mattered.

⁂

In time the left-behind stopped waiting at the rim and began to build. Others found them — from other pits, other shaved valleys, straggling in along roads that now ended in air — men and elves and the slab-shouldered giants of the mountain forges, delvers, marsh-folk, ash-nomads, everyone the theft had spilled and nobody had counted worth taking. The tower grew over them ring by ring until it shut out the northern stars, and the camp grew under it into a town of every people on the ground, hammered out of wreckage, an aether-forge beside a horse trough. It stood at the root of the stolen world, in the hollow the theft had left, and the name followed of itself.

The boy grew up with the town. He learned his letters, and he learned his sums, and he learned — quicker than the hedgers’ boys, quicker than the drover’s sons — the one lesson the clerk had taught him without meaning to, or by meaning it entirely, and which no one who was fed that night ever wholly unlearned: that the world can be taken up like a tray of loaves; that somebody keeps the ledger of the taking; and that the ledger decides who eats.

On some nights, when the wind came down the black iron and the undersides of the low floors glimmered with their hanging lamps, he would stand at the edge of the town and look up. Somewhere above him, in a kitchen he could have found blindfolded, the oven was long cold, and the loaves were still in it.

PART I

THE DEBT

Roothollow · floors 1–20

Chapter 1 — The Trainer of Other People's Recruits

The recruits came to the yard at first light, because Ede Stonhal had told them the tower kept no honest mornings and they had better bring their own. They came yawning out of the lodging-rows with their borrowed shields on their backs, past the aether-forge already breathing blue and the horse trough already skimmed with dust, and they made their line in the cold without being told, which was the first thing she had taught them and the last thing most captains bothered to.

There were eleven of them that season. A giant lad called Brom, sixteen and the size of a door, who spoke slowly and finished every sentence he began. The ferry twins, Lark and Fell, marsh-line stock, quick-handed, raised on punts and eel-traps by grandmothers who remembered a drowned country. A delver girl named Pike who could climb anything with a rope on it and most things without. Seven more of the usual Roothollow mix — a smith’s daughter, two hedgers’ sons, an elf boy who had never seen the woods his mother sang about. Every people the theft had spilled, drilling in one yard, under one voice.

“Shields,” Ede said, and eleven shields came up, and she walked the line and put two of them a hand higher with her fist.

She drilled them the way she had been drilled: hedge-lane work first, because the first floor was hedges. How to take a wall of blackthorn on your shoulder and not your face. How a lane fights — narrow front, no flank, everything decided by whoever holds their feet. Then the counts. She made them say the counts back to her until they could say them asleep, because the counts were what came home and everything else was luck.

“Brackjaw,” she said.

“Circles once,” the line answered. “Always once. Then the head goes down and it comes straight.”

“So where are you when the head goes down?”

“Not in front of it,” said Lark.

“Beside it,” said Brom, “with my shield on the servo shoulder, because a straight line cannot turn. I have finished.”

“You’re all dead a breath early,” Ede said. “It circles once. You count the circle. You don’t spend the count admiring it.” But she let them see the nod, because they had earned the nod, and a line that is never fed starves the same as any other living thing.

Two years she had run this yard. Two years she had drunk nothing the horse trough couldn’t give her, a ladle at a time, morning and noon, and if her hand still went looking for something else at dusk, that was between her and the dusk. Six companies she had trained in that time, and walked to the Root Gate, and watched go up. Four of them had put names on the Stone of the Climb — you could see the glow of the new cuts from the yard on a clear night, floors broken, lifts opened, letters lit from within like coals under a grate. Two of them had put names on the north face instead, where nothing glows.

She trained other people’s recruits and other people climbed with them. That was the arrangement. Nobody in Roothollow said it to her face anymore, which is how a town tells you it has given up on you politely.

The elf was watching her before she saw him, which she would later learn was the usual order of things where Rhovan was concerned.

⁂

He was leaning at the yard’s edge where the forge-light didn’t reach, grey from boots to collar, riding clothes cut for the high bridleways, with an unstrung bow across his back and the stillness of a man who has made a profession of being furniture. He had been there long enough to watch the whole shield drill. When Pike’s arrow-stance sagged on the practice butts he crossed the yard without being asked and moved her back elbow two fingers’ width, and the next three shafts went where she meant them, and he was back in his patch of no-light before Ede had finished deciding to be annoyed.

She dismissed the line at noon and he was still there.

“You’re either buying or begging,” she said. “The yard fee’s posted.”

“Neither.” He came into the light like something deciding to be seen. Close up he was older than elves usually let you notice. “Rhovan. Of the Grey Rides.”

“That’s a place, not an employer.”

“It’s both, at a distance.” He looked at the trough, at the forge, at the Stone standing off by the gate with its lit names and its dark ones, the way a surveyor looks at ground he has already bought. “I keep other people’s secrets. It’s steadier work than yours. I have one of yours in my coat, and I’d rather hand it over than keep carrying it. It’s heavier than it looks.”

“I don’t have secrets. I have a record. Everybody knows my record.”

“Everybody knows a story,” Rhovan said. “The record’s better.”

She took him to the tack-shed she used for an office, because whatever this was, it wasn’t for the yard. Harness on the walls, a plank table, the season’s pay-ledger, a stool she didn’t offer him. He laid a folded page on the plank and set one finger on it and left the finger there, the way you hold down something that might blow away or bite.

It was heavy paper, toll-house ruling, folded in three and sealed with lead-grey wax that carried no sigil at all — which in Roothollow, where every guild stamped everything down to its firewood, was its own kind of signature. The fold was old. The wax was not the first wax; someone had sealed this page more than once, in more than one place.

“Where’s it from?”

“A ledger.”

“Whose?”

“That,” Rhovan said, “is the wrong end of the question. Read it.”

She broke the wax. A clerk’s fair hand, ruled entries, the ink gone tea-brown. Dated at the top in the tower’s own reckoning — she did the sum against her own calendar twice, because the first time her mind refused to hold the answer still. The week before she had marched. The very week.

Item. For the road of the Shieldwall Company, gate to gate, floors nine to twenty, with hours, fords, and watchwords entire: paid in full.

Item. Delivery to be taken at the Red Warcamp, at the drums.

Item. Goods: one company, standards, stores, and persons, thirty and some.

Received against the above the sum of —

The page was cut. Not torn — cut, clean, with a knife and a straightedge, the way a clerk trims what a clerk means to keep elsewhere. Whatever sum had been paid, and whatever hand had taken it, lived on the half that was still in the ledger this had been cut from.

The tack-shed went away for a while.

She stood in the mine-dark under floor twenty with her company breathing around her and the drums starting up, and heard, for the first time truly, what she had heard that night and buried under five years of asking the wrong question: the drums had been beating Shieldwall’s own march. Their own signal-cadence, the beat she used to move the line in the dark, played back at them out of the black like a landlord jingling your own keys. She had taken it for mockery, improvised, some drummer’s cruel good ear. It was not improvised. They had the watchwords. The gates had barred behind the company, not before it — she had cursed her own scouting for five years over those gates — and Skarn’s honor guard had already been formed up on the spoil-heaps above the road, in the dark, in silence, torches shuttered, waiting the way you wait for something whose hours you have in writing. The whole floor had not fought them. It had received them.

Not a defeat. She had led thirty-one people into a mine and it was never a battle at all. It was a delivery, and she had carried it, on time, by the marked road, to the drums.

She had spent five years asking what she had done wrong. Here was the answer, in a clerk’s fair hand, with the important half cut away: nothing. She had done nothing wrong, and it should have lifted something off her, and instead it set down something far heavier in its place, because a captain who fails can drill recruits and drink trough-water and call it penance — but goods that were sold are owed a different debt entirely, and the debt had a seller’s name on it, and the name was on the half of the page she didn’t have.

“Who sold us,” she said. Not a question. A requirement.

“The page doesn’t say. Pages rarely do.” Rhovan’s voice stayed level as a toll-bar. “Ledgers do.”

“Then where’s the ledger?”

He pointed one finger at the roof of the tack-shed, and through it, at the whole stacked weight of the world. “Up.”

⁂

He gave her the rest standing, in the fractions that she would come to know as the whole of his conversation. The page had come to the Grey Rides folded inside a dead courier’s boot-lining, and who the courier was was a second question, and who had killed him was a third, and Rhovan answered neither. The people he worked for — he never once that afternoon said the word Wake, and she noticed the not-saying — wanted the tower down. They had wanted it down for a long time, and they had concluded, in whatever grey room such things are concluded in, that towers do not come down for secrets. They come down for armies, and armies gather around a spine, and spines were scarcer than secrets.

“You want me to climb,” she said.

“I want to hand this back to the person it belongs to and be done,” said Rhovan. “The people I answer to want you climbing. They are prepared to be unpleasant about it. I’d sooner not be. Consider that my one virtue.”

“Unpleasant.”

“The page could go up the tower with you,” he said, “or it could go to the criers. Half this town already half-believes the captain sold her own company and drank herself kind about it after. The page doesn’t say she didn’t. Read it again if you doubt me. It names no seller. Papers like this hang whoever’s standing nearest when they’re read aloud, and you have been standing nearest for five years.”

Her hand had closed on the edge of the plank table. She watched her own knuckles whiten with a sort of distant interest, the way you watch weather over somebody else’s fields. Two years dry, and she had never once wanted the other thing so precisely, so much like thirst.

“Get out of my yard,” she said.

“I will. One thing more.” He said it with his hand already on the door, his fraction of mercy, or his fraction of craft; she never did learn to tell them apart. “I’m paid to ask three times. That’s the term of my contract. I’ll be at the Root Gate at dawn for the first answer, and I’ll take whichever one you bring — but bring one.” He looked once around the tack-shed, at the harness and the pay-ledger and the stool she hadn’t offered, and then at her. “You count well. Everyone says so. The counts, the recruits, the ladles from that trough. Count this: thirty and some, sold by the head, and the seller’s name cut clean, and kept. Somebody has been carrying that name for five years, the way you’ve been carrying the blame for it. Of the two of you, I’d say the name weighs less. Its owner has never once looked for the bottom of a cup.”

Then he was gone, grey into grey, and the yard was just a yard.

⁂

She walked the town that evening the long way, which she had not done in a year. Roothollow at lamplighting: every race the theft had spilled, shoulder to shoulder in the wreck-built streets. Giants stooping under lintels made of ship-keel. Elf children playing knucklebones on the steps of a chapel built from four kinds of salvage and roofed in stair-lift crating. The gate-queue for tomorrow’s climbs already forming along the wall, cook-fires, oiled shields, some captain’s hired piper practicing the same eight notes over and over, hopeful and thin. Past the granary row the air went warm and brown — Berrin’s ovens were awake, working the night shift so the gate-queue could carry fresh bread up at dawn — and she stood in the smell of it a breath longer than she meant to. That smell had fed her through worse years than this one. It was the one thing in Roothollow that had never once asked her how she was.

The Stone of the Climb stood by the Root Gate, a standing slab the height of a giant, cut on every face. On the gate side glowed the names of the breakers — every climber who had ever put a Warden down and opened a lift, lit from within, oldest at the base, so that the whole stone stood in its own shallow light. At the very base, under all that light, the first name ever cut stood worn nearly flat — A. BRIGHT, the letters more thumb now than chisel, its glow gone thin as breath under two generations of hands touching it on the way to the gate. Nobody in Roothollow could say who Bright had been. Nobody asked. It was only where the counting started. On the north face, in plain dead letters, the lost.

She had cut thirty of those letters’ names herself, one each night, the first winter she was sober, with a mason’s chisel she returned every morning and borrowed again every dusk. The mason had never charged her. The town watched her do it and said nothing, which is how a town forgives you for something it still believes you did.

Her thumb found the fourth name down without her eyes’ help. DUN HARROW, STANDARD-BEARER. The thumb had worn the letters faintly smooth already, two winters of this, and she stood in the Stone’s low light and did the arithmetic Rhovan had left her with, and hated him for the neatness of it.

If it was a sale, there was a seller. If there was a seller, there was a buyer, and buyers keep what they pay for. Nine years she had mourned her thirty as dead because dead was the worst thing, and she had trained herself to believe the worst thing the way you train any recruit: daily, until it holds its line. But the page said goods. The page said delivery. You do not write persons, thirty and some on a bill of sale for a massacre.

At her throat, under her shirt, hung a small tin on a leather thong, and the tin was empty, and had been empty two years. She had carried a splinter in it once — a sliver of smoky crystal no longer than a finger, old Aldervale’s own thinking aether, the shardmind that had chosen her at this gate when she was younger than Brom. It had gone up with her every climb. It had come down with her from the Warcamp, the only thing that did. The day she cut the first name into the north face she had walked it back to the shard-house and laid it in the rack with the unchosen, and the keeper had said nothing at all, and the splinter had said one word, one only, dry as a seed in the dark of the tin:

Later.

She had never repeated it to anyone. There was no one it could be repeated to who would not ask what it meant, and she did not know what it meant, and a captain does not hand out questions she cannot drill an answer to.

She stood a long while at the Stone, until the piper by the wall gave up on his eight notes and the cook-fires burned down to keeping-coals. Then she walked home past the yard, and checked the trough’s skim of dust out of pure habit, and did not sleep.

Rhovan would be at the Root Gate at dawn, wanting the first of his three answers. She knew the shape of the word already. It was small, and it fit her mouth, and she had said it to herself so many years running that it sounded like her name.

Chapter 2 — The Third Refusal

Rhovan was at the Root Gate before the light was, standing against the wall with the patience of a man being paid by the day. Behind him the gate-queue was waking: captains kicking their people upright, shields being oiled by feel, somebody’s kettle, somebody’s prayer. The great arch of the stair-lift stood over it all in the half-dark, black iron carved by no hand anyone loved, and the queue kept its backs to it the way men keep their backs to a cliff-edge they work beside every day.

“No,” Ede said.

He didn’t move. “You’re sure.”

“I train them. Other people climb with them. That’s the arrangement, and the arrangement holds. I led thirty-one people up that stair once and I came down alone, and I will not stand at the front of a line again while there’s breath in me. If the Wake wants a spine, buy one. The queue’s full of captains.”

“It is,” Rhovan agreed, looking at the queue, and managed to make the two words do the work of an insult with no part of them you could answer. He straightened off the wall. “That’s one, then.”

“That’s the only.”

“That’s one,” he said again, mild as milk, and walked into the waking market, and was grey, and then was gone.

She ran the yard hard that day, and harder the day after. If the recruits noticed that the counts came faster and the nod came never, they had the sense to keep it among themselves. The page she kept folded in the lining of her coat, against her ribs, where she could feel the corner of it when she breathed deep, and so she breathed shallow, and told herself that was drill discipline, and knew better all the while.

Goods. Delivery. Thirty and some. She woke at the hour of the theft’s old thunder every night that week, with the drums in her ears, beating her own march.

⁂

Gate-day came at the week’s end, as it came every week: the morning the lifts took up whoever had signed the climb-roll, and Roothollow turned out to watch its bravest and its most foolish walk under the arch. Her eleven were signed to go up under a hired captain named Corvel — a southern-wall man with good teeth and a bought company, who had inspected her recruits the way you inspect a horse’s mouth, and paid the yard fee in full, and called her the trainer to her face without once asking if she had another name.

On the eve of it, when drill was done and the others had gone to their suppers, Brom stayed behind in the yard. He stood in front of her with his shield still on his arm, and she watched him build the sentence first in his head, stone by stone, the way giants do, so that it would stand up on its own when he set it down between them.

“You taught us the counts of the first nineteen jailers,” he said. “You taught us where Brackjaw’s shoulder is, and what the flooded drift hides, and how a lane fights. Tomorrow we climb behind a man who paid a fee, and you will stand at this trough, and count ladles. I would rather climb behind the counts’ own keeper. So would all eleven. We took a vote, which Captain Corvel does not know about. I am asking you to come and stand at the front of us. I have finished.”

The yard was very quiet. Out past the wall the gate-queue’s fires were being lit, one after another, little answers to the dark.

“No,” Ede said.

It came out of her flat and fast, before anything gentler could get in front of it — the second refusal, and the first one that cost her. Brom’s face did not change; giants’ faces don’t; the hurt goes down into them like frost into ground, to work all winter where you can’t see it. He nodded once, and thanked her for the trough water — for two seasons of it, formally, the whole courtesy, while she stood there with the page against her ribs — and shouldered his shield, and went to his supper.

She heard the tale had traveled by the way the lodging-row went silent when she passed it. A town the size of Roothollow has no secrets, only kindnesses, and it was being kind to her again, and she could not bear it.

She did not go home. She went where she had gone through every bad night of the drunk years and half the good ones since, down the granary row, into the brown warmth, through the door that was never barred to her: Berrin’s bakehouse.

⁂

He was at the night ovens with his sleeves rolled and flour to the elbows, a big man gone comfortably heavy, grey in the beard and quick in the hands, sliding loaves for tomorrow’s gate-queue off the peel in ranks as neat as any shield-line. The whole room smelled like the one good memory everyone in Roothollow had in common. Grain-ledgers stood shelved along the wall by the door, spines cracked with use — he kept the counts of half the town’s eating, did Berrin Or, and had since before she’d owned a shield.

He looked her over once, the way he’d looked her over across that threshold a hundred times, and reached down two bowls, and filled them from the pot that lived at the back of the oven-top, and set hers on the flour-barrel she always sat on. That was Berrin entire: he fed you first and found out what was wrong with you after, on the sound theory that half of what was wrong with anyone was supper.

“Eleven good recruits,” he said, tearing bread for them both, “one hired captain with teeth like a fair-day pony, and my second-best flour going up the lift with them at dawn. And you’ve eaten nothing since noon, by the look you’re wearing. Sit.”

“You’ve heard, then.”

“I’ve heard six versions. Roothollow’s a wonderful town; you can hear your own supper argued about before you’ve cooked it.” He settled onto the sacks across from her with his bowl. “I’ve also heard about your grey visitor. That elf drinks at the Long Trough and says nothing so loudly you can hear it two streets off. There’s paper involved, they say. Old paper.”

So she told him. All of it — the page, the fair hand, the cut edge, the week of the date; the drums beating her own march; Rhovan and his three askings. It came out of her in drill-order, flat, itemized, because that was the only way she could carry it across a room, and Berrin listened the way he did everything, entirely, wiping the bowl with his bread and watching her over it, and when she had finished he was quiet for a long breath.

“Well,” he said at last. “First thing. Whoever sold a company by the head and let the page out of his sight was a fool, and I’d tell him so to his face, and then I’d hold him while you settled up.” He said it easily, the way he said everything, and reached to top up her bowl. “Second thing, and look at me while I say it, because it’s the thing you walked here not to hear. Nobody pays warcamp prices for the dead, Ede. The dead are free. It’s the living that cost.”

“Don’t.”

“Goods,” he said, not letting her off. “Delivery. Persons, thirty and some. You’re the counter, girl, not me — but I’ve kept ledgers since before you could lift a shield, and I’ll tell you what that paper is. That’s not a butcher’s bill. That’s a bill of lading. You don’t write persons on what you mean to spoil.”

“They’ve been five years in cages, if they’re anywhere at all.”

“Then they’ve been five years counting doors,” Berrin said, “and nobody’s come. Suppose Dun is breathing. Just suppose it, one breath’s worth, you can put it down after. He’s kept your standard and your counts five years in the dark, because that’s what that man is. What’s his count of you at, do you reckon, by now?”

It was the cruelest thing anyone had said to her in two years, and he said it with such plain love that she couldn’t even stand up to leave.

“I’d get them killed,” she said. “Whoever follows me — that’s what follows me. You know what I am now. I train. I don’t lead.”

“What you are now,” Berrin said, “is hidden. And hiding’s had two good years, I’ll grant it — it got you sober, it got six companies trained, I’ll not sneer at it. But grief you tend like a garden isn’t grief anymore, it’s comfort, and I’ve sold enough comfort across this counter to know it by smell.” He set his bowl aside. “You want to go up alone and settle it and die of it tidily — no, don’t trouble to deny it, I’ve watched you want things across this room for ten years. That’s the drink talking in a new voice, that’s all that is. The other road is worse and better: take the children. Stand at the front. Teach them from the front, where it costs — you’ve been teaching from the safe end so long you’ve forgotten the front is where the trade’s honest.”

The ovens ticked. Somewhere in the back a prentice was stacking wood.

“And there’s a last thing,” he said, getting up, brushing flour, “the practical thing, so you know I mean it. Sign the roll at dawn and I’ll victual you to floor twenty myself — flour, boots, lamp-oil, the good wax against the wet floors, all of it on the granary’s book. You’ll sign for every sack, mind; friendship’s friendship and a ledger’s a ledger.” He stood over her, and for a moment all the ease went out of him and what was left was old and steady as the Stone. “And when you find the book that page was cut from — and you will, you find everything you’ve ever gone looking for, it’s the rest of us that lose things — bring me the name that signed it. Just the name. I’ll bake for the hanging.”

She looked up at him — the flour, the grey beard, thirty years of other people’s suppers — and felt the no she had carried for a week come apart quietly, like a knot that had only ever needed one honest pull.

“The good boots,” she said.

“Take the good boots,” Berrin agreed, and his grin came back, and he went to see to his loaves.

⁂

She was at the Root Gate before the light was, and this time it was Rhovan who found her already standing there.

He did not gloat, she gave him that. He watched her sign the climb-roll at the gate-warden’s table — Ede Stonhal, of Roothollow, and company — in the grey before dawn, with the queue stirring and craning all down the wall, and all he said was, “That’s a strange spelling of no.”

“You’re still owed a third refusal,” she said. “Keep it. I may want it someday.”

Word ran down the queue faster than the light did. Corvel came up with his contract in his fist and his teeth on display and got as far as “the fee” before Brom stepped out of his line — unhurried, complete, like a wall deciding to move — and set himself behind Ede’s shoulder. “I had not yet signed your roll,” he said. “Now I will not. I have finished.” Lark and Fell were already there. Pike came over the baggage rather than around it. The rest followed the way the rest always follow, and Corvel looked at his eleven recruits become none, and did the one wise thing she ever saw him do: he pocketed the contract and kept his teeth shut.

The last of it happened at the shard-house.

It stood beside the arch as it had stood all her life: a dry stone vault, older-built than anything else in Roothollow because the shards abide no damp, ranked inside with a hundred waiting splinters on their felted racks like a choir that has been holding one long note since the Theft. Every climber signed and then walked the racks, and mostly nothing happened, and once in a season some crystal woke for a stranger and the town had a feast. Mother Cail kept it, an old delver woman with a lamp on her brow at all hours, who had kept it since before the Stone had names.

Ede had no intention of walking the racks. She was past the doorway, head down, making for the arch and the assembling line, when the light changed.

It came through the vault door sideways, thin and white, and the queue’s murmur died from the near end to the far one like a sluice shutting. In the racks, in the third row, in the place it had lain for two years among the unchosen, a splinter of smoky crystal no longer than a finger had kindled from the inside — and then it rose, unhurried, over the felt and the sleeping ranks of its kin, and crossed the vault, and came out under the lintel into the morning, and hung in the air before her at the height of her throat. Exactly at the height, she understood dully, of a small tin on a leather thong.

“They don’t re-choose,” Mother Cail said from the doorway. Her lamp was shaking. She said it to the queue, to the gate-warden, to forty years of her own keeping. “Write it down, someone. They have never — in the whole record, back to the first gate, a shard set down has never once—”

I told you: later, said Sliver, dry as a seed, ancient as the lamps of drowned Aldervale, and not quietly. It spoke the way the Stone glowed, from the inside of things; every soul along the wall heard it in their own bones, and she watched the nearest captains take their caps off without knowing they did it. It is later.

“You could have said more,” Ede managed. “At the time.”

Forty-one climbers I have carried. Nine were stubborn enough to matter. You were the ninth. You handed me back like a borrowed knife, Ede Stonhal, and I have spent two years in a drawer full of children, listening to your counts across the yard through six feet of stone. They have improved. You have not. We will work on it as we go.

Her hand came up, and the splinter settled onto her palm with the weight of nothing at all, of a name, of a debt — warm, familiar, hers. The tin was open at her throat before she had decided to open it.

One more thing said publicly, since we are being public. The light in it steadied to a working glow. I do not re-choose. Let the record stand: she set me down. I never set her down.

The queue made a sound then, all down the wall — not a cheer, nothing so tidy; more the sound a town makes when it puts something down after carrying it for years. And Ede Stonhal turned from the shard-house with her splinter lit at her throat and her eleven at her back and Rhovan falling in grey and silent at her shoulder, and walked under the black arch of the Root Gate, onto the stair-lift her name had been absent from for five years, and climbed.

The lift ran up its chain into the dark of the first floor’s underside — roots, hanging stone, the old severed veins of a stolen country — and then through, into a flat white glare that was no sun’s, where the hedges of a farm valley ran their straight green lines to the horizon under the Yoke’s false light, hung where a sun should hang, which never set, and never moved a shadow.

Somewhere out in that stillness, she knew, an old farmer was already counting the lift-chain’s links by ear, tallying the climbers coming up into his fields against the ones who would come back to the gate.

The counts were about to change.

Chapter 3 — Hedge and Hollow

The farmer was waiting at the lift-head with a tally-stick, and Rhovan liked him before he spoke, which in Rhovan’s trade was a fault and he noted it as one.

“Thirteen in,” the old man said, cutting a notch. Not a greeting: an entry. He was small and weathered-grey, in a hedger’s coat mended with four kinds of thread, and behind him the Fencerows ran to the white horizon — hedged fields in their old straight lines under a false light that never set, hay half-cut and left to grey a lifetime ago, grass gone pale as paper, and no shadow on the whole floor that had ever moved. “Hobb Fennick. I count what goes into the hedges, and I count what comes out. The counts don’t match, and haven’t in forty years, and you’ll forgive me for saying I don’t expect you thirteen to mend the arithmetic.”

“We’re not here to mend it,” said Ede. “We’re here to change what’s being counted.”

Hobb looked at her a long breath, then at the splinter lit at her throat, and cut a second notch that Rhovan could see no reason for, and led them in along the fence-line toward Lamplit Steading with its barred doors and its rationed lamps.

Rhovan walked last, where he could see all of it, and kept his book.

It was a plain grey book, oilcloth-bound, and it was his employer as truly as any spymaster on the Grey Rides. The Wake liked its wars auditable. Three columns, ruled by his own hand at the top of every page, in the tongue of the low floors so that anyone finding it would read piety instead of accountancy: Cured. Evicted. Dead. A kill of a Native was a cure — the fever cut out of the land’s own beast. A kill of a Wrongmade was an eviction — the King’s craft turned out of ground that was never his. And a kill of one of the Pressed was a death, plainly, a death that should not have been needed, and the Wake’s doctrine held that a rising which could not keep its third column short deserved to lose. Rhovan had kept the three columns through two wars and one long peace, and had learned more about every company he’d ever traveled with from the shape of its entries than from anything its captains said.

He was here to keep a fourth account beside the three, though it was written nowhere: the woman. What she looked at first. What she counted. What her hands did before her orders caught up with them. The people he answered to had bought themselves a spine for their army, sight unseen, on the strength of a cut page and an old story. Rhovan’s work was to learn what they had actually paid for.

The hedges taught him quickly.

⁂

The first fight came before the steading did — a rat-hunt, the low war in miniature. Hedgerow rats, dog-big, teeth first, boiling out of a bramble gap at the smell of the bread in the packs. It should have been chaos, thirteen strangers in a lane. It was drill. Ede’s voice went out flat and unhurried — lane fights narrow, shields make the wall, spears work over — and the recruits folded around her words like a gate swinging shut. Brom’s shield was the wall. The twins’ spears did the working over. It lasted twenty heartbeats and cost one bitten boot.

The wolf was different, and the wolf was the lesson.

It came at them where the third hedge gapped, a grey horror the size of a pony, ribbed and fever-gaunt, all its hunger pointed the wrong way out. Ede put it down herself, one spear-thrust, stepping toward it as its charge broke around Brom’s shield — and then the recruits saw what every climber sees once and no one believes secondhand. The dead bulk did not lie there. It dropped away — sloughed, like a coat let fall, like a fist unclenching — and what got up out of it was a wolf. A plain grey wolf, lean and shy and right-sized, that looked at fourteen humans with an animal’s honest arithmetic and flowed away through the gap in the hedge, and was gone toward the wild edge of the floor.

“What we kill was never the beast,” Ede said, into their silence. “It’s the thing the tower set riding it. Steel’s the only exorcism on offer up here. Say it back.”

They said it back. Rhovan wrote cured, one wolf in the first column, and underneath it, in the private shorthand of the fourth account: she stepped toward it.

The floor gave them one more mercy and one more test before the Warden. The mercy was a sheepdog — the steading’s last pack ran the fields in a silent ring, fevered, hunting the very fences they’d been whelped to keep, and when the ring closed on the party at the Mother Ditch it went the way of the wolf: cured shapes shaking off the wrongness, scattering. But one of them, a black-and-white bitch with one wall eye, stopped at Fell’s low whistle. Turned her head. Remembered, visibly, in front of everyone, the whole of a stolen life — the whistle, the work, the name. She came in to heel shaking like a leaf, and Hobb Fennick knelt down in the pale grass and took her face in his two hands and said a name Rhovan didn’t catch, and the count of the floor changed by one in a way no tally-stick had a notch for.

The test stood guard at the Burnt Steading, and it was small, and it carried a longsword too heavy for it by half.

A goblin. A straggler of the garrison, left behind sick when its war-band climbed on years ago — collared, footsore, starved down to wire, holding a door into a roofless black-timbered ruin that no one had lived in for a lifetime. Nobody had told it the post was abandoned. Or somebody had, and it had decided that a post was a post. It set its feet in the doorway as thirteen armed climbers came up the lane, and got the big blade’s point more or less off the ground, and stood.

“Hold,” Ede said, and the line held, and Rhovan watched her walk out of it alone, empty-handed, into the space where the goblin’s reach would have been if the goblin could have swung.

“The collar, not the body,” she said to it. “That’s the only thing on this floor we want broken. Put the iron down and it’s done. No one here is owed your blood, and you’re not paid enough to owe them yours. You’re not paid at all.”

The goblin looked at her for the space of ten heartbeats — Rhovan counted; counting was how he kept his hands still — and then the longsword went down in the weeds, and its claws went to its own throat, and the collar came off over its ears, and it dropped the thing in the lane-mud where anyone could tread it, and it did not run. It stood there, half-naked and shaking, unpaid, unowned, guarding nothing, free.

“I’ll count him,” Hobb Fennick said, in the tone of a man ruling a line into a ledger kept for forty years. “There’s fences want laying. He’ll eat what we eat.”

Rhovan stood a while over his book at the day’s end. Three columns, and none of them fit. Cured was for beasts, evicted for craft, dead for Pressed — there was no heading in the Wake’s whole doctrine for stood down and stayed. He ruled a fourth column narrow along the margin and left the header blank, and in the fourth account, the unwritten one, he recorded that the woman had walked out of her own shield-wall with empty hands, and that his employers had no idea what they had bought.

Brackjaw died the next morning in the meadow before the chalk bluff, and died exactly by the counts she’d taught in the yard: it circled once — always once — servo-jaws ticking, wolf-and-plate, the meadow’s own grey hunter welded into a mockery of itself; and when the head went down and it came straight, the line was not in front of it. Brom’s shield turned its shoulder, Rhovan’s shaft found the servo-joint from the flank, and Ede went in over the wreck of its charge and finished it. The false light overhead dimmed by a hair, the whole sky of it at once, like a held breath let out. Away across the fields, the doors of Lamplit Steading began, one by one, to unbar.

There were names on the Stone of the Climb, Rhovan knew, of climbers who had broken this same floor before — two generations of them. The tower had re-welded its jailer each time within a few winters, a new wolf under the old name, Brackjaw the third of that name, and the fever had crept back over the hedges like damp into a house no one keeps. The lifts a Warden’s death opened stayed open; the peace did not, not unless somebody held it. That was the whole argument of the people Rhovan worked for, written in one meadow: floors do not stay freed. They are held freed, or they are lost again — and no lone company, however good its counts, holds anything. For that you need the thing the tower was built to prevent.

Hobb notched them out at the bluff: “Thirteen up. First time in forty years the counts came out ahead.” He said it accusingly, as if they’d broken something he’d grown fond of, and stood at the lift-head with the wall-eyed bitch at his knee, watching them rise, until the chalk and the hedges and the old man and the dog were all one pale country below, and then were the underside of one.

⁂

Floor two was the inside of a mountain that no longer had an outside.

The Rustwater Adit: a slab of the giants’ deep-forge realm cut off at the mine-mouth, ore-carts loaded on rails that ran off the floor’s edge into open air, bats hung thick in the entrance vaults, and everything not iron furred orange with rust. The mine still wept — iron-water running down the cut faces, sweet-smelling and wrong — and the dark past the third lamp was the first true dark most of the recruits had ever stood in.

The keeper met them under the headframe, a giant woman with a dead safety-lamp on her belt, who filled the gallery she stood in. Bergda Ironkettle, pit-foreman of a pit with no bottom left. She dipped two fingers in the runnel by the rail and tasted them, unhurried, while the party stood dripping in their pooled lamplight.

“Sweet today,” she said. “It lies. There are kobolds cutting my seams in the dark, poor collared vermin, and an orc set over them with a whip, and the Warden hunts the flooded drift where the water hides its feet. You’ll want the rails. Walk between them, not on them. Rails carry sound the way ledgers carry debt: farther than you’d credit, and always to somebody.”

The ambush came at the Cart-End all the same, where the loaded carts stood halted at the world’s edge — kobold diggers rising out of the side-cuts with mattocks and eyes like dulled coins, driven from behind by a Red Orc outrider who bawled them forward in the tower’s tongue and kept himself behind the line of them the whole while. It was ugly, and close, and won the way lane-fights are won, with the shield-wall and the flat voice and the spears working over. The orc died last, at the lip of the drop, contemptuous to the end of the vermin he’d spent. Rhovan’s third column took its first entries of the climb, and he wrote them small and hated the necessity: dead, kobolds, five. Dead, Red Orc outrider, one. Deaths that should not have been needed. The bill of the low war, presented at the usual counter.

And then the last kobold put its mattock down.

It came out of the side-cut after the fight was already over — half blind, its lamp long dead, following the sound of breathing. It saw the lamplight and the bodies and the wall of shields, and it did the thing that every recruit in the party had been taught at the trough-side in Roothollow, the sign the whole mercy of the climb turned upon: it laid the mattock on the stone, and knelt, and raised both hands to its own collar.

Ede killed it.

Rhovan did not see her decide, and that was the whole of what he saw: there was no deciding in it. The kobold’s hands went to its collar and her blade was already through it — one motion, old and oiled and perfect, a stroke from another war, from a mine-dark five years deep where things that knelt in front of Shieldwall had killed the ones who believed the kneeling. The sound the body made was small. The sound the water made, dripping somewhere off in the galleries, went on exactly as before, and was suddenly the loudest thing on the floor.

Nobody spoke. Brom looked away, once, and made himself look back. The splinter at Ede’s throat burned steady and said nothing at all, which was its own kind of entry.

Ede stood over the body with the blade still in her hand, and Rhovan watched her look at it — at the mattock lying flat on the stone, at the raised hands now not raised — and understood that she had arrived in the same heartbeat everyone else had, one motion too late, at the knowledge of what her hands had done before her orders could catch them.

He took out the grey book. He was aware of her eyes coming up as the oilcloth cracked, aware of the recruits watching him do it, aware that there were words that could be said — warcamp reflexes, five years, no one blames — and that saying any of them was not his work. His work was the columns. He set the book on his knee and wrote, in the third column, in a fair hand, without hurry and without a word:

Dead. One kobold, collared. Mattock grounded. Hands at the throat.

The pen-scratch was the only sound in the gallery. When he closed the book she was still looking at him, and he met it, and gave her nothing, because nothing was the truest thing he had. It went into the fourth account too, the unwritten one, and what he wrote there was: she will carry this better than absolution would let her. See that she is never absolved cheaply. The people he worked for would read a different report. They would get the fraction they had paid for.

Rustmaw they took in the flooded drift that evening, by Bergda’s counsel and the counts: a rust hound welded under an ore-crusher’s jaw, dragging lengths of rail behind it like a penance, hunting where the black water hid its footing. Pike found the dry spine of the old sump-causeway with a plumb-line and a delver’s patience; they baited the charge along it; Brom met the crusher-jaw with the boss of his shield and the whole floor rang like a struck bell; and Ede finished it in the water, and stood after, breathing, while the orange stain of the drift settled around her knees.

That night, walking the watch below the Headframe, Rhovan passed the banked fire where the captain sat alone and heard the splinter speaking, low, in the dark over her collar-bone, and did not stop walking, and heard it anyway.

I have carried one-and-forty climbers, it said, and every one of them was certain of something that killed somebody. A sword is quick, Ede Stonhal. It answers today’s question and deafens you to tomorrow’s. Spend your certainty slowly. It is the only coin in this tower that does not come back.

If she answered, the fire kept it. He wrote nothing in any column. There are entries a book cannot hold.

The Headframe’s great wheel turned that night for the first time in forty years, raising crews out of galleries the tower had written off, and the lamps of Lampfall came up all together — and if any soul in the party thought the low floors would now simply open before them, mercy by mercy, cure by cure, the third floor was waiting to correct them.

⁂

The Drowned Pasture lay under its own unmoving light like a dropped mirror: a water-meadow taken mid-flood and never allowed to drain, grey water sheeted to the horizon, hay-ricks standing out of it as rotting islands, willows trailing in their own reflections, and somewhere a sluice still turning, patient as a clerk, keeping the wrong things wet.

Old Nan Sedge poled out of the reeds to meet them standing up in a punt, a dry stick of a woman who had not, by her own accounting, been dry in a year. “The weir still works,” she told them, by way of a keeper’s greeting. “That’s the joke of it. The weir works, and everything it kept dry is under. You’ll walk the Sunken Drove if you’ve sense — the old road runs a hand’s breadth under the flood, straight across, and the beasts won’t cross it. Single file. Where the water goes white over your boots, that’s the bridge, and you’ll want to be nowhere else.”

The drove crossing was the longest half-league of the climb so far: thirteen climbers walking on water they could not see through, on a road they had to trust was there, while things moved in the reed-beds to either side and the white glare came off the flat grey sheet like a blade held to the eyes. A fence-wire eel took Lark’s calf open at the midpoint — a barbed length of the drowned fences come alive, wire and eel grown into one long wrongness — and Fell killed it on her sister’s leg without breaking stride, and it shed its wire as it died and slid away into the deep channel, cured, only an eel, while Lark bled and laughed and swore in marsh-cant all the rest of the way across.

The drowned lantern came at dusk, and it came for the wounded.

A cold light, drifting over the water, sweet and steady, making for the party’s island camp like a ferryman’s lamp coming out to the rescue — and every line of it wrong to anyone who looked slantwise. Rhovan looked slantwise for a living. A trapper’s lamp lost in the flood, the keeper had warned them, its dead flame soaked in spilled aether until the light learned to walk and to call; punts followed it onto the black cutting-reed and were found, later, lightened. He let it come on until it stopped pretending to drift, and then he put one shaft through the heart of the glow, and it did not die, because it had never lived: it guttered, wick-fashion, and came apart, and drained downward through the dark water like something being poured back where it was drawn from. Evicted, one lantern, said the second column. The oldest climbers’ word for it was the right one. You did not kill the Wrongmade. You turned it out of the house.

Sedgeback they broke at the mill-pool at dawn — a mire boar under weir-iron, green bronze plates shedding arrows like a roof sheds rain, charging through the shallows in a bow-wave that hid its feet. They denied it the flood: baited its charge up onto the drove-causeway where the water ran only boot-deep and every footfall showed, and Brom turned it, and the twins hamstrung it, and Ede put it down clean. By the time the party had breath again, Nan Sedge had the drain-cock’s wheel groaning, and the pasture had begun — floor by inch, as she put it — to remember it was a pasture. Fence-lines breaking the surface. Drowned hay steaming under the false light. An ancient tortoise, cured days since by someone’s passing spear and too old to bother being afraid, sunning itself on the emerging drove like the floor’s own seal of survivorship.

That was when the soldiers came up the lift behind them.

They came in two grey files, forty strong, up through the weir-arch from the floor below — which was open, of course; every lift the party had won stayed won, and Rhovan had walked past that fact three times without seeing what it meant coming up behind them. They wore slate-grey, and each carried a knotted cord at the shoulder, tallied with notches like Hobb Fennick’s stick, and they moved with the unhurried thoroughness of men who had done this many times on many floors and expected to do it many more. Where they passed the reed-beds they worked in pairs, spear and net, and what they killed they killed entirely, and counted.

At their head walked a big man gone iron-grey, older than Ede by twenty years, with a serjeant’s plain harness and a face like the north side of the Stone, and Rhovan knew his voice before he ever heard it — knew it the first breath the man opened his mouth — because he had been listening to a copy of it for weeks across a Roothollow drill-yard.

“Stonhal,” the man said.

“Serjeant Hale.” Her spine had changed before the name was out of her. Rhovan filed the sight of it: the whole shape of her stood to a lesson learned thirty years down, deeper than the Warcamp, deeper than the drink. The man who had taught her to fight. The voice her voice was built on.

“You put three jailers down in nine days with children and a poacher.” Hale’s eyes went along the party once, pricing it — lingering a half-breath on Rhovan, poacher, which Rhovan chose to find fair — and came back to Ede. “That’s good work spoiled by luck, and luck’s not doctrine. This is a writ of the militia charter.” He didn’t produce it; men like Hale are their own paper. “The Cullers hold wardship of the low floors, one through nineteen, and everything armed that walks them walks under my count. There’s a muster forming at the Beacon Field for the Kingsfield above it, and your lot are announced to it. You’ll have escort.”

“Escort,” Ede said.

“Escort,” said Hale, in the voice that had invented the flatness hers copied, and turned to give his files their order of march, the matter being — visibly, entirely — settled.

So the party that had walked onto floor one counting thirteen walked off floor three counted, by somebody else, among fifty-three, and the difference between those two arithmetics was the whole of what Rhovan put in the grey book that night. He sat late over it by the drying fence-lines, listening to the Cullers’ pickets call their tallies around the camp like clock-bells, and ruled a fresh page, and headed it, in the plain tongue of the low floors where anyone might read it:

The low war has an owner.

And underneath, in the fourth account, the one with no book: He taught her. She stood to him like a recruit. Above us there’s a meadow drowned in a hundred stolen banners and a king the tower crowned as a joke, and this man means to storm it by doctrine, kill-all, cull and count. What she does in that meadow will tell my employers what they bought. What he does there will tell me what she was, before she was ours.

The false light of the Drowned Pasture never dimmed for night. But at the weir-arch, where the freed lift ran its chain up out of sight, the clearest air showed a faint dark grain far overhead — the underside of the next country, a grain and no more, at the top of miles of sky — and what hung four floors above that no eye could reach; it came down the lifts as rumor instead, a meadow drowned in color that Nan Sedge swore was banners.

A hundred of them. All stolen. All waiting.

Chapter 4 — The King of Nothing

Nix counted cups, because counting was the whole of the work and most of the staying alive. Eleven cups on the board that morning: four chipped, two mended with wire, one true silver with a swan on it that Gnarl called the royal service, the crown treasure, the last civilized object in the world. Nix had poured into all eleven since lamp-trim. The king had thrown two, drunk one, and made the other eight into an audience.

“My lords,” said Gnarl, to the cups. “My ladies. My esteemed and colorful dead.”

The dead were the banners. A hundred of them and more — Nix had counted a hundred and six, then a hundred and nine, then given up; the king kept sending guards out to steal fresh ones. They hung from poles, from the muster-oaks, from the great Standard-Elm at the field’s middle, from the frame of the throne itself: colors of companies that no longer mustered, kings that no longer reigned, causes carried up the tower and dropped. In the dead air they did not fly. They hung, wept their dye into the mud, and listened.

The throne was crates. Nix wished to be exact about that, being the one who dusted it. Supply-crates of the old muster, stacked two high, lashed round with banner-poles, a plank and a cushion atop, Gnarl on the cushion, and the crown on Gnarl. The crown was the only part of it the tower had made. It sat low on his brow, iron and thorn-brass, and it did not come off — Nix had watched him try once, in the dark, three years back, and had poured very carefully for a month after and said nothing, one, two, never. From the crown the weld ran down under the king’s collar-bone and into the throne and into the field, and that was the truth of the court: the throne was not Gnarl’s seat. It was his sentence.

“Cup,” said Gnarl.

“Cup, majesty. Third of the morning, second of the good, last of the jug.” Nix poured. Fast hands, small pour, back away. He had held this post six years by three rules: count everything, expect nothing, be elsewhere when the cup flies.

Gnarl drank and looked out at his kingdom. The muster-meadow ran away from the throne in trampled grass and barbed banner-thistle, past the Long Camp’s empty tents, rotted horse-lines, and cold cook-fires, to the bluff at the far end where the stair-lift was cut into stone shaped like a triumphal arch, and past the bluff the downs of the stolen realm ran out to their own horizon. Ten kings’ marks were carved on that arch. Men had come to this field for a thousand years to gather armies, and the tower had stolen it a breath before the last gathering finished, and then — this was the joke, and Gnarl told it every day — the tower had crowned a goblin to keep it.

“Do you know what a king is, cup-bearer?” said Gnarl.

“A king is a man the field agrees on, majesty,” said Nix, because that was the day’s expected answer, one of nine, he had them numbered.

“A king,” said Gnarl, “is a joke the tower tells, and the field is the only one not laughing.” He raised the swan cup to the banners. “Patience, my dears. The house fills. The house always fills.”

Out past the arch, on the gate road, the horns began.

⁂

Nix knew horns the way he knew cups. Goblin horns were short and rude and said run. These were long, and they were brass, and they said form. He had never heard them before, and he had heard them all his life, in the marrow: somewhere below, a freed people was gathering, and gathering was the one thing the tower forbade.

They came through the hedged mouth of the gate road in ranks that were not good and did not need to be. Militia of the low floors — Nix picked out eel-trappers, pressers with orchard-bills, miners with hammers on their shoulders — and on the left of them, apart from them, a company in boiled leather the color of ash, moving quiet, killing everything, and everything they killed they killed twice. Cullers. Even the warrens knew Cullers.

Gnarl stood up as far as the weld allowed, which was not far, and flung out an arm to the field, and his voice went to the music-hall pitch he saved for endings.

“Ladies and lords! The late house is IN!” He was laughing. Nix, thirty paces back — never twenty-nine — saw that his eyes were not. “Honor- guard! Give them the show!”

The honor-guard went. They hated him — every collar on the field hated him, the joke crowned at their own expense, and Nix had hated him longest and poured anyway — but the collars took them down the meadow all the same, over-armored, under-fed, dying in the mud of a field where Men once made kings. The fevered things of the meadow went too, the Yoke driving all its cattle with one stick: wolves tangled in dragging colors, courier-hounds on their dead routes, a parade-horse screaming, riderless, huge. Where the militia’s line met them it bent, and held, and one voice held it — flat, carrying, unhurried, a serjeant’s voice with a captain under it.

“Wall. Wall. Step and close.”

Nix stopped watching the battle then, because an arrow went through the tent-fly above his head, and he did what six years of court had trained into him: he dropped the cup and got behind something.

The something was a banner.

It lay where a guard had dropped it, off the pile of taken climbers’ colors Gnarl kept nearest the throne. It was grey. It had a device Nix had dusted a hundred times and never once asked about — a wall of squared stones, and above the wall an open hand — and it was sewn heavy, double-heavy, war-heavy, and when Nix hauled it up on its cracked pole and huddled in behind the cloth, an arrow hit it and did not come through. He counted his blessings. He arrived at one. He held onto it.

The line came up the meadow. The tents of the Long Camp went over. The banner-poles went down like a hedge falling, a hundred dead causes dropping into the mud, and Nix, peering around his wall of cloth, watched the ash-grey company reach the goblins who had thrown their weapons down at the throne-fence and kill them where they knelt. Quick and orderly. A man with iron in his beard walked behind the Cullers’ line, not hurrying, pointing left, pointing right, the way a farmer points at rows.

“Nothing collared leaves the field,” the man said, not loudly, and his company made it true. Nix stopped counting the dead — a thing he had never once managed before — and it frightened him worse than the arrows.

Then the woman with the serjeant’s voice put her shoulder through the throne-tent’s lacing, and the court of the Kingsfield fell in behind her, and the last act began.

⁂

Nix saw it from behind the grey banner, twelve paces from the throne, which was too close and also the safest ground on the field, and both of those were his whole life in one place, so he stayed.

She was of Men, past the middle of her life, grey woven through the braid, a shield on her arm with the paint battered off it. Behind her an elf with a bow half-drawn, watching everything and aiming at nothing. At her collar-bone a splinter of crystal burned steady, and a dry voice came off it that was not hers. Welded to the throne, it said. Root and crown. He cannot rise, and the working is a collar, whatever shape they hammered it into.

“Gnarl,” said the woman.

“Majesty,” said Gnarl, agreeably, correcting her. He had the swan cup still, or again. His guard was dead or fled or kneeling, his banners were down, and his sword — the great notched thing the tower had given him, too big for any goblin to lift — stood planted in the mud, done with. The crown sat askew. He did not straighten it. “You’ve brought a crowd at last. Do you know how long I’ve held this venue on promises?”

“It’s over,” she said. “Your garrison’s broken. Whoever kneels, we keep. Whoever runs, we let run.” She said it loudly, for the field, and Nix heard goblins put iron down behind him. “That leaves you.”

“It always did,” said Gnarl.

The elf came forward then, and out of his sleeve he took a skein of pale cord, and even Nix, who knew no working grander than a steady pour, felt his teeth ache at it. Silver script ran along the cord like light along a wet hair.

“A Break-collar,” the elf said. “Written small. Perhaps six hands living can write one; my people paid for this the way you’d ransom a town. Spoken once, it is spent, and the collar it is spoken over is ended — crown, weld, root and all. We were told to spend it on something that would matter. A king would matter.”

The field had gone very quiet. The woman looked at Gnarl and her voice came down off its wall. “No one else has to die a jailer,” she said. “You least of all. Yours were the first people taken. The tower crowned you to mock them. Let us take the mockery off you, and be a goblin among goblins, and live.”

And Nix, twelve paces off, behind a dead company’s banner, watched his king listen to the only kind thing anyone had said to him in thirty years, and watched him weigh it, and watched him put it down.

“A goblin among goblins,” said Gnarl softly. “Madam, there are ten thousand of those, and the tower has a use for every one.” He turned the swan cup in his fingers. “Take off the crown, and what remains? Nothing remains. I have been nothing — I was nothing for years before the tower found me, and it saw exactly what I was, and crowned me for it. That is more than any of you ever offered.” His voice climbed back to the pitch for endings, and he spread his arms to the fallen banners as to a full house. “I am the King of Nothing, and nothing is a realm, my dears — it is the only realm the tower deals in honestly. I’ll not be pardoned out of my own punchline.”

“You’ll die for a joke,” said the woman.

“Every king does,” said Gnarl. “The good ones know it’s theirs.” He set the swan cup down on the arm of the crate-throne, with care, the way you set down the last civilized object in the world, and he took up the great notched sword that no goblin could lift, and lifted it.

“Applause at the end,” he said. “That is the whole of the contract.”

She gave him a soldier’s finish. One stroke, under the crown. Nix counted it, because counting was how he wept: one.

The crown came off with him. It rolled in the mud and lay there, iron and thorn-brass, no one’s. Under the arch of the ten kings the stair- lift’s stone split its seam with a sound like a held breath let go, and light came down the gap; and everywhere on the field, goblins felt the Yoke go silent and stood in the wreck of their watch-fires, feeling the whip lift for the first time in their lives. Most ran for the opened lift, up, toward the next Warden’s signal, because up was the only direction the collar had ever taught them. A few threw the collars down and walked away downfield, toward Roothollow, unsure how walking was done when nobody drove you.

Nix stood behind the grey banner and did not run, one, and did not walk away, two, and could not have said what the third thing was, only that he was doing it.

⁂

The elf still held the skein of silver script. Half its light had gone out of it — unsealed for the offer; once shown its work, a working like that is spent whether it is spoken or not, and the cost stood in the elf’s face like a debt called early. He looked at the dead king, and asked the woman a question with his eyebrows.

She was looking at Nix. At the banner, first — something had gone through her when she saw the banner, a blade’s worth of something — and then at the goblin holding it up between himself and the world with both hands and one knee.

“You,” she said. “Cup-bearer.”

“Majesty,” said Nix, from habit, and could have bitten his tongue off. “Captain. Serjeant. Whichever’s biggest, that one, twice.”

“Why are you still here?”

Nix considered the question. “Because it’s the first field I ever stood on where the man doing the counting wasn’t counting me,” he said. “And because this wall stops arrows, and I’ve been hiding behind worse walls all my life, and this one’s better, that’s all, it’s a good wall, I’d like to keep it.”

The dry voice from her collar-bone spoke. He held his post through the battle. Forty-one climbers I have carried, and I have seen generals do worse.

The woman looked at the elf. The elf looked at the half-spent skein, and shrugged with one shoulder — it is already dying; let it die working — and knelt in the mud in front of a goblin cup-bearer as he would have knelt for a king, and spoke the script off the cord.

It did not hurt. That was the part Nix told worst, ever afterward, because there was nothing to tell: a weight he had carried so long he had stopped calling it weight was, between one heartbeat and the next, not. The collar opened like a hand unclenching. He caught it as it fell, the way he’d catch a dropped cup — and then he understood what he was holding, and he threw it down at the foot of the crate-throne, hard, in front of the crown, for whatever was left of the court to see what its cup-bearer thought of the service.

“That’s my notice given,” he said, three words where one would have done, and his voice going all directions. “That’s me resigned.”

The woman put out her hand for the banner-pole, and Nix’s stomach dropped, one wall gone, none left — and she only set the pole upright, squared it in his grip, and folded his fingers back around it.

“It’s kept people behind it before,” she said. “Carry it where I can see it.”

⁂

They did not climb that day. There was a field to clear, and dead of four kinds to do four things with, and the muster-folk of Bannerline to call up out of hiding among the rotting standards. Their keeper came first — a muster-serjeant, Rung by name, with a color kept folded and dry under her coat through the whole occupation — and at dusk-lamp she climbed the Standard-Elm and raised it, a true color on the king’s own tree, and the field gave up its people, drummers and farriers and banner-wrights walking out toward the elm as if the horns of a thousand years had all sounded at once. The gathering the tower had frozen a breath too soon stood on the Kingsfield and finished itself.

Nix counted them until he could not.

He kept the grey banner. No one claimed it; the woman — Ede, the militia called her, and something older and harder when they thought she couldn’t hear — would not look at it long, and would not let anyone take it from him either. Wall and open hand. He asked the elf whose it had been, and got a fraction: “A company’s.” He asked the splinter of crystal, and got: Hers.

In the morning the host went up through the arch of the ten kings, and Nix went with it, the first of his people to climb that stair free since the stair was built, holding a dead company’s colors to hide behind and hiding behind nothing, counting the steps — forty-one, forty-two — because counting was how he sang. Above lay the counting country, the giants’ great ledger-halls, and the rumor was already running down the column like spilled coin: the lifts up there stood open, men of Roothollow had hung chains across them, and the chains had a price on.

Nix had been climbing all his life in one collar or another. He knew before anyone said it that the next floor would want paying.

Chapter 5 — The Ledger of Gates

Rhovan of the Grey Rides told the truth for a living, in fractions, and he had learned the trade in rooms like this one: a hall too large for its lamps, full of paper that remembered more than the people did.

The Counting Halls ran away from him in shelf-canyons of ledger-stone and brass, glittering faintly where the lamplight caught the coin-dust hanging in the air. Abacuses the size of gates hung frozen mid-count overhead. Somewhere off in the stacks a ledger wisp was reading numbers aloud to nobody, a clerk’s ghost caught in an unfinished sum, and the host’s children had already learned to walk wide of it. The van had broken the Warden here three days before Rhovan came up — Brassbone, a vault-beetle welded round a core of tally-gears, which had counted every blow it took and answered in kind until Ede stopped feeding it blows and fed it a lie of numbers instead, and the count choked, and the great ledger-door of the stair-lift read out its final balance and opened.

Freed, for everyone, forever. That was the canon of the climb, cut into the Stone at Roothollow under every name: a lift once opened can never be shut again.

No one had thought to write down that it could be rented.

The chain was hung across the stair-lift’s arch at the height of a man’s chest, good new iron, and coins had been nailed to the post beside it in a neat rising column — the tariff, displayed the way a fair displays prizes. A head-price for a man. A head-and-a-half for a giant. A price for a mule, a price for a barrel, a price for a bale of arrows going up to the war. The men who kept it wore good boots and coin-belts and the gate-companies’ grey, and they were not soldiers, and were not pretending to be. There were nine of them. Rhovan had counted the same way Nix counted, out of the same instinct, though he would not have admitted the kinship.

Below the chain the queue stood. That was the part Ede could not look at and could not stop looking at: muster-folk of Bannerline with their kit on their backs, come up to join the rising; Ledgerstone clerks with their salvaged books; a family of Weirsend eel-trappers who had walked free out of a floor their grandmothers were stolen onto, forty years of waiting paid off at last — stopped at an open door by men of their own kind, for coin.

“Read it to me again,” Ede said.

The toll-captain — Weck, a round, patient, untroubled man — held up his warrant so the seals caught the light. He had shown it four times and would show it forty. “By charter of the gate-companies of Roothollow,” he said, “and under the seal of Gilder Voss, Tollmaster-General: maintenance of freed gates, upkeep of stairs, surety of passage. All lawful, captain. All stamped.”

“The lift was freed three days ago. You’ve maintained nothing but the chain.”

“The chain’s the upkeep,” said Weck, comfortably. “Anarchy’s free, captain. Order costs.”

At Ede’s shoulder the splinter burned. In eighty years, said Sliver, dry as coin-dust, I have watched this tower try every cruelty it knows. It never once thought of this. He undercut the Demon King with a toll-chain.

Serjeant Hale’s Cullers stood off to the left in their ash-grey, watching the Tollmen the way they watched everything, sorting it into what wanted killing now and what wanted killing later. Hale himself said only, “Nine men. One order,” and let it hang. And that was the trap in it, Rhovan saw, laid by no one and worse for it: cut the chain and cut nine men of Roothollow down before the queue they were robbing, and the rising became a thing that killed Men at open gates — and every toll-post from here to the Made would buy itself a garrison and call it defense. Voss did not need to win the argument. He only needed Ede to end it with iron.

“No one touches them,” Ede said. “We do not pay, and we do not cut. Not today.”

“The bread pays,” Weck observed, mild as milk, nodding at the supply- sledges. “Or the bread waits.”

Rhovan watched Ede take that, and bank it, and walk away, and he made his own tally, in the currency he dealt in. Warrants have ledgers behind them. Ledgers have rooms. Rooms have locks, and locks have a whole family of honest little truths they will tell a patient elf in the dark.

“Where are you going?” Nix asked him at the fire-line, fast, three questions in one, as ever. “You’ve got your quiet boots on, you’ve got your nothing-face on, where?”

“To do some maintenance,” said Rhovan.

⁂

The Tollmen had requisitioned a counting-vault off the Shelf-Maze for their strong-room, which told Rhovan most of what he needed before he ever touched the door: men who keep books they are proud of leave them at the post, under the lamp, for showing. Books that get walked into a giants’ vault at night are books with two kinds of truth in them.

He went in at the hour when the paper-fungus drifts — the halls’ one blessing, for his purposes, since the Tollmen’s watch spent it indoors with the shutters fast, having learned what settled on warmth. Tolla Brasspen, chief reckoner of Ledgerstone, had drawn him the vault’s approach on the back of a cancelled account and asked no questions aloud, though her face had asked one, and he had paid it a fraction: “Your halls kept honest count for a thousand years. Someone up here is keeping the other kind.” That had been enough for her. It was true, too. Rhovan told the truth whenever it would do the work of a lie.

The tally-lock was giants’ work, older than the Tollmen, and it opened not to a key but to a correct reckoning; the Tollmen had not changed it, because they could not read it. Brasspen could. He turned the sum she had taught him — so much ore raised, so much owed, so much paid, the mountain’s own arithmetic — and the door heard its answer and let him in as courteously as a clerk.
