The toll-ledger lay on a shelf between a strongbox and a wheel of sealing wax. He did not touch the strongbox. Silver leaves marks on a man; paper only leaves it on the world.

He read for the time a lamp takes to burn a finger’s width, and it was what he had expected, and it bored him, and he blessed it for boring him: the skim. Two counts kept side by side, the tariff taken at the chain and the tariff reported to Voss, and the difference sleeping in Weck’s strongbox. Every toll-post since coin was minted has kept that second column. It was leverage, it would open the chain, and an hour earlier it was all he had come for.

Then he turned the back leaves, where the ledger had been sewn onto an older book — the gate-companies’ remittance-book, brought up from Roothollow, because the Tollmen’s chain was new but their accounts were not — and the boredom went out of him like heat out of iron.

The entries in the old book ran level and dull for years. Gate-fees, carters’ bonds, lamp-oil, wages. And then, alone on its page, ruled off from the entries above and below it as if the clerk himself had not wanted it touching the honest sums:

Received of a Roothollow hand, by warrant, the sum entire — carried up by the posts of the companies, stage by stage, to the high tower’s receiver, and there discharged. No goods. No consignment. Settlement of account.

No goods. Money does not climb a tower where money buys nothing — unless the thing it bought was already sold, and delivered, and the tower was settling. Payment did not come down for the Shieldwall Company. Payment went up — and Rhovan set his fingertip under the date, and held it there, and made himself read it three times, because an agent who believes a thing at first reading will believe anything.

He carried, sewn into his coat’s lining, the ledger-page that had started all of this — the page he had laid in front of a sober, retired, furious woman in Roothollow half a year ago, the page that proved the Red Warcamp had been given her company’s route before her company ever climbed. He did not need to take it out. He knew its week the way other men know their name-day.

The same week. The sale below; the settlement above; seven days between, the time a laden post takes to climb twenty floors.

And at the foot of the entry, where a name should have sat, there was no name. There was a warrant-mark, stamped in the gate-companies’ fashion: Roothollow’s own device, the hollow root under the bar, and in its quarter the small countersign that meant a private hand — some one person, of the town, with the standing to move coin under the companies’ seal and the sense not to sign for it.

Someone in Roothollow had signed the tower’s receipt for Ede’s company. Someone from home.

Rhovan stood in a strong-room a mile inside a stolen mountain and heard his own heart, and around him the coin-dust glittered and settled on the shelves, patient, the way the truth settles on everything given time. Then he took out his copying-lead and did his work: the skim, the entry, the date, the mark, each of them twice.

He put the ledger back with its dust squared, and reckoned the door shut behind him, and walked back through the drifting stacks dividing what he had learned into shares — because that was his trade, and because, he told himself, a truth this heavy dropped whole on Ede Stonhal would break something in her that the climb still needed. The skim was Weck’s, to be spent at the chain tomorrow. The payment and the week were Ede’s; she had earned them at prices he did not like to think about. The mark, the little countersign, the shape of a single private hand — that was the Wake’s, and went that same night in cipher down the column, in the quill-work of a bored elf writing home.

Three shares, honestly divided. He would have a long climb to learn what he learned too late: that a truth divided in three is three lies, and that he had just written the first letter of the last one.

⁂

At the chain, in the morning, Rhovan spent his first share.

He gave Weck the numbers quietly, at the post, in the tone one book- keeping man uses to another, and watched the comfort leave the toll- captain’s face by weights and measures. He did not threaten. He recited. The skim, the sum in the strongbox, the tariff Voss believed and the tariff Voss was owed — and the only word anything like a blade came at the end, and was one syllable: “Choose.”

The chain came down before the lamp above the arch had guttered. Weck posted it as an amnesty of the gate-companies — passage free to the freed, in honor of the rising — which was, Rhovan thought, the most Roothollow sentence ever nailed to a post: a robbery, repented in the grammar of a gift. The queue went up. The eel-trapper family crossed under the arch, last of a forty-year wait discharged at no charge, and Ede stood watching with her jaw set wrong.

Rhovan gave her the second share on the stairs, plainly, both facts and no third: money had gone up the tower through the Tollmen’s posts, no goods against it; and its date sat in the same week as the sale of her company. He watched it land. She did not rage. She had spent her rage years ago on cheaper truths. She only said, “Through Roothollow,” not as a question, and looked back down the stair a long breath — down the shaft of the freed lift, down the column, toward home — and then turned and climbed, which was the whole of her answer, and the only one the climb ever accepts.

He did not give her the mark. He fed the fraction to himself the way he always did: when it means something. When it is more than a stamp. When telling her arms her instead of gutting her. An elf can keep a truth warm for years. It is the trade’s one talent.

⁂

They made the next five floors in five weeks, and the war let them be small weeks, which the rising had learned to distrust and take anyway.

On the Shearing Downs the chalk lay open under the false light like a page, and shear-mad rams came down the wind at the column and were cured one by one, shaking their great heads, trotting away ordinary. The downs were three days wide, bare white country rolling to a white horizon with the wind never once stopping, the dew-ponds skinned grey with wool-mould and every fold-wall on the high ground penning nothing. Ewan Woolgate walked a day of it beside the van with his crook and his flock-book, counting a flock the Theft had scattered, because the count was hopeless and the keeping of it was not; and the night the column lay at Woolgate the shepherds raised the broken beacon on the Long Barrow, and folk came in off the far combes who had been hiding since the shearing. At the Eaves the dark wood stood full of dead signals, the hum of messages sent an age ago to no one; the edge-elves of Lastlamp came out to see elves climbing armed, and said almost nothing, and gave them almost everything — lamp-oil, guides, the wood’s edge-paths. Under the boots the listening-moss gave back scraps of the dead lamp-song, and from the crown of the Watchpine, strung with old signal-copper, a scout could look out over gloom lying level to the floor’s own horizon — a night the width of a country, with one watch-fire in it. Rhovan walked those under his hood. Whatever else the Grey Rides had made of him, it had begun somewhere like this, and he told no one, which was itself a fraction.

In the Gear Galleries the machinists of Sprocket Row threw the master clutch for the first time since the Theft, and the halls’ endless rumble stopped, and giants wept at a silence. The Grinding Pit gave up its pressed pit-fighters, Red Orcs with chain-scarred fists who looked at the freed camp with no idea what to do in it; three stayed, grim as tax, and Ede took them, over Hale’s one flat objection: “You’ll want killing men soon enough, captain. You needn’t recruit them from the kennel.” Above the Pit lay the Cable Roots, and the column walked that country four days on lines the color of old blood: a cavern strung floor to roof with copper grown root-thick, live bundles humming and spitting blue static into a dark the sparks lit in stutters. Fend Wiregate, the line-master, one hand scarred white, led them the whole way along the dead lines out of the Deadline Camp’s logbooks, reading the tangle by the pitch of its static, and nobody strayed an arm’s length, and nobody needed telling twice. The column crossed the Hollow Lanes behind Cobb Hedgeharbor’s notched staff, hedge-blind wolves striking from the joining lanes and going cured down the green tunnels, and came up at last into the open at Hedgeharbor, sixteen floors above the world’s floor, with the mine-tier’s last country above them and everyone’s boots done for.

The word was waiting for them there, having come up the lanes faster than baggage ever moves: a giant had arrived at Sprocket Row, alone, off the freed lifts from below. A woman of the forge-realm, grey with road-dust, who had asked one question at every landing since Roothollow — where is the captain called Stonhal — and who carried, wrapped in oil-cloth and iron bands like something that might otherwise get loose, a sheaf of the tower’s own papers.

“Asking for me by name,” Ede said.

“By name,” said the runner, “and captain — she wouldn’t set the papers down. Not to eat. Not to sleep. The machinists offered her a strong-box and she looked at them like they’d offered to hold her hands to a forge.”

Ede turned back down the lanes that same evening, and Rhovan went with her, and found he was cataloguing already, out of habit: a giant, off the lifts, alone, with documents she would not be parted from and a question she asked by name. In his trade that shape had a meaning. In his trade, that shape was a confession, walking uphill to be heard.

He was, for once in his fractional life, entirely right.

Chapter 6 — The Adit

While she waited for the captain, Vega Coalbrow mended the bearing.

It was the great flywheel’s outboard bearing, and it had been running dry and half a hair out of true for longer than the machinists of Sprocket Row wanted to say in front of a stranger, because Grib Sprocket had been keeping the wheel balanced by ear for thirty years and his ear had grown old along with the wheel. Vega did not say any of that either. She asked for the loan of a jack and a ladle of grease, and she set her shoulder under a housing that three men could not have shifted, and she took up the slack the way you take up a grief — slowly, evenly, all the way to the end of the thread, so that nothing is left loose to work itself worse. It cost her the evening. She had the evening. The wheel came true, and the hall’s note came true with it, and old Grib stood listening to it with his eyes shut and did not trust himself to speak, and that was all the payment there was, and it was plenty.

She was oiling the ladle when the captain came — a woman of Men, worn sharp, with an elf at her shoulder and a goblin at her heel carrying a grey banner bigger than himself. Vega stood. She was used to what happened when she stood, and waited through it, patient as a doorway.

“You’ve been asking for me by name,” said Ede Stonhal.

“I have,” said Vega. “My name is Vega Coalbrow. I am a forge-matron of the mountain folk, out of Roothollow these twenty years, and before I say anything else to you I am going to show you papers that I have carried up sixteen floors, because if I talk first I will talk myself kind, and the papers do not have that failing.”

She unwrapped the oil-cloth. She unlocked the iron bands. She had made the bands herself, and the lock; she had wanted the carrying of it to be hard.

The manifests were the tower’s own paper, salvaged out of the Counting Halls’ unpicking and sent down the column to Roothollow with a hundred other recovered accounts, for the freed to search for their dead in. Cage-work, the sheets said. Collar-stock, the sheets said. Lock-plates, hasps, chain — so many hundredweight, received in good order at the twentieth landing, for the use of the garrison there. And at the foot of every sheet, stamped into the tally-wax the way a smith stamps her warrant into steel that she is proud of, a maker’s mark: a brow, and under it three sparks.

“That is my mark,” Vega said. “I will say it plainly, and once, and you may do as you like with me after. The steel of the cages on the floor above us came off my anvils. I forged it for a factor who paid in good coin and changed his name every season, and he told me it was drove-chain and beast-collars and gate-locks for the low farms, and I had lean years and two apprentices to feed, and so I chose — you must hear the word, captain, I chose — not to wonder why a drover would want a lock I could not pick myself.” She laid her hand flat over the mark, and it covered it entire, and that did not help. “I make things that hold. It is the one gift I have. Somebody borrowed it, and I let them, and your people are behind it.”

The camp had gone quiet around them, hall-quiet, so the flywheel’s true note ran under everything like water under ice.

“Why bring it to me?” said Ede. “Roothollow has magistrates.”

“Because a magistrate would fine me,” said Vega, “and I can pay a fine, and that is why it would be worthless.” She knelt, which brought her eyes only a little above the captain’s own, and she said the thing she had climbed sixteen floors to say, in order, to the end. “Every lock has two doors in it. The one it was forged for, and the one the smith leaves in it, the flaw only she knows, because no honest smith in the mountains will make a thing she cannot unmake. I know my locks, captain. I know their tempers, and their prides, and where each one keeps its fear. Take me up to the warcamp, and put me at the cages, and I will open every door I ever shut on your people. And after that, if there is an after, I will spend what’s left of me arming the freed, and mending what this tower has broken, until the account is paid or I am.”

The elf said nothing, in the way of a man filing everything. The goblin counted the manifests aloud, softly — nine sheets, nine — as if putting a number on a thing could tame it. And the captain looked at the giant kneeling in the grease-light with her mark under her hand, and Vega watched her decide, and saw — she told no one this, for years — that it was not mercy and was not arithmetic. It was recognition. One woman who had led her people into cages, looking at one woman who had forged them.

“Get up,” said Ede. “We march on the vaults at lamp-trim. You’ll want a hammer.”

“I brought four,” said Vega.

⁂

The approach country took them nine days, and Vega mended her way up it, because her hands shook if she left them empty.

On the Glass Flats the mirages walked, and the heat lied in sheets, and the gleaners of Shardside taught the column the blindfold trick — trust the feet, the glass rings true or it rings false — which suited Vega, who had trusted her feet over her eyes since girlhood, the mountain way. The flats tried her once anyway: showed her, off on the shimmer, a forge with its fire lit and two apprentices at the bellows, waving her over, no older than the day she’d sold her first lock. She stood a long breath looking at it. Then she said, aloud, “You have built it wrong. My forge has the flue on the north wall,” and walked on, and the heat-ghost gave her up for a bad bargain.

At the Millrace she re-hung a wheel that had run crooked since the Theft, and the millers ground true flour under it the same evening, and she watched Nix eat griddle-bread so hot it burned him, counting his bites, and found she was smiling, which she had not expected to do again on this climb. It was there the sappers took on the millers’ blasting-powder — weir-clearing charge, kept bone-dry in the Drowned Mill’s loft through everything — and Vega packed the casks herself, each one wedged and wrapped and cradled the way she’d carry eggs, because powder is only a servant while somebody respects it.

Then the vaults.

The Fusion Vaults were her people’s work and her people’s shame now: a country of racked aether-rods glowing their sick perpetual dusk, where shadows hummed and had edges, and the vault-crews of Half-Light came out lead-aproned and grey to meet the host, counting their exposures even as they cheered. The Warden they called Coreburn held the lead blast-door at the floor’s head — a forge-remnant grown vast, half- melted iron walking, burning from the inside around a cracked rod. The van broke it the hard way, in relays, no shame in any of it; Vega’s part was after, and it was the part she was made for: drawing the cracked rod from the cooling wreck with tongs she re-jawed on the spot, sleeving it in lead, making the terrible thing merely dangerous. A cure is quick. Mending takes longer, and holds.

The blast-door swung on the last floor before the warcamp, and the adit lay beyond — the old approach-gallery, giants’ work, arrow- straight into the dark with the drums of the twentieth floor coming down it, faint and endless, like a heart heard through a wall.

And at the mouth of the adit, the dig-crew put down its tools.

⁂

They were kobolds, twelve of them, a rod-handling crew spent down to the wick — glow-sick, every one, luminous at the knuckles and the throat where the sickness pools, set by the garrison above to cut a counter-gallery so the warcamp might drop the stair behind them if the climb came. The Yoke had gone quiet on the floor when Coreburn fell, and they had felt their orders go quiet with it, and they had talked among themselves in the dark, and then they had done the bravest thing Vega ever saw done on the Ascent. They came out to the lamp-line with their picks laid down and their hands open and their collars shown, kneeling, all twelve in a row on the stone — trusting, on no evidence the tower had ever given them, that the people coming up the gallery were better than the people who had sent them down it.

Ede was forward at the shaft-head with the sappers. What came up the adit first was the rear-sweep, and the rear-sweep was Cullers.

Vega was thirty paces back, helping a vault-crew rack the last loose rods, when the sound began, and her hands knew it before she did, because it was a sound out of her own trade: steel going into something soft, in rhythm, workmanlike. No battle-noise. No cries but short ones. By the time she had turned and covered the ground — giants cover ground, when they choose — it was nearly finished. Eleven kobolds lay where they had knelt. They had not run. Kneeling was the one card they had thought to play, and they had gone on playing it while it was trumped, one, and the next, and the next, in good order, down the row.

Serjeant Hale stood over the twelfth with his blade drawn back, and between his blade and the kobold stood the goblin.

Nix had no weapon. He had the banner, the grey one, wall and open hand, and he had planted it in front of a glow-sick kobold child of a conscript race that goblins had no love for, and he was holding the pole with both hands, and he was shaking so hard the cloth above him shivered as if in wind. His voice, when it came, came fast and counting, because that was the only way it knew.

“Eleven,” he said. “You’ve got eleven. Eleven of twelve, that’s your count, that’s plenty, you don’t need the twelfth, you never needed the first — ” and then, done counting, with everything in him: “They put their picks down.”

“A collar is a uniform,” said Hale. He was not angry. Vega understood, later, that this was the whole horror of the man: he was never angry. “Doctrine’s plain, goblin. What’s collared serves the tower, and what serves the tower dies, and whether it kneels first is between it and whatever gods conscripts keep. Stand off my line.”

“Then the doctrine’s counted me,” said Nix, not moving. “Say it’s counted me. I wore one twenty years, it’s on the floor of the Kingsfield if you want to go and cull it — “

Hale’s blade did not waver, and the ash-grey line behind him did not waver, and Vega — arriving, enormous, too late for eleven and perhaps in time for one — saw the next heartbeat the way a smith sees a crack running in cooling steel: certain, and only not yet.

Ede came through the Cullers’ line from behind, from the shaft-head, at a dead run that ended in a stillness, and put herself between the blade and the banner without a word. Then she drew. She did it slowly, the way you do a thing you can never take back, and she laid her point low, at guard, at her own teacher.

“Serjeant Hale,” she said. “Put it up.”

“You’re interposing for the enemy, captain.”

“I’m interposing for my standard-bearer,” said Ede, “who is stood in front of a surrendered prisoner, on a freed floor, over the bodies of eleven more. Look down, Hale. Look at what your doctrine holds when it’s held all the way to the floor.”

And he looked. Vega gave him that much, always, in every telling: he looked. The lamp-light stood in the blood between the cobbles, and the twelfth kobold breathed in whistles behind the banner-cloth, and something crossed the old serjeant’s face that had no doctrine in it — crossed it, and found nothing to hold onto, and went out.

“I taught you what a line is for,” he said.

“You taught me to hold one,” said Ede. “Hold this: you and yours are out of my column. Sweep behind us no more. Guard the gates you’ve taken, kill what comes at you armed, and when this war has a law, it will come looking for the ledger of today. Until then keep west of my fires, serjeant, because I am the only thing on this floor arguing against the goblin’s arithmetic, and I am losing.”

For three long breaths the two lines stood, ash-grey and mudded motley, and the oldest quarrel in the world stood with them: whether a thing that surrenders is a person or a problem. Then Hale put up his blade, and turned his company with a hand-sign, and the Cullers went back down the gallery in perfect order, taking their dead-count of nobody and leaving their work on the ground. He did not look back. Vega had mended enough broken things to know one walking away from her when she saw it.

⁂

They buried the eleven at the adit’s mouth, in the giants’ fashion, under raised stone, because Vega asked for it and dug first, and no one in that column was going to tell a grieving giant with four hammers that conscripts go in a pit unmarked. Pressed dead. Deaths that should not have been needed — the shardminds’ own word for it, and the only epitaph the twelve had, eleven under stone and the twelfth wrapped in a militia cloak with fever-light coming through at the knuckles, asleep against the banner-pole with a fistful of Nix’s sleeve in one hand.

Vega sat by the cairn through the camp’s first watch and mended nothing. Some things are not for mending. Some things you are only supposed to carry — the mountain folk teach that early — and she was still learning the difference in her fortieth year at the anvil, and suspected the learning was the climb, the whole of it, for her.

Up the adit’s dark, close now, always, the drums beat the march that never departs. Somewhere above the stone the Red Warcamp burned its watch-fires over a caged people. Somewhere in it stood her steel.

Ede came out of the lamp-line near watch-end and stood by the cairn, and for a while said nothing, in the manner of a woman out of practice at asking.

“The cages,” she said at last. “You’re sure of your two doors.”

“I am sure of my steel,” said Vega. “I made it well. That has become the best news on this floor, captain, because it means I know it to the grain — and tomorrow I am going to stand at those locks and be the flaw the smith left in them.”

Ede nodded, slowly, and looked up the adit toward the drums, and Vega watched the muster begin behind her eyes — three companies, and the mine-dark, and twenty years of names — before a word of it was spoken aloud.

“Then sleep,” the captain said. “We open the mountain tomorrow.”

Chapter 7 — The Red Warcamp

Ede Stonhal had cut thirty names into the Stone of the Climb with her own hand, in the winter she stopped drinking, one name a night because her wrist would not stay true for more. Five years she had lived below a tower with her company’s tomb twenty floors up it. Now she stood in the mine-dark under that floor, with the drums coming down through a hundred feet of stone — boom, and boom, and boom, the march that never departs — and she made herself say the truth of it in her own skull, once, before she said anything to anyone else.

Not a tomb. Skarn keeps cages. Some of them are alive, and you are going to walk into the ground where you lost them, and this time the plan has to be better than you were.

It is, said Sliver, from her collar-bone, uninvited as always. I have audited it twice. It has one flaw only, which is that you intend to be at the front of it.

“That’s the load-bearing part,” Ede said, and went out to the lamps.

Three companies stood to in the assembly-gallery, and she walked the line of them the way a smith walks a weld, looking for the cold spots.

The Muster of Bannerline held the right: pikes and bills and the good brass horns, muster-folk who had waited eighty years to finish gathering and did not intend to be late for it, Serjeant Mella Rung at their head with the True Color cased on her shoulder. They would go at the warcamp’s gate — the front door, the loud door — with the rams and the two ballistae built on the Gear Galleries’ beds. Their work was to be believed. Skarn had to read them as the whole attack, and he would, because a frontal storm against a shield-wall was exactly the mistake the tower expected of freed farmers.

The Wrights held the center: miners of the freed adits, machinists of Sprocket Row, millers of Wheelhouse with their powder-casks slung in cradles, vault-crews of Half-Light, Grib Sprocket standing among them listening to the drums the way he listened to bearings. Their war was underneath. The giants’ mine had a throat the orcs had never dug and did not know: the main downshaft, capped and pitched over with tents. The Wrights had found the cap from beneath — giants’ work answers giants — and packed the seams with the millers’ weir-charge. Their work was the door in the floor.

And the Van held the left, which meant it held the shaft: Ede’s own, the smallest of the three and the strangest muster she had ever signed — veterans of nine freed floors, three Red Orc deserters out of the Grinding Pit, an elf of the Grey Rides with his quiver full and his opinions filed, a forge-matron with four hammers, and a goblin with a banner. Their work was the pens. Up the shaft the heartbeat after it opened, through the tent-lines while the camp’s face was at the gate, and open the cages before any blade in that camp remembered what hostages are for.

No Cullers. West of her fires, holding the taken gates below, exactly as ordered — and the lack of them sat in the plan where a reserve should have been, and she had signed that too, and would sign it again. An army that kills the surrendered is not a reserve. It is a second enemy, waiting for your worst hour.

She stopped at the Van’s front, where Vega knelt re-wrapping the haft of the smallest hammer, and Nix stood under the grey banner with the glow-sick kobold child asleep against the pole, and she said what captains say, which is nothing anyone remembers after, and then the thing she had climbed five years to say.

“The people in those cages watched me leave this floor without them. Whatever they say to me when the doors open — that’s mine to stand. Your work ends at the locks. Understood?”

“Understood,” said Vega, “though I will tell you, captain, that you have described my work also, and we will see whose account is paid first.”

Wrathsmoke on the wind above, said Sliver. The camp’s lichen breathes rage. You have each had the antidote; you will each still feel it. When your temper rises tonight, count it as weather.

“Weather,” said Nix. “Right. Lovely. I’m counting.”

Far above, muffled by the mountain, the Muster’s horns sounded at the warcamp’s gate, and the drums above went from march to storm, every frame in the camp waking at once, the whole floor becoming one instrument.

“Wrights,” said Ede. “Open the mountain.”

⁂

The charge went up through her boot-soles first, then through the world.

The gallery breathed dust. Somewhere over their heads a hundred tons of cap-stone and tent-ground stood up into the drum-light and sat back down as a hole, and the shaft drank the war-noise of the camp down into the dark like a horn blown backward. The Wrights’ ladder- crews were moving before the stone finished falling — up the old tool-cut wall-stairs, giants first, rigging the winch-frames — and Ede went up the ropes in the second wave with her shield slung, into the smoke, into the red.

The Red Warcamp burned its light out of watch-fires and drum-tower braziers, and the stone of the stolen mine-hall threw it all back bloody. Tent-lines ran away in every direction, hide and pole, guy- ropes like a snare-wood; the drum-towers stood over them, four great frames of lashed timber, each with its team of kobolds beating the storm; and at the gallery’s cut end, lamplit and terrible, the cages: barred galleries stacked two high into the mine’s old workings, and the shapes of people in them, gripping the bars, because the floor was shaking and the horns were the wrong horns and something in the middle of the camp had just become a hole.

The Van had names for that ground before any of them stood on it. Five years of whisper-maps had come out through the bars of the pens, scratched on hide, sung into counting-songs, carried in the heads of the traded and the dead: Kitchen Row, where the tent-lanes ran widest; the Gallery Steps, the old miners’ walk above the drum-towers; the Broadgate, which the Muster was even now making loud; and between the shaft and the cages, straight as a pit-prop, the lane the miners called the Paylane, because wages had walked it once, going down. The camp was bigger than a camp has any right to be — a stolen country’s whole mine-hall, its watch-fires running away to their own red horizon, its smoke hanging under a dark too high for any lamp to find a roof in — and the plan needed none of it but the Paylane.

Ede came out of that hole with her shield up and her point low, and for ten heartbeats the plan was perfect: the camp’s whole face was at the gate, the alarm was a gate alarm, and the Van poured out of the floor behind her unmet, spreading into the lanes, running toward the pens with the grey banner jogging above them like a gull over a wake.

Then the camp turned around.

Not all of it. Enough. Some serjeant of Skarn’s with better ears than his drums deserved read the new noise in the middle of his camp, and the lanes ahead of her filled with the honor-guard — Red Orcs in welded plate, first-taken and hardest-collared, disciplined as millwork, coming on with their shields locked and no war-cry at all, which was the frightening thing. The Pressed do not fight for anything. They fight because the collar leaves no other door, and it makes them terrible, and it makes killing them a wound you deal yourself.

“Wedge!” Ede called, and the Van made its wedge, and the two walls met between the tents.

Shield-work in a tent-lane is millstone work: no room, no light, no quarter asked because there is no breath to ask it with. Ede took a blow on the boss that numbed her arm to the shoulder, gave the point back over the rim, felt it bite. Beside her a Grinding Pit deserter caught an axe out of the air the way pit-fighters learn to and paid its owner in chain-scarred iron. An orc’s face came over her shield- rim close enough to share breath — young, furious, terrified, the fever-smoke riding him — and she killed him, and hated the tower with a completeness she had not managed even for Gnarl, because this was the bill it ran up every day it stood: a death that should not have been needed, dealt by her own hand, again.

Left lane’s clearing, said Sliver, cold and quick. The champion is coming to you. Twenty of its paces. It walks through tents.

The warframe came through the tent-row like weather through a hedge — an orc champion once, and none of that left but the height of it, welded into a walking siege-frame, a mockery in plate, the camp’s one soldier with no collar because it needed none, having no self left to collar. The lane cleared before it. Her line bent.

“Vega!” Ede called, going back a step, and another, buying the lane with ground. “Your trade!”

The forge-matron came up the lane already swinging — not at the frame, Ede saw, never at the frame’s strength, but at its joints, at the welds, hammer-work called in a language of blows: this seam, that hinge, the knee, the knee, the knee. “It is only made!” Vega was saying as she worked, loud and level, teaching the whole Van in the middle of the fight the way she’d teach an apprentice-hall. “It is made, and badly, in a hurry, by the yard — nothing made cannot be UN-made — ” and the last blow went in under the frame’s knee-weld with everything a mountain owes its daughter, and the champion came down like scaffolding, and what leaked out of the frame as it broke was not blood but a scrap of the King’s will, thin as smoke, draining away down the shaft as if called home. An eviction. The only mercy that thing could still be paid.

The lane stood open. At its end stood the cages.

⁂

Afterward, the histories made the locks the quiet part, and the histories were wrong. The locks were the battle. Everything else was noise to cover them.

The night had a shape, and the histories, for all their noise, drew it true: three doors and a thread. The Muster at the Broadgate, loud on purpose, rams swinging, believed — the camp’s whole face turned to it and held there. The Wrights at the shaft-mouth in the camp’s middle, keeping the rim of their own hole against everything that turned. The Van’s one thin lane running up the Paylane, shaft to pens, a stitched thread through tent-cloth. And past the pens, at the gallery’s cut end, Skarn’s shield-wall standing around the stair, unmoved, the still eye the whole turning camp went round. Everything the camp threw that night, it threw at one of those four places, and the war was decided by which of them it forgot.

Vega worked the first pen’s lock with her eyes shut, hands flat on the plate, reading her own steel like a letter she’d posted twenty years before — and swore, once, in the mountain tongue, because it was good work, because it was hers, because the tower had bought her best and gotten it. Then she went in through the flaw she had left in the ward-spring in a leaner, honester year, and the lock gave up its whole pride at once, and the door swung, and the first of Skarn’s cages stood open on a gallery of staring, starved, unbroken giants.

“OUT,” Vega thundered, in the voice that fills doorways. “Dropped plate at the fires — arm yourselves and turn this camp!”

And Shieldwall came out.

Not her thirty. Ede knew that even then, counting faces at the bars faster than any battle should have let her — the floor’s own folk first, miners and shield-folk of the seized gallery, Dara Shieldwall at their head with her fists full of a gaoler’s keys she’d lifted in the first confusion, already running for the second pen. But among them, threaded through them, in the wrong-colored rags of a company five years dead: her people. A face from the south wall at the Warcamp. A face from the baggage-line. A woman who had stood the rearguard of the rout with her, shoulder to shoulder, the last time this floor had Ede Stonhal in it.

They came out of the cages, and took up the dropped warplate of their own gaolers, and the Red Warcamp began — there is no other word, and the histories for once found the right one — to turn. Every pen Vega and Dara opened was a squad the camp lost and the rising gained, armed at the enemy’s own fires. The Muster’s rams had the gate groaning. The drum-storm was tearing at its own rhythm now, orders crossing orders.

It was Nix who silenced it. Later she pieced it together from a dozen tellings: the goblin at the third drum-tower with the banner planted at its foot, scrambling up the lashed frame to the kobold drummers collared to their drums — and the working scratched into the wall of the Cage Pens, the fragment Vega had found with her hands flat on the steel and read aloud once, wondering: Break-collar, written rough, by some caged hand that never got to speak it. Costly still — speaking it over the drum-team’s collars one at a time took Vega grey, each collar the price of a day at the anvil — but after that night it was no longer rare. It was learned. The rising owned it, and would be assembling the strength to speak it at scale for thirty floors to come.

The drums stopped. One frame, then the next, then all four — collars snapping like frost-bound branches, drummers’ hands falling still for the first time in years — and into that silence, enormous, came the sound the drums had been drowning all along: the gate coming down, and the Muster’s horns, and under the horns a floor’s worth of freed voices finding out together what they sounded like.

Of all that floor gave the histories, one picture outlived the rest, and the freed drew it on hearth-walls for a hundred years: the third drum-tower black against the brazier-light, its drums fallen still, and on top of it a goblin no bigger than the drum he had silenced, holding the frame with one hand in the rising smoke — not waving, not crying the victory, only counting, lips moving, with the grey banner planted small and crooked at the tower’s foot below him.

The shield-wall, said Sliver. He is still at the stair, Ede. He has not moved. He is waiting for you.

⁂

Warlord Skarn stood before the stair-lift at the gallery’s cut end, behind the shield-wall of welded plate that was the last unbroken thing in his camp, and watched his war turn inside out, and did not move, because the stair was his post and the post was his collar and the collar — she understood, walking toward him down the cleared lane, and the understanding was the worst thing that floor did to her — the collar was his honor.

He was huge even for his kind, first-taken, older than his scars. His honor-guard had thinned around him; some lay where the pens’ freed squads had met them; some looked at the opened cages, and at the silent drum-frames, and broke ranks at last, backing away from the stair with their shields still up, deserting in good order, the only way orcs ever desert. He let them go. He said something after them in the Red tongue that was not a curse, and let them go.

“Warlord,” Ede said.

“Captain.” He knew her. Something in her went through ice at it. Two years, and the cages, and he knew her at a word. “You climbed short, last time. The King said you would come again. The King is never wrong about Men; it is his study.”

“Your camp’s turned. Your garrison’s freeing itself by the pen-load. It’s finished, Skarn.” She kept her point low. Behind her she could hear the Van coming up the lane and stopping, because she had raised her left hand, because this next part she owed no audience. “The word that snaps collars is loose on this floor. First-taken, same as the goblins, same as the kobolds you drummed — none of this was ever yours to want. Put down the wall, let us speak the working, and walk out of this camp a free soldier — the first Red Orc in an age to own his own mornings. Enough has been done tonight that can’t be undone. Not this.”

And Skarn laughed — not cruelly, that was the unbearable part, but the way a veteran laughs at a recruit’s honest, hopeless question — and hefted the great cleaver-blade that was his rank’s regalia.

“Free,” he said, the way you’d say a word in a language you’d once heard traders use. “Little captain. The King took me up out of the mud himself. His own hands, this collar. Do you know what a collar is, below? It is that they will feed you before the uncollared. It is that your word moves ten thousand spears. It is the King saying: you, of all the mud, I will keep. You call it a chain because no one ever offered you one. It is a rank. It is the only rank the world ever gave my people — and you would have me lay it down and be mud again, and call the mud mornings.” He brought the cleaver up to guard, and his voice went formal, parade-ground, almost gentle. “Come and take my post, captain of Shieldwall. That is how this is done between soldiers.”

He cannot hear you, said Sliver, very quietly. The Yoke is out of the floor, Ede. What is left is only what he built to survive it. Some collars we forge ourselves, of what they did to us, and no working written on any wall will open those.

“I know,” Ede said, to both of them.

She went and took his post.

It was a long fight and she remembered it short: plate and cleaver against shield and point, his strength against her years of knowing exactly what her own body would forgive, the shield-wall’s welded plates screaming where his missed strokes met them. He fought like what he was — a conscript made general, the tower’s best pupil, collared so long the collar had grown a soldier around itself — and she killed him the way you close a door on winter: braced, both hands, all the way to the latch. Under the last of it he said something in the Red tongue to no one — a report, by the shape of it; a post, handed over — and the histories can argue forever about what she owed him and whether she wept, because the smoke was thick, and the lamps were behind her, and it was nobody’s business but the floor’s.

Warlord Skarn died at his stair believing his collar had been a rank, because no one in a hundred floors of the tower’s mercy had ever been given less reason to believe anything else. She stood over him and made herself say it, silently, the way the shardminds taught: a death that should not have been needed. Then the seam of the stair-lift split behind the shield-wall with a sound like a held breath let go, and the second tier of the Ascent stood open, and Ede Stonhal turned her back on it, because the stair was not the thing she had climbed this floor for.

⁂

The cages stood open. All of them — Dara’s keys and Vega’s hands had finished the work while the warlord held her at the stair, and the pens’ galleries gaped, and the freed were everywhere in the burning camp, arming, weeping, calling names into the smoke and being answered, or not.

Ede walked down the line of the pens with her sword still bare, because her hands had not caught up to the war being over, and the count she had come twenty floors to take came to meet her, face by face out of the dark. Shieldwall lived. Not whole — nothing the tower touches stays whole; there were pens at the line’s end standing empty in a way that had nothing of tonight in it, and on a post by the emptied galleries a manifest nailed up in the tower’s neat clerking hand, consigned upward, in good order, to the high works — and she would climb with that paper folded over her heart for thirty floors, and it would cost the tower more than any army did. But here, now, in the wreck of Skarn’s camp: her people, alive, freed, standing in the firelight in gaoler’s plate with their own company’s grey banner — Nix had planted it at the pens’ head, and stood by it, small and smoke-black and counting the freed out loud because counting was how he wept — coming out of five years of cages to find the woman who had led them into this floor standing in the ashes of it with a drawn sword and no speech in her at all.

A one-armed man came out of the third pen last, slow, unhurried, as if cages were a thing that happened to other people. Dun Harrow. Her standard-bearer. The arm he’d carried her colors with five years gone, gone. He looked at her a long moment across the fire-lane, the whole camp falling quiet around the look, and in it she read everything the histories would spend a chapter on: that being freed is not the same as forgiving, that half that silence believed she had sold them, that the account between a captain and her caged company does not open at the lock. He looked at her, and at the banner, and back, and said nothing at all.

Tomorrow this camp would have to decide what she was.

Tonight the drums were silent, the pens were iron scrap, the second tier stood open overhead, and in the space where the march that never departs had beaten for a generation, a floor’s worth of the freed were learning the sound of their own gathering — the one sound the tower was built to prevent, rising through the smoke of the Red Warcamp like heat off a forge that would not now go out.

Shieldwall lived. The rest was owed, and morning would come collecting.

Chapter 8 — What the Freed Forgive

The drums had been silent a day and a night, and Ede had not yet learned how to hear the quiet.

The Red Warcamp had been a mine before Skarn pitched his war in it, and now the mine was coming back through the camp the way green comes back through a burn. Lantern-light stood in the great galleries where drum-fire had stood. The tents were down, or burning in orderly heaps, and the looted dwarven tools were being sorted from the orcish scrap by giants who wept while they worked, because they knew the makers’ marks. Above it all the cages hung from their gallery chains, doors open, swaying a little, empty at last. Nobody would take them down yet. Vega had offered. The freed had said no. They wanted to look at them open for a while.

Ede walked the cage-rows with the manifest Rhovan had cut from the warcamp’s counting-tent, and she made the only accounting that mattered.

A hundred and twelve had marched behind her into this floor, in the year she did not name anymore. Thirty names she had cut into the Stone of the Climb with her own hand, in Roothollow, in the winters after, one name a night when the drink let her hold a chisel. The cages had given back sixty-three. That left nineteen, and the manifest had them too, in a clerk’s neat fist, in a column headed sent above.

Sent above. Two words for nineteen people. She read the column three times, as if the names would change their minds.

“They kept good books,” Sliver said against her palm, dry as old paper. “Evil generally does. It is the one habit I would have you learn from it.”

“I’ll keep the books,” Ede said. “I won’t keep them politely.”

Below the cage-galleries the dead were being laid out in two grounds, and the distance between the grounds was the whole war. In the first lay the rising’s own — militia of the low floors, miners of Lampfall and Half-Light, four of Hale’s Cullers, and not one of the freed, which Ede counted the only clean thing the day had done. In the second ground lay the garrison. Red Orc honor guard, big men made small by stillness. The looters, who had died running. And the drum kobolds — a row of them, drums still corded to their backs because nobody had the heart to cut the knots, each body the size of a child’s. They had been beaten forward in front of the shield-line to keep the rhythm of an army that despised them, and they had died keeping it.

The Wrongmade left no bodies at all. Where the warframe champion had fought there was only a spill of parted iron and a scorch on the gallery floor, going cold. The thing that had worn the iron was not dead — it had been put out, evicted, dragged back down to the country it was poured from, and the floor it had stood on was ground again, and nobody mourned at that spot or ever would.

But a dead kobold is only a dead kobold. That was the arithmetic the shardminds would not soften and Ede would not let herself forget: the Natives were cured by killing, and the Wrongmade were evicted by it, and the Pressed just died, in their collars, for a due nobody had ever paid them. She stood at the second ground longer than the first.

Dun Harrow found her there, because Dun Harrow spent that whole first day finding people who were standing too long in one place.

He came up the gallery with a crate of boots on his hip, his one arm doing the work of two the way a river does the work of rain — no strain, just a redistribution of the world. The left sleeve of his coat was pinned flat at the shoulder, and the pin was a Shieldwall clasp, which was either the saddest thing in the camp or the finest; with Dun it was always both.

“Boots,” he said, by way of a greeting. “Skarn’s quartermaster hoarded them. Sixty pair, dwarven-made, hardly worn. The lads’ feet rotted in those cages five — ” he caught himself, shrugged, ” — in those cages a long while. Good boots will do more for this company than a speech, so I’ve took the liberty of cancelling your speech.”

“I hadn’t written one.”

“I know. I’ve heard you speak.” He said it so gravely that it took her a breath to catch the shine in his eye, and by then he was already moving, calling ahead of himself: “Boots! Dry boots! Form a line, you beautiful skeletons, the war’s begun paying wages!”

She watched him go. Eleven years he had carried her standard, and he had lost the arm at the drum-hill carrying it, in the hour everything else was lost, and five years of cages had taken his flesh and half his teeth and none of him. Every night in the cages — the freed told it in pieces, none of them able to tell it whole — Dun had sung the muster. Called the roll, name by name, all hundred and twelve, so the dark could not have their names whatever else it took, and the living had answered until they couldn’t, and when a voice stopped answering Dun answered for it, and sang on.

He had not asked her a single question about the Warcamp. Not one. She did not know what she had done to deserve that, and suspected the answer was nothing, and that this was simply what Dun was for.

⁂

The trouble came at the fires, the second night, after the work ran out and before the sleep came.

The freed camped apart. That was natural; Vega had said so in her slow way, and Nix had said so in his fast one — “you don’t come out of a cage and straight into a crowd, boss, a crowd is just a cage that moves” — and Ede had agreed and hated it. Their fires burned in the old mine’s east gallery, and the rising’s burned in the west, and Ede walked into the east with a kettle of stew and her hood down so everyone could see her face, because going where you are not wanted with your face showing was the only kind of courage she had left over that day.

Serle Danner stood up across the fire before the kettle touched the ground.

She remembered him as a corporal, quick and proud, the best hedge-fighter in the second file. The cages had made him a long shadow of that man, but the quickness was still there, and the pride had gone to an edge.

“I’ll ask it plain,” Serle said, “since no one else will ask it at all. The orcs knew our route. They knew the watch-words. They knew the hour we changed the pickets — they were inside the change. I’ve had five years to think about how many people knew those three things, Captain, and I keep counting the same small number, and every count ends with the one of us who walked back down that stair alive.”

The gallery went quiet as the inside of a bell.

“Say it all,” Ede said.

“You sold us.” His voice cracked in the middle of it, which made it worse, because it meant he did not fully believe it and had to live with it anyway. “Or tell me who did.”

Half the fire looked away from him in shame. The other half looked at her, and did not look away — five years in the dark with Serle’s same count, and the same answer, because it was the only answer the dark sold.

She had rehearsed nothing. Everything she might say she had already said to herself, drunk and sober, for five years, and none of it had ever acquitted her, because the charge that mattered was not treason. The charge was that she had led them in, and come out.

“I didn’t sell you,” she said. “I can’t prove that tonight. Proof is above us somewhere, and I mean to climb until I stand on it. What I can tell you tonight is only what you already know: I came back. It took me five years and it is worth exactly what it is worth.”

“It’s worth nothing — ” Serle began, and did not finish, because Dun Harrow was walking into the firelight, and the thing across his shoulder was the banner.

Nix had carried it up from the Kingsfield, that shabby glorious meadow of stolen flags where he had first snatched it up to hide behind — a goblin’s shield of good wool and better ghosts. He had surrendered it that afternoon without being asked, the way you give back something you were keeping for someone. “It never fit me,” he’d said. “Too tall. Wrong number of stones in the wall. Also it’s yours.” And Dun had taken it in his one hand and said nothing at all, and gone away with his face very still, and now here he was.

He stopped at the fire’s edge, where everyone could see, and he did not raise his voice, because he had never in eleven years needed to.

“This came off the drum-hill,” Dun said. “It’s been in a goblin king’s trophy-meadow five years, and it was minded there better than we were minded here, and now it’s home. So. There’s a question at this fire about who sold us. It’s a fair question. Here’s mine back: the one who sold us — did that man ever, once, in five years, come back for us?”

Nobody spoke.

“No,” Dun said. “He got his price. We were spent goods. Whoever he is, wherever he sleeps, we are the one debt he’ll never come to collect, because we cost him nothing and we were worth it.” He turned to Ede then, and held the banner out across the fire, the pole steady in his single fist, the sewn wall and its crooked star swaying in the heat-shimmer. “She came back. Walked up the worst stair in the world to do her collecting. That sums it, for me. I’d plant this for you, Captain, but the floor’s dwarven granite and I’m short a hand, so you’ll have to hold it yourself, and God help you, so will everyone else.”

She took it. There was nothing else to do. The wool was heavier than she remembered, and then it was lighter.

Dun stepped back, and lifted his chin, and began — quietly, in the tune worn smooth by five years of dark — to sing the muster. “Aldric of the first file.” And from the fire: “Standing.” “Bett Aldwyn.” “Standing.” “Cole Danner.” Silence — and Dun sang the answer himself, unhurried, the way he had five hundred nights: “With us.” On down the roll, all hundred and twelve, the living answering and Dun answering for the dead and for the nineteen sent above, and Serle Danner sat down somewhere in the middle of it with his face in his hands, not forgiving her, not yet, but weeping at last on the right side of the fire.

What the freed forgive, Ede thought, holding the banner while the names went by, is not the ones who wronged them. That comes late or never, and it is theirs to give. What they forgive first, if you are lucky, is the one who lived.

⁂

Mag Weller came to her past midnight, when the fires were coals.

She had been the company’s baker, before — one of the followers who had walked in with the baggage and been swept up in the sale with everyone else — and the cages had spared her hands nothing. Skarn’s orcs had put her to pouring at the Warlord’s councils, and so for five years she had stood in the smoke of the enemy’s tent with a jug, being furniture, and hearing everything.

Rhovan was at Ede’s fire when she came, folded in his grey way into the shadow of a gallery post, and Mag looked at him a long moment and decided, visibly, that a woman who had poured for orcs could talk in front of an elf.

“There was a courier,” Mag said. “Before the drum-hill. Twice he came. Hooded, gloved, and he never ate at the fire and never drank what I poured, and I’ll tell you for nothing, Captain — a man who won’t eat at a fire is hiding his face or his manners, and this one had manners.” Her scarred hands worked in her lap as if kneading. “He brought Skarn a paper. I couldn’t read it upside down, I only saw it had our shape on it — the route has a shape, you know it when you’ve walked it. And when he leaned past me to lay it down, I smelled him.”

The coals ticked. Somewhere off in the east gallery a voice was still humming Dun’s muster-tune, too low for words.

“Smoke and orc-iron, that’s every tent on this floor. But under it —” Mag’s face folded up. “Ash-barley and honeyroot. The sweet dark loaf. I baked at the gate ovens in Roothollow nineteen years before ever I followed this company, and you don’t forget the smell of a bread you’ve baked, it gets into a body’s clothes and won’t be washed out; we used to laugh about it, we all smelled of it, the whole street. He smelled of Roothollow bread, Captain. Fresh. Not a week old.” She looked up. “Whoever carried us to the orcs had stood in our own market inside that same week. He came from home.”

For a while nobody said anything at all.

“The toll-ledger,” Rhovan said at last, to the fire, in the tone he used for handing over a fraction and keeping the rest. “The payment that went up the tower, dated the week of the sale. I told you money moved. I did not tell you the receipt was countersigned, because the countersign is a mark, not a name. The mark is a Roothollow factor’s mark.” A pause, measured to the coal-tick. “I do not know whose. That is the truth, and I would know if it were not.”

Home. Ede sat with the word like a stone in the mouth. She had built the Warcamp’s ghosts a hundred bodies over five years — orc spies, tower clerks, Wake men, once in a black month even Hale — and every body she had built for it had lived conveniently far away, up the tower, in the enemy’s country. Not once had she built it a body that queued at the gate ovens of a morning, and watched her drill recruits in the market square, and said good morning.

Berrin would take this hardest of anyone, she thought, and the thought arrived with its own ache. He had fed half the town from those ovens’ street — stood godfather to the bakers’ children, forgiven their debts in bad winters, carted the sweet dark loaves up the gate roads at his own cost the day the climb reopened. Home was his religion. She would have to write to him, and she did not know how a person writes it was one of us.

“So,” Sliver said, into her palm, very softly for a thing with no softness in it. “The wound is not up the tower. You have been climbing toward it and carrying it the whole while. I have known forty-one climbers, Ede Stonhal, and I will tell you what the strong ones knew: home is the wound. Climb with it open. It bleeds less in the wind.”

In the morning the freed mustered under the banner — sixty-three of them in dwarven boots, Vega having mended the old wall-and-star’s torn hoist with silver forge-wire, one-handed Dun directing the stitching like a man conducting music — and when Ede asked, formally, who among them would go down to Roothollow and rest, not one soul stepped out of the line, and Serle Danner stood at the end of it with his jaw set, daring her to send him anywhere but up.

Above them waited the Burnt Common, a heath the fire had taken the same season the tower took it, green pushing through black char, a country stopped halfway through healing — and above that, eighty floors of other people’s cages. Somewhere below, in the streets of home, walked a man who smelled of sweet dark bread, counting his money.

Ede put her hand to the lift-gate, and the freed came up behind her, and they climbed.

Chapter 9 — The Tale of the First Banner

Three days up from the cages, the freed were learning fires again.

The Burnt Common ran black to every horizon — standing char and gorse-skeleton and bracken stopped forever at knee-high, mile on mile of it under the floor’s own deep sky. By day that sky was the pale sourceless glare the low floors kept. At night it banked down to an ash grey that was not darkness and was not anything else either, and there were no stars in it, because whatever this stolen country had kept of its nights, it had not been allowed to keep those.

The host camped in a burn-scar the width of a parish, pickets out to the char-ridges, and for the first time since the pens opened, the fires were not sorted. Freed sat with militia; miners of Half-Light argued mule-loads with muster-folk of Bannerline. It was not forgiveness. It was only that the Common was very large and very dead, and people will sit with anyone rather than sit with that.

She walked the lines at dusk with her hood down, because that had worked once, and found the biggest fire in the camp by following the sound of a goblin counting.

“— four hundred and six, four hundred and seven — you’ve gone back, you’re going back, that’s the same stitch twice —”

“It’s a lock-stitch,” Dun Harrow said, patiently. “You go back to go on. Sit down or hold something.”

The banner of Shieldwall lay across the standard-bearer’s knee, and Dun was sewing. He had the cloth pinned to his thigh under the flat of his boot-knife’s sheath and the needle in his one fist, and he sewed the way rivers cut country: not fast, and not stopping. The march had opened the fly-hem; Vega’s silver forge-wire held the hoist, but wire was for wounds, Dun said, and hems wanted thread. Around him the company sat mending — boots, straps, gaolers’ warplate taken in for narrower shoulders — because Dun mending drew mending, the way one man eating draws appetite.

“Captain.” He did not look up. “Two hands wanted. The hem, if you please. Pull it flat and keep it flat.”

So the general of the rising knelt down in the char and held cloth taut between her fists while her standard-bearer sewed, the old grey field coming flat under her hands, the sewn wall and its one star lying out across her knees, and nobody found it strange — which was, she thought, the whole distance the company had come in three days.

It was Wick who asked — a hedger’s son from the Fencerows, sixteen, serious, his first blooding at the Warcamp still sitting behind his eyes. It came out louder than he meant, the way rehearsed questions do.

“Why is it a wall?”

Dun’s needle kept moving.

“Every other banner on this climb has a killing thing on it,” Wick said, doggedly. “The Cullers’ pennons have the axe. Bannerline’s got the horns. Ours is a wall. A wall and a star, and the star’s not even —” he caught himself.

“Say it,” said Nix, delighted.

“— straight,” said Wick, miserably.

The fire got quiet in a ring, outward, the way water stills. Because the question was not about cloth at all: three nights ago a baker had said he came from home, and every soul at that fire had climbed since with the same stone in the boot. If home could sell them, what was under the banner? What was it a banner of?

Dun sewed to the end of his thread, and bit it, and was still a moment.

“That’s a tale,” he said. “And I’m a poor hand at tales. I carry the thing; carrying’s not telling. Hetta Marle could tell it — she carried before me; she’d have done the voices.” He looked into the fire. “But Hetta’s on the north face of the Stone, so you’ll have it from me, wrong in the corners, and you’ll sit on your objections till I’m done.”

“They won’t,” said Ede.

“No,” Dun agreed. “They never do. That’s the correct way to hear it.”

⁂

“There was a wall at Foldwater,” Dun began. “Before the tower. Before Roothollow was anything but a ford and a bad inn —”

“Shepherd’s End,” said Mag Weller.

“— it was Foldwater —”

“My gran poured at the Shepherd’s End inn forty years, Dun Harrow, and she called the village Shepherd’s End, and she called people who said Foldwater upstream folk.”

“Your gran,” said Dun, “poured short measures.” He let the laugh go by. “Foldwater or Shepherd’s End. It’s under the granary row now; Roothollow ate the village and the name both, and mind that, because it’s a piece of the tale. There was a village, and the village had a waller. A woman who set stones for hire. Field walls, fold walls, the in-field dyke — dry work, no mortar in it. Her name was Aldis Bright.”

Ede’s hands were full of banner-hem, and so she did not move. But something in her went and stood at the foot of a standing slab by the Root Gate — the lit face, oldest names at the base, and lowest of all, first cut of all, a name worn nearly flat, its glow gone thin as breath under two generations of thumbs. A. BRIGHT. She had cut thirty names into the other face of that stone, and never once asked anybody who the first name was, because in Roothollow nobody asked, because nobody knew.

“First winter after the Theft,” Dun said, “the world was wreckage — you’ve had your grandmothers on it; I’ll not compete. What was left on the ground was edge-folk and orphans and whatever the tower spilled or didn’t want, and out of the root-gates that winter came things. Masterless welded things. Fever-mad beasts. And men gone raider, which was worse, because the beasts at least were sick.

“Foldwater couldn’t run. Lambing, and snow, and you don’t run old folk and children across open country in the dark on the say-so of smoke. So they stood, and the place they stood was Aldis Bright’s in-field wall, the only made thing between the village and the night — barn doors for shields, lambing-crooks, one boar-spear, and her order, the first order of this company, one you’ve all had from me on a bad night whether you knew it or not: hold till the star clears the wall.”

“What’s a star?” said Nix.

It was not a heckle, and nobody laughed: the goblin had been born under a roof the size of a country, under a meadow’s false noon.

“A lamp,” Dun said, “that nobody lights and nobody owns. The ground keeps thousands; you’ll see them when we’ve done the work — that’s in the terms, far as I’m concerned. The morning star is the last one up before dawn, and it clears a waist-high wall about an hour short of first light — and she’d sent her sister’s boy running for the ford muster at first smoke, and the muster could be there by dawn, and she knew it, because a waller knows what’s an hour’s work. So: hold till the star. It wasn’t poetry. It was the time the relief was due.”

“Waist-high,” said Nix. “Waist-high. The first Shieldwall in the world was a stile.”

“A wall,” said Dun Harrow, “is as tall as what’s stood behind it. So, yes. That night it was about the height of a frightened farmer, which is the tallest thing there is.”

“And the relief came?” Wick asked.

“The relief came. First light, ford-men and hedgers, the night’s work lying in the snow on the wrong side of the wall, and the wall still standing — which a dry wall will do, mind. No mortar. Nothing holding it up but every stone set right against its neighbors.”

“I have always liked a dry wall,” Vega said from beyond the fire, slow, finishing her sentence into the quiet. “The only made thing that stands by nothing but its neighbors. You cannot weld that. You can only set it.”

“That’s the wall,” Dun said. “Now the sword. Because there nearly was a sword. Years on — call it ten; Hetta said seven; every count in this tale is argued — the wall’s famous, the taken are up there, everyone’s kin, and the first climb is gathering at the root: every hedge-company and ford-muster sewing itself a banner at the gate camp that would grow up to be Roothollow. And a lord among the spilled — been somebody grand on a floor that was now weather — looks at the grey cloth her village women had sewed and says: a company wants a device that puts fear into what’s in front of it. Sew her a sword.

“And Aldis Bright looked at him — Hetta would do the look; I can’t; imagine a woman pricing a bad load of stone — and she said —” Dun stopped. He went at it again, carefully, like a man laying a course. “She said a sword on a banner tells the ones in front of you what you mean to do to them. And she’d no quarrel with that, in its place. But a banner doesn’t march facing forward. A banner is read from behind — by the ones walking after it, the frightened ones, the ones carrying children — and what the cloth owes them is not fear. So sew what they need to read. A wall. That the thing they’re walking behind will stand between.”

He turned the cloth on his knee and found the star with his thumb.

“She sewed this herself. Night before the first climb, by firelight, with a waller’s hands — she could set a ten-ton dyke to a hair’s line, and she could not sew, and she knew it, and she wouldn’t give the star to the women who could. Her wall, her star. It came out crooked. They offered to pick it out and set it straight, and she looked at it a long while, and she said —”

“‘It’s the star as we saw it,’” said Mag Weller, softly, into her lap. “‘A star looks crooked to folk lying under a wall with their ears to the ground. Leave it.’”

“Your gran had that right, anyway,” said Dun. “I’ve sewn this star four times. Crooked every time, the same crooked, measured. You keep the cloth alive. You don’t correct her.”

⁂

It was Serle Danner who asked it. He was at the fire’s edge, the way he took all his places now — half in, half ready to stand — and he asked it flat, like a man testing a plank before he puts weight on it.

“Who sold her?”

The fire ticked. Nobody looked at anybody.

“Nobody sold her,” Dun said. “Nobody sold anybody, those years. The world was too poor to buy a company and too frightened to want one dead. That trade needed peace to invent it — the King’s kind of peace, eighty years of it, fat enough that a route could be worth silver.” He said it without heat. “What took the first climb was the climb. They went up with the whole root at their backs — first banners, first lifts opened, first names on the Stone, hers cut first of all. Past the drum floors. Past the silk, some say. How high is arguing. Hetta held she reached the King’s own country, the Made, all iron and no weather. And then —”

He was quiet.

“And then no word came down,” he said. “Ever. The tower stood. The lifts she’d opened closed again over the years, one by one, the way a burn heals wrong. The songs give her endings — she fell at the last gate; she took a floor no one’s found and holds it yet; the King keeps her banner in his hall to look at. Songs can’t stand a door left open. I don’t sing those verses.”

“How much of it is true?” Ede asked. The general’s question, asked plainly, wanting the count.

“All of it’s true,” said Dun Harrow. “Some of it happened.”

The laugh went round low, and died decently. Ede watched him thread the needle again, one-handed, against his knee, and asked the other question — the one she had been holding since the cloth first came flat under her hands.

“Five years in the pens, Dun. You sang the roll every night. Hundred and twelve names in the dark. You had this tale the whole while.” She kept her eyes on the hem. “You never told it. Why.”

The needle stopped.

It was a long time, for Dun. Three breaths, and the whole fire heard them.

“Because it has no ending,” he said at last. “And a cage wants endings. You can hand caged folk a roll of names — a name’s a fact, the dark can’t argue a fact. But hand them a tale where the best of us goes up and the night just — keeps her — ” and there his voice did not waver, because Dun’s voice never wavered; it simply stopped, and he waited for it, and went on. “I was afraid of the ending. There. A standard-bearer, afraid of a story. I sang the roll instead because the roll was the part I could stand behind. And I’m telling it tonight because of what Mag smelled on that courier — because half this fire has been lying awake on it. Home sold us. Fine. Look at the cloth, all of you, and hear this: the cloth is older than home. This wall was standing before Roothollow had a name to sell anything under. Whatever home did — this didn’t.”

He drew the thread through and squared the hem and looked, for the first time in the telling, up.

“And nobody ever stood her down,” he said. “That’s the piece I hold. A wall’s not like a sword; there’s no losing one. A wall is relieved, or it’s still standing. Nobody relieved the first wall. So wherever she stopped, she stopped standing, and the watch is still hers, and it’s been a long watch.” He folded the banner once across his knee, gently, the way you settle a sleeper’s blanket. “An age is late for a relief. But I’ve stood posts where the relief came late, and I’ll tell you what you think in the dark of one. You don’t think they’re not coming. You think: they’ll come. Step at a time. Someone will come up that stair.”

Nix, for once, had no arithmetic. He sat hugging his knees with the firelight on him, and after a while he said, in a smaller voice than the camp was used to, “I’d have run. That first night. Waist-high wall and lambing-crooks — I’d have run.”

“You didn’t run at the Kingsfield,” said Dun.

“I hid. Behind this exact cloth. It’s good thick wool.”

“That’s a wall doing its work,” said Dun Harrow. “Ask the star if it minds who’s under it.”

⁂

The fire burned down to keeping-coals and the company went to its blankets, and Ede sat with the mended banner rolled across her knees until only Dun was left, packing his needle-case one-handed.

She held it out to him. For a heartbeat it was the fire-lane at the Warcamp again — the silence, the held-out pole, the whole camp watching to see what she was — and then he took it the same way he had given it, without ceremony, grounding the butt by his boot.

“Yours to hold,” he said. “Mine to carry. That’s the arrangement, Captain, and it’s older than both of us.”

She walked her rounds. At the third picket she stopped, out past the last fire, where the Common ran black and mute to a horizon you felt more than saw, and she took the splinter out against her palm.

“You were below in those years,” she said quietly. “The first climb — the wall, the star, the woman. Is any of it so?”

Sliver was a long time answering.

“The wall is true,” it said at last. “The star is true. The rest is sewing.” And then, unasked: “Every fire in this camp tells tales about someone I could not keep, Ede Stonhal. It is the trade of being old. Let the man have his telling. He told it better than he knows.”

She stood a while longer than the watch needed her. Somewhere behind her, small in the dark of a country with no stars in its sky, the banner of Shieldwall hung still against a cart-frame by the coals — a grey field, a sewn wall, and above it, crooked exactly as a waller’s cold hands had left it an age ago, the only star on that whole floor.

Hold till the star, Ede thought, and went to sleep a general, and dreamed, for the first time in five years, of nothing at all.

From Malgrim's Ledger

Item: the Warcamp is spent. Write off the garrison, the drums, and the Warlord entire. Skarn priced his collar as a rank and defended it as one; this was always the flaw in him, and I enter it here so the flaw is not repeated: never issue a collar to a creature that wants it. Wanting it is the beginning of accounts we cannot audit.

Item: the woman climbs.

Item: sixty-three units of aged inventory have been recovered by the enemy at floor twenty. I decline to enter this as a loss. Grief is a bond that matures; every cage she opens, she carries. Let her portage.

Item: our gate-toll investment matures. The Tollmaster’s chains have taught three factions to hate one another at their own expense, which is the cheapest garrison I have ever kept on the books. Recommend no interference. Men quarreling over a gate will guard it for nothing.

Item: a goblin has been observed at the head of parleys. Entered under sundries. Small things are not small costs — I know this better than most — but there is no line in any ledger for what he is spending, and a currency we cannot mint we also cannot counterfeit. Watch him.

Item: on the question of reprisal against the low floors — denied, and I will have this minuted, because below they think me soft for it. Reprisal is spending fear to buy fear you already own. The quiet floors are quiet because they remember; a memory is a standing asset; burn a village and you convert it, at a loss, into a grievance. We do not buy what we hold.

Item: the King’s peace is misunderstood by its beneficiaries, which is the usual fate of sound administration. A hundred floors, a hundred quarrels kept apart, and not one war among them in an age — the woman below is climbing to repeal the only peace that has ever balanced. I keep its books gladly, and I will keep hers when she is entered in them.

Note to the willing below: feed her the trail slowly. Small findings, honestly come by, at decent intervals — a mark here, a manifest there. Nothing that cannot bear her checking, for she will check. A hope led is a hope steered; it is the hauling of it that keeps her ours. And see to it you grieve well when the trail warms. You have always grieved beautifully. It is why I collared you.

PART II

THE BANNER WAR

floors 21–40

Chapter 10 — The Quiet

They learned the shape of winning on the Burnt Common, and it fit them so well it should have frightened her sooner.

Floor twenty-one was a heath the fire had taken the same season the tower did — a black country of standing char and gorse-skeletons, with new green bracken pushing through the ash and stopping, held forever at knee-high, at the moment after the burn. The Warden was a boar in a shell of ember-iron that glowed as it fought, and it died the way the lesser Wardens died, which is to say usefully: Vega’s crews dropped a fire-break across its charge, the militia held, and when the welded shell came apart there was nothing inside to bury — only the King’s scrap of will draining down and out of the world, evicted, and a floor’s worth of quiet falling like the first rain after a drought.

Then the doors of Ashfold opened, and the pattern played out as it had played on every floor since the Warcamp. The commoners came out blinking. The keeper — Goodwife Ash, a common-reeve with a face like a kindly hatchet — wept exactly once, briefly, as at the end of a long count, and then put the whole town to work. The parish seed-store came up out of the glazed sett where it had waited out the burn, and was sown across the cooled black before the host had finished burying its dead. And when the rising moved for the stair, the floor rose with it: forty foragers and fire-wardens of Ashfold in the column, carrying the char-country’s hard plain spell for putting fires out, because — Ash said — the floors above would be burning, one way or the other, and somebody sensible ought to come.

That was the shape: the Warden falls, the doors open, the floor rises. Twenty-two gave them the Whisperwood, an elf-wood that repeated what it heard, where the host marched between trees that murmured its own words back a day late — she heard Serle Danner’s you sold us drift out of the gall-oaks twice, and once her own voice answering — and where the only thing that had to be killed with grief was a quoted shade, a knot of dead signal wearing a dead elf’s sentences, which came apart under Rhovan’s arrows still reciting and was evicted, and the sentences went with it. Twenty-three gave them the Anvil Commons and the smiths of Hammerhome, and cost them what the Pressed floors always cost: the Red Orc armorers held the forge-line for a tower that had chained them to it, and three threw down the lash and lived, and eleven did not, and Ede had Rhovan enter the eleven in the roll in their own column, the one with no heading, because there was no word for a death that should not have been needed and she refused to borrow one.

Twelve hundred mouths climbed off the Anvil Commons. The host had outgrown the word party somewhere below the Warcamp and was outgrowing the word company now.
