It ate like a town, argued like a family, and won like a machine, and Ede stood at its head watching the pattern hold, and let herself begin to believe the pattern was a law.

The Broodwarrens taught her something better, and the Gallows Fair taught her something true.

⁂

Nobody planned the Broodwarrens. That was the wonder of it.

Floor twenty-four was silk from floor to roof — a Deep-town swallowed by its own neighbors, caverns packed with sheets and funnels and hanging egg-sacs like grey fruit, where lamplight died six feet out and the stair-lift stood cocooned in the deepest chamber. The vanguard’s plan was fire and sever-work, gallery by gallery, and the plan’s price was written on every face that had seen the silk.

Then Wend Silkstead’s people, barricaded five years in their sealed vault with a lamp and a knife, tapped out through the door the old truce-signs their town had kept with the brood since before the Theft — and Nix asked what they were, and learned them in an afternoon, and walked into the Nursery with a lamp and no blade at all.

Ede stood at the gallery mouth for the length of that walk with her heart in her teeth. She could hear him talking the whole way — fast, ridiculous, fearless, a cup-bearer’s patter poured out into the dark that had eaten a town: “— big mothers, listen, I know the signs, one knock for guest, two for trade, I’m knocking, hear me knocking? — nobody’s burning anything, I told them, I said she’ll want to hear us out, I said she’s got ten thousand children, you don’t fight a lady with ten thousand children, you ask her what they eat —”

And the brood-midwives came down the web-lanes, huge and patient, and listened.

What the fever could not unmake, it turned out, was older than the fever. The truce ran deeper than the Yoke’s whole voice. By dusk the warren had yielded entire — not broken, yielded, the first floor in the tower’s history to come over on a goblin’s word — and the brood itself did what no blade could have done cheaply: the midwives took their own Warden, the great silk-engine Cradlesilk that wore their shape as a mockery, and bound it in a cathedral of cable no thrashing could part. Vega walked in alone, laid her hands on the welded core, and drew the tower’s rivets the way a surgeon draws a splinter, and the thing that had ridden the warren five years was evicted without one arrow loosed in anger — it came apart and drained down and was gone, and the silk went slack across three miles of cavern all at once, like a held breath let go.

The wrapped came free. Some of them living. Silkstead’s survivors walked streets they had not seen in five years, and the first freed brood-mother spun silksteel cable for the host’s baggage-train as calmly as if the whole world had merely been a bad season. Nix came out of the Nursery shaking so hard he had to sit down on a coil of rope, and counted his own fingers twice — “ten, good, they felt like six in there” — and the host cheered him until the silk trembled.

That night Ede allowed herself the thought in full, deliberately, to see how it tasted: perhaps the whole tower can be talked down. Perhaps the pattern bends all the way up.

The next floor was the Gallows Fair.

⁂

They heard it before they saw it: music. Thin, wheezing, cheerful music, box-tunes ground out by broken organs for no one, drifting down the gate road with the smell of old sugar.

Floor twenty-five was a travelling-fair caught mid-season and abandoned — booth-rows gone grey, a big wheel frozen against the white sky, bunting strung everywhere and rotted to rags, the menagerie cages standing open. A carousel turned at the heart of it, slowly, riderless, and had been turning for five years. It was a country built for crowds and joy, held at the moment after everyone fled, and it was the wrongest place Ede had stood in twenty-five floors, because everything in it was still trying to be delightful.

The keeper met them on the road a mile short of his own town, alone, carrying not a white flag but a grey one — a strip of the dead bunting on a tent-pole, which they would learn was the same thing, in fair-country.

“Pol Fairstead,” he said. “Ringwright. I keep the true accounts and the cage-keys, and I’ve come out this far so you’d hear me before you’re heard in there. We know who you are. We know what you’ve done below. And I’m asking you — the whole fair is asking you, every wagon of it —” he took a breath like a man lifting a weight, “— pass us by.”

Ede had been offered surrender, defiance, worship, and toll. Nobody had yet asked her to not exist.

They gave him supper at the head of the column and he told it plainly, the way keepers tell things, and the telling was worse than any Warden. His grandmother had cheered a rising once. Everyone Pol’s age had grown up on the story — climbers on the low stairs, banners, a season of believing — and then the climbers were dead somewhere above, and the tower’s answer had come down through the floors that cheered, methodical as rent-day. The fair remembered what the answer had been. He did not describe it. His hands described it, going still in his lap.

“You’ll go up,” Pol said. “Maybe you’ll win. I’ll pray so. But if you don’t — and nobody yet has — the tower will come asking who lit lamps for you, and it keeps better books than God. So kill our Warden if you must, I know you need the stair. But do it quiet, take nothing, muster no one, and let me keep my people hid. Don’t light the wheel. A crowd is the one thing the fair can’t survive.”

Rhovan said quietly that the Wake had a name for such floors, the ones that begged the war to look away. They were called the Quiet, and there were more of them above than anyone at this fire would like to know.

The council that night was the worst since the Warcamp, and it was the worst because nobody in it was wrong.

Ede argued for the fear. She was surprised by her own vehemence. She stood under the wheel’s frozen shadow and argued that fear earned over five years was not ignorance to be corrected but wisdom they hadn’t paid for, that a floor was not freed if its people were dragged blinking into a war they had begged to be spared, that the rising could kill the Warden, free the fever, and leave the fair its silence — a gift with no blade behind it.

And she lost, to the best people she knew.

Goodwife Ash said, gently, terribly: nobody had asked Ashfold whether it wanted the fire put out, and thank the stone for that, because fear votes against its own rescue every time; that is what fear is for. Serle Danner stood up with his jaw working and said the cages had also been quiet, that he had personally spent five years being passed by, and that he would not march one floor higher under a banner that had learned to do it politely. Vega, slowest and kindest and most immovable, said that a broken thing left unmended does not stay broken, it breaks other things, and that she had never yet met the fear that was healed by being obeyed. And the quartermasters said, simply, bread: twelve hundred mouths, winter somewhere above, and the fair’s stores standing shuttered.

The host voted with its hunger and its scars. The fair would be freed — fully, loudly, mustered and provisioned and its wheel lit, like every floor before it. When Ede carried the answer out to Pol, he did not argue. He thanked her, with a courtesy that was worse than a curse, for coming to tell him herself. Then he went back to his wagons, and hid his people, and hid his keys, and told the rising nothing at all about the ring it was about to walk into.

She would have done the same, she thought. That was the thing about fear they had all been too well-fed to remember. It doesn’t argue. It just stops helping you.

⁂

The assault went in at first light, and everything that went wrong went wrong because the fair fought them blind.

No keys, so the menagerie undercroft stood unsecured at their backs, and the carousel wolves came up out of it through the booth-rows. No ring-lore, so nobody had told the Ashfold foragers about the tunes — and a whole file of them walked off the flank toward the carousel, smiling, helmets in their hands, following a music-box compulsion straight into the ring, and had to be dragged back one by one with wax in every ear the host could stop up. And the Warden — Hookmaster, a fair-cat welded into a rig of grabbing-hooks off the strongman games — worked the fight like the fight was a fixed stall, snatching soldiers off their feet and reeling them through the wreckage, always where the carousel’s turn had already sickened their footing.

They broke it. Of course they broke it; twelve hundred veterans of twenty-four floors, against one floor’s worth of wrongness — the games fell still, the carousel stopped for the first time in five years, the tunes died mid-phrase, and the fever lifted off the fair like heat off a griddle. The cost inside the battle was seven dead, which the host had learned to call light.

The cost outside the battle was the village.

Nobody ever pieced together whose lamp it was — a dropped one, a thrown one, it changed nothing. Fire took the toffee-nettle, ran up the bunting-bindweed along the guy-ropes, and walked across the tent-tops into the wagon-rows west of the ring, where the fair-folk were hiding from their own liberation, in deep cover, in silence, exactly as they had survived everything for five years — and silence is the one thing you cannot do when the fire comes. The rising’s soldiers pulled wagons apart with their hands. Vega stood in the burning row and lifted a cook-van off a family whole. The Ashfold fire-wardens spent their new spell until they dropped. It wasn’t enough, and it was never going to be, because the people in the wagons had spent five years learning not to cry out, and they didn’t.

Thirty-one of Fairstead dead, and not one of them in the fight.

Ede stood in the black lane afterward while the lists were made. Pol Fairstead walked the row behind her, not looking at her, and she did not defend herself, then or ever. There was nothing to defend. Every argument at the council had been sound, and the wagons had burned anyway, and both of those things were true at once and would go on being true all the way up the tower.

At dusk, Pol lit the Big Wheel.

Not for a crowd. The fair-folk stood in the ash of their own street and watched the great ring of lamps kindle against the dusk, and Pol worked the crank himself, one-armed with weariness, and when someone asked him softly why now, he said: “Step right up,” to nobody at all, in a barker’s voice with nothing left in it, and Ede understood that it was a funeral, the only kind a fair knows how to hold — every light burning, for thirty-one people who had hidden from the light, and been right to.

“Count what you have learned,” Sliver said in her palm, that night, when she could not sleep. “You knew the tower was your enemy. You have met the second one now. It does not hold a floor. It holds the people, and it was here before Vharuk, and it will not die at the summit.”

“Fear,” Ede said.

“Theirs,” Sliver said. “And yours. You feared to be the kind of general who overrules a frightened town, so you argued and washed your hands in losing. The freed feared the quiet, so they shouted it down. Nobody in that council served the fair. Every soul in it was paying an old debt to themselves.” A pause, dry as chalk. “I have carried forty-one climbers. The ones who frightened me had stopped being frightened. Stay as you are.”

In the morning the fair rose anyway — that was the bitter wonder of it; eleven fair-folk mustered under the banners, tumblers and sharps and one grim rigger whose wagon had burned, because, the rigger said, hiding hadn’t worked, so.

Pol Fairstead did not muster. He came to the column’s head instead, ring-keys on his belt, grey flag furled, to see the army off his floor, and what he said to Ede at the gate went up the tower with her. “You’ll break more Wardens, captain; I don’t doubt it now. Break this too, then. The tower is only the stone. The prison is what we learned to want. You can break stone.” He looked back down the burned street, where the great wheel still burned its funeral lamps and the fair’s children were out at last in the ash. “Now break the wanting.”

Behind him, thin and clear over the ash-lane, the children were already skipping rope to an old counting-rhyme of the Men floors — run from the horn and the horn runs after; walk to the horn and the horn falls silent — and Ede heard it without knowing she heard it, the way you bank a coal against a colder year.

The host climbed toward the Slag Barrens with its first unwilling floor behind it and the wheel-light on its back, and at the rear of the column Ede watched the Cullers’ grey pennons peel away and drop down the freed lifts, Hale’s companies taking up the rear-gate watch across the floors the host had won — the Whisperwood, the Anvil Commons, the silk-quiet Broodwarrens sleeping on their yielded word.

She felt nothing about it. It was sound soldiering; the rear had to be held by somebody, and the Cullers held things well. Later — all the rest of her life — she would remember that she felt nothing, and count it the most expensive nothing she ever felt.

Chapter 11 — Hale's Lesson

Nix kept his counts on a tally-stick of bog-oak, because paper burned and goblins forgot nothing but were believed about everything less than sticks.

By the Smelterworks the stick read like this: nine floors since the Kingsfield, notch. One collar thrown down at a banner-meadow, his own, deepest notch on the stick, still tender to the thumb. One warren yielded whole to one goblin’s word, notch with a ring around it, which was Nix’s notation for don’t you dare ever stop being able to do that. Two ridge ogres of the Slag Barrens deserted alive, who had rolled a whole slag-tip flat instead of rolling it onto the column, and now walked with the baggage-train eating for six and pointing out good metal in the heaps like enormous shy uncles. And eleven of the Pressed dead across floors twenty-six and twenty-seven, eleven flat ugly notches Nix cut slowly, because a dead conscript freed exactly nothing, and somebody in this host had to keep the column with no heading, and Ede kept it in ink and Nix kept it in oak and neither of them had ever discussed it.

The Mirefields they had crossed dry-shod on a drowned road an elder remembered — an elder the peat had kept whole a hundred years, which Nix had opinions about and kept to himself — and the cairn-lights that tried to lead the column into the pools had been broken one by one, guttering out and draining down into the peat, evicted, ta-ta, no tears; things made of drowned men’s aether did not get notches. And then the Smelterworks, floor twenty-eight, which was the loudest hottest floor in the world and the first one Nix had walked onto and smelled his own childhood: molten metal, wet stone, and small folk being spent.

The ladle kobolds worked the tap-bridges over the metal rivers, collared, burn-scarred, flinging molten shot at the vanguard because a smelter-boss with a lash stood behind them and a drop into the runnels stood behind him. The host’s shields could have pushed the bridges in an hour and the cost would have been every kobold on them.

Nix went out onto the middle span instead, alone, hands empty, and stood in ladle-shot range being visibly, unmistakably, a goblin.

“Evening,” he called. “Look at me. No, properly look. You know what I am? I’m the after. There’s an after, that’s the whole message, I’m the proof and the messenger both — collar came off me nine floors down and nobody’s put one back on, and I eat twice a day, bread in the morning, soup at night, sometimes a sausage when morale wants building — you’re throwing that at me, by the way, you’re throwing sausages away every time you miss —”

A ladle wavered.

“— and I know, I know, the boss behind you has a lash, but count him. Go on. One. One boss. Now count us.” He swept his arm back at the gallery-mouth, where twelve hundred stood. “I ran out of numbers, and I count everything.”

It took until the middle of the night watch, and it was not clean — nothing on a Pressed floor was ever clean. Four kobolds died before the end: two to a smelter-boss who saw the yielding start, two on the shields of men too tired to check their push, and Nix cut four flat notches by firelight with his jaw aching, because a kill of the Pressed was not a cure and not an eviction, just a bill the tower ran up in other people’s blood. But nineteen collars came off on the tap-bridges before dawn. Vega snapped them cold in her fists, one by one, like a woman shelling nuts, and the freed kobolds stood in a wobbly row not knowing where to put their eyes.

The smallest one answered every question with her whole title: Third Ladle, Second Bridge. Nix hunkered down in front of her.

“That’s a job,” he said. “Worse — it’s a shift. You don’t wear a shift home. What’s your name?”

She looked at him for a long time. Kobolds and goblins were cousins the way a millstone and a hearthstone are cousins — same quarry, different lives — and Nix waited, and the runnels glowed, and finally she said a word in the old warren-tongue that meant the click a lock makes when it opens.

“Pib,” Nix said, delighted. “Tremendous. Best name on the floor. Pib, first of her line.” Notch — a new kind, a little door cut clean through the edge of the stick. He was still admiring it when the runner came.

⁂

She came up the freed lifts from below, which nobody did, which was the first wrongness — a Silkstead vault-girl, fifteen maybe, silk-cut across both palms and burned along one arm, and she had run three floors’ worth of gate roads alone, day and night, through half-cured country, which told you everything about the message before she said it.

She said it anyway, to Ede, in front of everyone, in the flat voice of someone who has decided to be a bell and ring once and fall over:

“They’re burning the Nursery. The grey soldiers. The ones your banner left to guard our gates. Wend says — Wend says come and see what your word is worth.”

Nix did not remember the next hundred heartbeats honestly, and knew it. He remembered noise, and Vega’s hand closing his shoulder like a gate, and Ede already giving orders — the van to hold the Smelterworks under Bruna and the banner, the party down the lifts, now, light, no baggage.

Four lifts down. Five days of hard march, through the fair where the great wheel still burned its funeral-light, through the Anvil Commons, past sentries of their own rear-guard who would not meet anyone’s eye, grey pennons over every gate and every gate opening slower than it should. Nix stood at the front of every platform with the tally-stick in both hands, doing the worst arithmetic of his life. A warren yields to a goblin’s word. A goblin’s word is guaranteed by the host. The host’s rear is guarded by men whose whole doctrine is that nothing with more than four legs, or fewer than two, can yield at all.

He had left the ring-notch floor in the keeping of the one company that did not believe in it.

“Too late” turned out to be a place. You could climb down into it. It had a smell: silk doesn’t burn clean, it melts and drips and stinks like hair and sugar, and the smell met them a whole gallery above the warren gate.

⁂

The Nursery was char. Where the egg-sacs had hung like grey fruit there were black strings, still swinging, and the great patient midwives who had listened to Nix in the dark — who had come down the web-lanes with ten thousand children behind them and chosen an old truce over a new fever — lay burst and curled at the chamber doors where they had died holding them. Nix counted. That was his work; someone had to. Fourteen midwives. The Nursery entire. And the first one, the very first, the one who had touched his knocking hand with one forefoot like a bishop conferring something — her he knew by the chip in her leg-armor, and he stood in front of her a long time, and the stick stayed in his belt, because there was no notch for this.

The brood had answered the way anything answers when its children burn. Not the fever — the fever was five floors dead; this was only nature, old honest nature, the kind that had kept truce for a thousand years and knew exactly what to do when truce died. The warren had taken the Cullers in the web-lanes where the silk still hung thickest.

Twenty of them, cocooned in grey, hanging in rows down the dark like the tower’s own larder — men who had marched in to burn a surrendered nursery before it hatched, that was the phrase, Nix heard it from three different grey mouths that evening, said each time with less conviction, before it hatched — and the silk had come down on them faster than any blade can cut, and what was in the cocoons was not anything Wend’s people would let the young girls see.

And Serjeant Hale was carrying them out.

He had led it. Nobody said so and everybody knew; you could read it in the way the Culler survivors stood, in a horseshoe, facing away from him, the way men face away from a fire too big to put out. He had led in forty to clear the rear, kill-all, standing doctrine, the doctrine he had taught to half the low tower and to Ede Stonhal herself once, in another life, in a training-yard in Roothollow — and the doctrine had walked into a yielded warren and met something that no longer had any reason to hold back, and now he came up out of the web-lanes with a dead man across his shoulders, and laid him in the row, and straightened his arms, and went back down. Alone. Every time.

Vega offered. He looked through her. Wend Silkstead stood at the vault door her people had opened on a goblin’s promise and had now barred again — barred against the rising, and that was a thing Nix was going to carry the rest of his life, that sound, the Sealed Vault sealing — and she said to Hale’s back, in a town-warden’s voice that carried the length of the gallery:

“Twice, now. The first time it was the tower’s fever broke the truce. Say your reason. I want to hear what the free one is.”

Hale did not answer. He went down, and came up with the eleventh body, and laid it straight.

Nix watched all twenty. He owed that, he had decided; his word had been the coin this was bought with, so he would stand at the ledger and watch it spent to the last. Eleven. Fourteen. Seventeen. The silk-shadow lay over the gallery in long grey bars, and in it Hale knelt each time beside each cocooned man and cut him down himself, one hand bracing, one hand working, armor of his own dead company hanging around him in the dark like moths, and by the twentieth Nix understood what he was looking at. This was not penance and not pride. The man was keeping a muster. It was the only liturgy he had.

When the last body lay in the row, Hale stood over it awhile. Then he walked to where Ede waited by the gate — she had not said one word to him in five hours, had only worked, hauling silk and water like a private soldier — and Nix, holding the lamp between them because somebody had to hold it, saw the oldest soldier in the tower come to the end of everything he knew.

“They yielded to a goblin.” Hale’s voice was ash and wire. “Ten thousand of them. To that.” A jerk of the head at Nix, without contempt — that was the terrible thing, there was no contempt left in it, only a man pointing at an answer he could not read. “Forty years I’ve held the low stairs. Everything I ever kept alive, I kept alive by clearing what could turn on it. It’s arithmetic. It has always been arithmetic.” He looked at the row of his dead, twenty long. “Then arithmetic walked in here and the silk took it. So you’ll tell me, Stonhal, because you were my recruit and you owe me one true answer and I’m calling it in.” A breath. “How does the goblin do it?”

Once. He asked it once, the way a man jumps off something.

Ede looked at Nix — asking leave, Nix realized, actually asking a goblin’s leave with her eyes, in front of Hale, which was its own kind of answer — and then she said, quietly:

“He means it. That’s the entire trick, and it can’t be counterfeited, and that’s why you can’t learn it the way you learned the sword. Every yielded thing in this tower has spent its whole life reading the blade behind the offer. Nix walks in and there’s no blade. Not hidden. Not anywhere. They look at him and see the after.”

Hale stood in the silk-shadow among the cocooned armor of his own, and Nix watched forty years of doctrine settle onto the man’s shoulders with its full weight, no longer load-bearing, just heavy — and because Nix was Nix, and because silence was a country he had never once been able to hold, he heard himself say:

“Twenty of yours. Fourteen of theirs. That’s the score doctrine kept today, serjeant. Next floor, try for nothing-nothing. It’s a very clean number. I can teach you the knocking in an afternoon — the signs are easy.” His voice cracked on the joke and he let it. “Meaning it takes longer.”

Hale looked at him — really looked, first time in ten floors — and said nothing, and picked up the first body of the twenty, and began carrying his dead up the long stair to the light, one at a time, refusing help to the last man.

⁂

The brood had gone deep, past all lamplight, and would not answer the knocking — not Nix’s, not the vault-folk’s, maybe not for a generation, said Wend, maybe not ever. The truce was dead. The floor stayed freed; freedom, it turned out, was the kind of thing you could not give back even when you wanted to. Silkstead would live. Everything else was notches.

They buried none of the Cullers on twenty-four — Wend’s one condition, delivered flat, and no one argued. The grey company wrapped its dead for carrying and turned up the stair, bound for the only ground on the climb that was made for it: the grave-country three floors above, the Tithe Barrows, where Men had buried Men since before the Theft, and where — the runners said — Voss’s Tollmen had already chained the lych-gate and were charging silver at the stair, and the Wake’s grey bows had taken the Tollmaster’s fee to stand guard on the chains, and the whole host was funneling up toward all of it with its blood still hot from the silk.

Nix climbed at the rear of the burial-column, keeping his counts. Twenty dead men ahead of him going up to be buried in a floor that charged admission, carried by a company that had stopped believing in its own arithmetic, into three armies’ worth of quarrel over one gate.

He got out the tally-stick, thumbed the ring-notch once, for courage, and cut a fresh mark of his own invention: a warning-notch, first of its kind, for a debt still running.

Above them, the crows were already gathering over the barrows.

Chapter 12 — The Banner War

Rhovan reached the Tithe Barrows two days ahead of everyone’s better nature, and spent them the way he spent most days: on a height, in the grey, counting other people’s fires.

Floor twenty-nine was grave-country — the old barrow-fields where Men had laid their dead with a tithe of grave-goods since before anyone now breathing, mound after grassed mound to the horizon under a still sky, each great barrow crowned with one black yew. It was the most peaceful floor he had seen in the tower, and it was patiently wrong. The grass grew too green over the newest mounds. Beetle-runs riddled the turf like veins. Pale rings of death-cap stitched themselves around barrow after barrow, which meant — the keeper had told him within the first hour — that the mounds were readying to open, and the only thing holding the floor asleep was that nobody had yet given the dead a grievance.

Three armies were now camped on it, working on that.

From the Great Yew ridge Rhovan read the fires like a ledger, because a ledger was what it was. Southeast, the Cullers: low fires, straight rows, grey pennons at half-staff for the twenty silk-wrapped dead they had carried up three floors to bury in the only grave-earth of Men on the climb. Northwest, the Tollmen: bright fires, good wine, and a chain — Voss’s factors had come up the freed lifts behind the host like gulls behind a plough, and they had found the one gate on the tier worth owning: the lych-gate stair, the arch of standing lych-stone through which every burial on this floor had passed for a thousand years, and through which the whole rising must pass to reach the Kingsbarrow above. The chain was across it. The toll was posted. Canon law grants free passage to freed gates, someone had painted underneath, and someone else had painted, in a factor’s tidy hand, the lift is free — the road to it is maintained.

And between the two, on the low mounds, his own people. Forty bows of the Wake in ride-grey, faces he had known three hundred years, camped in a crescent around the Tollmen’s chain — because the Wake’s masters had taken Gilder Voss’s fee to guard his tolls. It was, Captain Sael had told him, with a courtesy sharpened on both edges, a contract, Rhovan, the kind of thing our employers sign; you used to know the word. He had answered with a fraction — that he was the rising’s scout and would remain one — and they had parted like relatives, which was the trouble.

Threading between all three camps went a small exhausted man with a lantern and a book: Sef Wakewater, barrow-warden, keeper of the floor, going fire to fire to fire like a moth working three flames. He carried the vigil-book, the rolling account of which mounds had stirred and in what order they would open, and read from it to any who would listen, always with the same sermon after: no blood on the grass. Quarrel where you like, but not on the mounds. Every soul below this turf is owed a tithe, the fever has soaked into the grave-gold, and the dead are light sleepers now. Spilled blood is a debt, and this floor — he would slap the book — this floor forecloses.

Rhovan believed him. Rhovan had been telling the truth in fractions for three hundred years and knew a whole truth when one walked past him twice a day with a lantern.

Nobody else believed him at all.

⁂

The spark, when it came, was a toll charged on the dead.

The Culler burial-column came up the gate road at dawn — twenty biers, forty bearers, Serjeant Hale walking in front with his helm under his arm, the first time in forty years that any man had seen the top of Hale’s head. They came to the lych-gate; there was no other door the ground would own. Rhovan, on the ridge, saw the column halt at the chain, and saw the factor come out of the toll-booth with his slate, and could not hear the words and did not need to. Voss’s schedule of rates was posted on the arch. It had a line for goods, a line for beasts, a line for armed men.

The factor — Quist, a careful man — consulted the slate, and the schedule, and named the rate for twenty biers.

For one heartbeat the whole floor seemed to hold its breath — the crows silent in the yews, the death-cap rings pale and patient — as if the ground itself wanted to hear whether the living would really do this.

Then a Culler corporal named Dree, who had carried his own brother up three floors of stairs, said something short, and took his axe to the chain — and a Tollman guard put a halberd through his shoulder — and Hale bellowed, too late, into the exact silence where his authority used to be — and from the crescent camp on the mounds, honoring the letter of a contract signed by people who had never seen this field, the Wake’s bows loosed.

Rhovan watched his own people’s arrows go up. Ride-grey fletching against a grey sky, a sight that had meant home to him for three centuries, rising off the burial mounds of Men in defense of a toll-chain. He did not remember starting to run.

What followed was the ugliest fight of the climb, and the smallest, and he saw all of it, because he took part in none of it. The Cullers stormed the toll-booth and the toll-booth’s silver; the Tollmen’s guards — half of them freed men, wearing Voss’s livery over Warcamp scars, killing men who had opened their cages — held the arch; Sael’s bows commanded the mounds and made the open turf a killing-ground, precise, contractual, each arrow an itemized regret. Three banners. Not one of them the tower’s. The real enemy was a floor up and seventy floors up and did not need to lift a finger, and on the vigil-ground by the lych-gate, Tithegrim — the field’s welded collector, a barrow-hound risen and saddled with a rider of grave-gold — stood motionless at the battle’s heart, and watched.

It had been waiting five years, Rhovan understood, cold to his boots. It was a collector. It never interrupted a payment.

Blood went into the barrow-grass — Culler blood, Tollman blood, and then, when Dree’s maddened friends rushed the crescent, Wake blood too, the first elf-blood spilled on the climb, and the turf took it the way a ledger takes a figure.

Sef Wakewater ran into the open between the lines, alone, both arms up, the vigil-book over his head like a shield, screaming at three armies that the rings were widening — and it says everything about that hour that the crows heard him and rose from the yews all together in a black sheet, and the men did not hear him at all.

That was when the wagons came.

⁂

Rhovan smelled them before he saw them, and so did every soldier on the field, and that was the whole difference — a smell that went through that battle like a rumor of another world: baking. Warm bread, wood smoke, salt pork, the sweet dark ash-barley loaf of the Roothollow gate ovens — the smell of the market square on a winter morning, of home before the war made everyone on that field a soldier. Up the gate road from the freed lifts came a supply-train twenty wagons long, ox-drawn, lamplit against the grey, and on the lead wagon, standing, reins in one fist and his hat in the other, bellowing names — not orders, names — came Quartermaster Berrin Or.

“DREE! Corporal Dree, you’re bleeding on good grass, sit down, your mother sold me eggs! QUIST! I hold your paper, Quist, you owe me eleven crowns and I am not collecting from a corpse, get your men behind the booth! SAEL! Captain! Strings down, my love, whatever he’s paying it isn’t worth arrows at grocers —”

He drove the lead wagon straight in between the lines — through the arrow-fall, at a walk, like weather — and halted it broadside in the killing-ground, and stood on the seat-board, a stout man of sixty with flour on his sleeves, and possibly the only person in the tower whom no faction on that field could imagine shooting. Behind him the train came on: bread, beer, salt, boot-leather, arrow-shafts, lamp-oil, climbing the freed tower because one man had spent the whole war making sure it did.

“Right,” Berrin said, into the ringing quiet, in the voice of a man opening a market. “Nobody fights well hungry. It’s wasteful, and I’ve brought supper. After supper, if you all still want killing each other, I’ll be no further use — but you’ll do it fed, in marching order, and off this grass, because the keeper here has been trying to tell you gentlemen something for two days, and he’s the only man on this floor who’s ever paid rent to the landlord you’re annoying.”

It should not have worked. It worked because there was not a captain in the three camps who did not owe Berrin Or something older than the quarrel — the Cullers marched on his flour; Quist’s factors banked on his credit and his silence about their debts; the Wake’s own supply-cache contracts ran through his warehouse-books; and Sael, who trusted no one of Men, had once been handed by this man — unasked, wordless — her company’s spent arrows, replaced to the exact count. He fed all three camps from the same wagons, taking no side but supper’s, and by full dark he had the principals under the one roof that awed all parties equally: the Lych House, the turf-roofed mortuary hall, where they argued terms across the cold stone washing-slabs of the honored dead while Sef held the lamp.

Rhovan stood in the doorway and watched his own trade practiced better than the Wake had ever taught it to him. No cipher, no handler, no blade behind the offer — just a man who knew what every soul in the room needed and had most of it on a wagon outside. The terms took shape the way honest terms did, each party losing the thing it could spare least easily: the toll-chain would come off the lych-gate, and Voss’s factors would keep their books instead for the host entire — quartermasters under Berrin’s countersign, paid, watched, and useful; the Cullers would surrender the rear-gate watch — Hale gave this up in one syllable, and only Rhovan understood he was watching a doctrine laid down, not a duty — and take the van’s flank under joint command; the Wake’s guard-contract would be bought out at cost, and Sael’s bows hired forward as the host’s far-scouts, answerable to the rising’s council alone.

“And the dead go in free,” Berrin said, last of all, writing it. “All dead. Everyone’s. Forever. Gate-law of this floor, witnessed on the —” he glanced up mildly, “— what am I signing on, keeper?”

“A drumhead,” Sef said. “Culler drum. It’s what there was.”

“Poetic,” said Berrin, and signed.

They came out of the Lych House with a peace, into the dark, and Rhovan, last through the door, heard the first passing-bell note come out of the lych-stones themselves, a tolling with no bell in it — and saw the death-cap rings of the Tithe Barrows begin to glow, mound beyond mound, away toward the horizon.

The blood had gone into the grass hours ago. This floor foreclosed.

⁂

The newest mounds opened first, exactly in the order of Sef’s book.

The turf lifted like lids. Up out of the barrow-earth came the tithe-wights in their grave-gold — tall, deliberate, dressed in the honor of Men a thousand years old, moving with the unhurried authority of creditors — and behind them, from the beetle-riddled slopes, the ghouls came up hunched and wet and wrong, and last of all Tithegrim moved: mound to mound at a slow parade walk, its grave-gold rider swaying, waking the field like a serjeant waking a barracks. And down through the lych-gate arch, from the floor above, from the moated Kingsbarrow itself, rolled a voice that was not a voice — the pressure of one, in the oldest funeral-forms of Men, felt in the chest like a tolling:

The court finds the field in debt. The court will have its tithe.

The Barrow King had heard the blood, and raised the reckoning.

What Rhovan witnessed next he set down only in his own private book, because no report he owed anyone had room for it: three armies that had spent the day killing each other discovering, under the tolling dark, that the dead of Men do not distinguish banners. The wights took their due where they found it — off the living, off the fallen, gold and gear and grave-goods lifted from screaming men with a courtesy worse than violence — and where a faction stood alone it died alone, and everyone saw it, and the lesson took one demonstration: a Culler file that tried doctrine — as if a wight could be cleared — folded into the dark.

Then Hale’s shield-ring anchored the center and held it, and held the gap in it open for anyone in any colors, bellowing them in; and Sael’s bows found work to suit a contract-keeper’s conscience, feathering the ghouls — evictions, clean ones, each unmade thing coming apart and draining down into the mounds it had robbed; and the factors, whose books were the best on the floor, did the thing that turned the night: they opened the toll-chests.

It was Sef who named the law — a wight walks for its withheld due; pay it and lay it — and it was Berrin who turned the law into quartermaster’s work before the sentence was cold. Every mound an account. Every account payable. The toll-silver went out across the barrows in grocers’ baskets, carried under Culler shields, laid in the mound-mouths with Sef’s rite while the Wake’s arrows kept the ghouls off the paying-parties, and Quist — who had balked exactly once, at the chest’s first opening, and been answered by Berrin, kindly, you’ll be repaid out of a freed tower, man; the dead bill first — knelt in the grass by lantern-light, counting coins into the hand of a thing in grave-gold, the oldest tool of his trade turned all the way true.

Wight by wight, mound by mound, the field was paid. And the wights, paid, lay down — that was the sight Rhovan kept: the tall figures in gold receiving their due, and turning, and lying back down into the opened earth with the contentment of a settled claim, the turf folding over them like a book closing.

Ede’s van came up the gate road at the worst hour, which was the hour generals kept; Rhovan saw the Shieldwall banner top the ridge with the host behind it, and saw it find nothing left to rescue — only three armies working the mounds in mixed parties under one bread-merchant’s tally-lanterns. Tithegrim made its stand at the vigil-ground near dawn, and it was not a battle, only a foreclosure run in reverse: a Culler shield-wall pinned the welded hound; a Tollman gate-chain, thrown and staked, bound its rider’s arm; Wake arrows took the joints; and Sef Wakewater walked up the vigil-mound under their joined shields carrying the oldest debt on the floor — the collector’s own tithe, withheld a thousand years, kept in the Lych House against a day no one had believed in — and paid it, and the rider bowed, and the whole welded engine came apart like a settled estate, the tower’s scrap of will draining down and out of a world it had no claim on — an eviction — while every yew on the floor let go its crows at once into a grey and ordinary and unbelievable morning.

The mounds were green and shut. The chain was off the lych-gate. Under it, in the new peace, three armies buried their dead side by side, in free ground, tithes paid in full — Culler and Tollman and one bowman of the Wake, and twenty silk-wrapped soldiers whose serjeant went down the row at the end touching each bier, paying something that did not go in any book.

Forty-one banners went up the freed stair that afternoon as one host, under a peace signed on a drumhead, brokered by a grocer.

Rhovan wrote his report to the Wake that night, in cipher: the toll dispute, the casualties, the new gate-law, the buy-out of Sael’s contract. It was accurate to the last figure, and it was a fraction, because he left out the one thing his masters would have paid the most to know — that on this floor the factions had stopped being factions; that the rising was now one thing, and he was in it, and he no longer knew, when he reached for the word we, whom he meant.

He sanded the page, and looked up the dark stair toward the next floor: the Kingsbarrow, the drowned king’s own moated mound, the decade-Warden that had just raised and settled a whole field of the dead without leaving its throne.

“The reeve is paid,” Sef had told them at the burying, nodding up the stair. “The King can’t be. He’s not a debt, he’s a grief, and he keeps the old forms.” He had said the rest very quietly, like a man handing over a key. “You can’t out-fight the forms. But they’re forms. They can be kept better than he keeps them. Bring your dead’s names up that stair with you, Captain. All of them. You’re going to need every one.”

Chapter 13 — The Out-Mourned King

The third assault died on the causeway like the first two, and Ede called it back before the moat could keep any more of her people.

They came off the stones in good order, shields up, dragging their hurt, and behind them the barrow guard re-formed. It always re-formed. A rank of drowned housecarls stood across the causeway where the line had broken them a hundred heartbeats before — water running out of their mail, spears levelled, patient the way only the dead are patient. Beyond them the mound rose against a heavy sky: one grassed hill the size of a town, ringed by nine black yews and a moat of water so dark it gave back no faces.

“Count,” Ede said.

“Eleven hurt, two bad.” Nix had the tally before she finished asking; he always did. “Four spears in the moat. One ram. Well. You saw the ram.”

She had seen the ram. Vega’s forge-crews had built it on the Tithe Barrows out of gate-timber and stubbornness, and the moat had opened under it like a mouth. The water had not flooded. It had risen — the way a man rises when someone enters his hall — and taken the ram, and the causeway’s third span, and a Tollman boy whose name Ede did not know yet and would have to learn. Fire had done no better. The mound drank fire. The wights her line broke drained into the black water, and the black water gave them back.

And through all of it, over all of it, the horn. The king’s herald stood on the mound’s shoulder with a silver horn that had rotted into something else, and what it blew was not a charge and not a warning. It was a summons. It got into the knees. Ede had watched a Culler serjeant of twenty years’ service kneel in the causeway mud with tears on his face and no idea why, and she had felt her own legs argue with her, and won, and known that one morning soon she would lose.

Berrin had bread and dry stockings moving down the line before the last shield came off the stones. He didn’t ask how it had gone. He never asked what a face had already told him; he just made sure the face got fed. Ede took the heel of a loaf from him without a word and stood chewing it, looking across the moat, while the barrow ravens went up off the yews in one black breath and settled again, and the horn, satisfied, went quiet.

“You cannot dig him out,” Sliver said against her palm, dry as dust in a reliquary. “You are laying siege to a funeral. I have seen it tried once. That army is in the moat.”

“Then tell me what takes a funeral.”

The splinter was quiet a long moment.

“A better one,” it said.

⁂

Mereth Wakesend received the war-council in a turf-roofed watch-house at the causeway’s landward end, because she would not leave the causeway, and after three days at the Kingsbarrow nobody asked her to. She was the last barrow-queen of Wakesend, hereditary warden of the royal grave, and she was old the way a fence-post is old: grey, checked, and not going anywhere.

“You can’t kill him,” she said. “You’ve spent three mornings learning what I could have told you the first. He isn’t a beast the fever rode. He’s a king the tower woke, and the mound is his hall and the moat is his wall and the court is his oath-men, and every one of them is made of the same grief. Steel goes into it like steel into water.”

“Everything the tower made can be broken,” Vega said. She said it slowly, the way giants say everything, and she finished her sentence, the way giants always do. “I have broken things I forged myself. I only need to find where the weld is.”

“The weld is grief, mistress,” Mereth said. “Go on. Put your hammer to that.”

The council was crowded and it was quarrelsome, because it was three armies pretending to be one. On the Tithe Barrows below, not a week gone, Cullers and Tollmen and the Wake’s hired bows had been killing each other over gate-rights when the mounds opened, and only the wights rising under all three banners at once had taught them the arithmetic — die separately or stand together. They had stood. They did not have to like it. Hale sat at the table’s end with his hands flat in front of him and said nothing at all; he had said very little since the Broodwarrens, since he had carried his own dead out of the silk.

“There is a rite,” Mereth said, when the arguing had burned itself down to coals. “Older than the tower. Older than my king, and he was old when the yews were saplings. The mound keeps the funeral-forms of Men, and the forms have a law in them, and the law is this: precedence of grief. Two mournings cannot hold one ground. The lesser yields to the greater. That is why no second king was ever howed on this floor. The mound would not have him. It was already full.”

“You’re saying we mourn at him,” said the Tollmen’s captain, and laughed, and stopped laughing when nobody joined him.

“I’m saying he cannot be out-fought, because fighting isn’t in the forms. And I am saying he can be out-mourned, because yielding is.” The barrow-queen looked around the table, at the banners, at the mud, at the scarred hands. “You are the largest company of the bereaved that has ever stood on this floor. Set it down at his moat, all of it, by the forms, with the names said aloud — and his court must answer the question the horn has been asking you for three days.”

“Which is what?” Ede said.

“Who mourns?“

Ede sat very still. She was thinking, unwillingly, of a stone at the foot of the tower with thirty names cut into it, and of who had cut them — with a chisel, at night, sober only technically — and of how cutting a name is a thing you can do instead of saying it. She had not said her dead aloud in the years since the Warcamp. Not once. Not sober.

“The forms,” she said. “Teach them to us.”

⁂

They began at dusk, because the forms began at dusk, and Wakesend’s barrow-keepers went down the line with rushlights until the moat’s whole landward bank was a thin river of fire under the heavy sky. The army knelt. It was not ordered to. The order had been only stand and keep silence, but kneeling came off the mound like weather, the horn low and long, and this time no one fought it, and so it did not take the knees. It asked.

The herald stood on the mound’s black shoulder. The horn spoke — that long, drowned summons — and Mereth Wakesend answered it from the causeway stones in a voice that carried like a bell over water.

“The living,” she called. “The living mourn.”

The horn again — shorter. A question.

“For our dead,” she answered. “And we will name them.”

Then she turned, and looked at Ede, because the forms said the greatest grief went first, and everyone on that bank knew whose that was.

Ede walked out onto the causeway alone. The barrow guard stood a spear’s throw away, dripping, still. Behind her, three thousand rushlights and not one cough. She had led charges that frightened her less.

“Corran Vael,” she said, and her voice did not carry like Mereth’s, and so she said it again, louder, and the name went out over the black water and did not come back. “Corran Vael. My first serjeant. He died on the gate of the Red Warcamp so the third file could get behind the drums. He sang badly and knew it and sang anyway.

“Bel Tarrow. Little Bel. She lied about her years to carry my banner, and I let her, and she was seventeen. Osric Pound. Marta of the Weir. Dun Fallow — the old one, not his nephew. Hewet. Cass Redmane, who owed me three coppers, and I forgave the debt on the stair and she said she’d pay me at the top anyway…”

Thirty names. She had cut them into the Stone of the Climb with her own hand, and she found that her hand remembered them better than her mouth did; twice she had to stop and stand in the silence with the rushlights burning until the name came, and the silence waited for her, and the moat waited, and even the horn waited. Nothing at the Kingsbarrow hurried grief. It was the first place in the tower where there was time enough.

Then the freed Shieldwall came out onto the stones behind her — the ones who had lived, whom she had marched into the massacre and left in Skarn’s cages, half of whom had spent a season believing she had sold them — and one-armed Dun Harrow planted the banner on the causeway and named the cage-dead. The ones who had not lasted. The winter names. His voice was a standard-bearer’s voice and it did not break, but it came close enough that no one on that bank was untouched, and when he finished, others were already walking forward, unbidden, because grief keeps its own muster.

The keepers named their floors’ dead — Fairstead’s, Cairnside’s, Wakewater’s, counts that had not matched for years finally read out in full. Vega knelt on the bank, too big for the causeway stones, and said nothing for a long while, and then, slowly, finishing every sentence, named the ones who had died wearing steel with her forge-mark on it, and asked their pardon — which was not in the forms. The mound allowed it.

Hale came last of the captains. He stood where Ede had stood and named his twenty — the Cullers the brood-silk took at the warrens — plainly, like a man reading a duty-roll. And then he did not step back. He stood there with his jaw working, and what he said next he said quietly, so that only the moat heard the whole of it: he began to name the warren’s dead. The surrendered ones. The ones his own doctrine had cost. He did not know their names — kill-all keeps no rolls — and so Nix went out and stood at his elbow on the cold stones, a goblin beside the man who had spent twenty years killing goblins, and supplied them, fast and low: “Threadfoot. Old Ochre. The midwife — they called her Two-Lamps, on account of — just Two-Lamps. Say it.” And Hale said it. All of them. It took until the middle of the night.

And the mound listened.

Ede felt it turn — not the moment the court broke, because the court did not break; the moment it conceded. The moat went still first, still as a held breath, the drowned lilies motionless on the black glass of it. Then the barrow guard grounded their spears. Rank by rank, the sound of iron butts on stone rolling around the moat’s whole ring like slow thunder, the dead housecarls set their weapons down and knelt — to the living. The tribute-bearer came down the mound with its arms full of grave-gold and laid it at the causeway’s far end, all of it, a king’s ransom set down like an offering at someone else’s funeral. The herald raised the silver horn one last time, and what came out of it was not a summons. It was a lament, long and falling, the note a horn makes when the hunt is over and everyone is coming home. Then it, too, knelt.

The mound’s lych-face opened, and the Barrow King came out into the rushlight.

He was drowned and colossal and crowned, grave-gold on his war-gear and a thousand years of moat in his bones, and the fever’s wrongness hung on him like a borrowed cloak, too small for what wore it. He stopped at the causeway’s end, above his kneeling court, and when he spoke it was in the funeral-forms and nothing else, because nothing else was left in him.

“Who mourns?”

“All of us,” Ede said.

“For whom?”

Ede looked back once — at the rushlights, at the banners of three armies that had been killing each other a week ago, at Dun’s standard and Hale’s bowed head and Nix small and straight-backed on the stones — and then she looked up at the dead king and gave him the answer the whole climb had been carrying since the night of the Theft.

“For a world.”

The king stood a long time. Rushlight moved on the black water. And then the Barrow King, who could not be beaten with steel, reached up with both hands and lifted the crown from his own head, and set it down on the causeway stones, and yielded.

“Precedence,” he said, “is given. My grief kept one man’s house. Yours keeps the world’s. Bury us well.”

He went back into the mound, and the mound went with him. It did not fall the way a wall falls. It folded — the whole grassed hill sighing inward with a sound like a breath let go after a thousand years of holding, the nine yews bowing until their crowns touched the grass, the moat pouring itself into the closing earth, the kneeling court sinking rank by rank into ground that received them like a bed long since turned down. The wrongness went out of it the way dark goes out of a room. When it settled, there was a low green barrow under a clearing sky, and a stone lych-face standing open like a door, and behind the door the stair-lift burned with its patient light, unlocked, for everyone, forever.

⁂

They did not cheer. Three days of assault had not taken the mound, and no one felt that a victory had now been won, exactly; a victory had been listened for, and granted. That was new. The army moved around it carefully all the next morning, the way you move around a sleeping child.

Mereth Wakesend took up the crown from the causeway with both hands, and did not put it on, and never would. Her keepers would climb with the rising now — crown-breakers, she called them, the folk who knew how to lay a wrongful ruler down, and Ede thought there might be work for that craft at the very top of the world. The grave-gold went to the quartermasters’ wagons under seal. The Tollman boy from the moat got his name back — Petch, a wharf-rat from Roothollow — and got it said aloud at the water’s edge, by the forms, with the whole line standing witness. The forms belonged to everyone now.

Word came up while they struck camp: Herald-fire on the freed floors — Voss’s toll-houses burned at three gates in the night. Reprisal, aimed at money. Voss, the report ran, was counting his losses out loud, and beginning, for the first time in his profiteering life, to count them in the rising’s favor. Ede filed that where she filed all news of Gilder Voss: useful, and not to be turned her back on.

She stood at the lych-face a while before she climbed, with Sliver warm in her palm.

“Three assaults,” the splinter said. “Rams, fire, and the finest steel on five floors. And the mound took a list of names.”

“Say it plainly.”

“You have been carrying that sword since Roothollow believing the sword was the weapon,” Sliver said. “It is not. It never was. The weapon is what the sword means — who it is drawn for, and who is named after. The tower has no answer to that. I have waited a long age for someone to notice.”

Ede looked up the stair, where the fourth tier waited: the Grey Rides first, by the keepers’ telling — dead lamps and a hunt that would not come home — and beyond them week upon week of floors, drifts and delvings and a funeral causeway they now knew how to walk. And at the head of the tier, at floor forty, the thing every keeper lowered their voice to mention: a throne of silk, and a queen who did not kill what she caught.

She kept what she kept, they said. And she liked to talk terms.

“Then we’d best learn what our grief is worth at market,” Ede said, and stepped onto the stair, and went up.

Chapter 14 — The Toll-Burning

A host of three thousand does not climb a stair. It queues for one.

Two days after the Kingsbarrow folded itself shut, the rising was still filing up through the lych-face lift by fifties, wagons broken down and carried in pieces, and Rhovan of the Grey Rides stood the rear watch on the last green ridge of floor thirty and did what he was for: he counted other people’s fires.

Except that the fires he was counting were not campfires, and they were not on this floor.

South and west, out over the barrow country and far beyond it, where the gate road ran away toward a horizon that was simply distance — no far wall, no rim, only the land curving off under its own grey sky — smoke stood up in the still air. One column, two days old, gone brown at the top. A second, blacker, younger, a day’s ride past it. And that morning, so far off it was less a sight than a suspicion, a third. News on this tier moved at the speed of a horse, and the smoke was the news outrunning its own riders: something was working its way down the freed floors, gate by gate, burning as it went.

The first rider had come up the lych road the night the mound closed, with the word that had gone into the reports as Herald-fire: Voss’s toll-houses burned at three gates in the night. The second rider made it five gates. The third made it eight, and brought a square of paper, and the paper was why Ede had sent for him.

She received him in the watch-house at the causeway’s end, Mereth Wakesend’s lamp still burning in it, the crown of the Barrow King sitting on the table between them wrapped in sacking, like something confiscated.

“Eight toll-houses on four floors,” Ede said. “In six nights. The riders say nobody’s seen who does it. I want someone who reads ash the way Voss reads margins. Go down, look, come back. The host won’t wait — we’re through the lych-face and climbing. Catch us up.”

“And if what I read wants acting on?”

“Then you’ll tell me what you read,” said Ede, “and I’ll act on it.” She had been two years learning how he parceled truth, and it had made her exact. He took the paper she slid across the table — the square the third rider had carried, singed at one corner. “That was on the post at the Fairstead gate. There’s one like it in every pile of ash so far. Read it on the road.”

He read it by the watch-house lamp before ever he took the road, because paper spoke to him louder than smoke.

It was a fair clerk’s hand, ruled, unhurried. No seal. No signature.

Item: the gate-companies of Roothollow, having extended their custom and their credit to the rebellion, are found in default of the King’s peace.

Item: their charter is void. Their credit is called in. Their houses are closed, as witnessed.

Item: the silver is left. The King has no need of your silver. You are invited to look at it, and to consider what it kept.

Settlement of account.

Rhovan stood very still for the time a lamp takes to gutter once.

Settlement of account. He had read that phrase before, in a strong-room a mile inside a stolen mountain, under an entry that had bored him and then unmade his month: money moved up the tower, no goods against it, the week Ede’s company was sold. The remittance-book’s grammar. Whoever clerked for the tower’s high offices, they wrote a burned gate and a bought company in the same ruled hand — and this time had wanted it read. A ledger whispers. This was a ledger raising its voice.

He folded the paper into his coat, next to the older page sewn in its lining, and went down.

⁂

It took him three days to reach the Fairstead gate, on a borrowed barrow pony, down gate roads he had walked up a season before, and the going down was a lesson in what the rising had been building. The toll-roads were arteries now. Grain went up them, and boot-leather, and arrow-shafts; the mail went up them, and freed families went down them, going home to floors their grandmothers had been stolen onto. At every gate the factors’ chalk-boards posted rates, and the rates were honest since the Tithe Barrows, and Rhovan passed queues at open chains and thought, not for the first time, that the tower had never once managed to build a thing this dangerous.

The Heralds had understood it too. That was the whole of what the ashes said.

The Fairstead toll-house had been a good stone building with a slate roof, and it was a black shell with its lintel cracked when Rhovan rode up to it through the fair-country dusk. The Quiet had got there first — the fair-folk of the floor, who had survived reprisal before and knew its etiquette — and they had done what they knew: taken the bunting off their own doors and shuttered their lamps and left the ruin scrupulously alone, in case looking at it too long counted as taking a side.

He read the burn the way he had been taught to read rooms.

They had come at night, on foot, without lamps. They had not hurried; the char-lines said the fire was laid, not thrown — ledgers first, stacked on the counting-table and lit like a hearth, the building fired from the paper outward, so that the books were cinders before the roof ever caught. Coin-boxes dragged into the yard, opened, and left. Silver fused into slabs by the heat, in ash where any hand could take it, and no hand had. Six nights, eight gates, and not one coin missing anywhere — which was the true message, and it was addressed to every carter and factor and gate-wife on twelve floors, and it said: this is not robbery. Robbery a man can price, and carry, and rebuild from.

The keeper of the toll-house had been a factor named Immer, a Warcamp survivor who wore Voss’s livery over the scars, and Immer had done the one thing the fire’s authors had no line ruled for: he had gone back in for the current-book, the season’s accounts, every debt owed to and by four floors of small traders. He had got it out, mostly. He sat in the yard with his burned hands wrapped in fair-cloth, the book in his lap under the bandages, and gave Rhovan his testimony in a voice flattened by two days of use.

Hooded men. Nine or ten, come out of the dark on the road’s blind side, the night watch face-down in the grass with their thumbs bound before a dog barked. No blades shown. No word said above a murmur. They had worked, Immer said, like inventory-men — one counting the ledgers into the fire, aloud, title by title, so that a man lying in the grass could hear his own life’s work enumerated as it burned. And when the roof fell in, they nailed their paper to the post and walked into the dark in no hurry at all, southward, downward, toward the next gate on their list.

“They knew the relay schedule,” Immer said. “They fired the beacon gates first — the three that carry the up-mail. Word crawled. Gate couldn’t warn gate.” His mouth worked. “The tower kept better books on my toll-road than I did.”

Rhovan looked at the melted silver in the ash, and at the coin column nailed up the gate-post — Voss’s proud tariff, a head-price for a man, a price for a mule — where the heat had run the nailed coins into one long bright smear down the wood, like a candle’s year of weeping done in an hour. He made his tally in the currency he dealt in: eight gates, chosen not for their riches but for their freight; the mail-gates first; the grammar of the high tower’s own receiver on the bill. The fire had read a manifest. Somebody’s manifest.

He wrote the Wake that night from the fair-country verge, in the flat cipher of facts: gates burned, coin left, credit dead, carters refusing the up-roads, the rising’s supply line bleeding out not from any wound but from fright. It was accurate to the last figure, and it was a fraction. He did not write the sentence he could not source: the tower has stopped sending soldiers down, because it has found the thing that works.

⁂

He caught the host at the Grey Rides’ first camps, eleven days later, and found the war-council already convened around a trestle in the bridleway dusk — because the war had come up the lift behind him, in the shape of a heavy man walking.

Gilder Voss, Tollmaster-General of the gate-companies of Roothollow, came up the last mile of the gate road on foot, at the head of what remained of his southern factorage: six clerks, two guards who had not been paid and had come anyway, and a mule that had given out a floor below and been left, its load shifted to the Tollmaster’s own back. He carried two strong-boxes in a porter’s frame, the straps cutting welts you could see from the ridge, and he would not give them up — not to the pickets, not to his clerks, not to the three soldiers who offered and were priced with a look and found wanting. Under one arm he carried his ledgers, or what the fire had left of them, singed to the spine at the corners, and those he held the way other men had carried their dead down from the Warcamp.

Rhovan watched him come and understood he was seeing a man carry his entire remaining self uphill.

They gave him the trestle’s end, and beer he did not drink, and the council looked at him — Ede at the table’s head with the day’s climb still on her, Dun Harrow at his post behind her shoulder, banner grounded; Sef Wakewater and his vigil-book; Hale silent at the foot; Berrin, who had come up with the evening’s bread and stayed, because supply would be the whole matter; Nix on a barrel, counting the strong-boxes at a glance. Voss set the frame down off his back with the care of a man lowering a coffin, and straightened, and winced, and looked around the trestle with his small shrewd unbeautiful eyes, and said:

“Before anyone begins. I am not here because I have seen a light.”

“Nobody supposed it,” said Ede.

“They burned eleven houses,” Voss said. “By now, likely thirteen. They left the silver. Do you understand what that is, captain? I have been robbed by professionals on four floors and I have robbed a few myself, in the way of business, and there is not a thief in the world who leaves the silver. Silver they left. Paper they burned. Forty years of paper — bonds, remittances, standing credit, the debts of every carter between your yard and my gates. A man’s coin is his purse, captain, but a man’s paper is his face, and the King has burned my face off the tier.” His voice stayed level the whole way through it, which was somehow worse than grief. “And nailed up a bill informing the world that only one house may keep accounts under this sky. His.”

“And so?” Ede said.

“And so I have re-read the market.” Voss put one hand flat on the strong-boxes. “I do not love your rising. It is loud, it is sentimental, and its arithmetic would shame a child. But it is now, as of six nights past, the only market left standing. A world where one hand writes all the paper is not a world a man of business inhabits; it is a world he decorates. I decline it. So.” He pushed the boxes, both of them, the width of the trestle. “The southern factorage’s reserve. It buys grain where fright has stopped credit. It pays carters in coin at the gate until the roads believe in paper again — my clerks will draw the schedule; they are the only men on this tier who know which debts still stand, because I went into the fire for the proof of it.” A short pause, the first. “Immer went into the fire. I employ men who go into fires. That is the whole of my character, captain, and I’d thank the council to expect nothing better of it.”

One of the keepers said what half the camp would say by morning: “And when the King’s clerks come to buy you back?”

“Buy me back?” For one heartbeat, Voss looked honestly affronted — the professional insulted on the one point of his pride. “Sir, I have just been shown the King’s terms of purchase. He paid eleven houses to teach the freed floors that my custom is death to take. There is no percentage under him. There is no under him at all; that is what a hundred floors means, I grasp it at last, it only wanted putting in figures I could read.” He turned back to Ede. “I am not converted. Let the criers say otherwise and I will sue the criers. I am invested. Every coin in those boxes is a wager that you reach the top, and I mean to stand at the settling of it holding the world’s entire paper in my two hands. You will find me insufferable. You will find me at my post.”

The silence had the particular texture of a council working out whether it was allowed to take what it needed.

Ede looked at the strong-boxes a long moment, and Rhovan, who had spent two years learning her face, watched her arrive at the only sane filing there was for Gilder Voss — useful, and never to be turned your back on — and then reach out, with her eyes open, and turn it into policy.

“No titles,” she said. “You’ll want one; you’ll not have it; paid on delivery, if we live. Your clerks keep their books under Berrin’s countersign, as at the Barrows. The boxes travel under Shieldwall guard, sealed, audited when the council says — and you will be watched, Tollmaster, all the way to the top, by people who remember your chains at open gates.”

“Watched,” said Voss, with his first and only smile of the evening, sitting down at last to the beer, “is another word for guarded. I accept.”

It was Berrin who closed it, because it was Berrin who closed things: he put bread in front of the Tollmaster, and sat down across from him with his lists, warm as an oven-door, and said, “Now then. Show me what’s left of your paper, Gilder, and we’ll draw up what the fire’s still owed” — and the two best ledger-minds in the freed world bent over the singed remains together in the lamplight, rebuilding the tier’s credit out of a quartermaster’s memory, and everyone at that trestle slept easier for the sight of it.

⁂

Rhovan wrote his report to the Wake two nights on, in cipher, from a bridleway verge: the burnings enumerated, the bill transcribed, the Tollmaster arrived, the reserve pledged, terms as stated. Accurate to the last figure. A fraction.

He left out the coins run down the gate-post like candle-wax. He left out that the mail-gates had burned first, in an order that wanted a manifest, and that the bill’s ruled hand had the same grammar as a receipt he carried sewn into his coat. Those he kept, the way he kept everything that mattered, for the day telling it would arm somebody instead of gutting them.

And he left out the thing he had watched at the trestle, because the Wake’s ciphers had no characters for it: the rising taking a man’s greed into its line of battle with its eyes open — no oath sworn, no heart changed, a wall and a purse agreeing to stand in the same shield-ring because the same enemy had priced them both for burning. It was the most cynical alliance the climb had yet signed, and Rhovan had been three hundred years in the trade of cynical arrangements, and he could not stop noticing that this one had been made in daylight, at a public table, in front of the bread.

The tower bought men in the dark and burned what it could not buy. The rising put its one thief on the books, and posted the audit.

Somewhere below, the last smoke of Voss’s old empire stood up into a country-wide sky, and above, at the head of the grey bridleways, the wagons were already rolling under new paper. Rhovan sanded the page, and pocketed the fraction, and climbed.

Chapter 15 — Silk Terms

The rising makes good pace, Rhovan wrote. Nine floors in a season. The host is fed and its factions hold.

He did not write: the host walked the Grey Rides behind a fourscore-year hunt and heard it called home. He did not write what that had sounded like. The Wake paid him for facts, and the Wake’s ciphers had no characters for the things that mattered.

The Rides had been the tier’s first gift and its first lesson. Grey bridleways running arrow-true through a dark elf-wood — his own people’s wood, a fragment of it, stranded thirty floors from its roots — with the lantern-crows dead on their grey posts and a spectral hunt coursing the ways forever after quarry freed long ago. The army had hugged the verges for two days while Aline Bridlerest, the last ride-master, taught Ede’s captains the shape of the thing: you did not fight a hunt, you recalled it. When Tallyhorn broke on the third morning — a courser welded to its dead rider, brought down on the open road — Aline had sounded the true recall from the Ride-Head crossroads, and the whole grey hunt had come home. Horse and rider and hound, laid down at the wayshrine, fourscore years late for their own supper. The ride-keepers climbed with the rising now, its outriders and its fastest word.

After that the floors ran together the way marching floors do. The Deep Drifts, winch-rope dark; the Troll Delvings; the Rig Graves, rust islands in a drowned marsh; the Vent Country’s scalded plain. Ran together, and did not shrink. The Drifts were a week of going down to go up, a country measured in fathoms, winch-ropes creaking under loads that would never come up, cold air sighing out of shafts that swallowed lamplight whole; Orla Ropewalk’s crews lowered the army cage by cage and raised it on the far headframe, reading the ropes by their pull the way other folk read sky. In the Delvings nobody spoke above a murmur for three days, because the trolls of the Deep shore up nothing, and the whole floor was one cave-in that had not finished falling, groaning and shedding stone at the lamplight’s edge while Hune Delverest’s pick-crews walked ahead chalking the hour’s safe ground. The Rig Graves stank of rust and still water from rim to rim — the fen country’s great harvest-rigs toppled to their own horizon, hulls for islands, derricks for dead trees — and Weld Rustwake’s salvage-folk poled the column between them across the orange meres. On the Vent Country’s crust Sela Steamgate walked the van herself for two days, timing the ground’s white jets by their whistles — read the whistle, not the steam, she said; the steam has already cooked you — and the army crossed a breathing yellow plain single file, on the clock of one keeper’s ear. The Processional Way they had dreaded — a funeral causeway of willows and passing-bells — they walked in three days, quietly, bells rung by the forms, Wakesend’s keepers leading; they knew how to walk a funeral road now. Then the Broken Doors, the Peat Cuts, and the Echo Glades, where the wood answered whatever it heard, and three thousand marching feet came back out of the trees like a second army keeping step. The Doors sobered even the giants: a tier of bulkhead-gates the deep-forge had built to hold an age, every one blown off its hinges in a single season, the corridors between them crow-loud and scorched where the tower’s charges had cut through; Bruna Lastlantern kept the one door left, and walked the army through the breaches slowly, so that it would understand what it was climbing against. The Peat Cuts were two days of black trenches slotted dead-straight to the horizon, house-deep, ankle-flooded, whining with midge-fog, Maddox Turfside’s cutters walking the banks above the column and calling down the firm ground — and in the deepest cut the peat gave up its dead whole and perfect, and the rising, which knew funerals by then, laid them back down.

Berrin’s supply-trains found them on every floor. That was the season’s quiet miracle, and Rhovan, whose trade was noticing, noticed it: Roothollow’s provisioner was never more than two days behind the van with flour and boot-leather and the mail from below. “You can plan a war around that man,” Ede said once, watching the wagons come up the Peat Cuts in the rain, and Rhovan had written that down for the Wake too, in the flat cipher of facts: supply reliable.

The tier ends at floor forty, he wrote instead, that last night on the Echo Glades. The Broodthrone. The keepers will not speak of it above a whisper. We treat with it inside a week.

He did not write: and I have heard what it keeps, and I am afraid in a way I have not been since the Theft.

⁂

Threadneedle was not a gate-town so much as a wound’s edge. Its people — the free ones, the few — lived in the rim-tunnels where the great cavern narrowed, and their keeper met the war-council at a barricade of anchor stone with silk-shears at her belt and both forearms scarred to the elbow. Wend Threadneedle, thread-cutter. She had spent nine years cutting cocoons open one strand at a time.

“You’ll want to see it before you talk about it,” was all she said.

They saw it. The Broodthrone was one cavern the size of a stolen city, and every span of it was spun. Silk-galleries rose tier over tier into the dark like the inside of some pale cathedral, and hung through them, soft-lit from within by the fever’s own glow, were the cocoons. Hundreds of them. They hung like lanterns at a festival, and each one held a living captive — courtiers, servants, tribute, the taken of a dozen floors. Some of them, Wend said flatly, had been hanging for years and would greet you by name if you cut them down. Vyx did not kill what she caught. That was the whole horror of her: she kept.

The war-council met that night in the rim-tunnels and planned an assault none of them believed in. Fire was madness — the silk would burn with everyone in it. Cutting was slow — nine years had freed Wend a few dozen. And the Matriarch’s court would fight the way it always fought — every blow landing on the wrapped and the puppeted, the very people the rising had come to free.
