Vega sat with her fists on her knees and said, slowly, that she had broken into every kind of lock the tower had yet shown her, and that this was the first one made entirely of hostages, and she did not finish her sentence, which from Vega was the most frightening thing said all night.

Then the thread-page came.

It was a child. That was the first thing that stopped every hand on every hilt — a child of Men, grey-faced, silk-collared, walking out of the dark web with a control-thread running from its neck back up into the vault. It stopped at the edge of the council’s firelight, and its mouth opened, and the voice that came out was not a child’s. It was silk drawn over glass, cool and faintly amused, and far away.

“A courtesy, before the parties waste one another,” it said. “The Matriarch Vyx, freeholder of this floor by right of survival, proposes that the company now trespassing at her rim send its principals to treat. She observes that you have fought your way up thirty-nine floors, and she wishes you to know that on her floor, and on her floor alone” — the child’s hand rose, palm up, in a gesture no child ever made — “everything is for sale.“

The thread went slack. The child blinked, and began to cry, and Wend Threadneedle crossed the firelight and held it, glaring up into the dark as if her eyes could cut silk.

⁂

They went in at noon by the great gallery, under a hundred hanging lanterns that were people: Ede, Vega, Rhovan, Wend to read the threads, and Mereth Wakesend, because after the Kingsbarrow the rising did not treat with crowned things without her. Nix came too. Nobody had decided that; he was simply there, walking point with the Shieldwall standard, counting cocoons under his breath and hating every number.

Matriarch Vyx received them on the Throne-Web, and Rhovan, who had spent a professional lifetime keeping his face still, needed all of it. She was vast — not swollen, the way the fever swells its beasts, but grown, the patient bulk of something that had been eating well and thinking long since before the Theft. Aether burned in her spinnerets like rings on a magistrate’s fingers. Below her, arranged down the web’s tiers like a court at levée, her thralls stood in their silk livery, each moving with the small borrowed grace of the puppeted; and among them, armored and motionless, hung the cocooned corpse of a mailed knight — her champion, Wend had whispered, dead in the silk a long age and still fencing with drilled skill when the lines pulled.

“The party of the first part,” Vyx said, in that silk-over-glass voice, each word set down precisely, like a clause. “Ede Stonhal, called the banner-general; her captains; her witnesses. Be welcome under my vault. You will forgive me if I do not descend. At my displacement, courtesy is a hazard.”

“You asked us here to sell us passage,” Ede said. “Name your price.”

“Directness. How refreshing, after forty floors of heralds.” The Matriarch shifted, and the whole vault’s silk sang one low note, and every thrall in the court swayed with it. “Then I shall be direct in my turn. I will not fight you. Understand this is not mercy; it is arithmetic. I have watched your kind since before the black iron came, and I know what an army that mourns its dead by name does to whatever stands in its road. You would win. It would cost you a tenth of your banners and me the whole of my house, and I have spent nine hundred years furnishing it. Ruin profits no signatory. Therefore: terms.”

“We’re listening,” Ede said.

“Clause the first. Passage. The host of the rising crosses my floor unhindered, by the gallery road, van to rear-guard, wagons and stragglers inclusive; and the stair-lift behind my throne, which I have kept wrapped these many years against exactly this negotiation, is unwrapped for its use. In perpetuity. Your reinforcements will follow you up this tier for years, little general. They pass too. That is what I am selling: floor forty, open, without a single funeral.

“Clause the second. Tribute, annual, in three parts.” She counted them on the air with one foreclaw, delicately. “Part the first: warmth. Once in each year, for one night, the host’s cook-fires are lit in my hall. You cannot know what you own, you surface things, who had a sun. My people have never once been warm. I will have the warmth of a nation’s suppers in my vault one night a year, and my brood will sleep in it.

“Part the second: a hostage of rank, to dwell in my court as surety while the host and its trains are upon my floor, and to be surrendered whole, unwrapped, and unpuppeted — the condition of the goods to be warranted by me — upon the rear-guard’s clearance. Rank, mark you. I do not warehouse spear-carriers. The pledge is only as dear as the head.

“Part the third.” And here the silk voice slowed, and something old moved in it, and every elf-instinct Rhovan owned sat up. “One true story, never before told to any ear, to be paid to me annually, at this court, for so long as the contract runs. I hold four hundred years of other people’s secrets in this web, and I am bored, and boredom in a creature of my displacement is a public danger. Truth, novelty, and delivery in person. Those are the coins of part the third, and I will know a false one, so do not try.”

Silence under the vault. A hundred cocoon-lanterns glowing softly.

“And your court,” Ede said. “The people in your silk. They’re not in these clauses.”

“They are my property and my furniture, and they are not on the table.” Flat. Final. A door closing. “You may not like a clause and sign, general. It is done every day.”

Rhovan watched Ede stand under the hanging lanterns with a dozen floors’ captives glowing above her head, and watched her measure it: the tenth of her banners, the winter coming, the years of reinforcements who would climb this tier behind them, against every hanging light she would be walking away from. He had blackmailed this woman up a tower, once, in another life, and he knew the exact shape of her when she was about to pay too much of herself. Vega’s fists were closed. Wend Threadneedle was not breathing.

“We will come back for them,” Ede said at last — to Wend, to the vault, to the lanterns; a promise laid down in front of the Matriarch like a counter-clause. “Every one. This contract buys passage. It does not buy our forgetting.”

“Forgetting was not offered,” said Vyx, silkily. “Have we terms?”

“One question first,” Ede said. “The hostage. Held how long?”

“Until your rear-guard clears my tier-gate. A season, as your trains straggle. The story-clause alone is perpetual.” The Matriarch’s foreclaw turned, palm up, a merchant’s shrug the size of a wagon. “Well? Who is of rank, and expendable enough to hang in my hall, and interesting enough that I do not regret the keeping? It is a narrow list, general. I have read your court from here and I count perhaps three names.”

“Two,” said Rhovan, and stepped forward before the fear could finish its sentence. “The others are load-bearing.”

Every head turned.

“Rhovan—”

“Of the Grey Rides,” he said to the Matriarch, and heard his own voice come out level. “Archer, agent of the Dusk Wake, resident with the rising since Roothollow.” A fraction. As always, a fraction. “Rank you may verify: the Wake does not send junior men to blackmail generals.”

“He is a captain of this council,” Ede said, slowly, formally, “and the rising counts him among its own. His head is dear to us.” She looked at him while she said it, and Rhovan understood that he was being given the rank that made him worth keeping alive, and that it was true, and that she had just told him so in front of a monster and an army, and that this was the most anyone had said of him in forty years.

“An elf of the Wake.” Vyx’s voice curled with pleasure, silk winding on a spindle. “Oh, accepted. The Wake has been declining my correspondence for three centuries. Clause the second, part the second: satisfied. Which leaves” — the foreclaw came around, unhurried, and pointed at him the way a clerk points at a ledger-line — “part the third. The first year’s story falls due on signature. It need not be yours, hostage. It need only be true, and new. Have you one about you?”

He had been an agent long enough to know that some purses only open all the way once. He thought of Calyne — his handler, his examiner, the Wake’s grey arithmetic in a woman’s shape — and of what she would do when this reached her, and it would reach her; the Wake marked its secrets the way shepherds mark lambs. And he thought: the rising needs this floor, and this queen’s goodwill after it, for years.

Then he paid.

“In the first winter after the Theft,” Rhovan said, “when the great wood was in nineteen pieces on nineteen floors and the lamps were going out in all of them, three lamplighter houses met in the only grove still lit, and founded a watch, and called it the Dusk Wake — those who stay waking while the wood sleeps.” Ede went very still at his shoulder. None of this was known outside the Wake; men had been quietly drowned for less. “The Wake does not climb to cast the Demon King down, whatever it lets its hirelings believe. It climbs to make the wood whole. And it keeps, in a place I will not name, a roll called the Leaf-Ledger: every fragment of the old forest, every floor that holds one, every lamp that might yet be relit. It has kept that ledger current for the whole age of the tower. No one outside the Wake has ever heard its name. You are the first.”

The vault was silent. Somewhere above, a cocoon turned slowly on its thread, glowing.

“Oh,” said Matriarch Vyx, with four hundred years of appetite in one soft syllable, “we are going to get on, hostage. Clause the third: satisfied, and handsomely. Scrivener!” — a thrall jerked upright with a silk-white book — “Take down the instrument. The parties will sign.”

⁂

The host passed the Broodthrone in three days, and no army in the world’s history had ever marched so quietly. They went by the gallery road under the hanging lanterns, thousands of boots on silk that gave back no sound at all, and the wrapped court watched them pass with whatever was left behind its hundred pairs of eyes, and every soldier looked up, and no one spoke. Nix walked the whole road backwards with the standard, watching the cocoons out of sight. Rhovan heard him counting, and heard the count stop, and heard him start again, quieter.

The floor is passed without loss, Rhovan wrote, on the last night, by the light of a cocoon he did not look at. Terms enclosed. The tribute binds the rising, not the Wake.

He did not write what he had spent. She would know by spring.

On the third evening the rear-guard’s horns sounded from the tier-gate, thin and far above, and the last fighting company’s lamp went up the unwrapped stair and out of sight. But the clause counted wagons and stragglers; Berrin’s trains would climb this road all winter, and the tier behind would keep sending its freed up for a season yet. The great web settled — the whole vault breathing one long settling breath of silk — and Rhovan of the Grey Rides was alone in the Broodthrone with the Matriarch and her kept court and a winter of nights to be got through one at a time.

Silk sang, far up in the dark, as something vast and patient descended.

“Now, hostage,” said Vyx, warmly, clause by clause, “we keep house.”

Chapter 16 — The Hostage Chapter

There was no day in the Broodthrone, and no night, and after the first stretch Rhovan stopped pretending to count either. The vault kept its own time. The cocoon-lanterns brightened and dimmed together on some slow tide of the fever, breathing light; the brood stirred in the high galleries at intervals he came to know; and twice in every cycle of the lamps, silk sang one long low note as the Matriarch moved upon her web, and the hall’s threads carried her weight like a shore carrying tide. He learned her tread inside ten sleeps. He could have told her from a thousand others with his eyes shut and his ear against a single strand.

Later he would understand that this was the winter’s whole lesson, and that she had let him learn it on purpose.

They gave him a chamber in the throne-web’s second tier — a room of white silk with a bed of white silk, dry, clean, and terrible, because it was a cocoon by any honest accounting; it simply had a door. A thread-page brought his meals: fair bread, blind cavern-fish, water without color or taste. He ate what the court ate. That was in the clauses. Everything was in the clauses. He was permitted any gallery whose silk bore his weight, and forbidden the Larder, and he tested neither rule: the first was a courtesy, the second a mercy, and he knew the difference.

He tried his trade, the first sleeps, out of habit and fear — mapping anchor-lines, marking which threads were leashes as Wend had taught him. He gave it up inside a week. There was nothing here to steal. The Broodthrone was the only court he had ever entered that kept no secrets from itself: every thread catalogued, every captive inventoried, every debt entered in the silk-white book. It was the Wake’s dream, achieved by a spider. He did not put that in a letter.

He was allowed letters. Clause the ninth: the hostage may write; the court may read. So he wrote to the rising, in the flat cipher of facts, knowing Vyx read every stroke before the ride-keeper courier took the page: I am well kept. The contract holds. The fish is better than the army’s. Fractions of fractions. Somewhere below, the Wake would already be moving on his first year’s story; and there was a letter he was not writing, to a grey woman who would not read it — every sleep he composed a line of it, and every waking unwrote it.

Mostly, there was the quiet. He had thought he knew quiet — he was an elf of the Rides, raised where the wood holds its breath at dusk — but the Broodthrone’s quiet was another element altogether, a sea of it, with the wrapped court hanging in it like drowned lights. A hundred living people around him, and no voice. He took to talking to the nearest cocoon on his gallery, a lantern-glow with the shape of a stout matron folded in it. He told her the news of the march and reported the fish. He never learned if she heard. On the worst sleeps he thought she leaned toward his voice, and on the best ones too.

⁂

The Matriarch came down to him on the twelfth sleep, or the fifteenth — the lamps had bred, by then, and he had lost the count on purpose — and taught him the game.

“You are dying of stillness,” she observed, settling into the web above his gallery with the terrible gentleness of the very heavy. Her voice at close quarters was worse and better: silk over glass, but warm glass, a lamp’s chimney. “I warranted you back whole. Boredom voids the warranty. Attend: this is called cradles. My court has played it since before your people had lamps to light.”

It was a game of knots. She spun him a strand of dry silk, cool as linen, and tied into it a figure — loops crossed on loops — and passed it to his spread fingers, and the law of the game was that he must take it onto his hands without collapsing a single loop, and return it changed: one knot added, none undone. The first evening he crashed the whole cradle inside five exchanges and she laughed at him, a sound like a bow drawn slow across a low string, and spun a new strand, and they began again.

The stakes, she said, were questions. Whoever crashed the cradle owed one true answer.

“I am a hostage of the Wake,” he said. “You know what my answers are worth.”

“I know what your people think they are worth. Play.”

He crashed it again — she had eight hands and patience like bedrock, and he never won a rally outright all winter, and he came to suspect the game could not be won, only lost more slowly, and that this too was the lesson. And the questions, when he owed them, were nothing his training had braced for. She did not ask about the rising’s strength, or the Wake’s roll of agents, or the Leaf-Ledger he had named at the signing. She asked:

“What does rain sound like?”

He sat with the crashed silk across his palms while his voice went somewhere and had to be fetched back.

“On leaves,” she added, precisely. “Begin with rain on leaves. I have read of it in four hundred years of tribute and the accounts do not agree, and it does not fall in the Deep, and it never once fell in this tower. I am owning to an ignorance, hostage. Do not make me regret it.”

So he told her rain, as well as he could — rain on the lamp-woods’ broad leaves, rain on the Rides’ grey stone, rain on a canvas fly with a friend asleep against your shoulder-blade, which is the best rain — and partway through it he understood that he was paying the story-clause again, in small coin, freely; and that she had known he would; and that four hundred years of contracts had taught her the one truth the Wake had never taught him: that the things people guard are rarely the things worth having, and the things worth having, they will give you for nothing, if you only sit still and ask.

When he owed no answer, she took none. When she owed him one — twice all winter, twice in hundreds of rallies — she paid in full, to the letter, like the honest instrument she was. The first time, he asked about the wrapped knight, her cocooned champion.

“Taken in the Men’s wars, four tiers up, ninety years gone,” she said. “Dead before he reached my floor; I do not warrant what I did not break. His skill is in the lines now, not in him. There is no man in that mail, hostage. There is only the drill, going on without him. You are asking whether I would keep you so. You are of rank, warranted, and alive. The clauses forbid it.”

“And if there were no clauses?”

“There are always clauses,” said Matriarch Vyx. “That is the difference between me and the tower.”

⁂

The second answer she owed him, she paid on the coldest sleep of the winter, when Berrin’s trains had stopped for the freeze on the tier above and the vault’s tide of light ran shallow. She had crashed the cradle herself — he never knew whether by mishap or by purchase — and instead of waiting for his question she settled lower on the web, all her weight gathered soft as snowfall, and said:

“I will tell you a story. True, and never told, because no one has ever been positioned to be told it. We shall call it interest on your Leaf-Ledger, which was a better coin than my clause demanded, and I do not like to owe. Attend, hostage.”

The lamps breathed. The court hung still on its threads.

“I was old already when the engines came,” she said. “This hall was my mother’s, and the caverns under the world were wide, and we heard it before we felt it: a note in the rock, below the deepest gallery, going along and never stopping for breath. My brood ran upward. I went down. I have never been able to leave an accounting unread.

“I hung in the dark of the deepest shaft, and beneath me — beneath the floor of the world, where there is not supposed to be an underneath — the engines of the Demon King went by. Hours of them. Patient as water. They were cutting, hostage, that is what I want you to understand; not burning, not breaking. Cutting, the way a clerk cuts a page from a ledger he means to keep. And above me through the rock I heard a whole country’s roots let go — the rivers first, then the stones, then the trees all together — and the world I was born under went up into the dark like a lamp being carried out of a room, and the cold came in where it had been.

“I watched the underside of a stolen country pass me in the dark. There were still lamps burning in it. That is the whole of what I saw, and I have thought about it for the age of the tower, and I will tell you the conclusion: I hated him, your Demon King, not for the theft. Theft I understood; I am a keeper of taken things myself, and I will not insult you by pretending otherwise. I hated him because he did not keep them. He took a world alive and he warehoused it, floor from floor, in the dark, and let it starve of itself. Even I warm what I wrap, hostage. Even I know that a kept thing kept badly is only a slow kill with a clerk’s face on it.”

The silk was silent a long while.

“There is a second chapter,” she said. “Smaller. You will want it more. Twenty years gone, or near it, a climber paid my toll — I sold passage retail then, to the rare mad soul the Tollmen sent up with their marks on his coin. He paid in toll-stamped silver and he would not pay the story-price, though I offered him the discount; he gave me warmth-jars instead, double weight. A hooded man of Men. Unremarkable, except in this: that four seasons later, my threads woke on the downward road. And it was the same tread, hostage. Coming down.”

Rhovan did not move. Loop by loop, very carefully, the way you hold a cradle you cannot afford to crash, he kept his hands still.

“Down,” he said.

“Down. Paid again, in the same silver, and would not meet my lamps, and went. The only soul in the age of this tower to pass my web twice. Your armies pass upward, and the Pressed flee upward, and the dead go nowhere. Nothing comes down, hostage. The tower is a valve. And once, just once, it ran backwards. A step on my thread is a name, though not one your mouths could say. I would know his tread tonight among ten thousand. Twenty years has not worn it off my web.”

“Who?”

“No clause bought his name, and I do not give what was not bought. He paid for silence with his silver, and my instruments hold whether the party is present or absent, or my word is worth nothing and you have wintered with a beast.” A soft note in the silk, almost regret. “The fact of him I give you freely, as interest. What he was, where he went, whose bread he ate after — those were never mine to sell.”

Whose bread he ate. Rhovan heard it, and it went down through him like a thread through a needle’s eye. A freed prisoner in the Warcamp’s wreckage, saying: the courier smelled of Roothollow bread. Everyone in the rising’s council had built the same quiet story from it: a bought man below, a letter passed at a gate-town, coin moving up through the Tollmen’s chains. A traitor who had never left home.

But nothing comes down. And once, the tower ran backwards.

Whoever had sold Shieldwall had not sold it by post. He had climbed — past the Rides, past this very web, twenty years of tread unchanged, up into the dark where the buyers were — and then he had come home, and hung up his hood, and eaten his bread at somebody’s table, and watched the search look everywhere but down. The traitor was not a hand the tower had reached. The traitor had gone up the tower and asked for it.

“You have stopped playing,” Vyx observed.

“I’m thinking about a hood,” Rhovan said.

“Mm.” Eight hands gathered in the crashed silk and began a new strand. “A last observation, then, gratis, because it has been a tolerable winter and you tell rain well. I have taken tribute from every kind of creature this tower holds, and the accounting is simple: the ones who pay and go are honest, and the ones who pay twice are buying something they cannot carry in their hands. He was not buying passage, hostage. Passage was the receipt. What he bought was the road itself — the proof it could be walked both ways and no one the wiser. Someone in your bright climbing world has owned a private road for twenty years.” The cradle rose, loops crossed on loops, and hung on the air between them. “I would ask myself, were I you and loose in the daylight: what has been traveling it since?”

⁂

The thaw came to the tier above; the trains moved; and on a sleep like every other sleep, a thread-page stood at his silk door and unpicked it, stitch by stitch, and the clauses let him go.

She received his leave-taking at the Throne-Web, enormous and courteous, and had the scrivener enter the hostage-clause discharged, warranty honored, goods returned whole. He was surprised, and then not surprised, to find something in him reluctant at the gallery road’s edge. It had been the strangest season of a long strange life, and the stillest, and no one in it had lied to him even once, which he could not say of any other winter he had lived.

“Clause the third falls due in a year, hostage,” said Matriarch Vyx. “Bring a better story. You have one; I heard you not telling it, all winter, every sleep. Knots carry sound, and untold stories knock.”

“Perhaps by next winter it will be tellable.”

“They ripen,” she agreed. “Everything I keep does.”

At the tier-gate above, in the first true daylight of the young year, the ride-keeper courier was waiting for him with the rising’s mail: two seasons of Ede’s dispatches, a wax-sealed note in Nix’s furious fist-print — FISH?? we saved you the good boots. the count is 41 banners. climb faster — and a grey slip of paper with no seal at all, edges singed in the Wake’s plain way of saying a channel was burned, five ciphered words long.

He stood in the wind and read it three times.

I am coming up. — C.

Rhovan of the Grey Rides looked up the tier, toward the chasm-mouth camps and the wintering army and the friend he had to reach before his handler did, and began, at last, to climb faster.

Chapter 17 — Calyne

The word came up with the ride-keepers, the way all word came up now: a grey rider on the Grey Rides, climbing alone.

Ede was at the chasm-mouth camps when the report reached her, standing on the black moraine above the tier-gate with the first true cold of the wintering in her teeth. Below, on the floors they had freed, the rising’s road ran like a lit wick — gate-towns waking, wagons climbing, the freed coming up behind their own liberation in numbers Berrin’s clerks could barely count fast enough. Above, past the boulder-fields and the wheeling glare hawks, the tier went on into ice. Forty-one banners now. A nation moving, and stopping soon to breathe.

“Alone,” the ride-keeper said again, as if the word itself needed guarding. “Aline sighted her at the Wayshrine. Grey cloak, grey horse, no banner. She rides the middle of the Rides, captain. Not the verge. The middle, like she owns them.” A pause. “The crows follow her. Aline says they’ve followed her three floors.”

“The Wake,” said Rhovan, from behind his cup.

He had come up from the Broodthrone a fortnight past, thin as a bowstring, smelling of silk under the woodsmoke, with a winter of quiet folded away behind his eyes and news that had kept the inner council up three nights running. Now he looked down the tier the way a man looks at weather coming.

“You know her, then,” Ede said.

“Mistress Calyne. My handler.” A fraction, offered like coin. Then, when she only waited: “My examiner, my paymaster, and the nearest thing the Wake keeps to a blade. She has run its eyes on this side of the tower since before I was recruited. If she has left her web and come up herself, she is not carrying a letter.” He set the cup down with great care. “Ede. What I paid at the Broodthrone, I paid from the Wake’s purse. She’s coming to collect.”

“From you?”

“From the rising. I’m merely the receipt.”

⁂

She came into the camps on the evening of the second day, and Ede, watching her dismount in the muster-yard, understood at once why the crows had followed. Calyne was small, grey-cloaked, past the middle of some long elvish age, with a face like a folded letter, and she moved through the crowding, banner-hung, cook-fire chaos of the rising’s camp as if it were an audit she had already completed and found wanting. She did not look at the banners. She looked at the picket-lines, the wagon tallies, the sight-lines to the tier-gate. Ede had known one other person to enter a strange camp that way, and she had buried the man who taught him, at the mound’s edge, by the forms.

The Wake’s mistress did not ask for the council. She walked to the council fire as if she had dug its pit, stood in the light, and said, without raising her voice: “Rhovan of the Grey Rides. Stand up.”

He stood. He had the sense not to speak.

“Item,” she said, in a voice like a door being locked. “One founding. Item: one purpose, whole. Item: the name — the name — of the Leaf-Ledger, in the hearing of a general, a giant, a barrow-queen, a goblin, and a beast that files what it hears and never dies. Fourscore years of silence, spent in an afternoon, by a field man, for road-toll.” Her eyes went around the fire then, deliberately, and Ede felt them stop on her and weigh her like freight. “You will want to know if I have come to kill him. The Wake does not spill what it can still pour. And you will want to know if I have come to take the Wake’s bows home. General Stonhal, whatever this one has told you of us, we are not sentimental, and our bows serve where our interest lies.”

“And where does your interest lie tonight?” Ede said.

“Here,” said Calyne. “That is the misfortune of it. What was spent cannot be recalled; it can only be audited. From tonight the Wake does not lend the rising its eyes from below. From tonight I am the eyes, and I sit on this council, and I read what it reads, and I count what it counts. What I cannot trust, I watch.” She unclasped the grey cloak, folded it once, and sat down at the fire in the manner of a woman who had been a member for years and merely stepped out. “Proceed.”

Around the ring, Ede’s captains looked at one another. It was Berrin who recovered first — Berrin, who could make a welcome out of two crusts and a bench-end — already signalling a cook for a bowl, already shifting down to make room the way he had for every stray the road had sent them. “There’s barley and there’s what the clerks are calling mutton,” he said comfortably. “Eat first, audit after. Nobody counts honestly on an empty stomach.”

“I count honestly on every stomach,” Calyne said, and took the bowl, and did not thank him, and Ede watched her not do it, and could not have said why the small cold of that stayed with her the rest of the night.

⁂

It was Nix who said it out loud first, because it was always Nix who said the things everyone else was keeping in.

“Right, so, the counting,” he said, three days on, in the small hours, by the wall where the wintering rosters were pinned. “One: she writes in cipher. All the time. In council she writes, boss — you’re talking, Vega’s talking, and scratch scratch scratch goes the little grey book, and I looked over her shoulder, on account of being short enough that it isn’t rude, and it’s not letters, it’s lace. Two: she goes out at night. Not sometimes-nights. Every-nights. Pickets seen her by the water-side going down, and back before dawn with peat on her boots — that’s down-floor peat, boss, we’re on gravel up here, I checked, I stole a boot. Three: she asked Harrow’s clerk for the muster-roll. The whole roll. Names, floors, who came up when. Four: she asked which wagons carry the mail. Five” — he had run out of fingers on one hand and looked at it with disapproval — “five, she doesn’t drink, and I don’t hold it against a body, but it does keep the memory sharp, doesn’t it, for a person taking an interest in everyone’s comings and goings.”

“She’s a spymaster,” Ede said. “You’ve described a spymaster doing her trade in the open.”

“That’s just it, boss. Openly’s the strange part.” Nix hugged the standard closer; he had taken to sleeping with it since the Broodthrone, and nobody teased him. “When I was cup-bearer, the ones as skimmed the cup always poured with both sleeves rolled. Look, nothing hid. It’s the rolled sleeves I don’t trust.”

Ede did not trust them either, and hated that she didn’t, and went looking for reasons the way you probe a tooth. They were not hard to find; that was the trouble. Calyne’s little grey cipher-book never left her body. Her night walks went down the freed stairs, always alone, always unlogged, and when Ede set a quiet watch on the tier-gate, the watch reported the Wake’s mistress had gone down by the water-stair instead, as if she had counted Ede’s pickets too. She would not say where she went. She would not say where she had been the week the Warcamp fell, either, when Ede asked her plainly across the ledger-table — she had folded her hands and said, “Below,” with the whole insolent economy of her people, and Ede, who had watched Rhovan spend a year telling the truth one coin at a time, knew a guarded purse when it was shut in her face.

And she was Roothollow-born. That surfaced by accident, at the fire, when Mereth Wakesend remarked on the grey lace-cipher and Calyne said, without looking up, that she had learned her first letters chalking tallies on the Stone of the Climb like every Roothollow child. Ede had kept her face still. She had needed practice at that lately, and was getting it. Roothollow. The word had not stopped ringing since a freed prisoner in the Warcamp’s wreckage had said the courier smelled of Roothollow bread — since the whole hunt had turned toward home like a weathervane. Berrin, who missed nothing at a fire, had only nodded along: he’d known her people a little, he said; ropewalk folk, off the market square; she’d left young. “Before my time on the granary books,” he added, apologetic, “so don’t ask me the year,” and passed the pot, and the talk had gone on, warm, ordinary, and Ede had sat in it hearing nothing but the crackle of her own suspicion.

Then came the night the inner council heard the Broodthrone’s second story, and everything got worse.

They were six around the map-table in the command tent: Ede, Vega, Rhovan, Dun Harrow, Nix, and the Wake’s mistress, self-seated, scratching lace. Rhovan told it the way he had told Ede — the engines under the world, the underside of a stolen country, and then the courier. The toll-marked silver. The hood. The tread that had crossed the Matriarch’s web twice, once going up, and once, four seasons later, coming down. The only soul in the age of the tower to walk the road both ways.

The scratching had stopped.

Ede watched Calyne without watching her — an old skill from card-tables in a worse life — and what she saw she could not afterward unsee. The Wake’s mistress had gone still. Not surprised-still; checked-still, the stillness of an abacus mid-sum, every bead in the air. When she finally spoke there was something new under the grey of her voice, and it took Ede a heartbeat to name it, because it had no business there.

It sounded like hunger.

“The tread,” Calyne said. “She kept it. Twenty years, and she could name it again — you are certain? Those were her words? If that creature’s web were brought a step to compare — if a man could be walked across silk under her lamps — she could say this one or not this one?”

“She keeps what she keeps,” Rhovan said. “She would know him among ten thousand. She said so, and she does not overstate; overstatement voids warranties.”

“Then the Broodthrone is not a floor,” the spymaster said, half to herself, writing again, fast now, the lace running off the pen in long grey loops. “It is a witness.” And she asked question after question — the courier’s years, the seasons between his passings, which Tollmen’s marks were on the silver, whether the Matriarch had smelled bread on him — no, Ede realized, listening with her skin: whether anyone had asked her about bread; who else knew this story; who had been in the tent each time it was told — and every question was the question of someone measuring a net, and Ede sat there in the lamplight unable to tell, for her life, for her army’s life, whether the net was being measured for casting or for slipping.

“You are grinding your teeth,” Sliver observed, from her palm, much later, when the tent was empty and the lamp was guttering out its last.

“She fits, Sliver. Every seam of her. The ciphers, the nights, the questions. She was born in Roothollow and she won’t say where she’s been, and the one story that should have frightened a loyal spy to death lit her up like a lamp instead.”

“I have carried forty-one climbers,” the splinter said, “and I will tell you the only thing four ages taught me about hunting hidden things: the creature you can see circling the trap is rarely the one that has been eating from it. Rarely,” it added, dry as old paper, “is not never. I make no defense of the grey woman. I merely decline to make the accusation while the ground is dark. So should you.”

“Then what do I do with her?”

“What the Barrow King did with grief, and Vyx with an army she could not afford to fight,” said Sliver. “You cannot expel her; the Wake’s bows hold half your left flank. You cannot try her; you have lace and peat and a birthplace. So keep her where the accounting is. Seat her close. Read what she reads. And Ede — count her nights, but count everyone’s. A camp that watches one back only is a camp with its own back bare.”

⁂

So the false winter’s-peace settled over the chasm-mouth camps: forty-one banners under the black moraine, the cook-fire smoke standing straight up in the cold, and at the council fire each evening, in the seat she had taken and no one had dared take back, a grey woman writing lace, watching everything, owed by nobody, trusted by nobody, going nowhere.

On the last night before the freeze closed the high road, word came up from the tier below with Berrin’s wagons: Vyx’s court had stood aside at the Broodthrone’s stair for the season’s last trains, correct to the clause, and the Matriarch had sent the rising a gift outside all contract — a bolt of websteel thread, enough to string every bow in the host, with a line in a clerk’s fair hand: For the party of the first part. A kept promise warms better than fires. Inquire of your hostage. Ede read it twice and put it away with the other things she did not understand yet and would.

Then she called the captains and gave the wintering order the whole camp had been waiting for.

“We stop climbing at the freeze,” she said. “We winter here, at the chasm-mouth, and while the ice holds the high road shut, we do the thing this army has never once had the breath to do.” She looked down the fire, at Vega and Rhovan and Dun with the banner, at Nix asleep against the standard-pole, at the grey woman writing, and past them all at the lit wick of the road running down through forty freed floors into the dark. “We count. Every banner. Every people. Every name, every loaf, every arrow, every soul this rising has carried up out of the fever — we set it all in one book, and come the thaw we will know for the first time what we are.

“Nine peoples and forty-one banners,” said Ede Stonhal, and the cold took her breath and made it a standard over the fire. “Come the wintering, we find out how big a world we’ve stolen back.”

Across the fire, without looking up, Mistress Calyne turned to a fresh page.

Chapter 18 — The Molt

The fortieth floor fell without an arrow loosed, and Nix counted the arrows anyway. None. Naught. Not one. It was his favorite count of the whole war, and he said it three ways to make it last.

The host went up through the Broodthrone in column, six abreast, under a ceiling that had eaten armies. Silk hung from the cavern’s throat to its roots — curtains of it, cables of it, swagged galleries thick as a ship’s rigging — and in the silk hung the court: wrapped knights turning slow on their threads, pale consorts folded like hands at prayer, thread-pages lying flat along the cables with their eyes politely down. Sixteen thousand boots went by beneath them and not one strand so much as shivered. A month ago that stillness would have meant ambush. Today it meant clause the fourth: the court shall stand aside, and the host shall neither burn, nor cut, nor take.

Nix walked the column’s flank, where he could watch the silk and the soldiers both, because somebody had to count the ones who stared up too long and strayed out of the line of march. Men, mostly. Giants never looked up; giants watched their feet, which Nix respected. The elves looked up and smiled, which he did not.

Matriarch Vyx received the passing of an army the way a lady receives weather. She sat vast and still upon the throne-web with her forelegs folded, the tribute of a hundred years hung about her like a dowry, and she spoke once, as Ede’s vanguard passed below the throne, in a voice like silk drawn over the back of a blade.

“Clause the seventh is observed,” she said. “The warmth of your fires rises and is received. The accounting is fair. Climb, general. And remember that I remember.”

There was one thing in all that cavern the contract let them break, and it was the thing the tower had nailed into her. High on the crown-cable, where the anchor-lines gathered, sat a welded ring of black iron the size of a millstone — the Yoke’s seat on the fortieth floor, the voice that came down the column from the throne and spoke through her web whether she would or no. Clause the ninth, the last, written at Vyx’s insistence in her own silk, said: the crown-iron is not of my court, and the host may do to it what it does to such things.

Vega’s crews went up the anchor-cables with cold chisels, for the contract barred fire, and they worked the welds the slow way, in shifts, by hand. Nix counted the hammer-taps until he lost the count on purpose. When the crown-iron came away at last it fell the height of the whole cavern and struck the chasm-bridge below with a sound like a bell nobody had wanted, and then —

Then the Yoke stopped.

It was not a sound ending. Nobody had ever heard it; that was the trick of it. It sat under hearing, the way a man with a stone in his boot stops feeling the stone by noon. What ended was a pressure. The whole cavern breathed. The silk shivered once, all of it, root to throat, the way a spider’s world shivers when she sheds her old skin and stands new — and ten thousand people who had not known they were clenched came unclenched at once, and some of them laughed without knowing why, and some of them wept and knew exactly.

Nix put two fingers to the old scar under his jaw, where his own collar had sat for nine years, and held them there a while.

They cut down the captives of Threadneedle that afternoon — the wrapped ones who could still be unwound, cocoon by cocoon, each one carried out into the lamplight and given soup and a name to answer to. The court watched from the high silk and stood aside, as contracted. By evening the stair-lift in the cavern’s crown stood open, and the first cold air any of them had tasted in forty floors came down it like a long drink of well-water.

Winter lived upstairs. The host camped at the mouth of it.

⁂

The cold came down the open stair from the forty-first floor and pooled in the low galleries, and the scouts who went up came back rimed to the eyebrows, talking of a country of gravel and balanced boulders under a hard white sky, with five floors of winter stacked above it and a frozen sea at the top of them. The tower has no weather but what each floor kept, and the floors upstairs had all been stolen in winter — winters with no spring behind them, kept cold since the day they were taken. An army does not walk onto pack-ice in summer boots. So the rising wintered where it stood, at the chasm-mouths: the wide cavern-mouths below the stair, where the Broodthrone’s silk gave way to honest stone and the Deep’s old chasm fell away black under the bridges.

The camps grew into a town in a market-week. Cook-fires by the hundred along the galleries. Tents of bought silk. The heat of eight thousand suppers rose nightly into the web above, where the court banked upon it and grew sleek — the tribute paying itself, which Vyx had foreseen when she wrote the clause.

Berrin Or came up the freed lifts with the winter stores a week ahead of the frost, which was Berrin all over. He knew without being told that the elves would not touch seal-fat; he knew which giant companies wanted their loaves baked black; he knew Nix liked his crusts burnt, and never once said how he knew. “Nobody sleeps cold on my books,” he said, and thumped the ledger like a drum, and nobody did. Nix kept a private count of the people in this host worth more than their own weight in bread, and Berrin sat high on it.

In the winter camps the council ordered its census, and like most things the council did, it was agreed unanimously for three reasons that had nothing to do with one another. Voss wanted heads, because rations are numbers and numbers were the only poetry he read. Calyne wanted names, for a spy’s reasons, which she did not give. Ede wanted musters — who could march, who could dig, who could draw a bow. So the word went out through the galleries: every soul in the rising to be counted and set down.

They made Nix a counter. He counted true, and the kobolds trusted him, and the quartermasters trusted him, and that was a short list to be on twice.

⁂

They smoothed a wall of slate at the widest chasm-mouth, tall as three giants and long as a toll-bridge, and the order was this: every soul to set their name on it — their own name, in their own hand, in their own script — and those who could not write to make their mark and stand by while a neighbor wrote the name beside it, so that the name and the hand that owned it were at least introduced.

It took nineteen days. Nix was there for all of them.

He counted scripts: forty. The rune-rows of the giants, straight as girders. The elves’ vine-writing, which could not stop being beautiful even when it was only spelling Fennick. The delvers wrote in knots and had to be issued pegs, and hung them in rows like a harvest. The Waste-folk scratched theirs into smoked glass and set the panes in the slate. There were chalk tallies from drovers, and a priest’s copperplate, and one name put down in dye on brood-silk by a woman of Threadneedle who said she had earned the right to like the stuff again. Some scripts held a thousand names. Some held three.

One held one.

The clerks brought the count to council on the twentieth day, and read it by lamplight while the snow-wind hunted down the stair. Souls: eight thousand, four hundred and six. Banners: forty — Shieldwall the oldest, then the keepers’ lantern-banner, Voss’s chain-and-key, and thirty-seven more named for floors and trades and dead friends. Peoples: nine. And there the counting had nearly come to blows, cheerful ones. Men. Giants. Elves. The delvers of the Deep, who were men and giants and elves by blood and would have none of it — “our nation is the rope and the lamp,” their foreman said, and the clerks gave way. The borderlanders of the Waste, ash-nomads and glass-gleaners, who claimed the same and got it. The kobolds: ninety-one. The Red Orcs: eleven, deserters since the Warcamp fell, who drilled alone and saluted nobody below Ede herself. Seven trolls of the Deep who had thrown down the collar together and would not be parted, counted as a people because no clerk wished to argue with seven trolls. And the ninth was not on the first draft: the shardminds asked to be counted, and they asked all together, in one breath, out of every splinter in the host, which ended the argument before it began. A people whose country was glass. The clerks gave them their line. Souls of crystal: two hundred and twelve.

Then the last line, which the clerk read without looking up, because clerks are kind in the only way their trade allows:

Goblins: one.

Nix went back to the wall that night with a lamp. His name sat small, between a drover’s tally and a knot-row: the hooked running-hand of the warrens, made for tally-sticks and insults, spelling the one word he had kept nine years under his tongue. His script, alone of all forty, had no neighbors.

He thought about the warrens, the first realm taken, the tower’s own foundation stones; about his people collared on every floor of the low country, handed spears and set to guard the doors of their own prison. Of all of them, one had climbed. He thought about Gnarl on his crate-throne in a meadow of stolen banners, who was offered the Break-collar and chose the crown, because the crown was at least his. Nix had loved him, and left him, and neither had been wrong.

He stood before the wall a long time, counting the one name in his own script, and decided the count was wrong in the only way that mattered.

Not one goblin. The first goblin. A seed is also a count of one.

⁂

He never called it a school. He called it his fire, and the fire had burnt crusts, and nobody who sat at it wore a sword, and that was the whole charter.

The freed kobolds who had stayed with the rising — out of the flooded mine and the gear-halls and the smelter floors, ninety-one by the wall’s own count — came to it in the evenings. They were free, and fed, and they answered to the names the collar had issued them, names handed out like tools: Ninth Shovel. Grease. Wedge. Tally. Sixth-of-the-Line. A kobold would come when you called, and what you called him was the work he had been chained to, and he had stopped hearing the insult years ago, which was the insult.

So every night, before the soup, Nix told the same story the same way, because a ritual is only a story with its boots on.

“They called me Cup for nine years. Cup, fetch. Cup, kneel. Cup, the king’s thirsty. And all nine years I kept my name under my tongue like a seed, and I’ll tell you what I told the dark: a name keeps. Yours kept too. It’s down there under nine years of shovel and grease, gone quiet. Quiet’s not gone. Spit it out.”

Then he waited. He was good at waiting. He had been a cup-bearer.

The names came slow, and they came crooked. A young digger from the gear-halls hissed hers all in one breath — Ssarik — and then looked about her like she’d dropped a plate. An old bilge-hand said his — Veshk — to the fire and not to Nix, which Nix allowed, because the fire wouldn’t repeat it wrong. Each name that surfaced, he made the whole fire say back three times: once to learn it, once to mean it, once for the count.

The hardest was the one they all called Ninth Shovel, who had worn his tool-name so long the skin had grown over it. Nine nights he sat at the fire with his hands around his bowl, digging. On the ninth he looked up.

“It’s gone, cup-bearer,” he said. “I dug where you said. The collar ate it.”

The fire went quiet. Nix counted his words for once, and spent them slowly.

“Then make one,” he said. “A made name is still a name. Half the best things in this camp are made things. The bread’s made. The banners are made. This army is the most made thing I ever saw, and it’s the best thing I ever saw. Make one, and we’ll wear it in with you.”

The old kobold looked a long time down the gallery, to where the great slate wall stood pale under the lamps with its eight thousand names.

“Chalk,” he said at last.

They said it back three times. Then Nix walked him to the wall by lamplight, the whole fire trailing after, and Chalk wrote it himself, the first thing he had ever written, the letters wobbling like a new foal — and the wall gained a name that had never been anybody’s tool. The kobolds’ line still read ninety-one souls. By Nix’s private count it now read one people, waking up.

Some nights there were Red Orcs at the edge of the firelight, arms folded, too proud to sit, listening. Nix counted them anyway. Counts, like seeds, are patient.

The last market-week of winter, the camps began to fold. The scouts came down from the Moraine Gates with Bruna Coldquay’s ice-road markers copied fair onto forty map-boards. Vega’s forges rang all night, cold-hammering sledge-runners of rime-iron, and the sound carried up into the silk where the court’s old shed skins hung translucent among the tribute — for spiders molt, and their whole world shakes when they do, and stands still when it’s done, and what steps out is the same creature, larger, in armor it grew itself.

On the last night Nix climbed to the high gallery and looked down at the winter city: fires by the hundred, the census-wall pale in the dark, the lamps going out in rows as orderly as anything Berrin kept. A company, he thought, is a thing you hire. An escort walks someone else’s road home. What slept below him owned a wall of eight thousand names in forty scripts; it had nine peoples and its own laws, a contract kept with a spider, a school for stolen names, and a census — and a census is a country’s way of looking in a mirror. The old skin hung behind them in the silk.

In the morning they went up the stair into winter, sixteen thousand boots on frozen gravel, and the sound they made on the moraine was not the sound of a company on the march. It was the sound of a nation moving. Six floors above, at the top of the cold, a frozen sea lay waiting to measure the size of the word.

From Malgrim's Ledger

Item: the Kingsbarrow account is closed at a loss. The drowned king was not carried by steel; he was carried by grief given precedence. They stood at his moat and named their dead until his court conceded the deeper mourning, and the mound, being honest, fell. I can fault no one’s conduct. They simply mourn well. Grief is a fuel like any other, and they have learned to burn it in good order. Recommendation to the throne: deny them occasions. They must not be permitted to mourn at scale.

Item: the Broodthrone. The Matriarch kept her contract to its final clause and stood her court aside while the crown-iron was broken. I decline to enter this as treason. A kept contract is the only piety the old powers have, and she was ours by welding, never by word. Write the fortieth relay off in full, and note for the archive: we held no paper on her. See that this is never again true of any floor we cannot afford.

Item: they have taken a census. Nine peoples, forty banners, one goblin. I confess the last line moved me, and I enter the feeling here as a caution to my successor: it will move you also, and it is meant to. A census is not a list. It is the mirror a people builds in order to see itself, and a people that has seen itself cannot afterward be unseen. They have added themselves up. That is the one arithmetic this house was built to prevent, and they have done it with chalk, on our own slate. Recommendation: against their clerks, nothing — clerks can be replaced and martyrs cannot. Against their occasions, everything.

Item: our friend below requests extraction. Denied. I have written to him kindly, for he serves best when he is calm. He fears the wall of names; I have reminded him that his safety has never lain in appearing on lists but in keeping them, and that no man is so unseen as the one holding the pen. He asks what he is to us after so many years. I will pay him the wage of the truth: he is the best-kept entry in my books. A ledger entry does not retire.

Item, for my own account: when the crown-iron fell, the fortieth floor wept for relief. I remember the feeling. I chose against it once, and every year since, the choosing has come easier. Let the record show the collar is not heavy. What is heavy is choosing, and the collar ends the choosing. In this one respect I envy nobody climbing.

PART III

THE GATHERING

floors 41–60

Chapter 19 — The Frozen Sea

Vega Coalbrow learned to forge without fire on the forty-first floor, and it humbled her the way only a new craft can humble an old master.

The Moraine Gates were a country of gravel — ridge upon ridge of it, ground and dropped by an ice-river that had left long before the Theft. Balanced boulders stood where the ice had set them down. Coldjaw had denned before the stair-scarp in a shell of ice and erratic-stone, and it had taken the host two days and eleven lives to break the welded thing and still the moraine; and when it was done, Bruna Coldquay came out of the Erratic Hollow with the warmth-charm in one fist and the ice-road markers in the other, and looked at Vega’s forge-wagons the way a reeve looks at a cart with a cracked axle.

“Your fires are no good upstairs,” she said. “Fire-forged iron goes brittle in the deep cold and shatters on the first hard knock. You want rime-iron. You work it cold.”

So Vega worked it cold. Rime-iron came out of the melt-exposed seams below the Terminal Ridge, and it would not be hurried: you hammered it by the hour, a little at a time, letting the metal warm itself from the work alone, never from a flame. A forge-matron of the Coalbrow line, who had lit her first furnace at seven and could sing a weld shut in her sleep, stood at an anvil in the wind and learned patience from a gravel-reeve. There was a kind of honesty in it. Heat lies, sometimes. Work does not.

They built the sledge-trains through the winter camp and finished them on the moraine: runner after runner of cold-hammered rime-iron, beds lashed with bought Broodthrone silk, nine sledges to a train and a train to every company. Bruna’s ice-folk taught the drivers Kindle, the warmth that a body could wear like a coat, and taught the cooks the coldsblood brew, and marked the safe lines across the tarn where the frozen scouts stood upright in the shallows like a harvest nobody had come back for. Vega broke four of those with her own maul — rime-crusted figures that walked the gravel dragging the living toward the melt-runnels — and watched each one thaw to slush and drain away among the stones, evicted. She did not enjoy it. She recorded that it was the mercy, and moved her crews on.

The floors went by like the verses of a winter psalm. The Signal Deeps, where dead racks stood in aisles of cold light and the delvers walked softly, out of respect for something that was not quite a grave. The Bone Garths, walled and picked and rattling with crows. The Ogre Steps, kiln-terraces climbing into frost-haze, where three collared terrace ogres threw down their mattocks at the sight of Nix’s banner and sat down at his fire, enormous and shy. The Sunken Chantry, where the faithful of Bellmarsh sang the host across the flooded fonts by vigil-light, and even Voss took his hat off. Five floors, five problems of cold and weight and carry; Vega solved them the way she solved everything, with iron, arithmetic, and not enough sleep.

Then the stair opened on the forty-sixth floor, and there were no more problems, only the one.

⁂

The Frozen Sea lay under a low iron sky, and it was exactly what its name confessed: a sea, frozen — the coolant-water of her own people’s deep forges, a reservoir the size of a stolen ocean, kept just above freezing for a thousand years by the heat it drank from the furnaces below. Then the tower took the forges and the forges went cold, and one night the whole sea locked at once, with the sealers out on it.

Pressure-ridges heaved up across the pack like city walls. Leads of black water opened and shut without warning. Brine-gulls cried over the floes, and the wind came off the ice with nothing to slow it for a horizon. It was the most beautiful country Vega had seen in forty floors, and it wanted them all dead, and it did not even mean anything by it.

Orla Floeside met the vanguard at the sea’s frozen shore with an ice-staff taller than a man and no patience whatever for heroes. She was a sealer-elder, giant-blooded, frost-white to the eyebrows, and she sounded every floe she stood on out of habit older than thought.

“The pack lies,” she told the assembled captains, “and under the Yoke it lies on purpose. Frost-flowers bloom thickest on the thinnest ice. The groan you hear is not the floe that breaks; the one that breaks is silent. Nobody crosses my sea in front of me.”

Nobody did. The trains went out onto the pack in her bootprints, a nation strung out in lines across a white plain, and Vega walked the length of her train with her maul on her shoulder and listened to the sea talk under her feet. It groaned like a full hold. It ticked like a cooling forge. Once, away north, it made a sound like a door closing, and Orla stopped the whole column with one raised fist, stood listening for the time a lamp takes to gutter, then moved them all four hundred paces west without explaining, and nobody asked.

The floe-wolves came at dusk on the third day, white out of white. They did not attack the sledges. That was the fever’s cunning: they came in wide crescents, silent, showing themselves only at the flanks, and they herded — turning the rear teams gently, patiently, toward a lead that lay skinned over with new ice and flowered with frost. It nearly worked. A whole sledge was on the skin before the driver knew, the ice sagging under the runners like wet vellum, dogs screaming, black water breathing underneath.

Vega had learned Icebind from the sealers in a single evening, because it was only quenching turned backward. She knelt at the lead’s edge, put both palms to the skin of it, and quenched. The water took the bind the way steel takes a temper: a crack, a shudder, and the lead stood solid, and the sledge slid across groaning onto sound pack. Then the shield-line turned and did its work on the wolves, and that was crueler and kinder at once, the way it always was with the fevered. Each one that fell shed its wrongness like a coat of rime: the frost-armored horror dropped away and left an ordinary wolf, lean and grey and blinking, that gathered itself up off the ice and loped away across a sound floe, cured. They were never evil, she reminded her crews, who knew it. They were sick. Steel is the only exorcism on offer up here.

The coolant-wights were worse, because they had been sealers once. Rime-shrouded figures that walked the pack on the old hunting-lines and opened leads under the living — aether caught in the ice with the dead, wearing the shapes of Orla’s own drowned. The host broke nine of them crossing the sea. Orla named each one before Vega’s maul came down, and afterward stood at each closing lead until the water stilled, and Vega stood with her. Two giants at a graveside. Neither said anything. There was nothing to say that the sea did not already know.

On the fifth day they came over the Great Ridge, and the Locked Fleet lay below them in the pack like a drowned town.

Masts first — a forest of them, raked and rimed, standing out of the ice at the angles the freeze had caught them. Then the hulls: eleven floe-boats, broad-beamed sealers’ craft, and in the middle of them, vast as a guildhall, the forge-barge — a floating forge-hall of the deep works, chimneys capped with ice, caught fast on the night the sea locked, her fires still lit. Orla stood on the ridge a long time, looking down.

“My grandmother’s boat is the third from the left,” she said. “We walked out to them the first winter, to strip the holds. Couldn’t do it. It would have meant believing the sea was never coming back.”

“The sea is coming back,” Vega said. Slowly, the way her people said things they intended to be true. “I am going to fetch it.”

Fire was no use; she knew that before she started. Flame against an iron hull in deep cold splits the plates and burns the rigging, and the ice, insulted, only grips harder. What she did instead took three days and three nights, and it was the finest work of her life to that hour. She sank warm-brine channels around each hull, sounding as she went; she set the Kindle-callers of four companies working in shifts to her measure, gentling heat into the ice — not melting it, persuading it, a breath of warmth at a time, the way a fist forgets an anger. She sang to the iron the long slow forge-songs that tell a metal what shape it is, so the hulls would remember themselves as boats and not as bones. And finger by finger, grudging every knuckle, the sea let go.

The forge-barge came free on the third night with a groan they heard in the camps two floes away, and settled into the black water of her own thaw-pool, floating, upright, whole. Orla’s sealers wept openly on the ice. Vega climbed aboard among them and walked the cold forge-hall with a lamp, and lit the barge’s great hearth with her own hands — the first fire the sea had allowed in a generation — and stood in its light while the sealers cheered, feeling, for once, entirely clean.

Then she found the manifest. It hung on the rail in a tin case, and it listed the cargo the barge had carried when the freeze took her, and halfway down the list, in a clerk’s tired hand: collar-blanks, four hundredweight, Coalbrow mark.

They were in the third hold, stacked like ingots, stamped with the hammer-and-ring she had worn on her own seal since girlhood. Four hundredweight of collars that had never reached their necks only because a sea froze. Vega stood over them for a long while. Then she carried them up to the new-lit hearth one crate at a time, letting no one help her, and struck every blank into boat-nails and sledge-shoes, and said nothing about it to anyone. Mending is not absolution. But it is the shape absolution would have, if it ever came.

Floeback met them at the sea’s far edge, where the stair-scarp stood sheathed in blue rime — a swell troll grown vast and welded under a shell of pressure-ice that regrew as fast as it broke, heaving whole floes on its shoulders to spill the host into the leads. Its one tactic was a good one: make the ground itself a trap-door, drag the battle into the black water. It had worked for eighty years on everything the sea had sent against it.

It had never fought a fleet. The freed floe-boats came on under sail and oar with shield-lines at their gunwales, and the ice the Warden heaved was only weather to a hull. Vega stood in the forge-barge’s bow with her palms out and thawed the welded shell as fast as it regrew — her new craft and her old one in the same two hands, heat and cold in harness — and the boats closed in from three sides, and it ended the way such things end. When the shell finally cracked, what drained out of the welded core went down through the ice like smoke through a grating, evicted, and the swell troll underneath — huge and slow and blinking, cured, never an ice-thing at all, only a Deep-dweller the forge-shafts had brought up long ago — put down the floe it was holding and lumbered off across the pack to find a way home and down.

The pack settled. The leads closed. In the Melt-Grotto the sealers opened the coolant-sluice, and somewhere far below, warmth their world owed them began, at very long last, to be repaid. By morning the whole sea was groaning — not the war-groan of the pack, but something longer and deeper and glad, the sound of an ocean remembering water. The old floe-roads would carry the host to the stair before they softened. It was, Orla said, the exact size of miracle a sealer could trust.

⁂

The Forge-Court came up the freed lifts and out onto the ice on the last morning, and Vega heard them before she saw them: horns of the deep halls, slow and enormous, the marching-note her childhood had gone to sleep to.

A legion. A thousand giants in slab-mail, in column, with engines on grav-sleds and coal-wagons and a rolling smithy, crossing the pack in perfect order past the standing masts of the Locked Fleet. At their head walked a thane she would have known at any distance, because he had their mother’s brow and their father’s way of walking as if the ground should be grateful — Ulfar Coalbrow, her brother, whom she had not seen since the day she left the Court with her forge-mark burning in her mind like a brand.

He picked her up. In front of both armies, in front of her own crews, a matron of the Coalbrow line, and swung her like a door — and she was laughing before she could stop herself, because he was her brother and he was here, and the whole white world smelled suddenly of home: coal and iron and the cold-tallow soap of the deep halls.

“Little sister,” he said. “You look thin. Your host looks thinner. We have brought food, coal, forges, bridging-engines, and a thousand of the Court’s best, and I have brought you an apology twenty years old. I mean to make it at supper, at length, sitting down.”

Welcome muscle. The camps cheered the column in, and there was reason: the rising had been outrunning its supply for ten floors, and here came the deep halls with the weight of a whole people behind them. That night the forge-barge’s hall held the first high table of the whole climb — sealers and thanes, keepers and captains, Berrin beaming over his manifests like a man whose prayers came itemized.

But Vega was a smith, and a smith reads a work by where the maker’s eyes go. Through the whole of that bright loud supper, her brother’s surveyors never once looked at the freed floors, the freed fleet, the freed people. They looked at the welds. They stood at the hall’s ports with plumb-lines and cold-glass, taking the measure of the great anchor-chains where they ran up into the dark, and writing.

And the banner. The Court’s banner had been the hammer and the hearth since before the Theft. What flew over the legion’s rolling smithy was new work: the Ascent itself, the whole tower, stitched in black — not breaking, not burning. Standing. And beneath it, cradling it, a giant’s open hand.

She asked him plainly, at the end of the night, when the hall had thinned and the two of them stood alone at the ports with the sea groaning its thaw below. Giants do not ambush one another; it is not in the grain. She asked what the Forge-Court had come for.

And because he was a giant too, and her brother, and had never in his life left a sentence unfinished, he told her.

“We have come to keep this host alive across the ice, and to put our shoulders under your war, and to see the Demon King cast down,” he said. “And when he is down, sister — we have not come to break the tower. We have come to inherit it.”

The sea groaned under them, long and deep, the sound of something enormous letting go. Vega stood beside her brother in the lamplight and understood that the rising had just been joined by its largest ally and its next war, and that they had arrived in the same column, under the same banner, wearing her mother’s brow.

“Bring me the survey,” she said at last. “All of it. Every figure. If you are going to be wrong, brother, be wrong in numbers, where I can show you.”

“And if I am right in numbers?” said Ulfar.

She did not answer. Below them the Frozen Sea worked at its own unlocking, patient, enormous, indifferent — and ahead, past floors of moor and meadow and frozen forest, something older than the tower was waiting in a ring of fire for whichever of them the mathematics spared.

Chapter 20 — Brother and Sister

The survey came to the forge-barge in a strongbox with two locks, and Ulfar wore both keys on a chain around his neck, under the shirt, the way other men carry a saint.

He laid it out for her at night, in the barge’s chart-room, on the light-table the sealers had used for reading the pack — a slab of cold-glass lit from below by a steady aether lamp. Vellum first: forty sheets, joined at the margins, in the drafting-hand of the deep halls, three generations of it, the early sheets in inks long out of use. Then the overlays. Then the figures, in ledgers with sewn spines. The Forge-Court had been surveying the Ascent since before either of them was born, and had never once said so out loud.

“Begin where I began,” Ulfar said, and set his finger on the first sheet, and the tower rose under the lamp in section: all hundred floors, root to throne, drawn true.

Vega had seen the Ascent every day of her life. She had never seen it whole. That was the tower’s oldest trick — from inside, it is only ever the floor you are on; the design does not fit through a human eye, nor a giant’s. But it fit on forty sheets of vellum, and laid out flat under lamplight it was not a horror. That was the first thing that frightened her. It was a work — the greatest made thing in the world, welded joint by joint with a rigor she knew in her own hands, and her hands, unbidden, respected it.

“Here,” said Ulfar. “And here, and here.” His finger moved up the column, touching the welds between floors, one by one. “At every joint, a grav-engine. The engines that lifted the floors. The thief’s own tackle, still rigged, still sound. We have taken their measure through the walls for sixty years — mass, seat, feed, and state. Not one has been removed. He built them in, sister. He could not take them out without unbuilding his own tower, so he left them, and stopped their ears, and trusted nobody would ever hold the throne but him.”

“And the throne is the winch-house,” Vega said slowly.

“The throne is the winch-house. Every engine in the column answers to the core and to nothing else. We have tried for three generations to wake one locally — the Court’s whole cunning, against one engine, on a floor we could reach. It cannot be done from below. The feed comes down the tower’s own spine, and the spine is lit from the core.” He spread the first overlay across the section, and the spine came alive under the lamp, a hair-fine tracery running root to crown through every weld. “Hold the core, and every engine in the Ascent answers you like a bellows-crew.”

“To do what?”

He looked at her across the table. Their mother’s brow, their father’s patience, and underneath both, banked and old, a hope so large she could feel the heat of it off him.

“To lower them,” he said. “All of them. Gently. Home.”

⁂

He walked her through it the way their mother had walked them through their first casting: nothing skipped, nothing hurried, every claim set down where she could strike it.

The floors could not simply be cut loose; she knew that, everyone knew that, and his figures said it with a terrible tidiness — a floor is a country, and a country dropped from the sky arrives as a graveyard. But the engines had lifted every one of those countries once, mid-harvest, mid-flood, without spilling a hay-rick, because the thief had needed his takings whole. What had gone up gently could come down gently. The ledgers laid it out: mass by floor, seat-loads at every weld, the order of release — lowest floors first, each one borne down and seated into the earth before the next unweld began, the column shortening from the root like a candle burning in reverse. A chain’s length a day, no more, each floor’s descent read against the strain-figures of every joint above it. He gave her the rate tables. He gave her the margins, and the margins’ margins.

“Thirty-one years,” he said. “A generation, bearing the whole world down to where it was stolen from. Every field seated back into its own scar. The delvers’ caverns rejoined to the Deep. The elves’ wood laid piece by piece back into the elves’ country. Nobody falls, Vega. Not one soul, not one fence-post. That is what the engines are for. That is what they have been waiting rigged to do for eighty years.”

She went at the mathematics for half the night, because she was a Coalbrow too, and because somewhere in the second hour she had understood that she desperately needed it to be wrong.

She tried the couplings first: differential strain across a part-lowered column, the live floors’ engines bearing against the dead weight of the seated ones. His tables had it, with the staging worked out weld by weld. She tried the seats: a floor’s underside is not a foundation, and ground settles. He had bore-samples. He had the original survey — the thief’s own, lifted from a records-floor by two Court agents who had spent eleven years on floor thirty-seven as door-wards to get it — every floor’s footprint, mapped against the scars still waiting in the earth below. She tried the feed: could the core light the engines at half-fire, quarter-fire, anything short of full? The load-curves said what they said. The descent drew the core’s full breath for thirty-one years, no less, or the margins went to nothing and countries began arriving as graveyards.

Three ways she went at it, and three ways it held. The mathematics held. She set down the last ledger some time in the black hours of the morning and looked at her own hands on the cold-glass, and said the thing plainly, because giants do, even when it costs.

“The figures are sound, brother. I cannot fault them. It could be done.”

Ulfar let out a breath he had been holding, by the sound of it, for twenty years.

“But,” she said.

⁂

“The core,” she said. “Say what it burns for, besides your engines. Say the whole of it.”

“The Yoke rides the core’s fire,” Ulfar said, evenly, because he had known this was coming and had chosen not to lead with it, and she made a note of that, in the flat ledger she kept of everyone, even him. “Keep the core lit, and the Yoke is lit. For thirty-one years.”

“On every floor.”

“On every floor. Damped — we would hold the throne; the broadcast can be throttled from the crown, we believe —”

“Show me the figure for that.”

Silence, the first of the night. Beneath the chart-room floor the freed sea groaned at its thaw. Ulfar turned to the last ledger, the thin one, and did not open it.

“There is no figure,” he said at last. “The Yoke is his craft, not ours. We know the engines because the engines are honest iron. The broadcast we know only by its shadow. That the core carries it, we have proven. That it can be throttled and the engines kept — that is the Court’s judgment, and I share it, and it is a judgment, not a figure.”

“A hope,” said Vega. “One page of hope, in the middle of forty sheets of proof, and the page is the one the whole work stands on. Brother. You have built a bridge of numbers across the world, and where it crosses the gorge, you have painted the span in.”

“And what has your rising painted?” He said it without heat; that was the worst of it, he said everything without heat, the way she did, the way their mother had. “You mean to scram the core. Say it plainly, that is the plan behind the plan — break every Warden, take the throne, and put out the fire. And then? The tower stands. Dead iron, root to crown. The engines’ ears stopped forever, because the only hand that could ever wake them again is the fire you killed. A hundred countries hanging in the dark on welds that will hold for — what? A century? Four? Forever is not a figure either, sister. Your plan ends with the world in the sky for good. Every farmer you free lives out his days whole skies above his own jorth —” he caught himself, the old tongue of the deep halls rising in him as it only did at the anvil, and set the word down gently, like a hammer: “— above his own ground. And his grandchildren after him, leaning on a fence in the wind, a world above the fields their fields were cut from. The climb gives the floors their days back. Only the engines can give back the earth. There is no third door. It is my hope, or your never.”

And there it was. She could not refute the mathematics. She had tried; it held. She could only name the price, so she named it.

“Thirty-one years of the fever, Ulfar. Damped, you say — believed damped, on a judgment with no figure under it. Thirty-one years of that hum under every cradle in the world. You have not lived under it. I have, floor by floor, for two years. You feel it lift when a relay falls — the whole floor breathes, people weep and don’t know why. Ask any shard what it hears at night, even here, even now, with every Warden dead below us. There is a hum under the world’s floorboards, and your plan keeps it humming for a generation, and calls it mercy.”

“Against eighty years in the sky, sister. Weigh it.”

“I am weighing it. Now weigh mine.” She flattened her palm on the overlay, over the spine, over the small bright thread of the core. “The tower’s crime is not its height. The crime is that a whole world’s ground answers to one throne. Your plan does not end that crime. It staffs it. For thirty-one years, someone sits the winch-house, and every country in the sky hangs from his good intentions — whose floor descends this year, whose waits a decade, whose village sits at the bottom of the ledger.
