The mathematics chooses, you’ll say. Brother, a ledger that chooses over people is a king. We have one of those. I have seen what the world looks like when it is something a hand can hold, and I have seen it from the wrong end, and I will not spend thirty-one years watching a Coalbrow learn to like the weight of it.”

“Oath-bound,” said Ulfar. “Stewards, not lords. The Court would swear on the first anvil —”

“Skarn believed his collar was a rank,” Vega said, “and died believing it, in a cage-yard, wearing my mark.”

That reached him. She watched it land, and hated herself for the aim, and would not have called it back. He stood very still over his forty years of vellum, this brother she had built dams with out of casting- sand, who had wept at their mother’s forge the day Vega left the Court, and had not argued, and had said only, someone must stay and think of the other way.

“You mend people,” he said at last, quietly. “It is your whole heart, little sister. You have spent your life on the belief that everything broken can be mended, and I watched you learn it at the same anvil where I learned mine. Well — this is my mending. The world is the broken thing, and the engines are the seam, and I have spent twenty years drawing the weld. Do not stand there in our mother’s face and tell me the flaw in my craft is that I believe things can be fixed. I learned that from you.”

The lamp under the cold-glass hummed. The sea groaned. Somewhere aft, a watch changed, boots on the deck-iron, ordinary as bread.

“No,” Vega said, slowly, because it had taken her the whole night to find it, and it was not a figure either. “The flaw is this. To mend a sword, you keep the forge lit. To mend a world, you must somewhere find the nerve to let the fire go out — because the fire is what broke it. Your weld is beautiful, brother. And it anneals the one crack that must not be annealed. He built a tower so the world would answer a throne. You would keep it answering — kindly, gently, and for the best reasons — for exactly one generation. Name me one fire in the history of our people that was kept lit for exactly one generation.”

Ulfar looked at his sheets a long time. When he looked up, his eyes were their mother’s too, and wet, and entirely unmoved. Both of those at once. She would know the look until she died.

“Then we will argue it before the banners,” he said. “You and I. In council, in the open, like Coalbrows. And the rising will choose with its eyes open, which is more than any king ever offered it.”

“Yes,” she said. “We will. And you will lose.”

“Perhaps. I am a patient man, sister. The engines have waited eighty years. They can wait out a vote.”

He gathered the vellum, sheet by sheet, squaring the edges the way he had squared everything since he was seven. At the door — filling it, the way their whole family filled doors — he stopped, and did not turn, and finished his sentence, because he always finished his sentences.

“I would rather lose the vote than you,” he said. “Let the record show it, if you keep one. I know that you keep one.”

Then he was gone, down the ringing corridor, and Vega Coalbrow stood alone in the chart-room with the lamp and the after-image of the whole tower in section — the world as a hand could hold it — and put her head down on the cold-glass, and did not weep. That she did privately, later, in the same hour she wrote to Ede that the survey was sound and the schism was real and the rising’s hardest argument had arrived under her own banner and her own name, wearing her own face.

The host marched on the fiftieth floor at the week’s end. Ash came down the stair-drafts two floors below it and fell grey on white, and the sledge-runners hissed through it, and the air by degrees forgot the sea and remembered fire. Ahead, at the mouth of a living caldera, in a ring of pyres that had not gone cold in a thousand years, something older than the tower — older than the argument, old enough to pity everyone in it — lay coiled around its hoard, waiting for the rising the way mountains wait for weather.

Brother and sister rode toward it in the same column, under different banners, and did not speak of the vellum again. The argument was not over. It had merely banked itself, the way a Coalbrow fire does — coals under ash, patient, holding its heat for a floor neither of them had seen yet, high in the Made, where a lever waits by a light that has no color, and every mathematics comes at last to its price.

Chapter 21 — The Wyrm's Pity

The ash began two floors below the fire. It greyed the frozen boughs of the Rime Forest; it lay on the Black Meadows like a mourning-cloth; by the Doorstone Moor it was falling steadily, warm as a lie, and the host marched under it with cloths tied over their faces and their banners going grey. Ede Stonhal had never before marched through the falling of a thing that had been burned. She did not let herself finish the thought of what, on this floor, got burned. The floor finished it for her soon enough.

The Cindermouth opened before the vanguard on the third day: the mouth of a living caldera, a ring of black glass terraces descending to a lake of slow fire that pulsed below like a heart too large to hurry. Around the whole rim stood the pyres, hundreds of them, banked and burning as they had burned for a thousand years, each one a mouth the floor fed so that the thing in the caldera would not come up and feed itself.

Sira Lastwater met the vanguard at the rim’s cold outer edge. Ede had met keepers on forty-nine floors and knew the breed by now. She had never met one who hated the thing she kept.

“I tend the pyres,” Sira said, without greeting. “My mother tended them. Hers carried the wood. Do you understand me, general? A thousand years, and it was never the wyrm that built the first pyre. It was us. We taught her that we would feed her, because we were afraid, and the fever made her glad to learn it. There is a rite for dousing them — the free-rite, older than the feeding, hidden in my family like a debt. I have kept it my whole life and never once been permitted to say it past the first line. The cult will kill you to keep the fires lit. They are not wicked. They are what a thousand years of being afraid looks like when it is given a uniform. Kill as few as you can.”

She is asking the right general, said Sliver, dry against the bone of Ede’s ear. You keep the count of the ones that could not be helped. I have watched you cut it in a stone.

“Kill as few as we can,” Ede said, to both of them, and gave the order to form on the rim.

⁂

The battle for the Cindermouth was fought along the rim-walk at mid-morning, in falling ash, and it was the strangest fight of the climb, because the enemy was not trying to kill the host. The enemy was trying to keep the pyres lit.

They came up out of the terraces in their ranks: tribute-ogres, collared and ash-scarred, enormous — some of them weeping, and Ede made herself keep seeing it — with the pyre-poles and dragging-hooks of their office; and among them walked the pyre-wights, figures of standing flame that had been the given once, aether caught in the burning. Where a pyre guttered, the wights walked to it and it roared back to life. Where a soldier fell, the hooks came for the body, because the cult had a thousand years of habit about the fallen.

Overhead the fevered rim-life joined in for its own reasons: ash-drakes strafing the line in flat screaming runs, rim-jackals at the flanks, wyrmlings boiling up the terraces spitting fire. The shield-line held the rim-walk; that was the whole battle. Hold the high walk, douse the pyres behind it, and do not be driven down the glass terraces toward the lake.

Ede walked the line, because that was her post. Where it bent, she was there, the freed Shieldwall around her and Dun Harrow’s banner overhead, going grey and bright again as the ash slid off the cloth. Rhovan’s archers shot the drakes out of their runs with a patience that looked like leisure and was not. Vega stood at the third pyre-field quenching fire as she had quenched water, and where her craft took a stack the wights around it faltered like lamps in wind.

The pyres were the battle. The free-rite went down the rim from stack to stack behind the fighting: Sira at the first pyre with the water of Lastwater’s well, and behind her the tribute-ogre deserters Nix had been gathering at his fire since the Ogre Steps. They knew the rite; they had always known it; the cult had kept it the way a jailer keeps a key he prays he will never be asked for. Ogre after ogre knelt at the stacks they had fed and said the old words and put the fires out — and the pyre-wights did not fight the dousing. Each one stood over its dying stack like a man over a grave, and when the last ember went black, it came apart into ash and warmth and was gone. The soldiers had watched the burning dead be put to bed.

By noon the rim was a line of cold stacks under a clearing sky, the ash thinning as the caldera calmed. The cult’s ranks broke, not in rout but in something worse to watch: ogres sitting down where they stood among the doused pyres, unhooking their collar-chains with shaking hands, done. The host took them in. The Pressed dead that morning numbered forty-one, and Ede stood over the laid-out rows and let the whole line watch her count them, because somebody had to own the arithmetic — forty-one deaths that should not have been needed, cut into her memory beside the thirty names on the Stone of the Climb, in the place she kept the accounts that would never balance.

Then the fire-lake fell quiet in its bed, and out of the caldera — mildly, the way weather arrives — came the voice.

It did not shout. It was simply large, a voice with geologic patience and no hurry anywhere in it, and it said: “The fires are out. A thousand years, and the fires are out. Come down, general of the walking-folk. I will not eat you. I have lost my appetite for being obeyed.”

⁂

She went down with four: Sliver at her collarbone, Nix because the Pressed trusted no parley without him, Rhovan because he heard the fractions in what powers said, and Sira, because a thousand years of her family had earned the seeing of this. The terraces descended in rings of black glass, still warm, and the light changed as they went — less of the sky in it, more of the lake — until they stood at the lip of the Nest.

Cindermaw lay coiled about her hoard, and the hoard was ash. The treasure of a thousand years of tribute was not gold — there was gold in it, sunk and unregarded — it was pyre-ash, drifted into dunes, and the wyrm lay wrapped around it the way an old dog wraps a dead master’s coat. She was a true wyrm, mountain-scaled, her folded wings like tarred ridgelines, and her eye, when it found Ede, was the size of a shield and older than anything else the climb had ever looked at. A campfire from the rim behind them stood reflected in it, one bright point in an acre of dark.

“I was old when the fields you were born in were a sea-bed,” said Cindermaw. Her voice moved the ash in slow waves. “I chose this mountain before your people had chosen a name for themselves. I am not of the tower. The tower came to me — built its floors around my caldera because even a thief knows better than to move a mountain that is watching him. Sit. Stone does not hurry, and I am mostly stone by now.”

They sat. It seemed the only thing to do, and Nix did it first.

She spoke the way strata lie: slowly, in order. She spoke of the Theft as a recent event, with a neighbor’s mild disapproval for new people who build ugly. Of the fever, with contempt, and of herself under it without mercy: “It found the hunger in me and fed it. Do not make me innocent, little general. The wanting was mine. The teaching was his. We will divide the shame when one of us is dead.” Then she lay silent a long while, and the ash settled, and nobody — not even Nix — filled the silence.

“You are the third army I have watched climb,” she said at last.

Ede felt Rhovan go still beside her, the particular stillness of a man memorizing.

“The first climbed angry. It reached the fortieth floor in its fury and fed the tower’s cages for a decade. The second climbed clever — quiet, patient, well-led. It got higher. Its banners hang in the high vaults of the Made, behind glass. The tower keeps what it is proud of.” The great eye moved over them. “You climb heavy. Nine peoples, and your dead all named. I have felt you through the rock for a season. You may be worse to stop than either. That is not prophecy. Mountains do not prophesy; we only remember forward.”

“You watched them fail,” Ede said. “Both. And him — you have watched him longest of all. The keepers say you are the only being in the tower that pities him.”

“And your keepers say it as a wonder,” said Cindermaw. “It is only age. I have watched him eighty years — longer than his Heralds have lasted, longer than his courts. He is young. He is frightened. He was frightened first and cruel after; it is nearly always that way around. He stole a world and stacked it into a tower so that he would never have to be alone with what it thought of him, and now he sits at the top of the one thing he made, deaf and watchful, and of every creature in it — the collared, the caged, the welded, the burned — he is the only one who can never leave. The tower has one true prisoner, general, and he built the cell himself, and it is shaped exactly like him. I pity him. Somebody must. It will not be you; you have work. Mountains have time.”

The fire-lake breathed. Ash moved over the hoard in its slow tide.

“Now,” said Cindermaw, “I will pay for my fires being put out. I am old enough to know a debt when one sits down in front of me. Not gold; the gold is under the ash, take it as you leave, it never meant anything to me. I will pay in what a mountain has. I will tell you what I smell.”

Her head came down and around, slow as a headland turning weather, until the shield-sized eye hung level with Ede alone.

“Every creature that has ever crossed my floor is in my nose for good. Smoke does not keep names, but it keeps everything else. And I tell you, general of the third army: I smell one of you twice — once going up, once having already been.”

The ash was loud in the silence. Ede heard her own blood.

“One of the host,” she said.

“One of yours. Fresh on the rim above me this morning, and old in the deep of my nose from years ago — going up then too, and back down after. Down is the rare direction, little general. Everything climbs past me; almost nothing returns. The second smell has come down and joined the third army going up. Who, I cannot give you. Smoke keeps no names, and eight thousand of you stood in my ash this morning. But the nose is a mountain’s ledger, and the entry is true. One of you has already been.”

“Why tell me?” Ede asked the wyrm. It came out quieter than she meant.

“Because the first army was betrayed at the fortieth floor,” said Cindermaw, “and the second at the ninetieth, and I am tired of the smell of it. Climb, third army. The rim is open, the road is yours, and my reasons go with you. If I pass you on the way up, remember that of everything you freed on this floor, the largest of it is grateful.” The great body began, tectonically, to gather itself. “And general. Whoever he is — when you find him, before you do what armies do. Ask him what they promised. It will be something small. It always is. That is the other thing worth pitying in this tower, and the discipline is harder.”

She rose. It was like watching a range decide against geology. The first true wingbeat in a thousand years knocked flat the ash-dunes of the hoard and bared, glinting, the tribute-gold of ten centuries — and Cindermaw the Wyrm went up out of her caldera into her floor’s own sky, once around the mouth of it, and was gone, upward, remembering who freed her.

On the rim above, Lastwater’s people stood at their cold stacks in the clearing air and wept, and the pyre-field’s thousand years were over, and the fifth tier was free.

⁂

That night Ede sat late at her fire, and the thing the wyrm had given her sat across from it like a guest who would not leave.

Once going up, once having already been. Set beside what Rhovan had brought down from the Broodthrone — Vyx’s courier, twice past her web — the two ledgers closed on a single figure. Someone in the host. Someone at these fires. Someone who had climbed before the rising was a rising, and come back down, which is the rare direction, and stood this morning in the ash among eight thousand of her people, grieving the Pressed dead, cheering the wyrm, counted on the census-wall like everyone else.

She found she was watching the fires down the terrace-line. Faces she had bled beside. She made herself stop, and could not, and understood with a cold thoroughness what had been done to her this day, and that it could not be undone. The wound the tower could not put into the rising with drum-fire and cages, it had put in with a sentence. The hunt was inside the walls now. Inside her.

Say it, said Sliver.

“Everyone who has ever climbed before,” Ede said slowly, “and come back down. Veterans of the first two armies, if any live. Old gate-runners. Tollmen who worked the high spans. Keepers’ kin who went up after the lost. All of them, mustered on a roll — quietly. If it is done loudly, the guilty one runs and the innocent are ruined. Quietly, then. A guides’ roll: everyone with prior knowledge of the floors, wanted for the van. It is even true. It is just not the truth.”

It began the next morning, without proclamation, a line in the quartermasters’ ordinary business: the rising sought those who had climbed in former days, that their knowledge might serve the van. Names came in slowly, then well — men proud to be asked, an elf who had run the Grey Rides before the Wake was the Wake, two sisters who had gone up to fifty-five after their father and come back down with his ice-axe and nothing else. Each went onto the roll in a fair clerk’s hand.

Berrin kept the rolls. He had kept every roll of the rising’s since Roothollow, and he kept these the way he kept all of them, neatly, gladly, late into the night, and had the first fair copy on Ede’s table within three days.

You will want a second copy, Sliver said, when she set it down. And a second keeper. You are building a net, and a net is only as honest as its knots.

“I know,” said Ede, and looked a while at the neat columns, and the fire behind them, and the dark beyond that, where her army lay asleep around ten thousand small lights — her nation — and somewhere among their breathing, one breath that had been up this tower before and had sold the going of it. “I have begun to look at my own fires the way I used to look at a treeline, Sliver. He has made me a general in my own camp.”

Then be a good one, said the shard, dry as ever, and ancient, and hers. You have always fought best uphill.

Above them the tower went on. Word came down with the evening meat: past the caldera the stair opened on the Deep again — a city hung over a chasm on bridges of spider-silk, lamps in the void like low stars. And past the silk, the delvers said in whispers, lay a floor with no name anyone used twice: a country of dust-funnels and hidden lids, a whole land built as one enormous trap, where the ground itself listened for footfall. The delvers had one sentence of advice about crossing it, and the sentence was: don’t be heavy.

Ede read the scouts’ report twice and gave her first order for the Trapdoor Fields that same night, and it went out to a nation of sixteen thousand boots, and it was one word long.

Quietly.

At his own fire, two terraces down, the lightest soldier in the host was already walking heel-to-toe along a rope his kobolds had stretched between two spears, arms wide, tongue between his teeth, counting his steps out loud. Practicing.

Chapter 22 — What Roothollow Sends

The rising ate eleven thousand loaves a day. Nix knew the number because he had asked, and he had asked because the number was getting harder to find in the wagons: nine thousand mouths, giants counted double, the new ogres counted honestly, which was worse. An army does not starve all at once. It starves the way a lamp goes out — a little less every night, until somebody finally says it out loud.

The quartermasters said it out loud two days past the caldera, at the morning muster, in the apologetic voice of men reporting weather. Three grain-trains overdue below the Barrows. A tangle at the thirtieth gate, lift-rights, nobody dead yet, the yet doing a soldier’s work in the sentence. And ahead lay the Deep — a city hung on silk over a hole in the world — and no general takes nine thousand people across a chasm on a half-empty sack.

Ede found him at his fire that evening. She had a paper in her hand and the look she wore when she was about to spend somebody she liked.

“The floors below trust the rising,” she said. “The rising is a rumor to them, Nix. A wind that went up. What they trust is a goblin who took a surrender at the Broodwarrens without one arrow loosed, and taught their cousins their own names back. I need the trains moving and I need it done without a sword anywhere in the doing. And —” the paper, folded once — “the season’s dead, for the masons. Somebody carries the list down every season. This season I’d have it be somebody who knew them.”

“Down,” Nix said. “Down is the rare direction. I had that from a wyrm, so it’s likely true.” He took the list, his hands doing the agreeing before the rest of him was consulted. “If I miss the Deep crossing, general, I’ll want it known I objected.”

“You’ll be back before the silk. Ride the post.”

He left at first light with his standard on his shoulder and the list against his chest, on a wagon of empty sacks going home to be filled — the most honest company, he decided by the second lift, that he had kept in years.

⁂

The post went down the world the way water goes down a stair, and Nix went with it, wagon to wagon, gate to gate, and made his counts.

The winter floors first, in reverse: the Doorstone Moor with the ash already washed off it, the Rime Forest, the ice-road across the Frozen Sea — staked now, inned now, Coldquay’s markers copied onto boards at every mile, a road where a battle had been. At the Moraine Gates he stood in his first true lift-queue and could not stop looking at it. It ran back from the gate-arch a quarter of a mile: grain-sledges, seed- casks, a rope-walk’s whole winter of cable coiled on drays, forty young folk of some low floor in new boots, spears tied in bundles like firewood, a bride going up to a wedding on the forty- fourth, two coffins coming down. Nobody had ordered any of it. Below the rising’s boots, without asking anybody, the world had grown itself an artery.

He rode it for six weeks, and the artery taught him its arithmetic.

A sack of Roothollow barley spent a season getting to the war: lift by lift, floor by floor, each floor a country wide, each gate-road a week of real weather — rain on the low pastures, frost above the fortieth, one stretch of the Grey Rides where the escort changed twice a day out of respect for old habits. Way-lofts stood at every gate-town now, and every way-loft kept the same fair-copied rolls: what came through, what was owed, which company wanted its loaves baked black, which floor’s oxen would not pass a windmill. Somebody in the provisioners’ trade had a hand like a hedge in winter, bare and exact, and that hand had copied itself down fifty floors until the whole road wrote alike. He asked a loft-keeper on the thirty-eighth who kept the master rolls, and the man looked at him the way you look at somebody who asks who keeps the sky up. “The office,” he said, and thumped the ledger, and fed him, and that was the whole theology of it.

At the Broodthrone the down-post crossed under the silk in file, and the wagon-masters banked their fires high at the crossing-camps, paying the warmth-clause the way you pay any toll you’d rather not discuss. The court hung folded and formal in its high galleries, took its tribute of heat, and let the world’s grain go by. Nix rode that whole floor with his head back. A contract kept by a spider, he thought, is worth ten kept by men, on account of the extra legs.

The quarrel at the thirtieth gate took him four days, and no swords.

It was harvest below, that was the root of it. Hedgeharbor and the drover floors and the warren country had all sent at once, and the Barrowgate lift could take sixty loads a day, and there were six hundred waiting, and the gate-clerks were working precedence from a Tollman ledger older than the rising, which ranked floors by what they had paid in the bad years — so the floors that had been robbed longest went up last, and knew it, and had begun quietly unhitching one another’s oxen at night. Nobody dead yet. Yet.

Nix stood on a toll-post where everyone could see the standard, and counted. It was all he did the first day: counted loads, out loud, floor by floor, until the counting shamed the ledger — because a count is honest the way a rank is not, and that gate knew the difference by smell. Then he made them a rota with no floors in it at all: grain by the ton, sons by the company, seed before beer, and every tenth lift held empty — down-space for the hurt and the letters and the coffins, because the road ran both ways or it was not a road. Last, because he had learned it from a spider, he made the two loudest floors swap stewards: your clerk rides their train, theirs rides yours, and each of you feeds the other’s man at your own fire. Hostages, said an old drover, approving. Guests, said Nix, and everybody was kind enough to let both words stand.

The trains moved on the fifth morning. Watching the first sixty go up, he heard a wagon-master say the thing he heard everywhere afterward, always with the same knock on wood: two years this road had run, through a war, past floors still fevered, and never once — not once — had a train been taken. Ambushes there had been, and burnt toll-houses, and one bad spring at the Fair — but always a march behind the grain, somehow, always a day late and a road short. The luck of the rolls, the road called it. Nix, who counted luck the way he counted everything, gave it a knot of respect and no further thought. Good paper, he decided. Good paper is a kind of armor.

At the Broodwarrens the gate was kept by kobolds, and the rota-board was chalked in the hooked running-hand of the warrens, and Nix sat at the gate-matron’s fire and was fed twice and argued with once, which is kobold for beloved. Her name was Sesk; she had chosen it herself two winters gone; she asked after the ninety-one by name until he ran out of news, and then sent her grandson to the wagon with a sack of seed-cakes, for the road, for the fire, for the ones learning letters. The grandson said his own name at the tail of the errand, unasked, the way you show a traveler your best knife. Nix rode away from that gate with his eyes forward and did not count anything for a while.

The twentieth floor he had meant to pass quickly, and did not. The Red Warcamp was a granary now. The mine that had eaten Shieldwall stood open and lamplit and smelled of dust and barley, and in the deep gallery the cages still stood in their rows — too much iron to haul out — so the cages held grain, rank on rank of them, filled and tallied and clean. The drums of the Warcamp had been cut down long since; somebody had made the drum-shells into measures, and the store-boys scooped barley with them, and did not know. Nix stood a long time in the lamplight, hearing what the shells did not say. Then he chalked his mark on the rota-board like any other courier, and went down.

⁂

He came out of the Root Gate queue on the fortieth morning, into Roothollow, and did what everyone from the up-floors did in the first hour: he looked for the tower, and could not find it.

There was no tower. From the yards of Roothollow there was a wall, and the wall was the sky. It went up past the roofs, past the kites, past the weather, black iron seamed with faint slow light, and it did not narrow and it did not lean and it did not end — you could walk a day out into the scrub and turn around and it had not become a shape; it had only become more of the world. On clear nights, they told him, you could see the undersides of the low floors glimmering far up the face, hanging lamps in the black, a country’s worth of them, like stars that paid rent. Nine years of his life hung somewhere up that face, too high to point at; he stood with his head back like any other pilgrim and did not try.

He delivered the list first, because the dead go first, that was the whole rule of him. The masons had the Stone of the Climb and a sister- slab beside it now, new-quarried, because the war wrote faster than one stone could hold. The gate face glowed with fresh cuts — Wardens broken, lifts opened, letters lit from within like coals — and the north face took the season’s dead in plain dead letters, floor by floor, while a mason’s boy read Nix’s list aloud slowly and got the warren names right, every one, because Nix stood at his elbow until he did.

Then the mason showed him the thirty.

They stood where Ede had cut them, the winter she was sober, and beside most of the thirty names — not all; a few stood bare still, and the mason’s face closed over the bare ones like a lid — there was new work: a small open gate, cut deep, the height of a thumb. “When the word came down from the twentieth,” the mason said, “half the town wanted them ground off the north face, seeing as they’d been found alive. Stood here shouting about it, whole families. I wouldn’t. You don’t unsay stone; stone’s not for unsaying. So: a gate. Cut it myself, one a night, same as she cut them.” He put his thumb in the gate beside DUN HARROW, STANDARD-BEARER, the way you test a mortise. “Came back through,” he said. “That’s all it means. It’s enough.”

The moot of Roothollow met in the long cellar under the gate-hall, and it was running a war the way a blind man runs a farm he was born on — by feel, by count, and by refusing to stop. On the cellar’s end wall someone had chalked the whole climb as a ladder of strokes, one to sixty, no map, no country, no names: just strokes, and a rag hanging by it that had rubbed out nothing in two years. They gave Nix the end of a bench and asked him everything — the silk, the sea, the wyrm. He did his best, and watched the words get to the door of the cellar and refuse to fit through it, and understood at last what the low sky did to a town: Roothollow was provisioning a story. It sent its barley up into a wall and the wall sent back lists — casualty lists, requisition lists, letters, once a season a wagon of broken collars for the forges — and it believed, and argued, and levied, and never once got to see. The gate-reeve put the year’s true question the way a man puts his last coin down: the seed-corn. Hold this year’s back, against the rising failing and the world coming down hungry? Or send it up whole, and bet the town’s next bread that the story was true?

They voted to send it. It took them until the lamps guttered, and the granary-mistress wept angrily through her own aye, and they voted to send it, all of it, the seed included, and Nix sat very still on his bench-end and made the list he had come down, without knowing it, to make. What Roothollow sends. Barley and seed-corn. Rope, arrowheads, lamp-oil, cable. Its sons and daughters, forty a season through the Root Gate in new boots. Letters by the hundredweight. The names of its dead, cut by lamplight, and gates beside the found. And all of it blind, all of it uphill, all of it on the word of people it could not see, to a war it would never watch, under a sky that was a wall.

In the morning he walked past the drill-yard, where a broad-backed woman he did not know was teaching eleven recruits how a hedge-lane fights, and the horse trough wore its skim of dust, and the counts came back at her in one voice, a breath early, the way counts always come back the first year. He stopped at the granary row, where the ovens had worked the night shift so the dawn queue could carry bread up warm, the smell lying down the whole street like a hand on your shoulder. Then he took his place in the up-queue with the levies and the seed-wagons, a goblin with a standard in a line of everything a town could give, and the Root Gate opened, and Roothollow sent.

⁂

The going up took longer, because the going up always does; he was eight weeks with the great spring train, and he spent them being useful in the manner of goblins — riding forward to chalk the rota-boards, teaching the new levies their counts at night until they could say them asleep, eating at fifty fires and hearing at every one some floor’s own war: Hedgeharbor’s flood-quarrel, the drover floors’ great question of the bulls, two warrens’ worth of kobold couriers who made him say the census-line out loud — goblins: one — and then argued about it, fiercely, as if the counting had wronged him. The train climbed through harvest and frost and the Broodthrone’s silk, paying warmth, losing not one wagon, and the higher it went the more it looked like what it was, which Nix put into words for nobody on the last night below the Deep:

Not a supply-train. A country, walking. The rising was nine thousand people up a stair — but the rising, the real one, was this: sixty floors breathing in and out along one road, trading and quarreling and marrying at the gate-towns, sending sons and grain and letters up into weather they had to take on faith. Kill the army, and the low country would grieve, and gather, and send another. The tower had spent eighty years making sure no two floors could want one thing together. Well. Here was one thing, in six hundred wagons, wanting.

The host lay camped at the chasm-mouth when the train came up the last gate-road, and it came out to meet its bread the way a town meets a bride. Nix rode the lead wagon in with the seed-cakes for the ninety- one in his lap and the moot’s letter for Ede buttoned inside his shirt, and gave the general her mail, and the freed kobolds their cakes, and the quartermasters their tally — down and back, every load accounted, nothing lost, nobody dead, yet retired from the sentence entirely.

“Well?” said Ede, when the letter was read.

“They sent the seed-corn,” Nix said. He had meant to say it lightly and it did not come out that way, so he let it stand. “All of it. Now feed me, general. There’s a city of bridges up ahead wanting walking, and I mean to count every one of them twice.”

Chapter 23 — The Long Stair

There were one hundred and eleven bridges in the Hanging City, or one hundred and nine if you did not count the two that were only a single rope with ideas above its station, and Nix had walked every one of them twice before the army was allowed to cross. That was the arrangement he had made with himself. He was the lightest thing in the rising that could carry a lantern and an opinion at the same time, and so he went first, and counted, and came back, and the counting was for the army and the coming back was for him.

The city hung over a chasm that had no bottom anyone had ever heard hit. Its streets were cables. Its plazas were woven silk, wide as threshing floors, swaying a little always, like a ship that had forgotten the sea. Lamps hung out in the dark on long lines, so that standing at the chasm’s lip you looked out at a town of low stars and no ground under any of them. The Deep-folk had built it with their spider partners, back when the spiders were partners, and the fever had ended the partnership the way the fever ended everything — by making the builders eat the building.

Fenn Skeinside met the vanguard at the first anchor-post with one hand on a line, which Nix would learn was where the man’s hand always was. He was a city-warden with rope-burn scars laid over rope-burn scars, and he talked the way his bridges hung: no slack anywhere.

“Your army crosses in file, on the lines I say, in the order I say,” he said. “The silk holds soldiers. It does not hold argument. Leave the argument on this side.”

They left the argument on that side. Vega’s forge-crews wanted to sling the wagons on doubled cables and Fenn allowed it, after walking each splice himself. His people taught the Threadwalk to the few who could learn it, elves mostly, and to Nix, who learned it in an afternoon and was insufferable about it for a week. For everyone else there was the vertigo-draught, mixed in Skeinside by the barrel, tasting of cold stone and courage. You drank it and the drop under your boots became a fact instead of a voice.

The crossing took four days. The spiders came drifting on their balloon- silk out of the black, sails up, knives of legs, and were cured one by one by the archers on the anchor-towers — each great wrong shape collapsing to an ordinary spinner that reeled in its sail and anchored to the wall, harmless, done. The husks were worse. Silk-bound figures walking the cables behind the living, cutting lines with no more malice than scissors. Broken, they fell, and the falling drained out of them before they landed, and what landed in the great net of the Undercatch was only wrapping.

On the third day a drover from Hedgeharbor stepped from a platform to a bridge exactly the way he had done two hundred times, and a cut line that had held all morning chose that heartbeat to give. He did not scream. Nix was near enough to see him go and not near enough to do anything but count him. The Undercatch took him, and the Undercatch was old, and there was a rent in it no wider than a door.

His name had been Tam Yoler. Nix learned it afterward, on purpose. He had a cord he kept under his shirt, a plain tally-cord, and that evening he put the first knot of the high floors in it, and told nobody, because it was not for anybody.

The city gave as well as took — everything did, that was the horrible arithmetic of the climb. Fenn’s cable-walkers went down into the Undercatch with lamps and came up with eleven living souls who had fallen in the fevered years and survived on dropped cargo and net-caught rain, including a woman who had been down there so long she asked, climbing out, whether the lamps were still lit in the Gossamer Market, and wept when they raised the market that same night for the answer.

⁂

After the city, the stair.

That was what the army came to call the nine floors of that season — the Long Stair — though it was lifts and gates like any other stretch of the Ascent. It felt like a stair. It felt like climbing a thing that had noticed you.

The Cloudline was ridges over white nothing, and the squall-drakes came sideways out of the weather; a kite of Voss’s supply-train went off the edge with two of its handlers, and the cliff-goats watched them fall with yellow uninterested eyes. The Ember Dunes cooked the host by day and froze it by night, and the dune-adders took three sleepers before anyone thought to sift the sand around the bedrolls. The Wreckfields were a forest of dead sky-ships where the salvage-folk of Hullside showed them which hulls were sound to camp in and which ones breathed. On the Troll Bridges the army crossed crevasse after crevasse on packed snow, and on the second day a span that had carried two thousand soldiers let go under a string of nine mules and the farrier leading them, and the raven-haunted gorge did not even echo.

Nine knots. Nix retied the cord twice to be sure.

That was the thing his hands understood before his head did. The rising had fought since the Fencerows — wolves, Wardens, Cullers, wights, its own quarrels — and every loss had an enemy attached to it, something with a face, something you could cure or evict or, worst and truest, kill. On the Long Stair the army bled and there was nothing to hit. A rope. A sandbank. A snow-bridge. The tower was done sending soldiers for a while. The tower had remembered that it was itself a weapon, a hundred countries of edge and drop and cold welded into one tall trap, and it had all the patience in the world.

Rest came strangely, where it came. The Rookeries deafened them with fledglings and stole their buttons. On the Shade Walks the lamplit avenues were quiet and wrong, and everyone’s shadow was advised in writing not to be trusted, and one poor watchman of the rearguard followed his own shadow around a corner and was not found. Then the Giant Steadings, and that floor was kindness: freehold farms sized for giants, fences a wagon could drive under without ducking, and the freeholders of Thanesrest putting up the whole footsore host in barns the size of market-squares. Vega walked among her own folk for three days with her shoulders down for the first time since the Frozen Sea. The freeholders sent forty scythes up with the army when it moved. Nix counted them too. The cord was for the dead but the counting was for everything; counting was how he held the world still enough to look at.

⁂

Then the lift opened on the fifty-ninth floor, and the world went flat and grey and silent, and every hair Nix owned stood up and pointed back the way he had come.

The Trapdoor Fields were nothing. That was the first thing you saw: a cavern-plain floored in fine grey dust, dead level, dead empty, running out past lantern-reach in every direction, quiet as a held breath. The second thing you saw was Wend Latchlight’s face, and then you knew the nothing was a lie.

“The whole floor’s a lid,” she told the war-council, up on the Watch- Mound, the one honest rise in all that flatness. She was a small hard woman with a delver’s lamp-squint, and she had scars on her forearms in neat rows, like tally-marks, which Nix respected and did not mention. “Every third step is a door, and under every door is a mouth. The lids are hinged spring-iron and dust-lichen; you will not see them. I have maps of the proven lines, and the lids move, so the maps are wrong somewhere, and I don’t know where. That’s the truth of it. Walk where I say, exactly, one behind the other, and be quiet doing it. Sound brings them up.”

Ede asked how an army of nine thousand crosses a floor in single file, in silence.

“Slowly,” said Wend.

They made the rules that night and the rules were these: one line, one pace, three arms’ lengths between souls. No voice. Orders by hand-sign, passed back rank to rank like a bucket at a fire. Wagons unloaded, the loads carried, the wagons drawn on skids by hand — hooves drum, and drumming brings them up. The Surestep draught to the point-walkers and the flank-wards, because there was not enough for nine thousand. And at the head of the whole column, in front of the vanguard, in front of Ede herself, one goblin with a delver’s pole, because the lids were sprung by weight, and Nix weighed less than the army’s kettles.

He did not volunteer. He wanted that understood by history. Wend Latchlight looked over the war-council, keepers and captains and one giant, and her eye came down on Nix like a lid, and she said, “Him,” and that was the whole argument. You cannot volunteer for a thing you were always going to be given.

At the plain’s edge, on the first morning, before the silence-rule took hold, Wend asked him quietly whether he was afraid of the floor. Nix considered the grey nothing running out to its unseen far rim, and gave her the only wisdom he owned outright. “Big folk count floors. Little folk count steps. Steps are truer — a floor can lie to you, but a step is either under your foot or it isn’t.” Wend looked at him a moment longer than the words wanted, and then signed the column forward.

The crossing took three days, and Nix remembered it afterward the way you remember a fever — in pieces, all of them too bright.

The pole, tapping. Two taps for a true step, laid soft as a moth. The dust taking his footprints and keeping them. Wend a spear-length behind him, reading the ground over his shoulder, her hand-signs small and calm: left, left again, hold. Behind her the army, two miles of the gathered world — men, elves, giants, delvers, freed kobolds, Tollmen, Cullers, thanes — walking one behind the other in a silence so complete he could hear the lamp-oil move. A hundred banners furled and carried flat, because a banner snaps, and snapping brings them up.

Once an hour, somewhere out on the plain, a lid would rise of itself — a hand’s width, to look — and settle again, soft, patient, like a man tucking a letter back in a drawer. Nobody had to pass a sign for that. The whole column felt it through its boot-soles and kept walking.

On the second day, in the middle of the middle mile, the floor ate Mole.

Mole was a kobold of the Broodwarrens, freed at the surrender Nix had talked into being, one of the ones Nix had taught to say a name again in the winter camps below the Hanging City — had taught, and then watched him pick a name for himself, which is a thing you never forget watching. He had picked Mole because he liked digging and was honest about his eyes. He carried kettle-loads without complaint and sang under his breath when he forgot the rules, and he was seven hundred and forty places back in the column when a lid that was on no map breathed open under his left foot and took him to the hip, and the rules held, that was the horror of it, the rules held — the soldiers either side of him had him by the arms in a heartbeat, and the sign came sprinting up the column hand to hand, sprung trap, seven hundred, hold the line — and Nix stood at the head of the world’s army with his pole in the dust, forbidden to run, forbidden to shout, doing the only thing he had, which was counting, while a half-mile behind him a thing under a lid was stronger than four men and then stronger than six, and then the sign that came up the column changed, and the line walked on.

They did not even have him to carry. That was what Nix could not put anywhere. The Warcamp had left bodies; the Barrows had left bodies; you carried them out and named them at the mound and the naming closed over the wound like water. The Trapdoor Fields left a closed lid in a flat plain, one among ten thousand, already re-dusted, already a lie again.

Four more knots went into the cord on that floor. A flank-ward who stepped one stride off the line to steady a skidding wagon. Two carriers at the dust-funnels, where the ground was honest about wanting them and swallowed slow, and the ropes came up empty. A Culler of the rearguard whose name Nix had to ask three people for, and the third one wept, so Nix carried the name and the weeping both.

At the King-Burrow they finally had a thing to fight, and it was almost a relief, and Nix hated that it was a relief. They gave Latchjaw a wagon of quarry-stone rolled down-slope on skids, and the great lid at the plain’s heart threw itself open to take the gift, and the trick of it was done — out of its throat and into the open, where a thing built of hinge and hunger met the rising’s bows, and Vega’s hammer, and the end it had been owed since the fever made it. The lids fell still, all of them at once, edge to edge across the plain, like a held breath let go. Out of the king-burrow’s throat the delvers brought up nineteen packs, six lid-maps that were not Wend’s, and one living climber of some company nobody knew, who had been down there in the dark keeping himself by counting, and when they lit a lamp near his face he asked them please to say the number he had reached, because he had lost it, and Nix — who understood completely — told him a good one.

⁂

At the far wall the stair-lift stood open, and beyond the gate the air came down cold and clean, and there was light in it.

Nix went up with the vanguard and stood in the snow of a high country under carved eaves, and looked across a frozen yard at the biggest building he had ever seen that wasn’t a floor — a mead-hall like a timber mountain, every window bleeding warm gold onto the snow, its doors thrown wide, and out of the doors, rolling down across the yard like something spilled, came firelight, and the smell of roasting, and a skald’s song that never stopped, and the sound of a hundred voices raised in welcome.

Sixty floors of the Ascent had taught Nix exactly one lesson without any exceptions in it, and the lesson was: nothing on a Warden’s floor is glad to see you.

He stood in the snow with his cord under his shirt — thirty-one knots now between the chasm and the dust, thirty-one, and not one of them taken by a blade, not one of them anybody’s kill to answer for; the tower itself had done its own work this season, and would again — and he listened to the warmest sound in the world pour out of the Frosthall’s open door, and he counted the doors, and the shadows on the gold, and his own slow steps forward, because counting was how you held a thing still enough to see what it really was.

“Well,” he said quietly, to the standard on his back, to the cord, to nobody. “Either it’s supper, or it’s us.”

It was both. But that is the next part of the story.

Chapter 24 — The Frosthall Parliament

The hall-steward of Hearthold kept her songs the way other keepers had kept weirs and lamps and lid-maps: hidden, mended, and ready. Her name was Bruna, and she met Ede in the Frozen Yard at dusk, among frost-birch hung with shields, and before she would speak a word of Wardens or lifts she walked the general down the row of trees and made her read.

Every shield bore a name in the giants’ deep script. Every name was a thane who had fallen at the endless feast and risen from it collared, bound to drink and fight and die and drink again at the pleasure of a song that never stopped. The firelight from the hall doors came out across the snow and touched the shield-rims gold, and inside, under that lovely light, the feast roared on as it had roared for years, tireless as weather.

“You’ll hear men tell you the Frosthall is a trap dressed as a welcome,” Bruna said. “It’s worse. It is a welcome, trapped. My jarl was the best of us. He’d give his own high seat to a cold stranger and stand for the length of the meal. The tower took that open hand and froze it to a fist, and set him at the head of his own table, feasting on his own folk.” She stopped at the last tree. “So understand me, general of the rising. You do not come to kill a Warden tonight. You come to wake a good lord from a bad dream, and I have kept what wakes him.”

Ede had broken eleven Wardens with steel and siege-craft, and one with mourning. She stood in the snow and listened to a hall-steward lay out a battle-plan made of a cask and a cup and a handful of old songs, and she thought of Skarn dying in the Warcamp still believing his collar was a rank, and she said, “Tell me what you need.”

She is asking you for an army that sings, Sliver said against her palm, dry as always, old as always. I have carried forty-one climbers and not one of them was asked for that before. I find I am glad to have lasted.

They went in at the middle of the night’s third course, when the war-feast was loudest and the ice skald’s harp had the whole hall by the blood. That was the fight, and it was like no fight in the annals Ede meant one day to write: a wedge of shields down the length of the longest table in the world, hearth-wolves cured left and right into dogs that dropped by the fire and slept through the rest of it, the freed thanes’ shields called out name by name from the yard so that collared warriors faltered mid-swing at the sound of themselves — and at the center of it Bruna Hearthold and every voice she had drilled, singing the true songs against the skald’s war-song, plain tunes, hearth tunes, the songs the feast had drowned, while Vega walked through the wreck of the benches like a tower moving and put her craft to collar after collar. The rime-shrouded skald bent its whole frozen art on them and was out-sung in its own hall, and when its song broke, it drained away into the hearth it had poisoned, and the harp fell silent for the first time in years, and the silence was like water on a burn.

Jarl Hrimgar stood alone at the High Seat in his war-plate of rime, huge even for his folk, the frost-crown welded to his brow, and Ede came up the dais steps with no sword in her hand. In her right was the cup. Vega’s shears had opened the great collar at his neck; the Mead of Memory did the rest, and the rest was the worst part to watch. Memory came back to him the way feeling comes back to a frozen limb.

He looked a long time down the ruin of his hall, at the burst casks and the cured hounds and the thanes kneeling over thanes.

“I remember all of it,” he said at last. Giants speak slowly, and finish their sentences, and this was the slowest sentence Ede ever heard. “That is the cruelty in the craft of it. I was not absent. I was at the head of my own table the whole while, with my own hands, in my own hall, and I remember every course of it.”

He took the frost-crown from his brow — it came away with a sound like ice leaving a rock — and he looked at the thing, and he did not throw it down in rage, which Ede half expected. He set it on the table the way a man sets down a debt.

“You will want the lift,” he said. “It stands behind my seat. It is yours, and the seat is yours, and the hall. I have fed on my folk for eleven years. It is a long time before I sit at the head of anything.” He looked at her then, and something came into the ruined face that had not been there under the crown, and it took her a moment to know it for the thing Bruna had promised: the open hand. “But before you climb, general — you and every soul you brought, and every soul on every floor you’ve freed who can make the journey. My hall has served eleven years of the wrong feast. Let it serve a true one. Stay. Eat at my table. Be welcome.”

Behind her, at the foot of the dais, Ede heard a goblin blow out a breath eleven floors long.

⁂

They stayed the winter, and the hall became something no floor of the Ascent had ever seen, and the word for what it became was old and half-forgotten and had to be dusted before use, like the good cups: a capital.

It began with the mundane things, the way everything real began. The host needed quarters, and Hearthold’s folk opened the steadings of the jarldom. The wounded of the Long Stair needed beds, and the Undercroft’s smaller vaults were swept and warmed. The freed floors below needed to send their tithes and their troubles somewhere, and suddenly there were couriers on every lift from the Fencerows to the Frosthall gate, and petitions piling on trestles, and Voss’s Tollmen keeping accounts of grain that no one had asked them to keep, and keepers arriving in person — keepers, who never left their floors — because word had gone down the whole freed tower that the rising was to hold a council of everyone, and everyone, for the first time since the Theft, meant everyone.

Ede had commanded companies and then columns and then a host. She had never in her life presided, and she learned in one winter why kings went grey. The parliament of the banners met in the great hall from the feast of first-frost onward, and it argued from the morning horn to the dousing of the lamps, and the arguing was the point — Rhovan said that to her, one night when she despaired of it, and he was right in the way that only a man who lies for a living can be right about truth. The tower had been built so this noise could never happen. Every voice raised over a grain-ledger in Hrimgar’s hall was a wall of it coming down.

The matters before the parliament were four, and each one nearly broke it.

The Forge-Court came first, because Ulfar Coalbrow did nothing second. The tower’s engines, he argued — and he argued well, slow and finished as all his sentences, with the survey-scrolls to hand — could lower the floors. Gently. Over a generation. The rising did not have to end at a throne with a sword in it; it could end with every stolen country set down whole in its own bed, if the engines were kept, and manned, and mastered. Against him stood his own sister, who would not have the Yoke’s core lit one hour past victory, and half the keepers, who had heard the word generation before from folk who did not have to live under the floor being lowered. The quarrel went four days. What came out was a clause, because that is what parliaments make instead of corpses: the engines’ fate would be decided by a freed world’s own council, on the ground, after the throne — nothing kept, nothing scrapped, until the world that owned them could vote. Vega signed it with her jaw set. Ulfar signed it with the look of a man who believes time is on his side. Ede began, that winter, to understand that a charter is a truce dressed for church.

The Wake came second. Calyne stood in the well of the hall, grey and exact, and asked for what the Wake always asked for — the elf-woods, the scattered fragments of the one great forest, raised to the head of the line of march — and the hall heard her out with folded arms, because nobody loves a spymaster, and Ede watched suspicion do its quiet work and did not altogether fight it in herself. The woods clause failed. Calyne took the defeat without a line of her face moving, gathered her papers, and stayed — which Ede noted, because it was what a loyal woman would do, and also what a patient traitor would do, and the two had become impossible to tell apart at any distance. Rhovan alone went and sat by her afterward. He told the truth in fractions, that man, but he seemed to know which fraction was owed where.

The gates came third, and this was the fight Ede had dreaded, because it was about money, and quarrels about money are the only ones nobody forgives. The Tollmen had kept lifts and ledgers on thirty floors since before the rising had a name, sometimes lawfully, mostly not. The keepers wanted every freed gate open and unkept forever. Voss wanted his investment back. The debate was proceeding toward knives when Ede did the thing she had been turning over since the Cindermouth, and stood, and offered Gilder Voss the post of quartermaster-general of the rising: every store, every train, every granary and ferry and toll-house of the freed tower under his hand, at a fixed honest fee, audited by the parliament twice a year.

“You are setting a thief to keep the thieves,” Voss said, into a silence you could have carved.

“I am,” said Ede. “You’ll find there’s no better warden for a lock than the man who has picked it.”

He laughed — the first time anyone in that hall had heard him do it — and haggled the fee for two hours as a point of honor, and signed. The gates clause passed the same evening: every lift of a freed floor stands open to all, for nothing, forever. The Tollmen wept no tears; they had, as of that afternoon, a larger concern to profit by honestly. Berrin, who had provisioned the rising out of nothing since Roothollow and knew Voss’s stores better than Voss did, offered the new quartermaster-general his own muster-rolls and manifests entire, the work of two years, without being asked. “A fuller cellar than mine,” he said mildly, handing over the keys of his office as if they weighed nothing, “and about time.” Half the hall cheered him. He was that kind of man; he gave a thing away and the room felt richer.

The last matter had no scrolls and no survey, and it was the one the tower would have understood least of all. A thane of the Frosthall — new-freed, still raw at the neck — rose to ask why there were goblins at a table of the peoples, and kobolds behind them, seeing as such folk had held every gate and fought under every Warden from the Fencerows up. The hall went bad-quiet. Nix stood on his bench so as to exist above the table-line and had got as far as “Well, now, that’s a question with a lot of floors under it—” when Dun Harrow rose from the Shieldwall benches with the banner in his one hand, and stood, and said nothing at all for a moment, in the way that made whole camps go still.

“This standard fell at the Kingsfield,” Dun said. “A goblin picked it up. It has come up forty floors since and it has never once been dropped. The Pressed were the tower’s first prisoners and the rising’s first proof. He sits, or I stand for the rest of this parliament.”

Nobody in the gathered world wanted Dun Harrow standing on their account. The clause passed: a seat for the freed Pressed among the peoples, in their own right, in their own names — the names, Ede thought, watching Nix sit down very carefully, that some of them had chosen for themselves in a winter camp with a goblin teaching them how.

⁂

They signed the charter on the last day of the deep winter, on the High Seat’s table, under the smoke-hole, in the light of the honest hearth.

It was not a beautiful document. It was four clauses and a preamble argued down to the bone, copied fair by three clerks, and the preamble said the only thing the whole tower agreed on: that the world was one country before the Theft, and shall be one country after, and that no floor climbs alone. Then the names. Hrimgar rose to put his down first, and stood a moment over the page, the pen a straw in his great hand, and said — slowly, the way his folk finish what they begin — “Alone, each of us was a floor. Stacked, we were his tower. Standing shoulder to shoulder, for the first time since the Theft, we are a stair.” Nobody wrote it into the charter. Nobody needed to; the hall would carry it up the rest of the tower for him. He signed in the deep script, and Vega and Ulfar after him, brother and sister, one pen. Calyne signed small and plain. Voss signed with a flourish that used up more ink than his whole clause. Hobb Fennick, sent up from the first floor of the world with his counts under his arm, signed for the keepers in a farmer’s careful hand, checking the letters twice, and after him the keepers of forty floors. Rhovan signed a name that may or may not have been his. Nix drew, slowly and with his tongue between his teeth, the character he had settled on for himself, and under the seat where no one could see, Ede heard the faint click of a tally-cord being counted. Dun signed one-handed, steadying the page with the banner’s butt. Berrin signed last but one, quartermaster’s neat fair hand, and looked up at Ede with the smile he had been handing her across a counter since she was a drunk nobody in a market town, and said, “Your mother would not have believed one word of this.”

Ede signed last. Sixty-one banners hung from the rafters above her head, and the wind from the smoke-hole moved them all together, softly, like one cloth.

Item, said Sliver, in the dry old voice it kept for the things it meant. Sixty floors ago you would not climb with thirty. You now hold a parliament of nine thousand. I am given to understand this is called recovery.

It did not feel like recovery. It felt, Ede thought, standing under the banners with the ink drying on the age’s strangest paper, like standing at the exact center of the one thing Vharuk had built a hundred floors to prevent. Every clause in the charter was a thing the tower was for stopping. Every name on it was a cell emptied. When word of this paper reaches the throne, she thought, he will stop sending Wardens to slow us and start sending something else — because from tonight we are not climbers to him. We are the thing he fears. We are the gathering.

She would remember thinking it, later, with the taste of ash in her mouth. She had been looking a hundred floors up for the answer, and the answer had been in the room.

But that night there was only the hall, warm to its rafters, and Hrimgar’s folk already laying the long tables for the great feast of the charter — a true feast, the first the Frosthall had served in eleven years, set for three nights hence — and Berrin in the thick of the planning as he always was, cheerful over the lists with Bruna and the goblin cooks, drawing up the seating in his neat quartermaster’s hand so that no two old quarrels would share a bench, so that Cullers sat far from keepers and Tollmen far from everyone, so that every soul in the gathered world would sit down that night among friends.

“Leave the doors open,” the jarl told his stewards, weeping, happy, his open hand come home. “All of them. Let the light out. Let anyone come in.”

Chapter 25 — The Wintering

The Frosthall’s own smithy had stood cold for eleven years, because a feast that never ends has no use for a forge, and Vega Coalbrow spent her first free morning on the sixtieth floor bringing it back.

She did it the way her mother had taught her to wake any fire that had been wronged: slowly, and with an apology. She raked the old dead charcoal out by hand and found under it the last smith’s work where he had dropped it the day the song took the hall — a sledge-shoe, half drawn, one end still in its curl. She set it on the bench where it could watch. Then small wood, then coal, then wind from the great bellows an arm-stroke at a time, until by noon the chimney remembered its trade and the ice let go of the roof-slates over the smithy in one long slide, like a held breath let out, and half the yard came to look at the smoke as if smoke were a banner.

It was, she thought. That one was.

The jarldom was a winter country the way the sea is a wet one: whitely, hugely, and without apology — steadings strung down the firth-vales a day’s sledge apart and then a week’s, herd-halls and byre-towns out to a horizon that smoked with snow, the last herds still walking in off the summer grass a full week’s droving out, and over it all a hard white sky of its own, going up further than kites went. Sixty floors below, in another life, there was a town at the tower’s root built out of everyone the theft had spilled, her own folk among them. She had not expected to meet its like again halfway up the sky. But the word had gone out from Hrimgar’s hall — the host winters here; open the doors — and the jarldom had opened them, all of them, a country of them, and the army poured out into it and thinned and settled, company by company, like meltwater finding old channels. Shieldwall quartered in the great hall and its crofts. The keepers went to the near steadings. The Red Orcs took the high shieling that nobody offered them and sent down smoked fish as rent, which after some study the thanes decided to be honored by.

And twice a week, out and back along the whole strung length of the jarldom, went the sledge-post — six teams, bells on the hames, carrying letters and salt and parliament-summonses and the hall’s slow argument out to the far steadings and the steadings’ answers in. The war had a front somewhere, floors above, past a lift none of them had opened yet. The winter had a route. Vega shod its runners, every team, twice over, and kept a tally of the shoeings on the smithy doorpost, and found she liked the tally better than most of what she had written that year.

In the hall the parliament argued from the morning horn to the dousing of the lamps; that has its own chapter in the annals, and her own worst days are in it, and this is not that chapter. What the annals kept less carefully was the yard. The hall made clauses that winter. The yard made a town.

⁂

The weddings began at midwinter, the way they always do in a camp that has stopped being sure it will die soon.

Eleven of them, before the thaw. Vega counted, because each one came through her smithy: the jarldom’s custom asked iron of a marriage, and the host’s dozen customs asked a dozen things, and somewhere in the first snows the two had struck a bargain at her anvil without asking her. The couple brought two pieces of their own iron — a buckle, a spearhead, a hinge-pin, once a whole ploughshare hauled up sixty floors in a dower-chest — and Vega forge-welded the two into one bar, and drew the bar, and cut it, and rung it into two rings, so that each wore the whole of both. It is beginner’s work, a weld like that, and it is also the whole craft: heat honest, strike true, and no seam to find afterward, though both grains go on all the way through.

The one she kept was Edda’s. A thane’s daughter of Hearthold, freed eight weeks, the collar-mark at her neck still pale, and her man a Shieldwall pikeman who had come out of the cages of the twentieth floor — the two of them standing in the smithy heat with their iron in their four hands like people holding a bird. Neither one had slept a whole night that autumn; the whole yard knew it; the yard knows everything. When the weld took — clean, first fire, the two grains biting — Edda laughed at nothing, the way the newly freed laugh, upward, as if the sound had been let out of a cellar. Vega made their rings a half-measure heavy, for the wear.

Bruna sang at that one. The feast after was barley-cakes and one cask, in a byre, among the wintering oxen, and the fiddler was a Culler and the cook was a kobold and the bride’s father had been a shield on a frost-birch tree eight weeks before, and nobody remarked on any of it. That was the thing about that winter Vega set down in her flat ledger of the world, the entry she went back to afterward more than any other: nobody remarked. The gathered world had stopped being a wonder to itself. It had begun keeping house.

⁂

Old Marn died in the second month, in her sleep, in the hay of the long byre with her boots set together by the wall, and the army learned that it had elders now, and that it could lose one to nothing at all — to seventy years and a life of lading — and the loss would still need carrying.

Marn Deepfurrow had come to the rising at the Gear Galleries the same week as Vega, a wagon-matron of the deep halls with a back like a keel, and had walked every floor since with the trains: hauled the pontoons at the Frozen Sea, corduroyed the ash-roads at the Cindermouth, pulled two oxen and a boy out of a crevasse on the Troll Bridges with her hands. She had no family in the host. She had the host.

The giants’ rite is short, because it is mostly digging. They took her out past the in-fields to a rise the jarl gave freely, and dug through five feet of snow and three of frost to the true ground, the jorth, turned dark and honest under all that white, and they laid her in it with her lading-hooks and her hame-bells and the little whetstone she had carried since her own mother’s forge. Her sledge-team stood unharnessed at the graveside the whole while, blowing steam, because the drovers judged the beasts had the right and no one argued. Then every soul who had ever ridden her wagons — and it took the muster- clerks no time at all to discover this was most of the army — filed past and put one thing each into the grave-fill: a nail, a button, a knot of rope, tokens of carried things, so that she went into the ground of the sixtieth floor under a yard of the whole rising’s freight. Vega spoke the forge-words at the head of it, the old ones, about iron coming out of the ground and work being the rent we pay for it. Then, because the rite asks it, the Frosthall smithy stood cold that night, one night, the fire let go out on purpose and lit new at dawn — a thing Vega had done for others perhaps thirty times in her life, and that cost her more that winter than it ever had, though she could not have said toward what the cost was paid.

Hrimgar came to the grave last, alone, bare-headed in the falling snow. He stood a long time. “Eleven years I fed on my own folk,” he said at last, to the mound, in the deep tongue, slowly, the way his kind finish what they start. “You died having fed mine. I will hold the difference where I hold my crown, which is at arm’s length, where I can see it.” Then he bowed, a jarl to a wagon-matron, and the snow went on filling the tokens’ little hollows, and that was the funeral.

The next sledge-post out, every far steading sent its children in to be counted on the rising’s rolls, which the clerks recorded as a courtesy of the jarldom, and Vega recorded otherwise: a country does not lend its children’s names to a passing army. It lends them to a neighbor.

⁂

Nix’s fire had become a school by then, and the school had walls, because Bruna had thrown the goblin out of the wind and into the Undercroft’s smallest vault, which had held the feast’s endless casks for eleven years and now held a hearth, nine benches, ninety-one kobolds in shifts, and one standard leaned in the corner like a broom.

He was teaching letters now. The names had come back first — that was old work, done in the winter camps below — and a name, Nix said, wants somewhere to stand. So: letters. Vega came down in the third month with what the school had commissioned of her, the only commission of her life she had filled for a fee of burnt crusts: letter-punches, a full set, sized for kobold hands, so a learner could strike a word into a strip of lead and hold the word after, heft it, carry it home in a pocket. Learning to write, the goblin explained, was mostly learning that a word will hold still. “Paper doesn’t convince them. Paper’s weather. Lead they believe.”

She stayed the evening and watched the one they called Chalk — an old digger with a made name and hands like tree-roots — strike his own five letters into lead, spell them back, and then, unasked, strike a second strip and carry it across the vault to her, formal as a herald, the metal still warm from the work. It said VEGA. It weighed what a key weighs. She has it still.

They were not her people, the Pressed. Her people had forged the collars; that mark was on her; she had stopped asking the mark to go away and started asking it to be useful. But she sat in the smallest vault of the Undercroft with lead-strips cooling on nine benches and thought: sixty floors down, the tower believes it is fighting an army. It is fighting this. It never once planned for this, because this cannot be planned for, only fed.

Upstairs, by the great hearth, the other school kept its own hours.

Dun Harrow had claimed the settle nearest the fire-dogs the first week of the wintering, and every evening after supper he mended the banner. It needed a winter of it. Sixty floors were written on that cloth in the only script cloth keeps: hedge-thorn rips from the Fencerows, drum-fire scorch from the Warcamp, the fine eight-legged snags of the Broodthrone silk, and all through the weave the grey of the Cindermouth’s ash, which would not full out, and which he had stopped trying to full out. “Cloth’s honest,” he said, when Bruna’s women offered him new bunting. “It shows what it’s been through. That’s most of what it’s for.” He sewed one-handed, the pole steadied under his boot, the needle slow and exact, and it was Vega’s place, fallen to her by the same wordless bargain as the wedding-iron, to sit by him with the thread and keep his needles fed. A giantess threading needles draws comment the first evening and none after. Her hands had set rivets finer than any needle’s eye. They talked, or they did not. He was the restfullest man she had ever kept a fire with.

And each week, a bench nearer, came Hale.

He had wintered at the hall’s far cold end at first, out past the dogs, grey cloak and grey face, mending the yard’s practice-blades that no one had asked him to mend, running a whetstone down nicked edges by ear in the half-dark. Then one week he was mending them six benches in. Then four. He never joined the settle’s talk; he corrected a young thane’s shield-grip once with two fingers, the way you’d straighten a picture in passing, and was gone before the boy turned. Vega watched the whole slow transit across a winter’s worth of evenings and entered it in the flat ledger under the only figure it fit: cold iron coming back to temper. You cannot hurry it. You can only keep the fire lit and the door unbarred, and let the metal make up its own mind about the distance. By the last month he sat at the bench nearest the kitchens, in the traffic of the cooks’ boys, who stepped around him the way you step around a post you trust, and if anyone had asked her — no one asked her — she would have said the serjeant was becoming, at his own pace, by his own stair, a man who would be all right.

Ulfar came to the smithy in the last month, too, on an evening she had not sent for him, with his sleeves rolled, and did not mention engines, or votes, or the hall. “Cast with me,” he said. “It has been twenty years, and you still shortcut your sand.” They built the molds together past midnight, brother and sister, four hands in one box the way it had been when the dams they built were knee-high and the river was their mother’s slack-tub, and they poured hinges for the Undercroft doors, and argued about the gating, and were happy, and did not say so, being Coalbrows. The hinges hang there yet. They carry well.

⁂

On the last day of the deep winter the parliament signed its charter — the hall’s bell went at noon, and the yard heard the roar through three walls and grinned at itself and went back to work, which is a town’s way of celebrating a treaty. The feast was set for three nights on. Bruna’s kitchens went to war. The goblin cooks swore an oath about a boar. Berrin came down the smithy row that evening with his lists, walking backward against the wind, and begged of her one great fire-crane for the main hearth by feast-night, and she forged it in a day and hung it herself, and he stood under it beaming like a man handed the keys of a larger kitchen, and the cooks cheered her out the door.

She banked her forge the night before the feast the way she banked it every night that winter: coals raked to the heart, ash drawn over, everything kept that could be kept. In the yard the snow came down soft through the light from the hall — every door of the Frosthall stood open, gold on the blue drifts, eleven years of darkness being paid back at the jarl’s own order — and from the doors came the sound of trestles going up, and Bruna’s singers at their rehearsal, and somewhere under it the small ring of a whetstone, evening-steady, from the bench nearest the kitchens.

Vega stood in the snow a while, warming her hands at the whole of it.

An army, her mother used to say, is a fire in a field: it eats and moves on, and the field remembers it a year, and then the field forgets. But a town — a town is a hearth. A hearth is a fire that has been kept: banked at night, apologized to, fed slow, built where walls could learn to hold its heat. The rising had come up sixty floors as a fire in a field. Somewhere in the wedding-iron and the grave-tokens and the lead-strips and the sledge-post’s bells, on one white country halfway up the sky, it had become the other thing.

She went in through the smithy-yard door, out of the cold. Behind her the hall stood open on the winter, all its lamps lit, all its doors wide, warm gold clean out to the frost-birches — a hearth the size of a country, banked and breathing, keeping its heat for everyone the world could send.

Chapter 26 — The Feast

On the morning of the feast of the charter, Mistress Calyne came to Ede in the Undercroft, among the casks, where the general had gone to be alone with the lists and had brought, out of old habit, a lamp and no cup.

The spymaster of the Wake did not waste her own time or anyone else’s. She stood in the lamp-light with her grey cloak still frosted from the yard, and she laid a strip of paper on the cask-head between them, and she said, “There is a Herald inside your charter’s circle.”
