The paper was a cipher-strip, short, torn at one end. Ede did not read ciphers. She looked at it the way you look at a knife someone has set on your table, and waited.

“It came off a drummer at the Cindermouth,” Calyne said. “Malgrim’s hand-code, third form. Most of it is nothing — musters, floors, the grain-count at the Moraine Gates, which any spy with boots could sell him. But this line prices something else.” Her finger touched the strip without moving it. “It prices the order of a council that had not yet met. Who would speak first at the Frosthall, and for what. That was not carried up by boots, general. That came out of the room where your parliament was planned — out of the circle that drew it. Someone who signs what you sign. Someone with a collar under their shirt, and it is a willing one, and it has been there a long time. I have hunted this hand for years and it is close enough now that I am telling you at last, plainly, in daylight: there is a Herald at your table, and they will be at your feast tonight.”

The Undercroft was quiet. Somewhere above, a bench scraped; the hall was being dressed. Ede looked at the strip of paper, and then at the woman who had brought it, and everything she knew arranged itself in the old order, the way a shieldwall forms without a command.

Because here was the thing about Mistress Calyne, laid out fair, the way Ede would later lay it out for herself a hundred times on a hundred sleepless floors: no one in the rising had her ciphers. No one had her absences — gone three days below, back with frost on her boots and nothing said. No one else had walked out of a Roothollow childhood into the Wake’s grey service with a past so clean it squeaked. The payment that sold Shieldwall had moved through toll-ledgers her faction audited. She had arrived at the rising’s council uninvited, embedded herself, survived every suspicion by being useful — and now, the morning the charter’s ink was dry, here she stood, pointing away from herself, at everyone. At Vega. At Voss. At Hobb Fennick and Dun Harrow. At Berrin.

That was where the wall closed. The accusation had no floor under it and it was aimed at a circle of people who had bled up sixty floors — and it was brought by the one person in the tower whose own account had never once been opened.

“You’ve hunted this hand for years,” Ede said. “So you have said. It occurs to me, Mistress, that a hunter is the one soul in a wood whose walking around at night needs no explaining.” She pushed the strip back across the cask-head. “Everyone in that circle has carried someone I love out of a fight. What has the Wake carried? Ciphers. Bring me a name and proof under it, and I will act though it break me. Bring me one more nameless someone on the morning of the first good day this army has ever had, and I will start to wonder why the Wake needs my council frightened of itself.”

Calyne looked at her for a long moment. Whatever moved behind that grey regard, it did not reach the surface; it never did; that was the whole trouble with her.

“You are guarding the wrong door,” she said at last, without heat, and folded the strip away. “I will be at the feast, general. Armed.”

She was at the turning of the stair when Berrin came down it — nearly into her — with a basket on his arm and flour to the elbow. He begged her pardon so warmly that even Calyne’s face conceded something, and then he was in the lamp-light unpacking the basket onto the cask-head: a loaf still hot, split, buttered, and a smear of honey, because he had known Ede since before the Warcamp and knew she forgot to eat on the days she was afraid of being happy.

“They’ve let me at the ovens,” he said. “Bruna’s folk bake big as they build; I’ve made them do a proper Roothollow batch for the low tables. Eat that while it’s warm.” He was already turning to go — there was no rest in him on a feast day, there never had been — and he paused at the stair-foot and looked back at her, flour on his hands, the lamp catching the good plain worry in his face. “You look like your watch never ended, Ede. It’s ended. Sixty floors and a signed paper says so. Tonight, for one night, let the army guard you.”

The bread did smell of home. That was the fact, and she chose it.

⁂

The Frosthall that night was the finest room in the world.

Every lamp in the jarldom was lit, and every door stood open, the great north door to the yard and the south door by the kitchens and the little postern doors between, all wide, spilling gold onto the snow, because Hrimgar had commanded it: the hall had eleven years of darkness to pay back. Beyond the doorways the jarldom ran out into its own night — snowfields going blue and then black toward hills no lamp would reach before morning, a country’s worth of quiet — and the hall stood up in all that dark like one coal in a swept hearth. The sixty-one banners hung overhead with the charter itself among them, hung from the center-beam over the High Seat where all the hall could see it. The tables ran the full length of the floor, and at them sat the gathered world according to Berrin’s careful pages: men and giants shoulder to shoulder, elves among the keepers, freed thanes pouring for freed kobolds, Cullers seated decently far from those they had wronged. Serving-lines flowed from the kitchens along both walls, laden, endless. The goblin cooks had outdone the age: Nix had sworn that the freed Pressed would feed this parliament a feast no giant would forget, and the boar came in on poles to a noise like a lift-gate opening.

Hrimgar would not take the High Seat. He stood before it the whole first course, cup in hand, and gave the seat to Ede with the hall watching — a jarl who’d give his own seat to a cold stranger — and the roar that went up shook soot from the smoke-hole. Ede sat where the Warden of the sixtieth floor had sat, under the charter, with Dun Harrow standing his post behind her shoulder, the banner’s butt grounded by her chair, as he had stood behind her in every hall and every mud-camp since the cages, because he was her standard-bearer, and he had told her once, in the way he said such things, that a standard-bearer’s post was wherever the standard’s general sat, and that had ended the discussion for two years.

Hale she marked far down the hall at a low bench, in his grey, drinking little, as he did now. The serjeant had come up sixty floors to become a quiet man. He had taken the seat nearest the kitchens, and when the goblin cooks’ boys went by under their great trays he steadied the smallest one’s elbow with two fingers, absent, unthanked, the way he had once corrected a recruit’s shield-grip. Ede caught herself watching him and could not have said why.

The toasts began. Hrimgar gave the dead of the Long Stair, all the hall on its feet, thirty-one names read slow by a goblin from a knotted cord, and no one sat until the last. Voss, sleek as a paid bill, gave the customers of the freed gates, which is everyone, forever — and got his laugh, and even the keepers drank. Vega gave her mother’s forge, and Ulfar drank to that with his eyes wet. Berrin would give no toast, though half the hall called for him; he was everywhere and nowhere all night, flour-dusted still at the cuffs, walking the serving-lines with his lists, chivvying, tasting, laughing — and it was Berrin, passing behind the High Seat with a jug in his hand, who leaned in between Ede and Dun and said the last easy words anyone would ever say in the old world, the world before:

“Look at them, Ede. Look what you fed.”

Then he straightened to go on down the line, jug in hand, facing out over the bright length of the hall the way a man at the High Seat’s back must face, and what happened next happened all at once.

A mead-horn dropped somewhere down the eastern line and did not bounce. It rang — struck stone edge-on, a sound too clean for an accident — and along both serving-lines, in one movement, as if the hall itself had been rehearsed, a dozen servers set down their trays and drew from under the linen the knives that were not carving knives, and above, at the smoke-hole gallery where no servers went, the first bowstring spoke.

The arrow was for Ede. Kill the general under her own signed peace, in her own parliament, and the paper over her head becomes a headstone for the age’s great foolish hope. She did not see it loosed. She saw the table-edge come up at her as something struck her whole side — not the arrow, Berrin, Berrin’s shoulder taking her out of the chair, Berrin’s arms around her head, the two of them going down together into the spilled gold of the mead as the arrow meant for her throat went through him high in the back with a sound she knew from twenty years of fields, and he made no cry at all, only a cook’s small annoyed grunt, as if a loaf had caught, and covered her with his body, and would not be moved.

Then the hall was a battle, and it was the battle the tower had always wanted: not army against army but a feast against itself, friend indistinguishable from knife, and fire — fire already in the rafters, pitch-arrows among the banners, sixty-one flags of the gathered world beginning to burn over the heads of the world they gathered.

One picture of that night outlasted all the rest in the tellings: the burning length of the Frosthall doubled in the mead sheeted across the stone floor, sixty-one banners burning twice, above and below, while the night wind came in through the fought-over doors off a hundred miles of snow and bent every flame in the hall toward the north door — as if the fire itself had been told where the way out was.

Ede got out from under her oldest friend with his blood on her neck, and stood up into it, and did what she was for.

“SHIELDWALL,” she said, in the voice that carried over drum-fire, and the tables of the freed company came over onto their sides down the length of the hall in one crash, oak two fingers thick, and the gathered world went behind it. Even then, in the noise, the fight had a shape, and she read it the way she read any field: fire along the center-beam, working at the roof; knives at the signers’ benches, midway down both tables, where the charter’s names were seated; Shieldwall’s overturned oak making a keep of the hall’s middle; and at either end of the long house the two great doors, north to the yard and south to the kitchens, which were the whole battle, because a burning hall is not a trap until it shuts. Vega was already moving — a giant among falling lamps, sweeping children and keepers behind her like a hand sweeping crumbs, her craft hurled up at the burning center-beam so that frost ran along it and the fire above the charter died hissing. Rhovan was on a table with his bow, loosing up into the gallery, elf-eyed in the smoke, and beside him, suddenly, greyly, Calyne — Calyne who had come armed, Calyne who was killing the men she had warned of with a short blade and no expression Ede could read across the reddening hall.

The serving-lines’ knives went for the benches where the charter’s signers sat. They knew the seating. Ede saw it and had no time to see it; she saw a Herald put his blade through a Frosthall steward who stood between him and Hobb Fennick, saw the old keeper of the first floor of the world swing a bench-end like a man knocking apples, saw Hrimgar tear a burning rafter-length down with his bare hands and lay about him, a jarl defending his guests with a beam of his own hall, roaring in the deep tongue, weeping another kind of tears than the night had begun with.

At the north door, they tried to close the hall.

That was the shape of the thing; the fire above, the knives within, and the doors — the doors Hrimgar had ordered open — swinging to, barred from without by Heralds in the yard, so that the gathered world should roast in its own capital. The great south door by the kitchens was already booming shut. The north door had Dun Harrow in it.

He had left her shoulder. Standard-bearer’s post is wherever the standard is needed, and the standard was needed in a doorway. He set the banner-pole of Shieldwall crosswise in the jamb, oak against oak, and set himself against the pole, one arm, all his weight, and the door met him and stopped. Through the gap the night came in cold and the folk streamed out past him into the yard, keepers and cooks’ boys and thanes’ children, a river of the saved running under his braced body, while the Heralds inside put an arrow into him, and then another, because he would not fall, because falling was a thing that happened to other men’s standards. Ede fought toward him the length of a burning hall and was too slow, was always going to be too slow, and knew it with every step, the way you know the end of a song.

He was still on his feet when she reached him. The door stood open. The pole stood true. Dun Harrow, one-armed, twice-shot, looked at his general the way he had looked at her at the cages of the Warcamp, when he had crossed a silent camp of men who blamed her and put the banner in her hands, and he said, in his ordinary voice, the voice that never wavered because it had never once needed to:

“Go on through.”

And that was all. That was Dun. He held his post until the last child was in the yard and the first shieldmen relieved him, and then he sat down against the doorpost he had defended, under the standard he had carried, with the snow of the yard blowing in past him, and was done.

Across the hall, the south door came open again.

Ede did not see how it began; the kitchens had no witnesses left standing who could tell it whole. But the hall saw how it ended, and the goblin cooks told the rest for the rest of their lives: how the far door boomed shut with all the kitchen-folk trapped on the hall side of it, how the Heralds turned from the bar to the cooks with their knives — the Pressed of the serving-lines, the smallest folk at the feast, the ones the tower had always spent first — and how the man who came out of the smoke to stand between was a Culler.

Hale unbarred the door alone. It cost him the time it costs, all of it paid out of his own blood, and then he stood in the open doorway with his grey cloak burning at the hem and held it — the serjeant who had taught half the army’s captains their trade, sword in the old high guard he had drilled into Ede when she was seventeen and stupid, holding the south door of the Frosthall against the tower’s own doctrine, kill them all, they cannot then be enemies — holding it open, this time, so that everything small could get out. He had a rule from the fighting-pits, older than any doctrine, the first thing he gave every recruit he ever ruined and saved: lose your sword, lose your teeth, but keep your gate. Ede had heard it at seventeen and carried it thirty years. He kept his gate. The cooks went through under his arms. The last of them, the smallest boy, the one whose elbow he had steadied, would say all his life that Serjeant Hale looked down at him going past and said something, and that it was, “Straight home. Don’t count us. Count yourself.”

They found him in the doorway after, among six of them.

⁂

The Heralds died to a man. Once the first surprise was spent there was no question of it — forty knives and a dozen bows against nine thousand of the rising — and by the time the fires were out there was not one left to put a question to. Each dead server, stripped, wore the same thing under the shirt: a collar, light-made, close-linked, willing. No one had forced these. They had eaten this hall’s bread with their own war under their clothes.

The gathered world spent the night counting, because counting was all there was.

Sixty-eight dead. Nineteen of them kitchen-folk and servers who had been true — the goblin cooks laid their own out in the Frozen Yard and would let no one else carry them, and then, at dawn, six goblins carried Serjeant Hale of the Cullers out to lie at the head of that row, and folded his burned grey cloak under his head, and nobody in the rising said one word about it, then or ever. Hobb Fennick lived; the steward who had stepped in front of him did not, and the old man sat by her the whole night with his hat off, doing the only thing he knew, which was keeping count, and for once the counting did not help. The charter lived. Calyne had cut it down from the center-beam with her own blade when the rafters caught — the grey spymaster, first to know, first armed, standing over the rising’s one page in a burning hall — and it had cost the document a scorched corner, and Ede, taking it from her hands, could not meet her eyes, and Calyne, being Calyne, said nothing at all, which was worse than anything she could have said.

Dun Harrow they laid on the High Seat’s table with the banner of Shieldwall over him, and the freed of the cages stood watch in turns, all night, one-armed men and hollow-eyed women who had been five years in the dark of the Warcamp because a route was sold, standing over the man who had carried their name up sixty floors and put it in a doorway.

And Berrin lived. The barber-surgeons drew the arrow before midnight and called it a clean wound, high through the meat of the back and shoulder — an inch of wind the other way, they said, and it would have found the general’s throat. Ede sat by his cot in the Undercroft among the casks where the morning had begun, and held his flour-scuffed hand while he slept, and did the arithmetic of it over and over: he saw nothing, knew nothing, had no shield and no sword and no reason, and he put his body over hers between one heartbeat and the next, the way you reach for bread before you know you are hungry. Whatever else the night had taken, it had shown her the bottom of one man, and the bottom was solid, and she had built on it half her life.

When he woke, near dawn, the first thing he asked was whether the cooks had got out.

The second thing, when she told him about Dun and about Hale, was nothing at all. He turned his face away and cried like a quartermaster: silently, briefly, and then he asked for the lists of the dead, because someone would have to strike them from the rolls, and it should be done kindly, and by someone who had known them, and he was already reaching for his coat.

She took the lists from him and blew out the lamp and ordered him, as his general, to sleep.

Then she went up alone into her burned capital, in the grey of the morning, past the row in the yard and the watch at the table, and stood under the black center-beam where the charter had hung, and understood what the tower had bought at her feast and what it had failed to buy. The paper was safe. The paper would be re-hung by noon and the parliament would sit again by week’s end, harder and colder and wiser; the rising would climb; none of that had died. What had died was the room where no one watched the serving-lines. Sixty floors of war had not managed to teach the gathered world suspicion, and one supper had. From now on there would be tasters, and guards at the gallery, and lists checked against lists, and every open door would have a man remembering the north one.

Somebody in this hall had known the seating, she thought. And the thought did not finish; it only stood there in her, cold, patient, the first stone of a thing she would be building for a long time.

You are still wearing his blood, Sliver said quietly, against her palm. The baker’s. You should sleep, Ede.

“After the names,” she said. “Bring me ink.”

Chapter 27 — The Reckoning of Names

Rhovan of the Grey Rides had spent a long life sorting what people said from what was so, and he had learned that the second thing lives in small rooms. Proclamations are for halls. Truth keeps to pantries, stair-feet, the space between a question and its answer. So in the days after the feast, while the Frosthall was scrubbed and re-beamed and the parliament talked bravely of sitting again, Rhovan did what he was made for. He found the small rooms, and he stood in their corners, and he watched the rising learn to be suspicious.

The first small room was the annex off the hall’s west wall, where the muster-rolls of the whole gathered world had been racked in their cases, company by company, floor by floor — two years of Berrin’s neat quartermaster’s hand, every name that had ever eaten the rising’s bread — and where the fire had done its most thorough work. The rafters of the great hall were scorched; the annex was ash. A pitch-arrow through its one window, the wardens judged, in the first breath of the attack. Of everything the Heralds burned that night, nothing had burned so well as the names.

Ede stood in the ash of it on the third morning and said she would rebuild the rolls from memory.

Rhovan was in the doorway and heard the whole of what followed, and counted it, as he counted all exchanges, in the private ledger he kept behind his eyes. Berrin — up from his cot against the surgeons’ word, arm slung, grey-faced, moving like a man twice his years — protested, and his protests were all of them sound. There were fair copies of the older rolls in Roothollow, he said; send down for them, six weeks and the bulk of it is back, why spend yourself. The new musters could be re-sworn company by company; let the captains carry it. She had not slept three nights; the rising needed its general, not its clerk. And when none of that moved her, the last protest, the true one, the one that made half the annex turn and look at him: “Ede. I kept those rolls. Every name in them went through this hand. Let the man who lost them be the man who digs them back.”

“You didn’t lose them, and you have one arm to dig with,” Ede said. “I am not spending you either, old friend. Sit down before you fall down.”

And then — Rhovan watched it happen — she called for trestles, and ink, and the captains of every company in the muster, and she began. Not from the Roothollow copies. From her own head, and from everyone’s: each captain called their company from memory, each soldier named their bench-neighbors, each keeper their floor-folk, cross-sworn, witnessed, written fair by three clerks at three separate tables so that no single hand held the record. It took nine days. It became, though no one had planned it — Rhovan was never sure whether even Ede had planned it — a thing the whole army passed through, a mustering of the living to answer the burning of the names; folk queued in the yard past the row of graves to swear to one another’s existence, and wept doing it, and came away steadier. The Frosthall’s skalds already had a song about the feast. By the ninth day they had a quieter one about the lists.

Berrin sat where she had put him, and could not write, and so did what he did: he fed the work. Broth to the clerks’ tables, warm lofts for captains who had queued past midnight, and his own prodigious memory laid open to anyone who was short a name — he knew every soul’s floor and trade and mess-line, all of it given freely, checked and re-checked against the sworn testimony like everything else. A ledger of him, Rhovan thought, watching him help an old Fencerows widow recall her dead husband’s company, and meant it in admiration, and filed the thought away in the manner of his trade, which files everything.

⁂

The second small room was the Undercroft, past midnight, nine days after the feast, among the casks. Calyne had chosen it. Rhovan understood the choosing; it was where the general had refused her warning, and Calyne, who was owed an apology from the world and would die before requesting one, took her satisfactions in geography.

Two lamps. One barrel-head. On it, side by side, for the first time in the twenty years he had served her, the whole of what each of them knew.

“Ciphers, then,” she said. “Mine first. Since half this army has me buried under the traitor’s cairn already, we may as well begin with the accused.”

It should be said plainly, because Rhovan said nothing plainly aloud in those years and someone must: agent and handler had never once opened their books to each other. That is the grammar of the Wake. A handler knows what her agent is for; an agent knows what he is fed; the space between is the faction’s fortune, and you do not spend the fortune on trust. What happened on that barrel-head was, by every law Rhovan had lived under since the Grey Rides, treason twice over — his fractions and her ledgers, laid flat in lamplight for a colleague to read whole. He gave her the last of what he had held back from the Broodthrone year, the pieces he had kept even after his leak to Vyx: the courier who had passed the spider-queen’s web twice, once going up, once having already been. She gave him five years of intercepts in Malgrim’s hand-code, third form, and her workings beneath them, and he began, strip by strip, to see the shape she had been walking around alone all this time.

The shape was this. One hand, appearing in the traffic across twenty years, always in fair-copy — a clerk’s trained script, Roothollow-schooled, the kind every counting-house south of the tower taught its apprentices, no flourish, no error. Rhovan set the strips in date-order down the barrel-head like a man laying out a hand of cards. The hand had priced a mercenary company’s route the season of the Red Warcamp. The hand had moved payment through the toll-ledgers — his own burglary, the page from floor five, matched two of Calyne’s intercepts to the week. The hand had requested extraction once, years back, and been refused — a ledger entry does not retire, the reply had said, and Calyne had kept both halves of that exchange like a pressed flower. And the hand had written, in the strip from the Cindermouth drummer, the speaking-order of a council that met behind shut doors at the top of the freed world.

“Malgrim’s willing ones sign nothing,” Calyne said. “No names, no marks. It is the whole craft of the collar — the tower owns them and never once writes it down. So we will not catch the hand by its name. We catch it by its reach.” She set one pale finger on the last strip. “Whoever wrote this was in the room, or was told by someone in the room within a day. I have been fifteen years narrowing a world to that room, Rhovan. I need eyes I did not train. Tell me what I have wrong.”

He looked a long time.

“Nothing wrong,” he said at last. “One thing missing. You’ve built it all on paper, and paper is your country, so you trust it. But the feast wasn’t paper. Whoever called that strike fed Malgrim something no cipher carries well.” He tapped the barrel-head. “Where everyone would be sitting.”

⁂

It was Nix who found it, because of course it was Nix. The dead Heralds had been searched by soldiers, and soldiers search for weapons and papers and gold, and had duly found knives and collars and nothing else, and the forty of them lay under canvas in the ice-house yard awaiting the parliament’s ruling on where you bury such men. Nix went down among them on the tenth day, alone, with a lamp. Asked later why, he said the count was wrong — the goblin cooks had served the eastern line with eleven jugs and only ten were smashed or found, and a missing jug had been itching him for a week, and no, the jug never did turn up, but you don’t find the thing you’re counting, you find the thing that’s there.

What was there was a fold of paper, flat along the inside of a dead server’s boot-shaft, between leather and lining, where a man keeps what must survive his pockets being turned out. The boot had been by the west wall when the rafters dripped fire; its owner had burned along one whole side. The paper had not.

Rhovan was fetched first, before even Ede, which told him what the goblin’s quick eyes had already made of it. They spread it on the ice-house floor by lamplight, weighted with pebbles at the corners like a treaty.

It was the seating of the feast of the charter. All of it — every table, every bench, the whole gathered world in its places, copied fair in an anonymous serving-hand onto one page. And it was not copied from the lists posted in the kitchens on the feast’s eve, because Rhovan walked to the kitchens that same hour and checked: the posted lists had the Coalbrows, brother and sister, at the west table, where Bruna Hearthold had moved them on the last day to honor the forge-oath, an old giants’ courtesy. The paper in the boot had them at the north table, among the keepers.

They had sat at the west table. But they had been going to sit at the north one — for exactly as long as the seating was a draft, in council, before it was ever posted. The paper in the Herald’s boot was a copy of the plan, not of the feast. Marked, in a small careful ring of ink, around the High Seat: the inner table. The charter’s circle.

“So,” said Calyne, into the ice-house cold.

Rhovan stood over the paper and did what he and the goblin both did in extremity. He counted.

Who had been in the room with the draft? The council of the charter’s precedence, called to settle who sat where before pride could unsettle it — five days before the feast, the annex, shutters barred, one page passed hand to hand around one small table. Ede, who had approved it. Berrin, who had drawn it. Voss, who had disputed three benches’ worth of Tollmen and been overruled. Ulfar Coalbrow, who had stood on precedence for the Forge-Court. Calyne, seated in council by right since the parliament’s founding, who had said nothing and remembered everything. And Rhovan of the Grey Rides, present at his handler’s shoulder, seen by all, suspected already by half.

Six souls. He said the count aloud, and the ice-house held it the way small rooms hold the truth.

Every soul of the six had signed the charter. Every soul of the six had bled for the rising, or fed it, or led it. One of the six had a light collar under the shirt and had walked out of that shuttered room and written the gathered world’s seating out fair for the tower — and knives had gone down the serving-lines by that paper, and Dun Harrow had two arrows by it, and a Culler lay at the head of a row of goblin graves by it, and none of the six could now be believed about anything, including him doing the counting.

“Keep all six on your list,” Calyne said, watching him. “Yourself among them. That is the discipline. The heart strikes names off; the ledger keeps them.” A pause; the lamp guttered; and the grey voice, for one breath, was almost gentle. “Hers will be the hardest to keep on. She took an arrow’s worth of proof at that table. Keep it on anyway.”

He never asked which name she meant. That was the other discipline.

⁂

The parliament sat again at the month’s turn, under the re-hung charter with its scorched corner, which the clerks had offered to trim square and Ede had forbidden — let it show what it cost — and voted the climb resumed. There was no argument. Grief had done what four days of debate never managed: the gathered world was of one mind, and the mind was cold, and it pointed up.

On the last night at the Frosthall, Rhovan went out alone into the Frozen Yard, past the graves with their new stones — sixty-eight, and two apart under one standard and one grey cloak, where the snow was kept swept — and stood at the yard’s north wall, and looked at where the army was going. Above the hall’s dark roofline the sixty-first gate stood open, and beyond it there was no more country. The world ran out. From here the Ascent climbed into its own sky: he could see, faint past the gate in the high air’s lamp-dark, the first of the great mooring-chains slung between the floors like the rigging of a moored world — a road of iron links, each wide as a wagon, hung over cloud and nothing, going up. Chainrest, the sky-folk called the town at its foot. Somewhere past it hung the wreck-fields, and the harpy-passes, and the storm with a queen inside it that the Wake’s old files spoke of in the tone reserved for debts.

The rising would walk that chain, all nine thousand of it, one behind the other over the fall of the world, and Rhovan would walk it carrying his ledger of six names, and one of the six would be walking it too, a knife’s length away, all the way up.

He found he was looking forward to the sky. Elves answer even themselves with fractions, but there it was, whole, and he let himself keep it: up there, at least, on a road one link wide, everybody’s hands would be where he could see them.

From Malgrim's Ledger

Item: the feast bill comes due and is worth it. Forty servers and twelve bows, spent to the last man, as was always the design — a willing collar that comes home is a story, and stories are the one cargo I will not ship. Against that column: one standard-bearer, one serjeant of the old doctrine, a charter scorched, a parliament that now tastes its own soup, and a general who has stopped sleeping. I have bought worse for more.

Item: let no one below read this as cruelty. Cruelty is waste, and I keep clean books. The kindness was ours: a gathered world is a world that can be broken at one table. We offered them, at supper, the lesson the King offers by architecture — that trust at scale is a door left open — and we charged only sixty-eight lives for a truth the tower usually prices in nations. They will not learn it. Hope never audits.

Item: the woman doubles her guard and guards the wrong thing. She watches the serving-lines now, the galleries, the doors. She has put tasters at the benches. She has, I am told, rebuilt her burned rolls out of the mouths of the living, nine days of oath and ink — admirable, useless. Names were never our way in. Love is. No taster sips at that.

Item: the spymaster and the archer have opened their books to each other. This was foreseen, and is convenient. Two hunters sharing one lamp cast one shadow; while they walk their paper trail of hands and ledgers, the hand itself rests where it has always rested — nearer the woman than any paper reaches, and thanked daily for its nearness.

Item: our friend performs grief convincingly. He always did. I collared him for it. The others below wear the collar the way a beast wears a yoke, resenting the plough; he wears his the way a clerk wears a wedding-ring — a thing chosen once, in daylight, and never since regretted aloud. He wept for the standard-bearer, and the weeping was, in the fullest sense, sincere. That is the whole art of a willing instrument: nothing in it is false but the sum.

Item: he has asked again what his service purchases, at the last. Tell him what I tell every entry in this book, gently, because it is true and because gentleness costs nothing: it purchases what he asked for on the night he knelt. To be necessary. He is. No man in the tower is more necessary than our friend, and no man will be, until the day the account closes.

Item: they climb for the chains. Advise the sky.

PART IV

THE SCATTERED SKY

floors 61–80

Chapter 28 — The Anchor Chains

Vega Coalbrow had drawn chain all her life, and she had never feared it until she walked upon one above the clouds.

The first link was longer than a mead-hall. It hung in a shallow arc from the anchor-tower behind her to a windlass-house she could not see, a road of grey iron slung across a gulf that had no floor. Cloud moved under it, white and slow. Wind moved over it and made it sing — a low hum, steady as a hearth, felt through the boot-soles before the ears caught it. She set her hand on the link’s worn crown and read the iron the way another woman might read a letter. Old. True. Tired in the pins.

— This one is sound, — said the chain-keeper beside her. Mera Chainrest was a woman of Men, small as a child against Vega’s knee, grease to the elbows, a striking-hammer at her belt. — You hear the hum? A sound link hums. A failing one goes quiet. The moss muffles it and the rust-flower eats the pins, so we walk, and we listen. It is slow work. It is the only work there is.

— I understand slow work, — Vega said. She said everything slowly, and she finished it. — Show me a quiet link, and I will mend it.

The rising crossed the Anchor Chains for nine days, and it crossed them quietly, because it had not sung since the Frosthall. Two months had not softened that. The army still turned its head at the smell of roast meat; it still flinched at a hall with two doors. Ede drove it upward because stopping was worse, and the tower, as if it knew, had given the grief a road exactly suited to it: a floor with no ground at all, where every soul went single file along the chains with a line clipped to a shackle and nothing below the boot but weather.

The chains had held floating isles once. The sky-mooring country of Men had anchored its islands to the mountain the way other folk moor barges, and the night of the Theft the isles had torn loose and drifted away into the dark, and the chains had hung empty ever since, humming, going nowhere. Chainrest’s folk had walked them for a thousand years regardless. A taut chain, a sound shackle. It was what they had instead of a country.

Vega walked, and listened, and learned the floor’s teeth. Link-rats ran the moss in packs and fouled the handholds; chain-ticks dropped from the high links, fat as fists. On the fourth day a harpy that had been a kite shoved a Frosthall spearman off the third great chain, and his line held, and the one who went to haul him up was cut instead — a wind-torn figure walking the links where no keeper walked, a severed safety-line knotted at its waist. A moss-farmer, Mera said, who fell in her grandmother’s time. They broke it over the gulf and it drained away into the cloud, and Mera spat after it, and then wept, because she had known the story of the man all her life and had never had to see him work.

Vega counted the crossing’s cost in her ledger of mendings. One spearman, cut and living. Two dray-goats, lost. One link gone quiet under the moss, found by its silence, re-pinned by her own hand. It was the first thing she had mended since the feast, and her hands shook when it rang true, and she was glad of the wind, because a forge-matron does not weep where her apprentices can see.

⁂

The Warden held the Windlass House, where the greatest chain came home to the mountain and the master mooring-brake stood taller than Vega herself.

Shacklewise had been a drake once, a link-roosting thing that slept coiled through the great iron. The tower had welded it into the windlass whole, wound a mooring-chain onto its tail, and set it to keep the brake it had slept beside. It fought the way the floor fought: it paid out chain and snapped it back, and a mile of iron road leapt like a whipped rope, and soldiers went off the links into the sky with their lines singing taut. For half a morning it kept three companies pinned in the anchor-towers, and the spans swayed worse with every heartbeat.

Ede’s answer was Vega. The forge-matron went out on the bucking chain alone, too heavy to be flicked loose, each step placed the way she placed ingots, and the drake’s tail cracked the iron under her and she rode it like a woman standing in a boat. All the while she was speaking the keepers’ word that Mera had taught her from the brake’s own carving — Moor, the art of the floor, the word that holds a thing immovable against wind and fall and force. She did not cast it at herself. She walked the whole screaming span and laid her hand on the windlass and cast it into the brake.

The chains stopped. All of them, floor-wide, one hum in a hundred voices, fast and still as the day they were forged. Shacklewise heaved against its own moored tail and could not pay out so much as a link, and the bowmen came out of the towers, and it ended. Broken, the welded thing sagged in the windlass like a chain off its sprocket, and something that had only ever been a drake slid loose of the iron and glided away down the wind, uncollared, free, small.

Vega stood by the brake a long time with her palm on the lever.

— A brake is a promise, — she said to Mera. — Whoever built this meant it to be kept. We will keep it.

Chainrest kept better than that. With the master brake set and every chain on the floor holding fast, the keepers ran out the slack chain to the one isle that had never drifted clear — snagged on its mooring a half-mile off the span, a hanging garden gone wild, tilting in the wind — and they hooked it, and rigged the capstans, and hauled their country home. Giants of the Forge-Court walked the capstan-bars beside windlass-crews of Men. It took three days. When the Drifted Isle came to rest against its anchor-stone the whole floor rang like a struck anvil, and the chain-keepers of Chainrest looked at solid ground floating in the sky, and did not know what to do with their hands.

That night the rising camped on grass, above the clouds, for the first time. And for the first time since the Frosthall, somebody sang. It was a chain-sounding song, not a soldier’s song, and half the army did not know the words. It listened. That was a beginning.

⁂

— It is a dead engine, — Ulfar said. — That offends me.

Her brother had found the isle’s heart by supper: a vault of lift- stones under the wild garden, the old buoyancy-craft of the sky-realm, cold now, cracked, spent the night of the Theft. He stood over them with a lamp, and Vega watched his face and saw the Forge-Court in it, the way you see weather coming over ice.

— It is a garden, — Vega said, — with a dead engine under it. The garden is the part that matters.

— The garden exists because of the engine. — Ulfar said it without heat. He never argued with heat; that was the worst of him. — Sister. The folk below us are sleeping on proof that the old world made things worth keeping. You would break the tower and let all its craft run out on the ground like blood. I would keep the craft and pour out only the cruelty. I do not think that makes me the wicked one.

— No, — Vega said slowly. — It makes you the tempted one. Those are different, and the second is worse, because it can vote.

He laughed, because they were family, and it was not finished between them, and both knew where it would finish: higher up, in the tower’s own country, where the engines were not dead. She thought of the feast, of how many arguments had ended that night unfinished forever, and let her brother laugh. She wrote in her ledger before she slept: lift-stones, cracked, nine. Query — what craft raises them. Mend the garden regardless.

Below, down the long chains, Berrin Or’s supply-trains came up the links by lamplight, crate after crate swayed across the gulf, bread and arrows and pitch arriving, as always, exactly when despair would have begun without them. The quartermaster himself came up with the last load, one arm still slung from the arrow he had taken at the feast, and the camp’s faces lifted at the sight of him as they lifted for no one else. He had brought, for no reason under heaven but kindness, a crate of seed-potatoes for a hanging garden nobody had known existed a week before.

— How, — Vega asked him, — did you know?

— I didn’t. — Berrin smiled tiredly and rubbed his bad shoulder. — But it stood to reason somebody up a tower would be missing a garden. Somebody always is.

It warmed her, like everyone else. That was what Berrin was for.

⁂

Three countries lay between the chains and the fleet, and the army was the better part of a month in them. It skirted the Reactor Scar along the cool rim, two days’ march of fused colorless glass ringing under the boots, drinking Grint Scarwatch’s palewater at every halt while the scar-warden walked ahead of the van reading a fire nobody could see by the prickle of his own skin. It crossed the Updrafts on Cole Kitegate’s re-rigged spanlines, a gorge-country that boomed, kite-pilots ferrying the baggage up the thermal columns while the ranks went hand over hand above a wind that wanted them. And it came up through the Ice Quarries walking canyon-floors of glass-clear blocks under Hune Hewnstone’s locked gantries, house-sized loads hanging dead still overhead, the quarry-master reading each block’s flaws by the light through it before he would let a company pass beneath.

Floor sixty-five was a fleet.

They came up the stair-lift into grey light and a forest, and the forest was masts — an entire sky-fleet that had lost its lift the night of the Theft and settled upright, hull-deep into the mountain’s flank, so that spar and shroud and rotted sail stood in ranks where a wood would have branch and leaf. The ground was a tangle of fallen rigging deeper than a giant is tall. The canopy was crosstrees. In between hung a thousand ropes, and mast-folk lived in the crowsnests and crossed their whole country hand over line, and the wind in the shrouds made the sound of a fleet forever about to sail.

— Don’t touch the floor, — said Weld Sparside, by way of welcome. The mast-warden was lean as a stay and barefoot, and he stood on a line thin as Vega’s finger as though it were a quay. — The tangle takes. Everything you want is up, or down in the Bilge, and the Bilge you go to only once, with me, on a day I choose.

The rising learned to rig. That was the campaign, more than any fight: freed sky-folk of Chainrest and Sparside teaching soldiers of nine peoples to walk a shroud, to trust a knot tied by their own cold hands, to fall correctly, which is to say onto a line and not through it. Vega, who could not walk the lines at all, went along the buried decks below with the shipwrights, and there Weld chose his day and took her down into the Bilge — the flooded hull-base where the sunken decks met the rock, black water standing over a drowned hold — and showed her, by one lamp, the flagship.

Her decks were whole. Her cabins were shut. In her admiral’s cabin lay the fleet’s muster-roll and her charts, and deeper, cradled below the waterline in a chamber sealed with wax and prayer, the thing Vega had climbed sixty-five floors to put her hands on without knowing it: a lift-cell, unbroken, asleep — the heart that had once held ten thousand tons of oak upon the air.

She read it the way she read iron, palms flat, eyes shut. No forge- mark of hers. Older. Kinder. A made thing with no cruelty in the making, and the first such she had touched since her own collar-steel had come back to shame her at the Warcamp cages.

— It can be woken, — she said, and her voice was not quite steady. — Nothing in it is broken. It is only unpaid. Craft like this is a debt, and lift is how the debt is honored.

— Then you’d best deal with the rig first, — Weld said dryly, and above them, through a hundred fathoms of tangle, something enormous began to haul on every rope on the floor at once.

Mastwrack fought from the tallest mainmast, a crowsnest drake welded into the trunk of it, wearing the whole fleet’s rigging for limbs. It did not come to them; it reeled them in. Shroud-lines whipped out of the dark and took soldiers up screaming into the canopy; spars came down the mast-lanes like felled pines. The army fought a forest of rope for a day and a night, cutting and never burning — Weld forbade all fire — while the topmen worked inward crosstree by crosstree. In the end the floor’s own word won it, Splice, the topmen’s art that commands rope: a hundred freed mast-folk speaking it together, line by line, until the Warden’s web answered them and not it, and it hung in its own stilled rigging, and the bows finished what obedience had begun. The rigging fell slack. The forest went quiet as a chapel. Somewhere under it a drake that remembered being a drake climbed out along a yard and flew.

They raised the lift-cell from the Bilge on the third morning, with chain-keepers’ capstans and topmen’s tackle and one giant standing hip-deep hauling like a windlass made flesh. They set it in the soundest hull of the wreck-fields, a slim sixty-foot skiff the mast- folk had kept caulked out of pure stubborn love, and Vega woke the cell with the fleet’s own charts open beside her, and the hull groaned, and lifted, and stood upon the air.

The Signal-Top was re-rigged by sunset. When its lamp was lit — the old muster-signal, ship to ship, that no lamp had answered in eighty years — the mast-folk came out of the whole forest canopy to look, and the light caught on the skiff riding at anchor above the wrecks, and Weld Sparside wept without once losing his footing on the line.

⁂

The bridge floors went by like beads told on a string, and every bead was a country: the Kennel Courts heard half a day before they were seen, tiered stone yards baying under the mews-yews; the Coolant Falls a mile-high cliff of stopped white river, its milk-light showing the pilgrims still standing in the curtain; the Silk Vaults cold and hushed for two days’ walking, aisle on aisle of neat white bundles; the Lightning Fields an ozone plain of verdigris poles under a ceiling that flashed and never rained. In the Kennel Courts the master horn sounded from the Mews-Tower and every ungentled pack on the floor heeled at last to its own name, and the rising marched out with hounds at its flanks and owls above its banners. At the Coolant Falls, Bruna Milkwater opened the source her people had kept a thousand years, and the frozen river thawed from the head down and roared again, giving up its pilgrim dead for burial. In the Silk Vaults the crews of Spoolgate read their ledgers by lamplight and cut free a vault’s worth of the wrapped-but-living, and some of the freed had been shelved so long they asked after kings dead an age. On the Lightning Fields the army crossed by the safe lanes under a sky that aimed, and Mera Copperpole threw the master switch in the Ground- Vault, and the harvested storm poured into the rising’s keeping instead of down upon its people. Four floors, four gate-towns, four keepers’ hands shaken; and behind the host, at every mooring, the shipwrights worked.

For that was the turn the sky-campaign made, quietly, while the army was busy grieving and climbing and learning knots. The wreck-fields gave hulls. The Bilge gave charts and the craft of lift. Chainrest gave anchor-chain steel and the word Moor; Sparside gave shroud-cable and the word Splice; the quarries below had given lenses and the Fields gave storm-copper for the cells. By the month’s end eleven skiffs rode at the chains above the Mast Forest, rigged, crewed, provisioned by Berrin’s trains — a navy. A small, patched, absurd navy, the first fleet the sky had carried since the Theft, and every plank of it mended rather than made.

Vega stood at the rail of the first skiff in the evening watch and looked up. Above the wreck-fields, above the Fields’ flashing sky, the next floor wore a crown of storm that never moved and never broke — the Tempest Court, where the seventh tier ended, where a queen the tower had crowned held her whole court captive inside a hurricane. The parliament would send an embassy; that was already decided. The storm did not look like a court that received embassies. It looked like weather with a will.

Everything broken can be mended, Vega told herself, palm on the rail, feeling the woken cell hum under the deck like a sound chain.

She wondered, watching the lightning stitch the queen’s walls, what it would cost to mend a storm — and whose price it would be.

Chapter 29 — The Storm Queen's Price

The wind hit Ede before the floor did.

The stair-lift opened onto a crag and a scream — not a voice, the air itself, tearing past the arch so hard it pulled the breath out of her mouth before she could spend it. Beyond the arch there was a country, or there had been: an open palace on the highest crag of a stolen sky-realm, towers worn round as river-stones, colonnades and perch- balconies wreathed in a storm that did not pass over the floor because it was the floor — a tempest a thousand years old, roaring in place, lightning stitching the cloud-decks below like a seam being sewn and resewn forever.

The Tempest Court, said Sliver against her palm, dry as always, old as always. I carried a climber here once before. He asked the wind to negotiate. The wind declined.

— We’re not asking the wind, — Ede said. — We’re asking the queen.

That is what he said.

They went roped, all of them, bent double against the gale along a groove in the rock worn by a thousand years of crawling courtiers. Cole Galesend came out to meet them at the first wind-shadow with storm-glass lanterns and a face like a man who had been apologizing for the weather his whole life. Court-steward, he named himself; his gate-town huddled in the lee of a broken colonnade, its people lashed to the stone by habit and by rope both.

— She knows you’ve come, — he shouted, mouth close to Ede’s ear. — She’s known since you took the chains. She stilled the lower gales for your climb. That’s her favor, general. Mind it. Everything she gives is an entry in a book, and she keeps the book.

— What was she, — Ede asked, — before?

The steward’s face did a thing that Ede had seen on many floors now, on the faces of many keepers: grief older than the griever. — Our queen. And a good one. She read weather like scripture and never lost a flyer. The tower crowned her into that, — he nodded at the storm, — and the crown is hers and the storm is hers and neither one is her. You’ll see. She’s in the eye. She’ll want to bargain. — He hesitated, rain running down his lashes. — General. She was a good queen. When you deal with her, remember you’re dealing with the crown.

The embassy crawled upward through the roar for half a day: Ede; Rhovan, hooded, eyes moving; Vega, the one body on the crag the wind could not argue with; Calyne, grey and silent; Ulfar Coalbrow for the Forge-Court; Voss for the purse; Nix, because Nix had flatly refused to be left, and the banner had to come, and he carried the banner. Berrin they had left below with the host and the stores, his arm still slung; he had loaded them down with wax-sealed bread for the court-captives — storm-soaked folk want dry bread, it stands to reason — and waved them up the crag like a man seeing family off at a gate.

Then the wind stopped.

They had reached the eye. It stood in the storm’s heart like a well of glass a hundred fathoms wide — air gone utterly still, gold light falling soft through the turning walls, a pale orchid blooming in the cracks of the paving, and the roar all around reduced to a hush like the sea in a shell. In the center of the stillness rose a dais of wind-worn stone.

On the dais was a throne, and on the throne sat the storm.

⁂

Zephyra had been a woman once; that was the cruelest part. The shape of it was still there — a queen seated straight-backed, robes moving in a wind that did not exist inside the eye, a face that had once read skies and steadied flyers and was still, under the tower’s work, beautiful the way a cliff is. Around her brow ran the storm- crown, and it was not a thing she wore. It went through her, the way the weld goes through every warden, and from it the whole thousand-year tempest unwound like thread off a spindle.

Her court clung to the perches at the eye’s rim, courtiers and weather-wrights lashed to stone, watching their queen with the particular stillness of people who love a thing that can kill them.

— The rising, — Zephyra said. Her voice was mild and enormous, a calm built over a gale the way her eye was. — Sixty-nine floors. Nine and sixty wardens, mine own outriders among them, and a fleet of patched hulls at your chains, and a charter, and a debt of graves. I have read you on the wind for two years, general. You are the most interesting weather to enter my sky in an age.

— Then you know what we’ve come to ask, — Ede said.

— No one asks me anything. — It was not a rebuke; it was a correction, made kindly, the way one corrects a child’s grammar. — They price things, or they leave. That is the whole of law, when you are a crown. Come. Price what you want of me.

— Passage. Your storm stands between my army and the eighth tier. Still your gales and let us climb.

— Small, — said Zephyra. — You disappoint me. Ask the larger thing, the one you have carried up my crag unspoken. You have hulls now, and charts, and the beginnings of sails. What you do not have is wind that loves you. — She leaned forward, and in the walls of the eye the lightning quickened. — I am offering to carry you.

Ede said nothing. The queen smiled and named her goods the way a merchant lays cloth on a table, bolt by bolt.

— My gales, harnessed, mine to command as this crown commands me. I will put your whole host upon the air — your skiffs, your giants, your goblin with his flag — and I will set you down at the gates of the Made in one day. One day, general. No Snare Woods. No Pale Court; the Huntsman himself does not course quarry down the throat of my wind. The season of dying that stands between you and the tower’s own country — I fold it shut like a fan. Every grave you would have dug between here and the iron, undug. You have buried, what — Ede felt the number in her own chest before the queen finished — eight hundred and forty, since the hall with two doors? Keep the rest. I have priced armies all my life. Yours is worth more than it knows.

— And your price, — Ede said, — for the sky?

— The throne, — said Zephyra, — his, — and for one breath the mildness opened, and what was under it was not rage; it was appetite with eighty years of patience, appetite worn smooth. — When your banners pull Vharuk down, you will not sit in his seat. You will not break it, either — do not pretend to me you know how. Someone will keep the tower’s crown, general, because a crown does not stop existing when it is refused; it only lies where anyone can pick it up. Let it be me. I kept a sky-realm well for forty years before the tower made me weather. I will keep this one better than the ten thousand fools who will otherwise spend a century killing each other for it. Wind is an honest ruler. It falls on all roofs alike. — She sat back. — That is the bargain. Carriage for a crown. Deliverance now, dominion after. I will even let you keep the word ‘rising.’ I ask only the top of it.

She has thought about this, Sliver said, very quietly, in Ede’s palm, for a very long time.

— The offer is not mine to answer, — Ede said. — We are a charter, not a captain. The banners vote.

— Of course they do. — The queen’s smile now was the worst thing on the floor, because it was genuinely fond. — I have watched your parliament walk up my sky trailing its arguments like a bride’s train. Vote, then. I give you until the lamps gutter. And general — mind the book. I have stilled my gales for your climb and received you in my calm and offered you your soldiers’ lives in a bundle. All entries. Whatever you answer, the book balances. It always balances.

⁂

The parliament met in the Weather-Hall, the court’s great open colonnade, under charts of winds that had not blown free in a thousand years. Fifty-eight banners; by the charter each spoke once and voted once. The court-captives lit the storm-glass lamps around them, and Zephyra, in courtesy or in cunning, kept the eye wide enough to hold them all in her stillness — so the rising’s parliament debated its soul in the one calm place on the floor, in the gift of the very power it was debating, and every voice that spoke knew she heard it.

Voss spoke first, and for the bargain, because someone honest about his own greed had to say it plainly. — I have priced the long road. Boots, bread, arrows, biers. One day of wind against a season of attrition is not a bargain, it is a rescue, and we would be mad to refuse it. Thrones are tomorrow’s problem. Corpses are today’s.

A keeper answered him — Mera Copperpole of the Lightning Fields, her voice still pitched to carry over storms. — I harvested the sky forty years. You do not take the storm’s gift and owe it nothing after. Ask my poles. Ask my dead.

Then the freed. A woman of the Welded Halls’ feeding-cages, sold at some floor Ede would never learn, stood up with her hands shaking and said only: — We were all of us carried somewhere fast, once, by something that had priced us, — and sat down, and the hall was quiet for a long time.

The Wake voted no; Calyne gave no reasons, which was her way, and it won her no love, which was also her way. The keepers split. The Tollmen went yes for the purse; the mast-folk and chain-keepers, sky- folk who knew exactly what wind was worth, went no almost as one, and old Mera Chainrest said the floor’s whole creed in six words: — A thing carried is a thing dropped.

And Ulfar Coalbrow rose last, and Ede watched Vega not look at him.

— I will be the honest one, — Ulfar said, slow and finished, like all his people. — I do not fear her crown. I fear the graves. The Reactor’s country is ahead of us, and the Welding Halls, and floors that make this storm look like a kind aunt, and every one of them will eat our people by the hundred while we walk. She offers to lift us over the worst of the dying, and asks in exchange for a thing that does not yet exist — the governance of a tower we have not taken. Sell it. Sell the bear’s skin before the bear is dead; if the price is delivered first, only a fool refuses. And when the tower falls and she comes to collect — she will find that what the Forge-Court keeps, it keeps. — He looked at his sister then, only then. — I vote to fly.

The tellers counted. Twenty-seven for the queen’s bargain. Twenty- seven against. Four banners would not vote at all, their captains sitting with their hands flat on their knees like men at a funeral.

Fifty-eight voices, and the general held the fifty-ninth. The charter said so; Ede had signed it in a hall that burned the same week. She stood a long moment in the queen’s own stillness with the casting voice in her mouth like a stone.

She thought of the day’s ride against the season’s graves, and she was a general, and the arithmetic was real, and it pulled at her with eight hundred and forty hands. And then she thought of a ledger-page Rhovan had put before her in Roothollow, two years and sixty-nine floors ago. Her company had been carried somewhere fast, once, by something that had priced it. The Warcamp had not been a defeat. It had been a sale. Everything since — every grave, every banner, every name cut on the Stone of the Climb — had grown from one entry in one book where living people stood written as goods.

The rising had not climbed two years to found the next book.

— The vote of the general, — Ede said, — is no. We do not pull down one throne to raise another. We are not a ladder for crowns. We will walk, and we will bury too many, and it will be the long way, and it will be ours — every floor of it freed, not flown over and left locked under someone new. — She turned to face the dais, far off through the gold light, where a shape sat unmoving with the storm threaded through its brow. — Tell your book, Majesty: refused, with thanks, twenty-eight voices to twenty-seven. We’ll take the sky the slow way. Nobody will hold it over us after.

⁂

For three breaths the eye stayed gold and still, and Ede allowed herself to believe the book of favors would balance gently.

— Thanks, — said Zephyra, from the dais, without rising, — do not appear in my ledger. There is no column for them.

The stillness died.

It did not fade; it was withdrawn, the way a merchant takes back cloth that will not sell, and the roar came down on the Weather-Hall like a hall collapsing. Through it, terribly, the queen’s voice stayed mild, stayed reasonable, threaded into the wind itself so that there was no place on the floor where it could not be heard keeping its accounts.

— You have had of me a stilled climb, a royal audience, and the only honest offer this tower has made you. Price of a stilled climb: a gale. Price of an audience: a gale. Price of an army’s insolence, general, priced as armies are priced — by the third part.

— And enter this in your own book, general, against the day you will want it: the Court does not refuse custom. When the wind profits, you shall have wind. My ledger keeps to the letter. You will learn, in time, what that is worth.

Below the court, deep in the crag, in the Windward Undercroft where a realm’s caught weather had been cellared in storm-glass against foul days for a thousand years, the shelves began to break.

Ede heard it through the stone, a chiming, like a toast in a hall of glasses, and then the bottled gales came up the stairways of the palace and out of the cellar-mouths — storms with no sky to spend themselves in, aether-quick, half-shaped, screaming — and the queen opened her hand and spent them.

The eye slammed shut. Ede went off her feet and into a colonnade; Vega caught her out of the air like a sack of meal and put her back against the stone and held her there, one arm each for her and for Nix, who had wrapped himself around the banner-staff and was shouting numbers no one could hear. Rope after rope of Cole Galesend’s crawling-lines came away from the crag. And down the long slopes, where the host lay camped by companies along the wind-shadows of the tier — down where the army was — the loosed gales fell upon the rising like hawks into a poultry-yard.

She saw it in flashes, between lightning and streaming rain: tents going up entire like blown leaves; a supply-train of Berrin’s lifted off a ridge, crates and oxen and drovers, and scattered gently, at leisure, across three leagues of sky; a whole regiment of the freed, lashed in march-order to a running-line, hauled off a cloud-ridge and out into the white and gone — like seed, she thought, numb, exactly like a sower flinging seed, broadcast across the wind floors above, the aeries and the ramparts and the wire-woods and the black glass forest, wherever the queen’s gales tired of carrying them.

It lasted the time a lamp takes to gutter. Then the loosed storms blew themselves ragged and thin and drained away down the tower’s sky, and the tempest closed back over its court like water over a stone, and it was only weather again — and the slopes below were swept clean of a third of everything Ede had spent two years gathering.

She kept the two parts, Sliver said at last, into the ringing silence of Ede’s skull. She is a queen of her word. That is the horror of her.

Ede stood up out of Vega’s arm. Far off through the storm-wall the dais showed in lightning, once, the seated shape upon it straight- backed and unmoved, a good queen keeping her book. Nix was already counting — alive, alive, alive, gone — his voice cracking on the fourth word every fourth time. Calyne was gray as her cloak. Somewhere below, scattered across seven floors of screaming sky, in snare-woods and egg-vaults and the runs of a court that hunted, the third part of the rising was landing alone.

— Sound the muster-horns, — Ede said. Her voice came out level, and she would pay for that levelness later, in the dark, alone; a general always did. — Every skiff aloft. Every chain manned. We go and get them back.

— All of them? — someone asked — meaning it kindly, meaning it cannot be done.

— Every name on the roll, — said Ede Stonhal, and walked into the wind. The gathering had begun.

Chapter 30 — Gathering the Blown

Rhovan had spent a life finding things that did not want to be found, and he had never hated the work until the things were his own.

The queen’s gales had sown the third part of the rising across seven floors of sky, and the sky’s floors were not kind ground. A company of freed kobolds came down whole in the Char Forest and lit no fire for four nights among the black glass trees, because Grint Stumpwell’s burners found them first and taught them which trunks still held a live coal. Two hundred of the Frosthall levy landed on the ledges of the Drake Aeries, among hoards, among drakes, and had the wit to touch no coin; the drake-keepers of Scalewatch talked the aeries calm over them, ledge by ledge, an old truce spoken in signals across the fever of a thousand wings. A supply-train fetched up against the Wind Walls and its drovers lived a week in a giant’s casemate on Berrin’s wax- sealed bread — the quartermaster’s crates had scattered as far as his people, and, being his, were packed to survive it.

Others fared as the wind willed. The Jarl’s Road took thirty-one of the blown into its ghost-march before anyone understood the road itself was hungry — swept them into a column that had been tramping toward a fallen feast for a thousand years — and Hune Horncall walked the verges for six days pulling the living out of the dead procession one man at a time. In the Egg Galleries the watchers of Palewatch cut nine climbers free of the racks; four of the cocoons they opened too late. And on the seventy-fourth floor, in the wire-seeded quiet of an elf-wood gone all to snares, a serjeant of Shieldwall gathered forty survivors and, quite correctly, refused to let them take a single step, and sat in the leaf-litter for nine days, waiting to be found by someone who could read a cocked wood.

Rhovan was who found them. That was the shape of his war, that season: down a chain, along a rampart, into the wire, whistling the old Rides recall-notes into country that answered with crows. Above him Vega’s eleven skiffs quartered the sky like patient hawks, lamps burning the fleet’s muster-signal, lifting the stranded off ledges no stair could reach; below him the keepers of seven gate- towns walked their own deadly floors, gathering strangers as if the strangers were their own lost isles. He kept the tallies in his head, where he kept everything. Found, four hundred and six. Found, six hundred and sixty. Found, nine hundred and one — and every evening he brought the numbers to the general’s lamp and watched Ede Stonhal take them the way a woman takes lashes she has agreed to.

She had stopped sleeping; anyone could see it. She heard each day’s count standing, then walked the recall-lines half the night, learning the name of every soldier the wind had given back and, worse, the name of every one it had not. The muster-roll she had rebuilt from memory after the feast rode in her breast like a second heart, and she was crossing names onto it and off it in her own hand, hour on hour, as if the ink itself were owed. Rhovan, who told the truth in fractions even to himself, permitted himself a whole one, privately: he had watched a queen offer this woman the sky for a crown she did not want, and it had cost her nothing to refuse; and he was watching the refusal’s price come due one name at a time, and that was costing her everything, and she paid it every evening at the lamp without once letting the line move behind her.

An army does not watch its general count the lost, night after night, and stay a stranger to her. The blown came back changed. They came back hers.

It was, all reckoned, Ede’s worst hour as a general, and the finest he ever saw her.

⁂

The Wake chose that hour to present its bill.

The council met in the Sheltered Casemate of the Wind Walls, the one wind-proof room on a floor of screaming ramparts — giants’ work, deep in the greatest wall, warm with lamps and crowded with the charter’s remnant: Ede grey with waking; Vega; Ulfar; Voss with his ledgers; two keepers; Nix in the corner on a crate, holding the banner and saying nothing, which in Nix was a mark of the times. And Calyne.

She stood when the tallies were done, grey as ever, precise as ever, and laid a strip of birch-paper on the table as though it were a blade.

— The Wake’s council has met, — she said. — Below this tier lie the lamp-woods of the Elves. The Kennel Courts you freed in passing. The Snare Woods and the Quarry Runs you now propose to cross — to walk through our stolen realm, wire and coursing-lanes and the hanging dead of the Gibbet Glade, and leave it as you found it, because the Made is calling and your charter is in a hurry. The Wake has climbed beside you since Roothollow. Its bows are on your walls tonight. Its couriers carry your muster-calls. Here is its price for remaining: the elf-woods detour. Now. The host turns, gentles, and holds the lamp-wood floors — all of them — before it climbs a step higher. The woods are cut down from our country, general. We are done watching every people’s realm freed but ours because ours is merely on the way.

Silence took the casemate. Rhovan, by the door, felt the old handler’s arithmetic assemble itself in him, smooth from long use: what the Wake wanted, what it would settle for, which fraction of the truth would move which vote. He had run that arithmetic for Calyne for twenty years.

— How long? — Ede asked. Flat.

— Three weeks, — Calyne said. — Perhaps five. The woods are not gentle work.

— I still have eleven hundred people on the wind floors, — Ede said. — On ledges. In wire. In the racks of a hatchery. Every skiff, every courier, every bow on this tier is a hand reaching for them, and you are asking me to take a third of those hands and send them to hold ground for five weeks.

— I am not asking, — said Calyne. — I am pricing. You have had a season of the Wake’s silver and the Wake’s arrows. Everything is bought, general; you learned that at the Warcamp. The only question is who pays, and when, and the Wake answers: its debt is due now, or its service ends tonight. — She did not raise her voice. She never raised her voice. — Refuse, and the bows come off your walls by morning.

And Rhovan of the Grey Rides heard himself say, from the door: — No.

⁂

He had not planned it. He wanted that understood, later, by the two or three souls he ever told it whole: he, who had planned everything since boyhood, who had walked into Roothollow with a sealed ledger- page and a blackmailer’s patience, did the one unplanned thing of his life in a giant’s casemate on the seventy-third floor, and it felt like stepping off a rampart into the wind.

Calyne turned. Everyone turned.

— Agent, — she said, and the word had twenty years of leash in it.

— The woods will keep. — His own voice sounded strange to him: it was not offering fractions. — I have served the Wake since before this army had a name, and I tell this council what every elf at this table knows and will not say: the lamp-woods have stood cut and stolen for eighty years. They grieve, and they wait, and they will still be grieving and waiting in the spring, because that is what woods do. The blown will not. The wire is at their throats tonight. The racks are quickening tonight. — He came forward into the lamplight, and laid his bow on the table beside her strip of birch-paper, gently, like a man laying down an argument. — You taught me the trade, mistress. You taught me a debt called at the worst hour is not a debt, it is a knife. This is a knife. The Wake is holding it to the throat of the only rising that will ever free the lamp-woods at all, and I will not carry the message, and I will not draw for it. Not now. The answer is the gathering first. The woods after — and I will walk into the wire at the head of that campaign myself, when the last name on the general’s roll is found. That is my price, and I have never once named one before.

— Your price. — Calyne looked at him for a long moment, and something moved behind her eyes that he could not read — he, who read everyone; it looked, absurdly, like a woman re-checking a sum that had come out wrong in a column she had trusted. Then it closed. — The Wake does not receive prices from its instruments. It bought you a name when you were nothing: Rhovan of the Grey Rides — the Rides are ours, agent, the name is livery, and livery comes off. Speak one word more and you speak it as no one.

— Then take it back, — said Rhovan, — and hear the answer from what is left: no.

He took the token from against his skin, where it had lain since he was seventeen — a thin disc of grey heart-elm, smooth as a knuckle, carved with the closed eye of the Wake — and he broke it over his knee, and the crack of it was very loud in the stone room, and he set the two halves on the table in front of his handler with the grain showing pale where it had never seen light.

— The bows will stay or go as the Wake pleases, — he said to the council, nameless, feeling the name go, feeling — he would tell Ede this much, one evening much later — feeling lighter, and colder, as if a hand had been let go at a great height. — I stay.

Calyne gathered the halves of the token with the strip of birch- paper, unhurried, exact, and left the casemate without a word — and the Wake’s bows did not come off the walls by morning. That was her answer, whatever it meant; he never learned what she wrote to her council, or what it cost her, or whether the sum she had re-checked in that long look had come out wrong in his favor. She was one floor ahead of everyone all her life. He mourned that too, later.

⁂

Ede found him at the rampart-stairs after, in the wind-shadow, and stood beside him awhile without speaking, which was one of her ways of speaking.

— You gave up your name, — she said at last.

— I gave up livery. — A fraction; he caught it leaving his mouth, old habit, and pushed the rest out after it, whole. — No. I gave up my name, and it hurts more than I expected, and I would do it again before supper. Put it in the roll however the roll wants me.

— The roll, — said Ede, — has you as Rhovan of the rising. It has had you that way, — she added, already turning back to the lamps and the lists, — since the Broodthrone. You’re the last to know.

The gathering took eleven more days. The last company came off the Quarry Runs’ border on a skiff flying Dun Harrow’s old signal, and Ede stood at the tally-lamp and crossed the final found name onto the roll, and the count of the never-found was three hundred and twelve, and she read every one of them aloud to the assembled banners in the wind, which took from moonrise until the fourth watch, and no one left.

Then the scouts came in from above with the thing Rhovan had already smelled on the wind: the tier ahead narrowing, the tempest bending strangely around its own heart, and past the roar — so said the freed courtiers of Zephyra’s court, who knew this sky — a stillness. The Eye Wall. A calm at the storm’s center, gold and windless, wide enough to rest an army in.

The host needed rest the way the drowning need air. It would follow by stages, regiments and skiffs, three days behind. The leadership would go ahead — Ede, Vega, Nix and the banner, and a nameless elf who was the best pathfinder alive on this tier — to read the calm before trusting an army to it.

They went up toward the stillness together, the four of them, ahead of everything they loved. It seemed, at the time, merely sensible.

The wood above was already watching the gap between the shepherds and the flock.

Chapter 31 — The Bottled Court

The wind’s arithmetic came out wrong on the ninth evening of the gathering, and it was Rhovan who caught the error, because catching what the world tried to keep was the whole of his use now that his name was gone.

He stood at the general’s lamp with the day’s count and did not read it out. Ede looked up from the roll. She had learned his silences the way she learned ground.

— Say it.

— A company of the Frosthall levy. Four-and-eighty by the roll, camped in the wind-shadow under the south face of the crag when the queen opened her cellars. — He set the tally-slip down. — Nothing above has seen them. Not the burners, not the drake-keepers, not the Walls, not the wire. Nine days, and the wind floors have given back some part of everything they took; of these four-and-eighty, not a boot. The wind is a bad liar, Ede. Whatever it takes, it puts down, and the putting-down is loud. Eighty people are not quiet anywhere. Unless they never went up at all.
