It was a freed perch-squire who closed the sum — Pye Galesend, a boy of the court’s perches whose collar Vega had broken at the crag’s foot on the embassy’s first climb. He heard the question at the lamp and answered it the way squires answer, terrified and exact.

— The south face is all cellar-mouths, begging it. When Her Majesty spent the vaults, the doors stood open after. — He swallowed. — You go to ground, don’t you. When the sky is the thing that’s wrong, you go down.

Ede looked at the roll a long moment. Eleven hundred names still stood uncrossed on it, strung across seven screaming floors, and Rhovan watched her do what she did every evening of that worst season: refuse to price one name against another.

— The roll has room, — she said. — Go down and get them.

⁂

They went down the next morning on one of Vega’s skiffs, through three floors of gale to the Tempest Court’s lee — Rhovan; Vega, who left her fleet to her mates because doors below ground were her trade before sails were; Pye, who knew the mouths; a file of six Shieldwall with rope and biers. At the crag’s foot, waiting on the crawling- lines with storm-glass lanterns, stood Cole Galesend, court-steward, keeper of the one floor the rising had climbed past unfreed.

— You came back, — he shouted, mouth close, in the roar. — Nobody comes back to this floor. I’d have bet the weather on it.

— We mislaid a company. In your cellars, we think.

The steward’s face did its lifelong work of apologizing for the sky, and he led them along the groove in the rock, bent double, to a mouth in the south face where broken storm-glass lay drifted like frost against the hinges of a door built for wagons.

One step, roar. The next step, nothing.

Rhovan had stood in the quiet of caverns, of snowfields, of a silk vault where a hundred people hung sleeping — and the stillness of the Windward Undercroft was like none of them, because all of those quiets had been alive. This was a dead calm. The air stood in the galleries the way water stands in a mine, without weather, without breath, older than any lung that had ever borrowed it; his own blood was suddenly the loudest thing on the floor. Lantern-light went out and out and found no wall — only shelf-rows running away into the dark like hedges in a plain, ten men high, built to cellar a realm’s worth of caught weather against foul days, and empty now, rank on rank, the glass of a thousand years’ husbandry lying spent and shattered on the flags where its own queen had broken it.

— Hood your lamps, and keep your voices under the shelf-line, — Cole said, low. — Not all the glass broke clean. There’s gales down here with a crack in the bottle and half a storm still in them, sleeping. Quick air wakes them. Walk like you’re walking a sickroom.

— And the wind can’t follow us in, — Vega said. It was not a question; a smith knows a made thing when she stands inside one.

— Nothing of hers comes down here. That’s the whole craft of the place, mistress. It was built to keep weather in. It keeps it out the same way. — He lifted his lantern, and grief older than the griever moved across his face and settled. — Her own cellars, and she’s never once stood in them. Remember that, when you’re wanting to hate her.

They found the company by chalk.

Serjeant Hesk of the levy had been a quarrywoman before she was a soldier, and she had done what quarry-folk do in the dark: tallied every gallery-mouth, chalked the turnings, and then stopped moving entirely, having worked out, correctly, that the vaults ran on for lightless miles and the way out could not be guessed. Nine days her people had sat in a foul-day store off the third gallery, on the court’s thousand-year biscuit and a smashed supply-crate the wind had posted down the mouth ahead of them — wax-sealed bread, of all the cargo on that ridge, and Rhovan noted the fact and filed it where he filed everything. Eighty-one stood up into the lantern- light when Pye’s whistle came down the gallery. Three had come down onto the flags too hard the first night, and lay cairned in broken storm-glass, named, waiting to be carried home.

— Knew someone would read the chalk, — Hesk said. Nine days under the shelf-line had ground her voice low and flat, a cellar voice. — Knew it’d be an elf or a goblin. No offense meant.

— None held. The goblin was busy.

It should have ended there: a found company, a good day in a bad season, eighty-one names to cross at the lamp. But Pye led them out by the farther stair, the squires’ stair — and it ran past a door that Cole Galesend stopped in front of as if he had walked into a wall.

⁂

It was iron, and it was black, and it was none of the court’s work. The Undercroft’s doors were wagon-wide timber, built by cellarers for cellarers. This was the other iron — the tower’s own, anchor- iron, seamed and riveted, set into the living rock with a fine contempt for the stone around it — and across its face, untouched by the loosing that had emptied every shelf behind them, ran seals of grey warden-wax stamped with the mark of the King.

— I have kept the lore of this cellar my whole life, — Cole said, quietly, to nobody, — and my mother kept it, and hers. There is no door here.

— There is a door everywhere the owner wants one. — Rhovan was reading the wax. The stamps ran in a clerk’s fair column down the seam, each with its date-mark, and the first of the dates stood within living grief of the Theft itself. — This was cut when the crown was. Whatever it keeps, it has kept from the start.

The thing at the desk inside had been keeping it too.

Vega opened the seam the way she had opened cage-locks in a mine- dark camp fifty floors below — hands, wedge, and a patience iron mistakes for kindness until too late — and the lanterns went in and found a vault cut like a chapel, and at its head a welded thing seated at a stone desk, pen still in its grip, mid-entry in a ledger whose pages had been filled for eighty years with a single line: no change. It rose when the light touched it — a cellarer once, or a clerk; latch and ledger now, wax and iron — and came at them in silence with the pen still in its fist. It was the gentlest eviction of the climb. Vega took its arms, the file its legs, Rhovan cut the aether-seam where the weld ran shallow, and the whole construct sighed apart on the flags — evicted, done — and nobody moved, because the lanterns had reached the shelves.

Storm-glass, whole. Rank upon rank of it, the only unbroken glass on the floor — great sealed vessels a forearm high, corked in lead and warden-wax, each with a paper label hung at its neck in that same fair hand. And inside each one, small and patient and alive, a storm. A snow-squall turning in its jar like a held breath. A summer thunderhead the size of two fists, flickering. A grey soft rain, falling forever inside a bottle, on nothing. They were beautiful, and they were wrong in a way the body knew before the mind did, and it was Pye — court-born, storm-born — who said it, in a whisper, from the door:

— Those are people.

The manifest lay on the custodian’s desk, and Rhovan read it aloud, because he had read other people’s ledgers all his life and someone owed these entries a voice.

Received: of the court of the crown of storms, one hundred and nine, being her weather-wrights, her flyers, her readers of the sky, and the officers of her first household. Bottled by the King’s wardens in the manner of the court’s own craft. To be held in the still cellar, where the crown does not reach. Held against the conduct of the crown. In the event of default: spend one.

Eleven of the shelves’ places stood empty. The dates of the spendings ran down the margin, eighty years of them, none within the last forty. The crown had learned.

— She was a good queen, — Cole Galesend said. He had his hat off; on his floor a bare head was something you paid the wind. — Everyone says it, and nobody asks what the tower does with a good queen. A bad one you crown and let run. A good one loves something. — He gestured, hand shaking, at the shelves, at the labels, at the small turning storms. — There’s the surety. There’s her eighty years of conduct. We thought the weld held her. The weld only steers her. This held her — under her own throne, in the one room in her realm the wind has never once been allowed to enter, and she has sat up there being good above it since before my house had a name.

— The bottles can be opened? — Vega asked. — Say it plain, steward.

— Opened, aye. Read backward, poured out into open sky, they come home to themselves; it’s their own craft, it was never meant for this. — He looked at Rhovan then with a keeper’s look, the terrible hope that arrives after the hope is over. — But hear me. This is the King’s vault, and her leash, and if you spend it the tower has lost its hold on the seventieth floor, and what she does then I cannot tell you. Nobody has ever been able to tell you what she does. That’s what a storm is.

Rhovan stood in the still cellar among a hundred shelved people and thought, with the part of him that had been a Wake man for twenty years, that his old faction would have priced this vault in a heartbeat: a queen’s whole heart in glass, leverage beyond the dreams of handlers. You do not spend such a thing. You hold it. He heard the thought all the way through in Calyne’s voice.

Then he sent it down the stair with the rest of his livery.

— Word goes up to the lamp tonight, — he said. — But I can tell you now what the lamp will say, because I have stood at it every evening of this season. It will say the rising does not hold people against anyone’s conduct. It will say the roll has room. — He took the manifest off the desk and folded it flat. — We came down for four-and-eighty. We carry out a hundred and eighty-two.

⁂

It took two days.

There was no craft to hurry it: each glass came off its shelf into two hands and went up the squires’ stair from hand to hand, a chain of soldiers standing on every third step in the dark, the rescued of the Frosthall levy carrying the shelved of the Tempest Court, lanterns hooded, voices under the shelf-line, the little storms turning patiently in their jars as they rose through the black country of their captivity toward the roar. Ninety-eight glasses whole; and the eleven empty bottles too, carried up with the same two hands apiece, because Cole would not leave them, and a court mourns by count.

At the south mouth, in the skirts of the gale, Cole Galesend read the lore backward one label at a time.

He spoke each name into the wind first — office and name, the Reader of the South Sky, the mistress of the mews, the wind-marshal of the first household — then broke the wax and poured, and what came out of each glass went up the storm like a hawk off a wrist: a squall unfolding into the gale, small, then not small, then vast and shapeless and shaped, riding its own thunder up the crag toward the court above. Not one of them turned. Not one paused, or dipped a wing of rain to the soldiers on the rock, or gave any sign a man could read as thanks. Country by country the rising had freed the grateful and the grieving and the ashamed; it had never yet freed weather, and weather owes nothing, and goes home.

Four of the ninety-eight poured out as plain rain, and fell on the stone, and did not rise. Too long shelved. Cole spoke their names into the wind anyway, the same as the rest, and the Shieldwall file stood bare-headed in the falling of them until the stone was dry.

The last glass was rain too, but living rain — it went up in a grey spiral, spun once around the whole party as if reading the tally chalked on their shoulders, and vanished into the storm-wall. And then, for the space of five heartbeats, the Tempest Court went quiet.

Not calm. Nothing so kind. The roar simply stood off them, the way a crowd stands back from a thing it has decided to look at, and down through the standing quiet came the queen’s voice, mild and enormous, threaded along the wind so that it arrived less as sound than as pressure on the small bones of the ear.

— Who prices this?

— No one, — Rhovan said, into the sky. — It was not for sale.

The quiet held one breath longer — one breath, on a crag where the wind had not rested in a thousand years — and Rhovan understood that he was hearing a ledger fail to balance for the first time in its keeper’s reign. Then the storm took its roar back and went up the sky, and the audience, if it had been one, was over.

⁂

They came up to the muster-lines on the third evening with eighty-one found names for the roll, three for the graves, and a fact for the general that fit in no column at all. Ede heard it standing, read the manifest twice, and looked south down the tier toward the storm on its crag for a while.

— She’ll hate it, — she said at last. — Owing us.

— She doesn’t owe us. Nothing was asked; nothing was priced; there’s no entry for her to pay against. To a crown that keeps a book the way she keeps hers, that isn’t a debt. It’s worse. It’s a thing in the book that will not close. — He thought of the eleven empty bottles, and the four rains that had not risen. — She’ll carry it up and down her sky until she finds a way to spend it that we never asked for and cannot refuse, at an hour of her own choosing, priced to the last breath. That’s the only mercy queens like her are built for. I wouldn’t stand under it.

Within the week the freed of the bottled court were on the muster- lines — weather-wrights and sky-readers who owed the rising nothing and paid it nothing, and taught its skiff-crews the winds of the seven floors above with a cold thoroughness that saved lives daily and was never once called a favor. It was one of them who first spoke to the scouts of a stillness past the roar of the tier ahead — a calm at the storm’s heart, gold and windless, wide enough to rest an army in.

The gathering went on. The roll filled, name by name. And far below, under a court of gales, in a black vault standing open and empty behind a broken door, a stone desk kept its last entry, pen laid down mid-line, in a fair clerk’s hand, in the ledger of eighty years of no change:

change.

Chapter 32 — The Eye

Nix heard the silence before he saw it, which he would have told you was impossible, wrong, and exactly what happened.

For three floors the world had been roar — wind like a mill, wind like a war, wind you chewed before you breathed it — and then the four of them came through a seam in the eye-wall behind a freed courtier who knew the way, and the roar did not fade. It stopped. One step, roar. Next step, nothing. His ears rang, and he counted his own heartbeats, one, two, three, because when the world did something impossible you counted what you could.

The eye of the great storm was a bowl of gold light, wide as a day’s walk. The rain did not fall in it; the rain hung — beads of bright water strung on nothing, close as barley in a field, and when Nix put out his hand and touched one it broke on his finger, warm, and did not wet the ones beside it. Still trees stood in ranks, not one leaf turning. Hush-grass grew silver over everything, and no wind had bent it in eighty years.

It was the most beautiful place he had seen in seventy-eight floors, and everything in him that had lived four years under a collar stood up in his skull and said, small friend, this is a trap.

— A trap, yes, — said the woman who came out of the trees, cheerful, as if agreeing with a thing he’d said aloud. She was grey and brisk and small for one of Men, with a rope of little copper bells across her chest that made no sound at all when she walked. — The calm is the trap. Out in the wall the wind kills you in a breath, and in here nothing kills you at all. You just sit down. Rest your eyes. And never get up again. Mera Lullside; I keep the eye. You’d be the rising, then. — She looked them over: the general grey as ash, the giant, the nameless elf, the goblin with a banner twice his height. — Four of you. Ahead of the whole host. Brave, that. The eye takes the tired first, and I never in my life saw anyone tireder.

She gave them the rules on the walk to Lullside, and Nix counted those too, because they were rules you counted or died of. One: no one sits alone. Two: hands busy, always — the eye can’t lull a hand that’s working. Three: mark the lull-poppies, pale in the hush- grass, and step wide; what you smell in them is your own funeral, made comfortable. Four: the Sharpwake draught, morning and evening, bitter as a debt — Nix drank his and his whole face mutinied, and Mera said the bitterness was the mercy; sweetness you’d stop noticing.

At Lullside her folk lived close to the eye-wall on purpose, in the skirts of the roar, using the noise the way other towns used walls. They were weather-readers and rain-wrights, who had fought the drowse by habit since the Theft, and they took the advance- party into the Barometer Vault — the one built place the lull could not reach, cool stone and a great storm-glass taller than Vega — and showed Ede the eye-wall’s turning, and the one safe seam by which an army could be fed into the calm, stage by stage, three days hence.

— They can rest here, — Mera said, and tapped the storm-glass. — I mean that two ways, general. It’s the only rest on this tier. And it wants to be the last one. We’ll walk your camps every hour. You’ll hate my bells before you leave. It’ll mean you’re alive.

⁂

The army came through the seam by regiments, and the eye did to every one of them what it had done to Nix: stopped their ears with kindness and buckled their knees.

Nix watched it happen from the top of a supply-crate, keeping his tally. He had appointed himself to the work because rule two was hands busy, and no work of his mattered more. Two years he had counted this army — up chains, across ice, out of cages — and what came through the eye-wall those three days had the walk of an army at the very end of its rope. Blown, gathered, marched, grieved. Soldiers stepped into the gold light and wept without stopping walking. A whole company of freed kobolds lay down in the hush- grass in march-order, in one motion, like grass going down under a scythe, and had to be walked awake again by Mera’s people, gently, with bells.

Ede put the camps where Lullside said: rings within rings, every fire double-watched, the standards planted at the center where the suspended rain caught the light and made a slow, drifting crown of bright beads above the banners. The rising slept in a circle of standing rain. And for three days, for the first time since the Frosthall, nobody died.

The eye had its price all the same; it showed them on the second day what sitting down cost. Off the camps’ western ring lay a garden — the Sitting Garden, Mera called it, and walked them through it herself, one hand on her bells — where the calm’s first captives had sat down to rest eighty years ago and never risen. They were still there. Folk of the old sky-realm, seated in the silver grass among the hanging rain, breathing. Their clothes had gone to moss and their hair to lichen and they breathed, one breath to the army’s hundred, their faces peaceful the way carvings are peaceful, and pale drifting figures moved among them like hosts among guests — pressure-ghosts, the seated who had finally stopped even breathing, welcoming the living to lie down beside them.

And at the garden’s edge stood a courtier.

Nix knew the collar before he knew the man — you always knew the collar first; it was the point of collars. One of Zephyra’s own court, first-taken, silk gone to rags, standing in the hush-grass with a serene and terrible courtesy, and as they passed he turned to Nix, of all of them to Nix, and bowed, and gestured to the grass with a hand like a dry leaf: sit, small master. Rest. You have carried that banner such a long way.

The banner-staff was in Nix’s hands. Dun Harrow had carried it up forty floors with one arm, and since the feast Nix had carried it because no one else could bear to, and it was twice his height and he had never once put it down where anyone could see.

— Can’t, — Nix told the Pressed man, hoarse. — It doesn’t sit, see. Flags don’t. It’s the one thing they’re for, staying up. I just hold on and get carried, me.

They broke the man’s collar that evening, Vega’s hands and a chisel of anchor-chain steel — and the freed courtier fell out of his serenity like a man dropped from a height, and wept, and could say only that the eye was quiet, so quiet, he had been sent to it for quiet — and then he slept, truly slept, guarded, with a bell by his head. Nix sat by him the first watch, holding the banner upright, counting the man’s breaths as they slowed from a courtier’s to a sleeper’s. Nineteen to the hundred. Twenty. Rule one: no one sits alone.

⁂

They buried the dead on the third day.

The gathering had given back eleven hundred living and, at the last, forty-one bodies, carried up the tier on the skiffs because Ede would not leave them to the wind floors; and there were the never-found besides, three hundred and twelve names with nothing under them at all. Lullside offered the ground: nothing rots in the eye, Mera said; nothing is troubled; the hush-grass closes over a grave like a hand over a coin, and keeps it.

So the rising dug in the silver grass, in the gold light, among the hanging rain. It dug forty-one graves and three hundred and twelve small pits the size of a helm, one for each name with no body, and into each pit went something — a bowstring, a die, a spoon, a plait of chain-moss, whatever the dead one’s mess-mates had kept. The peoples made their rites in their own tongues, nine kinds of grief in one garden, and the eye hushed all of them equally, and the suspended rain hung over the graves like the sky holding its breath.

It was Nix who started the song. He did not decide to; his mouth did.

Dun’s muster-song. Every soldier of Shieldwall had known it, and every soldier of the rising had learned it since, in the cages or on the ice or walking the chains — the counting-song the one-armed standard-bearer had drummed on his own staff at every muster from floor twenty to floor sixty, plain as bread, older than anyone knew:

One for the gate-post, two for the wall, three for the shield that shoulders them all; count off, count off, and answer the stone — nobody musters alone.

His voice cracked on the first line. It was a small voice at the best of times, a cup-bearer’s voice, built for announcing wine — and the eye drank it, the way it drank everything, hush-grass and soft gold swallowing the notes almost before they left him. He sang the second line anyway, because you finished a count once you had started it, Dun had beaten that into the whole army, you never left a count hanging —

— and the graves’ whole company answered the stone.

The rising sang. Quietly — it could not be loud, nothing in the eye could be loud — but ten thousand voices low is not a sound at all, it is a weather, and the counting-song rolled around the bowl of gold light in a slow ring, verse on verse, gate-post and wall and shield, name after name folded into the counting the way the song had always taken names, four for Dun Harrow, who held the north door — and Nix, singing, felt the eye change around them.

The stillness pushed back. He felt it the way you feel a hill under a cart: a great, patient, downward softness leaning on the song, on the mourners, on every busy hand — the lull gathering itself at the garden the way it had gathered on sitting folk for a lifetime, the pressure-ghosts drifting in from the silver grass, drawn to grief as to tiredness, and among them, rising out of the Still Point at the garden’s heart like cream rising in a pan, something vast and calm and almost kind — the stillness itself with a shape on, the thing Lullside’s folk would not name, the welded peace of the whole eye, come to welcome the rising to rest.

And the song met it, and the song was a muster.

That was the whole of the miracle, and Nix understood it even as his neck-hair stood: the eye had one art, and the art was rest, and Dun’s song was the one human thing built exactly contrary — a count that demands an answer, a music you cannot sit down in. The seated of the Sitting Garden stirred. Breath by eighty-year breath, moss cracking along their shoulders, folk who had rested since before Roothollow had a name lifted their heads at the old, old shape of a muster-count — count off, count off — and answered the stone. An arm rose here. A grey head there. The pressure-ghosts thinned like breath-fog in sun, their peace cracking off them, and the vast calm shape at the Still Point came apart last and slowest, not slain — there was nothing in it to slay — but evicted, dismissed like a servant whose office has ended, dissolving into a shower of ordinary rain that fell, that fell, the first falling rain the eye had known in eighty years, straight down onto the graves like a blessing with its weight returned.

All over the bowl of gold light, the suspended beads let go.

It rained on the rising for the time a lamp takes to gutter — warm, real, downward rain — and the seated of the garden rose, forty-and-nine of them by Nix’s count, tottering, blinking, alive, caught by Lullside hands; and the army sang the count around one more time for the risen, and Mera Lullside stood in the falling rain with her bells ringing at last, unmuffled, and cried like a girl.

They slept that night — the whole host, in the rings of camps, under a sky rinsed clean — the truest sleep of the climb. Nix took the middle watch and did not need the draught to stay sharp; he was too full. He walked the rings with the banner on his shoulder, counting sleepers the way a miser counts gold. Eleven thousand and some, breathing. Rule one, kept eleven- thousandfold: nobody musters alone.

He was at the western ring, by the graves, when the horns came.

⁂

They came through the eye-wall, which nothing came through — not sound, not wind; Mera had said so, the roar ate everything — and these came through it the way a knife comes through a curtain. Three notes, long, cold, courteous, pitched in some register the storm declined to argue with. Not loud. Clear. The kind of sound that has never needed to be loud.

Every hound the rising had brought heeled at once, whining, and lay flat. The roar itself seemed to bend aside to let the notes pass, and they crossed the bowl of the eye and went on into the tier above, unhurried, like a rider crossing a room to deliver a card.

The nameless elf was beside Nix before Nix knew he had moved — Rhovan, with his bow already strung, listening the way other folk pray. And out of the graveside dark came one of the blown they had gathered off the seventy-fourth floor, a snare-folk elf of Brambleside, wire-scarred, who had gone white to the lips.

— Coursing-horns, — she said. — The Pale Court’s. I grew up under those horns. They blow the count of the quarry, always, first call. It’s the law of their hunt: the quarry is entitled to know it is chosen, and to know how many. — Listen. They’ll confirm it.

The horns spoke again through the eye-wall, and Nix, who counted everything, counted.

Four notes.

Not eleven thousand. Not the army, breathing safe in its rings of rain. Four — the count of the little party that had walked ahead of everything it loved, through open sky, for three days, under eyes that read the gap between shepherds and flock the way Mera read her glass. Ede. Vega. Rhovan. And a goblin who held the banner because flags don’t sit.

The Pale Court had noticed.

Nix looked at the graves, and the sleeping rings, and the wall of roaring dark between this floor and the next, and he hugged the banner-staff to his chest and said, to no one, in a voice too small for the size of the count:

— Four. It counted us, and it’s polite. Wake the general.

Above the eye, in the wood beyond the wall, the hunt was already moving.

Chapter 33 — The Coursing

The horns took them at moonset, on the third night in the eye.

Ede had gone forward with three because a general who sends scouts to a Warden’s door has stopped being worth following. That was what she told the council. The truer reason was smaller and harder: the road to the pale court had to be walked before the army walked it, and she could not sit in the standing rain another day listening to Dun’s muster-song sung without Dun.

So they were four on the ridge above the eye-wall when the calm ended — herself, Rhovan, Vega, and Nix — a bow, a hammer, a splinter of crystal, and a goblin with a kettle he refused to leave with the baggage. Four, and the army a day behind, still burying, still counting its blown regiments in.

The first horn sounded through the rain-wall like something remembered rather than heard. The second answered it from the west, where no road was. The third came from behind them, between them and the camp, and by then Rhovan had already stopped walking.

“Three horns,” he said. “That is a drawn covert.”

“Meaning?”

“Meaning the quarry has been found.” He did not look at her when he said it. He was looking at the ridgeline, where pale shapes were coming through the standing rain without disturbing it — hounds, moon-white, running mute. A hunt that meant to kill would have given tongue. These ran silent, spread wide, and every gap they left led one way.

“They’re herding us,” Ede said.

“They are coursing us,” Rhovan said. “It is not the same. Herded beasts arrive somewhere. Coursed ones are enjoyed first.”

Vega set her hammer head-down on the stone and leaned on it, and for one breath Ede watched her decide whether to argue for standing. Two heartbeats. Three. The hounds on the ridge were forty at a glance and more behind, and somewhere under the horns was the thing that had welded them.

“We run,” Vega said slowly, finishing the thought the way giants do, “and we choose our running, or they choose it for us. I have shod enough hounds’ masters to know which is worse.”

They ran. The gaps in the pale line opened before them, courteous as a door — down off the ridge, away from the army, away from the eye, into the dark of the Huntsman’s country. Behind them, one glade back, the horns.

⁂

The Hunt kept its own ways between its floors. Ede learned that the first night, when the ground they were driven down was suddenly not the Eye’s drowned terraces but a beaters’ stair sunk in the hollow of a black iron anchor-root — a hidden run, hound-worn, rank with old scent, that went down when every law of the climb said the way was up. Sliver burned cold against her palm the whole descent.

“They are not taking you toward the stair-lifts,” it said. “They are taking you where the running is good. I have carried climbers through this country twice. Neither time did the country decide the road. It has decided tonight.”

“Then we’re quarry.”

“You have been quarry since the horns found the eye. What you are now is sport.”

The beaters’ stair let out into a wood, and the wood was wrong before she had taken ten steps into it. Green, quiet, lamp-dark — and strung. Wire glinted between the trunks at shin height, at throat height, fine as spider- work. Spring-poles stood cocked in the underbrush like drawn bows nobody had loosed in years. The leaf-litter lay too smooth in places, the way a pit lies smooth. Rhovan put out his arm and stopped them all at the treeline.

“The Snare Woods,” he said. “Floor seventy-four. The elves here hunted with wire and patience, and the fever took the wood and multiplied every snare a thousandfold. Nothing in this wood is a path. Some of it is pretending.”

“How do we cross it?” Ede asked.

“Slowly,” Rhovan said, which was a fraction, and then the horn sounded one glade behind them, which was the rest of the answer. Slowly was not on offer.

They went in with Nix leading, because Nix was lightest and had walked point across the Trapdoor Fields and had, he announced, a gift. “Three gifts,” he said, testing the ground with a kettle-hook lashed to a pole. “Small feet, big eyes, and no pride at all about crawling. Wire here. And here. And — oh, that’s clever, that one’s under the other one. Wicked wood. Lovely wicked wood.”

The wood tried for them all night. A noose-creeper took Nix’s pole out of his hands and hoisted it into the canopy quick as a fish taking bait, and they stood in the dark listening to the springpole thrum and did not speak for a long while after. Pale flowers glowed in the black ahead, marking clearings that looked like mercy, and Rhovan said, “Bait-flower. The wood grows its own lies. Where it looks safest, kneel,” and they crawled through a clearing whose smooth ground was a lidded pit wide enough to take a cart.

Vega suffered worst. The wood was strung for men and elves, and she was neither, and the third wire she could not fold herself under she took across the forearm — a spring-blade, wire-flung, that laid her open elbow to wrist before her forge-skin stopped it. She made no sound. She bound it walking. But after that the funnel hounds had blood on the air, and the wood’s own beasts began to work them in earnest: dog-shapes ghosting the flanks, never closing, leaning them always leftward, always leftward, onto ground they had not tested.

“They funnel us,” Rhovan said. “It is what they were bred for, before they were welded to it. They will bend us onto the wire like water into a race.”

“Then we bend back,” Ede said, and they spent the strength to fight the bending — doubling on their own line, crossing a stream to fool nothing that hunted by more than scent, cutting north when every gap invited them west. It bought them the night. It cost them the night’s strength. And at grey dawn, when they lay in a bramble-hollow chewing cold meal, the horn sounded — one glade behind, exactly one glade, no nearer for all the hounds could plainly have closed, no farther for all Ede’s doubling.

Nix counted on his fingers, lips moving. “Eleven times now. Eleven horns, one glade. I had a master who did that with a whip once. Never hit me. Just let me hear where the whip was.” He put his fingers away. “I didn’t love him either.”

⁂

They crossed the Gibbet Glade at noon because there was no way around it that the hounds would allow.

Ede had seen fields after battle. She had seen the cages under the Red Warcamp opened, and what twenty years in them had made of her thirty. She had thought herself past being taught anything new about what the tower did to the caught. The glade taught her anyway: a clearing of green light where the sprung snares had kept their catch, game and folk together, hanging in the trees season past season — and among the hanging dead, things that moved, wire-tangled figures that had died in their own snares and now walked the wood setting snares for the living.

“Don’t cut them down,” Rhovan said, low, as her hand went to her knife. “Not with the horns behind us. The snare-folk cut them down when this floor is freed, and give their kin graves. If we start now we finish never.”

“You know this wood.”

“I know its cousins.” A fraction. He gave her a little more, walking: “The Grey Rides are mine. Floor sixty-six. All the elf-woods in this span are the Hunt’s country — the Rides, the Kennel Courts, the Quarry Runs, and this. Every one takes its law from the pale court above us. I was raised under that law.” He tested a branch, ducked the wire it hid. “I left before it could collect me.”

“And the law is chasing us.”

“The law is exercising us,” Rhovan said. “The chase comes when it tires of the game. Or when we do.”

Beyond the glade the wood rose toward a scarp, and on the scarp stood the tallest elm Ede had ever seen, every trip-line in the wood converging on its crown like the strings of an instrument. Under it, briefly, they found people: snare-folk from the gate-town, three of them, light-footed and grey- eyed, freezing at the sight of armed climbers and unfreezing at the sight of Nix. What they gave was little and everything — a tested path, a skin of water, and a warning delivered by their eldest with an elf’s economy.

“The Hunt has doors,” she said. “It will put you through one tonight, onto the Runs. Nothing that goes onto the Runs comes off them. The runs remember the chase.” She looked at Vega’s bound arm, at Ede’s face, and something in her face shifted — pity, or its harder elvish cousin. “Test every step. And when you cannot run any more — ” she stopped, the way Rhovan stopped, on the edge of a whole truth.

“Say it,” Ede said.

“It is kennel-talk. A child’s tale.”

“We are being coursed by a child’s tale. Say it.”

But the horn sounded, one glade back, and the snare-folk were gone the way elves go, and the door the Hunt had chosen for them stood open in the scarp: a beaters’ way, hound-worn, breathing the smell of run-grass and old blood.

⁂

The Quarry Runs were made for exactly this, and that was the horror of them.

The wood opened into coursing-lanes, broad grass rides walled with run-oak and covert-thorn, streaming away downhill so sweetly that tired legs turned onto them of their own accord. The grass itself lied: it laid a clear green path ahead of them that Rhovan swore was false, a groomed line pointing the one way in this wood they must not go — down, always down, toward a clearing ringed pale with foxglove where every course on this floor had ended for a thousand years. On the high stands along the lanes sat crows in their hundreds, and the crows called the course. Ede learned to hate the crows worse than the hounds. The hounds only followed. The crows told the whole wood, always, exactly, where the quarry ran.

And the hounds here did not funnel. They paced. Fresh hounds, run-bred, falling in beside them a lane’s width off, matching Nix’s trot and Vega’s stride and slowing when they slowed — keeping them, the way a drover keeps cattle, spending nothing, while the quarry spent everything.

They ran the Runs for a night and a day. Ede kept the count because keeping counts was the last discipline that survived in her when the rest went — four of them still; two skins of water, then one, then none until Nix smelled out a covert-spring; Vega’s arm going hot under the binding; Rhovan’s arrows, eleven, then nine, spent on a covert-boar that burst from the thorn onto Nix and died apologizing to nobody. The foxglove was the worst of it. Its pollen rode the lane-wind, and it slowed the heart — gently, courteously, the way everything in this country killed. She would surface from a grey drift and find she had been walking a lane she never chose, downhill, on the clear green path, toward the pale ring. Twice Vega’s hand on her shoulder turned her. Once she turned Vega.

They went to ground at last in a covert, a green warren of bramble the quarry-folk of this floor had kept safe for things that could not run any more. That was what it was for. That was what they were.

“Count it for me,” Ede said, “like a position.” Her voice was a stranger’s, scraped thin.

Rhovan did, dry as a sapper reporting a wall. “The hounds hold every lane. The crows hold the sky, so darkness buys nothing. The wood funnels toward the killing-ground, and the ground between the lanes is thorn we no longer have the blood to spare. Vega’s arm needs fire and a needle. We have neither. The army does not know where we are, and if it did, it is a day behind and would arrive as a second quarry.” He put his last arrows in the earth in front of him, stood them upright in a row, a fraction of a defense. “The Hunt has run us to the arithmetic. That was always its purpose. It does not tire. We do.”

“Then we stand here,” Vega said. Slowly; she finished it. “In the thorn, where the horse cannot come and the hounds come two abreast, and we make the freeing of this floor cost the tower a price it remembers. I have been a debt on this climb since floor six. I would rather end as a payment.”

“Two abreast, that’s good, that’s — ” Nix’s fast count died in his mouth. He was looking at his own hands, and his hands would not stop. “Four lanes into this covert. I counted. Four lanes, forty hounds a lane, and a horn, and a whip, and a man on a horse who doesn’t talk.” He laughed, high and short. “I carried a cup for a king once. Nobody chased me at all. Terrible job. Lovely hours.”

Ede lay against the bramble-root with her heart going slow and wrong under the foxglove, and felt the old arithmetic close over her like water over a weir. This was the Warcamp again, thirty names on a stone. This was where she was always going to arrive: the ground where the people who followed her stopped living because they had followed her.

“Your heart is running,” Sliver said against her palm, very quietly, so the others got none of it. “Not from the foxglove. Ask him the thing the snare-wife would not say.”

She turned her head. “Rhovan. On the scarp. The kennel-talk. Say it now.”

For three breaths he was silent, and she watched him fight his own nature — the elf who told the truth only in fractions, weighing which fraction this was. Then, flat: “There is one quarry the Hunt does not take.”

“Which?”

“The quarry that turns,” Rhovan said, “and walks toward the horns. Not runs — walks. It is the first law of the pale court, older than the Huntsman, older than the Theft. A hunt is a hunt only while the quarry flees. What walks toward the horns is not quarry, and the Hunt may not take what is not quarry. Every child of the Rides has it before they have their letters.”

“And it holds? Even now? Even welded?”

“The tower did not unmake his law. It nailed him to it.” He looked away. “But I have never once seen it tried, Ede. In a thousand years of the pale court, the kennel-talk counts the quarry who walked on the fingers of one hand. You know why no one tries it.”

“Because no quarry believes it,” Ede said.

“Because no quarry believes it.”

The horn sounded. One glade behind. It had been one glade behind for three nights, and it would be one glade behind until their hearts stopped, because that was the sport: not the catching, the keeping. And Ede Stonhal, who had been kept by one thing or another her whole life — by drink, by grief, by thirty names cut in a stone — got her feet under her and stood up out of the covert into the open lane.

“Ede.” Vega’s voice. “Ede, the arm I would give to stop you is the bad one. Do not make me offer the good one.”

“I’m not spending myself,” Ede said, and was surprised to find it true. She had spent herself down every year of her life and knew the feel of it, and this was not that feel. “If the law holds, I walk into his court and his court must receive me, and I bargain for the army from inside instead of dying out here as an afternoon’s sport. If the law doesn’t hold — ” she tightened the strap of the shield that wasn’t there, an old habit from a company twenty years dead ” — then I’ve bought you the length of a hunt’s gloat, and you go through the thorn and you tell Berrin to feed them whatever he must feed them to keep them climbing.”

She said the name and it cost her nothing. Later she would remember that: the last hour the name cost her nothing.

She turned toward the horns and walked.

The lane ran uphill, against the wood’s whole design, and the walking was the hardest work of her life. Every stride her legs begged to run — anywhere, even downhill toward the foxglove, anything but this — and the horns came on, and the hounds came with them. They broke from the covert-walls to either side, moon-white, silent, at a full course, and her body screamed and her step did not change, and at ten strides the pale line — parted. Flowed around her like water around a post, hackles down, heads low, and fell in beside her at a walk. Behind her she heard the others come out of the thorn and do what she did; heard Nix’s breath sobbing with the effort of not running; heard Vega humming a forge-song slow enough to walk to.

The horns sounded a call she had not heard in three nights — not the drive, not the drawn covert. Something older. Ahead, the lanes gave onto a glade lit horn-gold with lantern-light, and it was full of the Hunt: whips and horn-callers, pale riders, the silent pack seated in rings like a court in session. And at its center, mounted, moon-pale, antler-crowned, the one figure that did not move at all.

He did not speak. She had been told he never spoke, and it was worse than any voice. It was his court that spoke around him, a pale whip-man’s words falling into the hush like coins onto cloth:

“The horn cannot take what walks to it. The court receives you.”

The hounds sat. The Huntsman turned his horse toward the pale road that climbed north out of the glade — toward the bone-white hall she could feel up there past the trees, hung with a thousand years of trophies, where whatever a hunt-lord’s word was worth would now be tested against the lives of everyone she had ever led.

Ede spat the taste of foxglove into the run-grass, and followed him up.

Chapter 34 — The Pale Court

The pale court leached the color out of things, and it did it slowly enough that you could watch it happen.

Ede noticed it first on Nix, whose green went grey at the edges as they came up the pale road, the way a leaf goes in frost. Then on her own hands. The light of floor eighty was a cold moon-pale wash that fell from nowhere and cast no shadows, and under it the wood’s oldest heart stood leafless and bone-white — birch and hunt-oak branched like antlers, hung with old horns that turned in no wind. Ahead, ringed by its kennels, the hall: long, low, white as a skull, a thousand years of hunting nailed to its walls.

“Say nothing you do not mean,” Rhovan murmured as they came to the doors. “In this court, speech is spoor. They will course whatever you let slip.”

“And him?”

“He will not speak. He never speaks. The court speaks around him, and watches what your face does while you wait to learn if it spoke true.”

The court received them in the manner of a hunt at rest. Pale hounds sat in rings on the moon-mossed stones. Whips and horn-callers stood along the walls — and these were no wights, most of them, but living elves, thin and colorless, with the eyes of people who had run as quarry when the horn wanted sport and served as court when it did not. Court-captives, Rhovan had named them on the road. The Hunt’s own folk, held as the Huntsman’s unwilling field.

At the hall’s end, on a high seat over the pale floor, sat the Master of the Hunt.

Ede made herself look at him the way she would read any position: worst first. Antler-crowned, moon-pale, still as the dead center of a storm. The crown was welded to him — she could see the seam of it, the tower’s iron under the pale silver — and beneath the crown was a face that had been gentle once. That was the unbearable part. Skarn had died believing his collar a rank; this one’s face believed nothing at all. The tower had hunted the Master of the Hunt longer than any lord in the stolen realms, the court-captives said, until the gentlest hand in the wood was made the pale hunter of his own people. He watched her cross his floor, and his eyes were the only fast thing in him, and they never once left her.

An elf came forward from the foot of the high seat. Old, straight-backed, carrying a horn-caller’s baldric with no horn on it — the emptiness of it plainly a rank of its own.

“I am Nael Hornsend, who was his horn-caller.” The old elf’s voice was even, but Ede had stood before enough courts now to hear what it cost. “You walked to the horns. Four of you, on your feet, at a walk, under a full course. The court has not received a walked quarry in living memory, and elves live long. Say what you are.”

“Ede Stonhal. Of Roothollow, and of the rising you’ve watched climb your runs.” She kept it flat, count-plain. “Behind me on the wind floors is an army the Storm Queen scattered — regiments of it blown down onto your country, onto the Snare Woods, the Runs, the Wyrmroad. Your hunt is coursing them now, piecemeal, as it coursed us. I walked to the horns to say this where the horns take their law: they are not quarry. They are mine. I have come to bargain for their passage.”

A stir along the walls, like wind in a leafless wood. Hornsend glanced once at the high seat — at the still figure, the unmoving face — and Ede understood that the glance itself was the court’s whole procedure: the old elf read his silent lord the way sailors read a sky, and spoke the weather he found there.

“The court hears a claim of quarry-right,” Hornsend said. “The walked-to may ask a boon. It is the oldest grace we have — the Master’s own law, from before the tower, from when a hunt here ended in mercy or did not end at all. Ask.”

“The horns silent over the Hunt’s whole country,” Ede said, “until the last of my scattered have come off your floors and mustered below this court. No course run on any who wear my charter’s mark. Guides on the runs for the lost, and the beaters’ ways opened to my provisioners. That is the boon.”

“And what does the court receive?”

She had thought about it the whole pale road up. Voss would have offered coin, Ulfar engines, Zephyra a throne. She offered the only thing a hunt that had forgotten mercy could be offered by the people climbing to free it — though she could not say that part aloud, with the welded crown’s iron listening for all she knew.

“A quarry worth the horn,” Ede said. “Above your court the stolen realms end. What sits up there is the tower’s own Making — the thing that took your wood, welded your pack, and nailed your Master to his crown. When my army passes your hall, the Hunt is done chasing farmers and cup-bearers and blown soldiers forever. I offer you the hunt you were made for. We climb to course the tower itself. Ride with the rising, or stand aside and watch it done — but the small game is over either way.”

Silence, while the old elf watched the high seat. Ede watched too. She saw nothing move in the pale face — and then the right hand, gloved, resting on the saddle-bow of a chair built like a saddle, turned palm upward. Two fingers opened. It was nothing. It was the most speech she would ever get from him, and the whole hall breathed.

“The boon is granted,” Hornsend said, and his even voice cracked at last, just once, on the next words. “And the court will add what the walked-to did not ask, because I knew him before the crown, and I say his law is still alive in there and wants this said: the stair beyond this hall opens only by his horn. There is no passage past the pale court but through its Master. When your army is gathered, that reckoning comes, and no boon can bargain it away.” The old elf mastered himself. “Until then: the horns are silent. The Hunt keeps its truces. It is the last thing about us the tower could not spoil.”

⁂

They were the court’s received guests for a night, and hunt-law required that received guests be shown the honor of the house. The honor of the house was the Trophy Hall.

Ede walked it because refusing was not among her choices. It was the long gallery of the bone-white hall, and it held a thousand years: horns and heads, antlers beyond counting, pelts gone pale in the pale light, the mounted gear of quarry that had run well enough to be remembered. Nothing in it was cruel, exactly. That was what made it dreadful. Every trophy hung with its story tagged in fine elvish tally-script, and a trophy-keeper walked beside the guests and read the stories out, and the stories were all of respect. Ran four days. Turned at the water and fought. Spared, and her line spared after her, for the running’s sake. The far end of the gallery held older, stranger honors — trophies of the Master’s freed peers, tokens of Skarn and Hrimgar and the Storm Queen from ages when the crowned captives had hunted each other’s borders for sport. And on a peg of pale silver between two horn-racks, small and out of place among the grandeur: a plain leather hound-collar, cracked with age, its name-tag worn to a shine. Ede saw the trophy-keeper’s hand hover past it without touching.

“Whose?” Nix asked — fast, curious, counting everything as always.

“The first hound of the pale line,” the trophy-keeper said. “Bell. She heeled to his hand before he ever carried a whip. He hung her collar here himself, the year she died, and said a hunt is worth what its mercy is worth.” The keeper moved on quickly, the way people move past a grave. “That was the Master we had.”

It was three racks later that Ede stopped walking.

The shield hung between a boar-spear and a wolf’s head, and it was a Shieldwall shield. She knew it before she knew she knew it — knew the pattern of the rim-studs the way you know a face at a market stall, wrong country, wrong year, impossible. Round, oak under hide, the wall-and-star of her own company blistered but plain. The paint of the star had been laid on in the Warcamp fashion, the star’s low point doubled. Third cohort. One of her thirty had carried it into the mine-dark at floor twenty, and it had not come out with the freed, because it was here.

“A shield of Men,” the trophy-keeper said, reading the tally-tag with a collector’s mild pride. “Not taken — bought. The court bought notable pieces off the toll-roads in the years the Hunt did not ride low. This one was sold upward from the twentieth floor. See the rim.” Ede saw the rim. Stamped into the hide binding, small and regular as hoofprints, were toll-marks — the crossed-key punch she had last seen on the Tithe Barrows, on ledgers Rhovan had burgled by lamplight, on every crate that had ever moved through a Tollman gate under Gilder Voss. “Six gates, six marks. A well-traveled piece. The tally names the consignment: routed through the gate-town under the tower’s root — Roothollow — in the spring of the sale-year, by a provisioner’s factor. Whoever sold it had gate-friends, to move a battle-trophy the length of the low tower so quick and so quiet.”

“Quick,” Ede said. Her voice came out level. Some part of her that had been a captain before it had been anything else took over her breathing, her shoulders, the muscles of her face, and did what it had done at a hundred gravesides. “How quick?”

The keeper consulted the tag, pleased to be asked. “Sold at the tenth gate within the month of its taking. The spring the Red Warcamp filled its cages.”

The spring the Warcamp filled its cages. Ede stood in the pale light with her hands loose at her sides and let the arithmetic finish itself, because it was going to finish whether she let it or not. The spring her company died, somebody in Roothollow had money moving up the toll-roads. She had known that much since floor eleven — a payment up the tower, the week of the sale; Rhovan’s stolen ledger-page; a courier who smelled of Roothollow bread. A signature without a name. But she had been in Roothollow that spring, drunk and dying of it, and she remembered the season anyway, because it was the season the granary went up.

The new granary. Raised in a month, timber bought outright, ale stood for the whole street at the roof-raising, and everyone saying what everyone always said — that no one in the low world was better to his neighbors, that Roothollow would have starved twice over in the bad years without him, that a man like that deserved a good year at last.

The week her thirty went into the cages, his granary got its beams.

She heard Rhovan come up beside her — felt him find the shield, the star, the doubled point, saw in the pale edge of her sight how still he went. Elves answer questions with fractions, and ask them the same way. He asked nothing. She turned to the trophy-keeper.

“It’s a good shield,” she said. “Third cohort hung their shields face-out in the wall. It ran well.” The words came from somewhere and her face held. “What does the tally give for the seller’s name?”

“No name. Consignments off the toll-roads carry the factor’s mark only.” The keeper touched the tag, apologetic. “A wheat-sheaf, this one. A provisioner’s sign, common as rain in the grain-towns. It would be no one you could find.”

“No,” Ede said. “No, I don’t suppose it would.”

She looked at the shield a moment longer, because a captain may look at her dead company’s shield for a moment, and no court in any world reads more into it than grief. Then she walked on down the gallery, past the horns of a thousand years, and asked the keeper about a wolf-pelt three racks along, and heard herself sound interested in the answer.

⁂

That night the court lodged them in the kennel-wing, in narrow white rooms that had been whips’ quarters, and Ede sat on the pallet’s edge with Sliver dark in her closed hand and did not sleep.

Say it, she thought, and could not, even soundlessly, even to herself. Saying it would make it a thing that had been said. So she counted instead, the old discipline, the one that survived when the rest went. The ledger payment, the week of the sale. The courier who smelled of bread. The serving-list at the Frosthall, drawn by someone who knew the seating. The muster-roll of everyone who had climbed before — the roll with no gaps in it, kept by the man who kept the rolls. The granary beams. Six toll-marks on a dead man’s rim, and a wheat-sheaf for a signature. Any one of them was rain. All of them together were a roof, and under the roof, dry and warm and beloved, stood her oldest friend, and the count would not come out any other way, and she was done pretending it might.

Below her window the pale court lay colorless under its moonless light. Somewhere north of it, a day down the beaters’ ways, her army was already moving — regiments taking the opened runs, provision-trains rolling for the muster, and with the trains, as sure as bread, would come Berrin, because Berrin never missed a muster, because the army could not eat without him. He would find her at the picket-line the way he always did. He would have counted her losses before she said them, and have the grain already moving to cover them, and he would look at her with the same steady kindness he had been looking at her with since she was a drunk woman he refused to give up on, and she would have to look back.

She would have to look back, and say nothing, and climb.

The rest of the way. Beside him. Twenty floors of pretending, while the collar under his shirt — if there was a collar; there was a collar; she made herself finish even that — carried everything her face let slip to whatever sat above them wearing the ninetieth floor like a crown.

In this court, speech is spoor, Rhovan had said. They will course whatever you let slip.

“Then nothing slips,” Ede said to the pale room, in a voice that had drilled recruits at Roothollow when it was the only thing left she was fit for. It was almost steady. It would be steady by morning; she knew her own tempering that well, and hated the knowing.

Her hand ached. She opened it and found Sliver’s light seeping between her fingers, faint and troubled, the splinter warm as a held coal.

“Your heart has been running for half the night,” it said. “You have not once moved. Ede. What did you take off that wall?”

She looked out at the bone-white court a long time.

“Tomorrow,” she said. “Ask me tomorrow, when the horns can’t hear.”

The pale light did not change, because it never changed. Morning in the Huntsman’s country was only the hour the horns woke. When they did, they sounded a truce-call down the runs — the first mercy this floor had been allowed in eighty years, bought by four walkers and a boon — and Ede Stonhal washed her face in cold water, and hung her grief where the court hung its trophies, face-out, and went down to muster her army beside the man who had sold the last one.

Chapter 35 — What Ede Carries

The trains came in on the fourth day of the truce, and Berrin came with them, because Berrin never missed a muster.

Ede watched the wagons roll up the opened beaters’ ways with pale hounds walking escort — the strangest supply-line of the climb, grain-carts under moon-white outriders — and she stood at the picket-line because a general stands at the picket-line when the bread arrives, and because standing anywhere else would have been a change, and changes were spoor.

He found her the way he always found her, before she had decided to be found. He looked older. He looked glad. He looked exactly, precisely the way he had looked for twenty years, which was the thing she had never once in twenty years thought to examine, and he swung down off the lead wagon favoring the shoulder that had taken an arrow for her at the Frosthall, and said what he always said at the end of a bad stretch:

“Well. You’re thinner. We’ll fix that.”

The bread on the lead wagon was Roothollow bread. It was always Roothollow bread; he had taught the ovens of six floors to bake it because he said an army fights on the smell of home; the whole climb had loved him for it, and she had loved him for it, and a freed prisoner at the Red Warcamp had put her face in her hands and said the courier smelled of it. He broke a loaf and gave Ede the heel, her share since her drunk years, when the heel was all she’d reliably keep down. She took it. Her hand was steady. She had promised herself in the pale court that her hand would be steady, and it was; that was one thing she carried now, the steadiness, worn like a shield on the wrong arm.

“You walked at the horns,” he said. Wonder in it, and reproach, and pride, in the proportions only he had ever mixed. “Woman, when the word came down the line I sat on a flour-sack and shook. Don’t you do that again. There’s maybe six people on this climb the rest of it can’t spare, and you’re four of them.”

“It came out right,” she said.

“It always comes out right, until the day it doesn’t.” He was looking at her the way he looked at short ledgers, kind and worried. “You’re not sleeping.”

“No,” she said, because he would know if she lied about that. He knew her sleep the way he knew her hands. There was nobody alive who could read her like Berrin Or, and that was the fact she had to walk around every hour now, the way you walk a floor you know is trapped. “The pale light. Nobody sleeps up here.”

“I’ll send you up something. Warm milk and a splash of the black bitter — your mother’s trick.” He said it easy, already turning to his tallies, already counting sacks, and she watched his back and thought: he will send it, and it will help, and he will have done it because he loves me, and every part of that sentence is true, and none of it matters at all. That was the country she lived in now. Everything true, and nothing safe.

She ate the heel of the bread on the way up the hill, every bite, because she had eaten it at a hundred musters and the crows of this country told the whole wood where the quarry ran.

⁂

She told Sliver at the watch-fire on the ridge above the kennels, past midnight by the candle-marks, in the hour the camp’s coughing stopped.

Not the name at first. Even alone, even in a whisper meant for a palmful of crystal, she came at it sideways, by the counts — the ledger week, the serving-list, the roll with no gaps, the wheat-sheaf mark, the granary beams — and let the splinter finish the arithmetic she had finished four nights ago in a white room. Sliver’s light stood very still in her hand, the way it went when it worked.

“The provisioner,” it said at last. “Berrin Or.”

Hearing it was worse than knowing it. Hearing it was also, in some way she was not proud of, like setting down one handle of a yoke she had been carrying alone — the load no lighter, but two shoulders under it now.

“Say the case against,” she said. “If there’s rain in my roof, find it.”

“There is no rain in your roof,” Sliver said. “I have watched him feed you for two years of climb and twenty years of memory that you have shown me, and I find nothing that contradicts and everything that fits. The kindness fits. Ede, hear me: the kindness fits. A man who does what he has done can live only inside a kindness that is real. That is what your kind will not understand about the collared willing. The love is not the mask. The love is the leash he holds himself by.”

“Then what do I do with it?”

“What you are doing. Nothing, said to no one, until the head of the snake is in reach of the axe. He is a collar, and every collar has an owner, and the owner is above us on the ninetieth floor reading whatever your face writes. You have seen what the Hunt does with spoor. So has the Herald. Give him none.”

The fire spat. Down the hill the pale kennels lay silent under their truce, and Ede sat with her splinter and understood that she had just made her council of war: one woman and a shard of old crystal, against a friend of twenty years. The loneliness of it reached her then, entire — not the burden, she had carried burdens, but the quarantine. Rhovan, who had half-seen the shield, could not be told, because Rhovan reported once to the Wake and habits outlive factions. Vega could not be told, because Vega believed everything broken could be mended and would try to mend him. Nix could not be told, because Nix’s face was the one honest thing on this climb and she would not be the one who taught it to lie. Every person she loved was a door the truth could not go through, and so she would be, for twenty floors, a woman with a wall where her people were.

“There is a thing I must say now,” Sliver said, “and I ask you not to open your hand while I say it, because I have practiced this for sixty floors and I will not manage it twice.”

She kept her hand closed. The splinter’s warmth changed — went old, somehow, went heavy.

“You wonder how the Yoke reaches everywhere. Why the fever takes every floor alike; how a collar in Roothollow speaks to a throne a hundred floors up with no wire and no rider. I know how. The tower’s veins are the bones of my people. The shard-network — the minds like me, the net that once thought across this whole country of countries — is dead, Ede. It died in the Theft. And Vharuk took the dead net and strung his Yoke along it, the way a man fences with the timbers of a burnt house. Every cruelty the Yoke carries, it carries on my kin’s bones. I have known it since I was split. I did not tell you, because you would look at me the way you are trying very hard not to look at me now.”

The fire burned. Ede looked at her own closed hand, and did not open it, as she had been asked.

“You’re telling me now,” she said, “so I’m not alone in it.”

“I am telling you now because you have this day learned what it is to love a thing the enemy is riding,” Sliver said. “I have carried that knowledge for an age of the world. You will carry it for twenty floors. It can be carried. I am the proof. And when we reach the thing that rides — his throne, my bones, your friend — I would have us be two who knew, and not two who were spared.”

She sat with that a long while. Then she opened her hand, and looked at the splinter the way she looked at Berrin at the picket-line: entire, unflinching, love and ruin in one frame.

“Two who knew,” she said. “Agreed.”

⁂

Calyne came up the ridge in the last black hour before the horns woke, which was her hour, and had been since floor thirty.

She did not sit. She stood at the fire’s edge in her grey, one floor ahead of everyone as always, and Ede waited, because Calyne began conversations in the middle and resented being met anywhere else.

“You looked at forty racks of trophies in that hall,” Calyne said, “and asked the keeper about a wolf-pelt.” Ede said nothing. “The wolf-pelt was three racks past the only thing on that wall a Roothollow woman would cross a street to see. You have the best field-discipline of anyone I have ever run, Stonhal, and that is the trouble with discipline. On a person who startles, nothing shows. On you, not-startling shows.”

“Is there a question coming?”

“No,” Calyne said. “No question.” And then the spymaster of the Wake, who had been suspected to her face for three parts of a climb and never once flinched, did the strangest thing Ede had seen her do: she took the cipher-book from her breast, the little grey book that never left her, that half the council believed held the rising’s death warrant in one pocket-sized parcel — and held it out across the fire.

“The hand is mine,” she said. “The keys are in the sewing of the cover, third gather, left board. It reads slow and it reads only to the patient, which is why I am giving it to you and not to the elf. In case I stop climbing.”

“Why would you stop climbing?”

“Because I am close to a thing that has killed to stay unfound,” Calyne said, with no more weight than she gave a supply-count, “and I have just watched it occur to me that you may be close to the same thing from the other side. I don’t ask. Ciphers keep better than speech. If I am right about you, you will know what the book is when you need it. If I am wrong, you’ll have a dull winter’s reading.”

Ede took the book. It weighed nothing. It weighed more than the shield on the trophy wall. Their eyes met over the fire, two women who had each been, in their turn, the most distrusted person in an army — and neither said a name, and neither looked away, and the alliance was made entire in the silence, the only place it could live.

“Sleep, Stonhal,” Calyne said, turning down the hill. “You look like a general the enemy would like.”

Ede sat until the horns woke and sounded the truce down the runs. In her left hand, a splinter that carried the Yoke’s road on the bones of its kin. In her breast, next to the muster-roll, a book she prayed she would never learn to read for the reason she’d been given it. On the wall of a white hall behind her, a shield, face-out. What Ede carried now went at her front like armor and at her back like debt, and none of it could be set down before the ninetieth floor.

Below, the camp was waking. Seventy banners going up the pale sky, and under them her army — and the Hunt still to break, the last horn still to sound, the stair to the Made still shut behind a welded crown. She stood, and banked the fire, and went down to break it.

Chapter 36 — Breaking the Hunt

Rhovan had been born under the Huntsman’s law and had spent three hundred years getting out from under it, and now he knelt in the Pale Kennels learning it back by lamplight, because the law was the only weapon on this floor that would loose.

Nael Hornsend taught him. The old horn-caller had kept the calloff-lore alive for eighty years the way the keepers kept everything on this climb — alone, at night, against the day someone would come who could use it. The lore was not long. That was the terrible economy of it. Recall, Houndcall, Mark, and over them all one word that gathered the rest the way a fist gathers fingers: Calloff, the merciful capstone of the elf-hunt’s arts, the word that ends any hunt. A child could learn the sounds in an evening. What could not be learned in an evening was the meaning them.

“You cannot call off a hunt you would rather win,” Hornsend said. “The word reads the heart it leaves. That is why the tower could not take it, and why I could not use it. A thousand years his horn-caller. I taught half his pack their names.” The old elf’s hands were steady on the master horn — pale-silver, plain as a shepherd’s, the Master’s own. “I cannot want his hunt ended more than I want my Master back, and the word knows it. It must be sounded by someone who wants nothing from him at all.”

“I am of the Grey Rides,” Rhovan said. “I grew up wanting nothing from him except distance.”

“I know what you are,” Hornsend said, not unkindly. “You are the one who broke his own token over his knee two floors down, and answers a plain question with a third of an answer, and walks wide of anything that could be a leash. The word may like that in you. Loose things call off cleanly.” He set the horn in Rhovan’s hands. “Or it may find the fraction you keep back, and fail. We will learn together, in front of everyone.”

Ede came to the kennels at middle-night with the trophy-keepers, and they brought the collar. Rhovan had thought there would be more argument about that — the taking down of anything from that wall had the feel of a sacrilege — but the keepers carried Bell’s collar folded in pale-grass like a relic going to war, which was what it was. A hunt is worth what its mercy is worth. The man who had said that was welded to a crown two hundred strides away, and everything that loved him was arming to break it.

“The army stands ready below the court,” Ede said. “Say the shape of it again. All of it. No fractions tonight, Rhovan.”

He gave her all of it, which for her he now did, a change two years and one disavowal in the making. “The Hunt must ride out — the stair opens only past its Master, and its Master will not sit his stand while an army walks his floor; the crown will make him course us. When the pale hunt comes, no one runs and no one charges. Every soul in the rising turns and walks toward the horns. The law receives what walks; the pack cannot take a single one of us and the crown cannot make it — his own first law, nailed into him deeper than the weld. That is the breaking. Not one law against another. His law against his collar.”

“And what can be taken,” Vega rumbled, from the kennel door she filled, “is the four welded dead who serve him — the whip, the horse, the horn- wight, and the hounds’ pale fever. Those we may fight. Those we will fight.” She had her hammer, and her arm was sewn and fired, and she had asked for the remount herself: she had shod horses for four hundred years, she said, and would not see a good horse ridden dead a stride longer than she could help.

“And I have the small folk on the kennel-roofs,” Nix said, “with sixty buckets of kennel-broth, on account of the pups.” He said it like a battle-order, because it was one; the unblooded hounds, the pack’s young, frantic for a first kill they had never been given — the cure for them was not steel, the quarry-folk swore, but the smell of the kennels at feeding, the puppy-memory under the fever. “Sixty buckets, twelve ladles, one goblin. I’ve held worse doors with less soup.”

⁂

The Hunt rode out at the hour the horns woke, and it was the last time, and some part of everything on that floor seemed to know it.

Rhovan stood in the front rank with the horn slung at his hip and watched the pale court empty itself at them: the pack in its rings unwinding into a flood of moon-white, the riders, the whips, the pale owls dropping from the antler-branches, and last, from the high seat, the Master of the Hunt on his dead horse, antler-crowned, coming down the bone-white ground at a walk that was worse than any charge. The horns sounded the drawn covert. Seventy banners stood on the pale-grass to receive it — Men and giants and goblins, snare-folk and quarry-folk and road-folk in wyrm-scale, the freed of thirty floors — and Rhovan heard, rippling down the line in forty languages, the order Ede had given at dusk like a mother teaching a prayer:

When the horns come, walk.

They walked. Gods and graves, they walked — an army turning as one thing into the teeth of a full course and going forward at a footpace, and Rhovan walked in it, and learned what Ede had bought alone in the Quarry Runs and then given to every soul who followed her. The pack came on like winter water. Every animal law in the body said run. Nobody ran. An old Shieldwall pike-woman beside him was weeping straight ahead of herself with her spear at rest; on his other hand a kobold matron had her whole fist in her mouth and her feet going steady as a drum. Ten strides. Five. And the flood of moon-white broke — parted, flowed round the walking army hackles-down, ten thousand years of law holding against ten years of weld — and the pale hunt came apart on its own mercy like a wave on a sea-wall.

The welded dead did not part. The first whip came through the checked pack with its silent lash taking heads of pale-grass, and the line met it with bills and rivet-shields; it broke on the third rank and drained into the white ground. The horn-wight rose on the Master’s left and set its horn to sound the kill-drive that would have burned the pack’s law out of it — and Rhovan, who had been given one task and a horn, sounded first.

He had wondered, kneeling in the kennels, what the word would find in him — which held-back fraction, which walked-wide loyalty, whether a name-broken elf three centuries out from under this law had standing left to speak it. The word took him entire and truthful as an arrow leaving: the Rides at lamp-lit evening, his mother whipping-in, every hunt he had ever fled ending in one breath of pale-silver sound. Calloff. The horn-wight’s call died unsounded in its throat. Across the pale ground ten thousand snares went slack in sympathy two floors down; the stand crows lifted from their stands and were only crows; the riderless remount, mid-gallop at the line, lost the fever’s rein — and Vega stepped out of the third rank and took its pale tack in her two hands and held, as a farrier holds, till the welded dead thing shuddered dry out of the harness and the tack fell empty across her arms. She folded it. Even then, even there, she folded it.

Which left, in the ringing quiet, one rider.

The Master of the Hunt sat his dead horse in the middle of his broken hunt, and the crown fought him. Rhovan saw it plain — the antlered iron grinding at the seam, hauling the gloved hand toward the great horn at the saddle-bow, the kill-drive, the last command the tower had left. And up the pale ground toward him, between the seated hounds, unarmed, at a walk, went Ede Stonhal with a cracked leather collar in her open hands.

She said nothing. This was his court; his court had taught her; speech was spoor and mercy was the only word he had left. She stopped at his stirrup and held up the little collar, name-tag worn to a shine, and the pale rider looked down at it for the space of ten heartbeats, while his hand strained against his own crown at the horn’s throat.

Bell. She heeled to his hand before he ever carried a whip.

The hand came off the great horn. It came down, slow, shaking with the iron’s whole weight against it, and took the collar off her palms with a gentleness Rhovan would see behind his eyes till he died. And the crown — which the tower had nailed to a law of mercy and then bet against it — cracked at the seam like frost-split stone, and the antlered iron fell away in halves onto the white grass, and the pale drained out of the court like a tide going home.

Color came back first to the hounds. Brindle and tan and wheaten, a whole pack’s plain dear colors flooding through the moon-white, and the pack went mad the way dogs go mad at a homecoming, swarming the stirrups of a man waking from fourscore years. The Master of the Hunt looked at his hands. He looked at his kennels, his court, his people standing in the wreck of his hunt wearing the colors he had forgotten. He did not speak. He never spoke, and Rhovan understood at last that this had stopped being the wound and become the dignity: what he did instead was raise the master horn — Hornsend ran to him and gave it over, weeping like rain — and sound one long call that Rhovan’s blood knew before his mind did, the call written under every other call, the hunt’s true ending, going out across the pale court and down the beaters’ ways and floor by floor through the whole wood-realm. Every hunt, ended. Every horn, answered and released.
