In the Snare Woods the last snares fell slack; on the Runs the lanes forgot the chase; and in the Gibbet Glade, the snare-folk were already cutting their caught kin down at last and laying them in the green ground.

Then the Master of the Hunt dismounted, hung the great horn on the kennel-post the way a laborer hangs a tool at the day’s end, for good, and knelt into his hounds.

⁂

The muster before the Made took three days to count, and Rhovan, who counted for the council now that the council trusted his counting, walked the whole of it on the last evening with the tally in his head like a psalm.

Seventy banners. He counted them again from the Vantage Stand as they went up the pale sky, which was pale no longer but a plain kind evening grey: the wall-and-star of Shieldwall, oldest and most mended; the Forge-Court’s hammer; Voss’s crossed keys, which nobody loved and everybody fed; the fair-folk’s bunting, the Broodwarrens’ knot, kobold tally-marks on cut tin; the horn-and-heel of the pale court, newest of all, sewn in three nights by the trophy-keepers from a thousand years of pale-silver thread. Thirty-one peoples. He said their names to himself, all of them, the way Nix had taught the freed to say their own: Men and elves and giants and goblins, kobolds and the fair-folk and the road-folk and the rest, thirty-one kinds of stolen, standing in one camp under one charter that had been signed at a mead-hall and burned at a feast and carried up sixty floors since like a banked coal. One army. The thing the tower was built to prevent, fed and mustered and singing badly around four hundred fires, at the top of the stolen world.

At the camp’s north edge the green ended.

He stood a long time at that line, where everyone stood a while that evening, the whole army taking its turn to look. Behind: the wood-realm falling away tier under tier, lamplight in Bayhollow and Brambleside, hunting-horns — sport-horns now, mercy-horns — calling clean and far away down the runs, a hundred greens breathing in the dark. The stolen realms, every floor of them walked and freed and left living. Ahead, above, where the next stair rose: iron. The Made. Black outworks welded to black ramparts, floor eighty-one going up into eighty-two like a fist into a forearm, no floor-file keeper alive up there to hand them a map, nothing up there the tower had stolen. Only what it had built. Rampart and rivet, forge-light in the seams, and — faint, when the camp-singing lulled — drums. Not Skarn’s drums, nor the mine-dark’s. Patient drums. Manufactory drums, keeping a rhythm no heart kept, the sound of the enemy’s own country getting ready to receive them.

Ede came and stood at the line beside him, wrapped in her plain cloak, with the splinter’s faint light in her closed left hand. She had not slept again; he could read that much, and had chosen — his own law now, and the hardest he kept — not to read further, though something rode her that was heavier than the Made, and had ridden her since a white hall he did not let himself think about too exactly. She would tell him in fractions, when she could. He was the last one entitled to complain of that.

“The last stolen floor is behind us,” she said. “Everything we freed stays freed. Whatever we are after tonight, we aren’t rescuers any more.”

“No,” Rhovan said. “After tonight we are the siege.”

The drums turned under the singing like a millwheel under water. Along the north edge the army looked up at the iron a while longer, and then, being an army, went back to its fires, because the bread was coming round again — and in the whole camp only the two of them stayed to see the forge-light in the outworks flare, once, twice, like a smith testing his fire before a long day’s work.

The tower had stopped losing things it had stolen. What it owned, it would spend. Rhovan went down to the fires and began, quietly, to count arrows.

Chapter 37 — The Wake's Price

The grain stopped moving on the second day of the muster, and it took another day for the reason to climb the gate roads, and when it came it came addressed, in part, to a man with no name.

Rhovan was at the arrow-counts when Voss found him — the quartermaster-general moving at the pace he reserved for money in danger, two clerks trotting. The rising was three days from touching the first rampart, and an army three days from a siege eats like a siege already.

“Bayhollow,” Voss said, without greeting, which was his greeting. “The gate below the Egg Galleries. Shut since yesterday’s dawn — a chain across the lift-arch, hired bows on it, and seventy banners’ worth of bread on the wrong side. I have eleven days of full rations at this camp, elf. Eleven.” He held out the letter. “It came up under a grey seal. It names you.”

The letter was a strip of birch-paper, and Rhovan knew the hand before he knew the words: not Calyne’s — the council’s own scriveners, the hand that had paid him for twenty years.

To the general of the rising, at her muster. Item: below your camps lie the lamp-woods of the Elves, freed in the passing of your army as a road is freed of weather. Item: freed is not restored. The wire stands slack in ten thousand snares, and slack is not gone; the lamps of nineteen fragments of the old wood stand dark, and dark is dark. Item: a price was named in the Sheltered Casemate of the Wind Walls by the instrument formerly styled Rhovan of the Grey Rides — the woods after the gathering, himself at the head of the work, when the last name on the general’s roll was found. Item: the last name is found. The roll is closed. The debt stands due. The gate at Bayhollow opens when the woods are made whole, and not a wagon sooner. The Wake’s bows are recalled from your walls; its seat at your charter stands vacated. The Wake wishes the rising victory entire, and will not feed it one more day on credit.

Rhovan read it twice and heard, under the scrivener’s hand, what the letter was actually saying: you left. See what your word is worth where it was given. Every item true, every truth aimed. That was the poison of it: the Wake would strangle the one army in the age of the tower that could deliver its own purpose, over the order of entries in a book.

Ede read the letter standing, grey with the muster and with the something else he had chosen not to read.

“No,” she said. “We don’t clear freed ground with soldiers. That’s the tower’s answer.” She folded the letter and held it out to Rhovan. “It’s your bill. Go down and pay what you can, short of the army. The muster can’t spare me and the iron won’t wait. Take Voss. Nothing frightens a blockade like an honest man who wants it priced.”

Berrin walked them to the skiff-stage in the morning dark, an old man doing up other people’s buckles, and loaded Rhovan down with two sacks of loaves — “for the queue, mind, not the parley; feed the ones stuck standing first. Nobody argues honestly on an empty stomach, and that goes double for elves, no offense to present company.” Rhovan watched him wave from the stage and thought that the rising ran on two arts — Ede’s, of making people brave, and Berrin’s, of making them fed — and that history would only ever write down the first.

⁂

Two days down the gate roads taught Rhovan what a chain across one arch does to a country of countries.

The blockade itself was nothing: a length of tower-iron slung across the lift-arch at the coursing-lane’s end, and on the stands above it two hundred hired bows of Men, toll-road companies with their pay in Wake silver and their faces arranged in the studied blankness of men holding a bridge nobody had attacked. But below the arch the queue began, and the queue did not end. Grain-wagons stood axle to axle down the whole vale of the runs, out of sight into the evening, five floors of harvest walking uphill into a wall: ox-teams grazing the verges, drovers’ fires in the wheel- shadows, freed folk of nineteen peoples sitting on their own sacks in a wood that had hunted them a season ago and fed them now. The smell of it reached a mile up the lane — bread wanting to be bread, going over sweet and sour in ten thousand sacks while the arch stayed dark. Voss took one look from the last rise and went down into the queue with his clerks, pricing spoilage, buying verge- feed, writing the rising’s paper against grain that could still be ground.

Nael Bayhollow met Rhovan at the gate-town with his tally in his fist like a hammer. The quarry-warden had kept run-lore alive since the Theft, had lived to see the runs crossed at last, and was now watching his floor’s first free harvest rot before his own arch.

“I want it said,” he told Rhovan, in the keeper’s voice that is never quite shouting, “that this wood was freed by a goblin’s soup, a giantess’s hands, a dead hound’s collar, and an army of every people but mostly not mine. And the first thing my own people build on it is a toll. You go up to that table and say Nael Bayhollow is ashamed in front of his floor.”

The Wake had come into the open. Grey canvas among the run-oaks, a camp without one banner, elves at their evening work who did not lower their hoods — faces he had never seen and had corresponded with for decades, signatures walking around on legs. For a thousand years the Wake had been a rumor with a purse. Now, with its purpose lying freed all around it and nothing left to hide from, it had surfaced into the daylight of the world at last — and its first public act in the age of the tower was a chain. The grief of that was so exact that Rhovan stood a moment in the lane and let it have him.

They received him at a trestle table under the hunting-arch: three elders of the three lamplighter houses, and between them, under oiled cloth, a book he had never seen and had served his whole life — the Leaf-Ledger, current for the age of the tower, out under open sky for the first time since the grove it was founded in.

The eldest did not open with his name, there being none to open with.

“You were our best,” she said. “Enter that first. The Wake does not regret the spending — regret is a gap in a ledger. State the rising’s answer.”

“The rising’s answer is me. The price stands as named in the casemate: I walk into the wire at the head of the clearing work, and I stay at it until the lamps burn, once the tower falls. Open the gate and I will sign it in whatever ink the houses keep. But the army climbs now. Every day this chain stands, the freed floors learn that the rising’s word is worth what a faction’s patience allows. You know what that lesson costs. You taught it to me.”

“The instrument’s price is accepted as to the instrument,” the eldest said, and one of the others entered it, pen moving, and Rhovan watched twenty years of his life become a line in a book with no name in it. “It is insufficient as to the host. The woods waited eighty years while every people’s realm was freed around them, because ours was merely on the way. The host gentles the woods before it climbs, or it climbs hungry. The gate stays shut.”

“You’d starve the rising you want to win.”

“We would collect from it,” said the eldest of the Wake, without heat, without hurry, fourscore years of patience folding its hands on a table. “Everything is bought. You sat at the feet of the one who taught us all to say so.”

And a voice Rhovan knew better than his own name — the old one or the new — said, from down the lane:

“She also taught you to keep books. Stand aside and I will show you how it is done.”

⁂

Calyne came up the queue on foot, dust to the knee, two days down from the muster and unannounced, as she arrived everywhere. She did not look at Rhovan. She walked to the trestle table as she had once walked to a council fire — as if she had cut its timber herself and found the workmanship wanting — and laid three papers on it, beside the Leaf-Ledger, squaring their edges with two fingers until they lay exact.

“Mistress,” the eldest said, and there was something in the word Rhovan had never heard an elder give her: the wariness of a creditor greeting a better creditor. “The council did not summon its eyes.”

“It never does, when it is doing something it would rather not see. Attend. Paper the first.” She turned it to face them. “The contract of hire for the bows on this arch, drawn on the founding purse. The founding purse spends for two objects only — the keeping of the lamps and the keeping of the watch — by a clause in the founders’ own hand, which I have copied out fair, the council’s memory wanting refreshing. A chain across the bread of nineteen freed peoples keeps no lamp and wakes no watch. The hire is void from its date; the silver is recalled by sundown; I have already served the captain his notice, and he is aware that bows follow pay. By dark you hold this arch with three elders and a book.

“Paper the second. The council has spent this season entering grievance — the woods crossed and not gentled, the wire slack and not drawn, the name of the Leaf-Ledger spent at a spider’s court by an instrument the Wake no longer owns. All true. All entered. And in the same season it has left the founding ledger in arrears, so I have brought the accounts current myself. Enter: the Grey Rides, freed. The kennel courts, freed. The Snare Woods, the Quarry Runs, the pale court and every green fragment of the sixty-sixth floor, freed. Enter: ten thousand snares slacked in one breath by a calloff no elf alive could sound. Enter: the Gibbet Glade’s dead, cut down and laid in green ground. Enter last, and rule it twice: the only army in the age of the tower that has ever moved the wood’s freedom from the credit column to the till — fed, this season, by the floors it freed, and starved, this week, by us. Read your own book, elders. The Wake is not the rising’s creditor. It has been living off the rising’s payments for two years and calling the income grievance.”

Silence went down the table. Below the arch a mile of queue made the sound a country makes waiting.

“The debt of the woods is real,” the eldest said at last, evenly, a creditor conceding nothing yet. “Slack is not gone. Dark is dark. Who clears the wire, mistress? Who relights the lamps?”

“They are owed this,” Calyne said, and laid down the third paper, and Rhovan, reading it upside down in the old reflex, felt the parley’s ground move. It was a grant in the charter’s form, sealed at the muster: wardship of the lamp-wood floors — named one by one, the Grey Rides to the pale court — vested in the Wake under the charter of the Frosthall, with the labor of the freed floors pledged to the clearing, and the timber-right and the lamp-right and the whole restoration standing in the Wake’s care until the wood was whole.

“Signed before I came down. I asked her for it the night before I followed your letter’s answer here. It took the general of the rising, at her own muster, on the eve of her own siege, as long to grant the Wake’s whole purpose as it takes to read it. You have spent the founding purse, the Wake’s name before nineteen peoples, and four days of harvest, to buy what was already yours for the sound of your own voices in the open — and the entry cannot be unmade, because the whole queue below this arch is the receipt.”

The eldest of the Wake sat still a long time. Then she rose and unhooked the chain’s near shackle with her own hands — it was done that fast, in the end, after eighty years; iron is always the smallest part of a blockade — and said, to the lane, in the formal voice, the one for entries: “The gate is open. The Wake takes up the wardship of the woods.” And to Calyne, quietly, in the other voice, the one for prices: “The council will require your seat, mistress. You have audited it in public. There is no reading of our law under which you keep it.”

“There is no reading under which I want it,” Calyne said. “My resignation has stood entered in the council’s own minute-book since before I came down — third gather, left margin, in my hand; you will find I dated it the day your letter went up.” She gathered her papers and was done with the table. “I was the Wake’s eyes for three hundred years. The eyes are the one part that cannot help seeing which way the ledger actually runs. Keep the woods well, elders. It was always the whole of what any of us wanted.”

⁂

The queue moved at dusk.

It moved the way a river moves when the ice goes — a shudder, a groan of axles taking weight, and then the whole vale of the runs turning slowly from a warehouse back into a road, wagon by wagon up through the hunting-arch into the lift-light, drovers’ horns answering down the lanes for miles, Berrin’s loaves going hand to hand to the ones stuck standing farthest. It would take four days to clear. It would clear. By the arch the Wake was folding its grey canvas, hoods down at last in front of strangers, its elders already deep in run-lore with the quarry-warden who had been ashamed of them — the best-kept ledger in the world bent, at last, over the only work it had ever wanted, and not one of them climbing another step. The rising’s rear was now held by a faction that had blockaded it a week gone and would die for its supply lines by spring, provided the entries were fair.

He found Calyne at the foot of the lift-queue, going up with the grain like any wagoner, and stood beside her awhile without speaking, which he had learned from a better general than either of them.

“You spent your seat,” he said at last. “Three hundred years, for a chain that would have come down in a month regardless, once the purse ran dry.”

“A month,” Calyne agreed. “On the twelfth day of which your army stands at half-bread under the first rampart, and the Made prices the difference. It was arithmetic, Rhovan, not sentiment. Do not make me regret telling you the sum came out.”

“And what do I call you now, with the seat gone? Mistress of nothing. Eyes of no council.”

“Calyne,” she said. “It was always the only part of it that was mine.” Then, as the queue took her forward, she put out one hand, palm down, the old signal — receive — and into his was pressed a small weight wrapped in grey paper: two halves of a broken disc of heart-elm, grain showing pale where it had never seen light, gathered off a casemate table on the Wind Walls by a handler who had not explained herself then either. “The Wake does not keep what it cannot pour,” she said, not turning around. “Carry your own livery, or bury it. It has been paid for twice now — once by you. The second entry is closed.”

He carried it. Up the lift-queue in the dark, up the gate roads under the wagon-lamps strung like a lit wick through five floors of freed country, the two pale halves of a name riding in his breast where the whole one used to, lighter than the whole had ever been. Behind him the artery ran full again, a country’s blood moving; and Rhovan of the rising climbed home toward the siege counting, floor by floor, the things his people had fought each other over in two years of climbing — gates, tolls, ledgers, woods, seats, names — and the one thing they had never fought over once.

The direction. Nobody had ever fought about the direction.

He was at the muster by middle-night and gave Ede the ledger of it standing, all of it, no fractions; and the last wagons of the relieved queue were still coming up through the gates behind the army three mornings later, when the rising touched the first rampart of the Made, and the war for the tower’s own country began.

From Malgrim's Ledger

Item: the Pale Court has fallen. Say rather: has been given away. The Huntsman kept better books than the Queen — every course entered, every quarry weighed, a thousand years in fair hand — and it availed him nothing, because his ledger carried one bad clause from before our time, and the woman found it and walked in through it. Both accounts are now spent. The stolen realms are a closed column. I do not mourn. Assets that were liabilities dressed in crowns.

Item: the pack is lost with its master. Enter under depreciation. A hound that has heard the calloff cannot be re-fevered; the kennel-clerks are not to waste the attempt.

Item: the rising musters below the outworks at a strength of seventy banners and thirty-one peoples, under one charter. I have read the charter. It is short, which is how I know it is dangerous. Long laws are written to be escaped. Short ones are written to be kept.

Item: they now hold Unspring, Mark, and the calloff-word, which is to say: our snares, our trackers, and our hunters are so much scrap. The Made does not hunt and cannot be snared. Let them bring a wood’s tricks to an anvil.

Item: provision to the outworks doubled. The manufactory drums to beat without interval; the garrisons above the eighty-fourth to be fed before the garrisons below it. What the tower owns, the tower may spend, and I intend to spend it by weight.

Item: our friend reports from the muster. The bread arrived before the army did, as ever; his standing has never been higher; they gave his wagons an escort of freed hounds, which he found — his word — merry. He reports also that the woman no longer sleeps. Good. Sleepless generals sign anything. Note to the willing below: press no advantage yet. A tired hand signs; a suspicious one checks the figures twice. Let her tire further.

Item: the hounds took no notice of him. I record this because the kennel-clerks assure me a hound cannot be deceived about a man, and he has now walked through ten thousand of them unremarked. Either the clerks flatter their stock, or my friend is by now so wholly the thing he pretends that there is nothing false left in him for a dog to find. I collared him for exactly this quality. On quiet evenings I confess it unsettles even me.

Item: the siege begins when they touch the first rampart. Open a new page. Head it: expenditures.

PART V

THE MADE

floors 81–100 · and the descent

Chapter 38 — The Siege of the Outworks

Nix counted the squad in the dark, because the dark was where counting mattered most.

“Eleven,” he said. “Eleven, and the eleventh is asleep. Mag. Mag, you beautiful idiot, you cannot sleep in a sap-trench, the trench is damper than you are and twice as likely to fall on us.”

“Wasn’t sleeping,” said Mag, who was a kobold, and had been asleep. “Was listening to the wall.”

That was the trouble. On this floor you listened to the wall, and the wall listened back.

Three days ago the rising had come up out of the Pale Court’s green dark into a country with no country in it. Nix had stood on the last stolen grass with the cut-down standard he’d hidden behind at the Kingsfield — two years and seventy floors ago, when hiding was the whole of his craft — and looked at the thing above them, and for once had found nothing funny to say. Below floor eighty-one, every land they’d crossed had been a place the King stole. From here up it was all a thing the King built. Slag-fill and rivet. Iron-bramble on the ramparts, grown like thorn and meaning it. A rampart of riveted plate ran across the whole world, out of sight both ways, and there was no soil under it, and nothing grew, and the crows that mobbed its embrasures were the only honest life in sight.

The First Outwork, the sapper-captain called it. Dara Sappersrest, her face grey with the fill her people had been made to dig, had said the rest plainly, the way keepers said things: the tower is built of us. My sappers are in that wall. Rivet by rivet. Some of them went in alive.

So the rising had gone into the ground, because the sappers had left them a road there. The sap-head ran in under the outwork’s footing, shored by dead men’s craft, and at its end sat a charge and a cache of demolition-lore twenty years in the hoarding. Above it, the Furnace-Trench ran with trench-gas and slag-light, and the plan — Vega’s plan, and Dara’s, argued out over a light-table until the lamps guttered twice — was simple the way a hammer is simple. Get the work-gangs clear. Fire the gas. Drop the King’s first wall into the King’s own hole.

Nix’s part came before the fire. It always did, now.

“Collar-breakers,” he said, “on me. Count off.”

They counted. Eleven voices, none of them born to the same floor, three of them wearing collars they had thrown down themselves — Nix had watched each one do it, and every time it was the finest thing he had ever seen, and he had seen a wyrm and a storm-queen and Vega eating soup. They went up the sap and out into the trench-dark below the plate, where the second wave crouched with rivet-steel shields — the tower’s own outwork-plate, stripped and turned, Vega’s forge-crews grinning behind armor with the King’s rivets still in it.

Somewhere above, a word went out that was not a word so much as a craft. Unrivet. Nix felt it in his teeth. All along the rampart’s face, seams that had held for an age let go together, plate shrieking off plate, and the whole first wall of the Made opened like a badly sewn coat.

Then it was noise, and Nix ran into it.

The outwork imps came scuttling down the breach the way imps fought — badly, and forever, rivet-fisted, driven onto the shields by something that was not courage. Nix had been driven like that once. He knew the gait of it. He went in low, under a swung fist, and got his hand flat on the imp’s collar, and said the word the rising had carried since floor twenty like a coal carried in a horn.

Break-collar.

The collar cracked. Not like iron. Like ice off a rope. The imp sat down in the slag with its mouth open — they always sat down, that was the first thing freedom did, it took the legs — and stared at its own fists as if they had just been given back.

“Up you get,” Nix said. “You’re one of mine now. What’s your name? You’ve got one. It’s under there somewhere. It’ll keep. Shield-line’s that way, hot food’s behind it, and nobody — ” he caught the imp’s chin, made it look at him, goblin to imp, small thing to small thing — “nobody drives you. Ever again. Go.”

It went. Toward the food, which was correct, and away from the King, which was the whole war.

Around him his eleven were doing the same, and behind the shields the second wave was doing it, and the count was running in Nix’s head the way counts did, all on its own. Two. Five. Nine. A gate-hellknight, faceless in riveted plate, froze with its halberd up while Mag hung off its arm shrieking the word, and when the collar went the knight knelt in the breach and would not rise for a long while, and then rose theirs — the heaviest infantry the rising owned, and it had walked in on the other side. Fourteen. Twenty. Twenty-two.

Not all of them. A hellknight took Brann’s spear through the collar and died before the word could reach it, and Nix stood over the body — the enemy’s body — and hated the arithmetic that made him glad the spear had been faster. A death that should not have been needed. There was a whole column of those in his count, and it never stopped running either.

Then the wall itself joined the battle, and the count stopped meaning anything for a while.

Wall-flesh, the sappers called it, because sappers name things true. A groaning length of rampart shrugged out of the line of the plate and reached — reached, with arms of mortared bone and rivet, the dead in the fill waking wrong and dragging at the living to bring them in. And behind it came the Warden. Rivetgrim: a walking section of the outwork, wall made beast, studded with the bones of the crews built into it, and it did not roar. Walls don’t. It came on quiet, at the pace of something that has never once needed to hurry, and it took a forge-hand into its plate and sealed the seam before anyone could reach her.

“Clear the trench!” Dara’s voice, cracked brass. “All gangs — clear the trench, down the sap, NOW!”

Nix ran the trench-lip the wrong way, because his squads were still in it. He counted them out — eight, nine, Mag hauling a freed imp by the wrist, eleven, and behind them Vega’s crews walking backward with the word, Unrivet, Unrivet, peeling the Warden’s plate off it a seam at a time so that it came on stripping itself like a man shedding coats in a fire.

The last sapper cleared the sap-mouth. Dara looked once at the wall her people were buried in, and lit the charge.

The trench-gas fired with a sound Nix felt in the ground rather than heard, and the First Outwork of the Made went down into its own footing — not toppled but dropped, all at once, the way a lie drops when the last man stops telling it. The dust stood over it in a column. When it cleared, Rivetgrim lay in the ruin as dead plate, and what had been driven out of it was already gone, drained down and out of any land that had ever been anyone’s — an eviction, the shardminds called that, and for once the word was gentle enough. The dead in the wall were only dead now. That was all liberation could give them, and it gave it.

In the quiet after, Nix did the count.

Eleven collar-breakers had gone in. Thirty-one people stood around him at the muster — his eleven, and twenty who had woken up on the wrong side of the battle and walked out of it on the right one, sitting in the slag learning their own names.

Thirty-one. He wrote it on his slate, and under it, because the number mattered more than any number he had ever counted, he wrote it again.

⁂

The Chain Yards never went quiet, and so nobody heard the rising come in.

Floor eighty-two was a din you wore like weather: hammer on link, link on drum, drum on gantry, and under it all the hiss of red chain going into black water. The tower forged its chains here — every shackle, every hoist-line, every drag-chain that bound every floor above and below — and it forged them with slave-gangs whose own shackles were the finest work on the floor. Wend Linkside said so himself, and showed them, turning his ankle to the forge-light with a craftsman’s disgusted pride: a quench-hardened link he had been filing at in the dark for eleven years with a file he kept hidden in a drain.

“Eleven years,” Nix said. “I like you. That’s a real number. That’s a number with teeth.”

The battle for the yards was fought the way Vega fought now, which was to say mostly against the machinery. Her forge-crews jammed the trip-hammer line with its own tongs, and the endless row of tower-driven hammers beat itself lame; they seized the Great Hoist, and then every chain on the floor answered the rising — drag-lines, hoists, and the shackles on ten hundred ankles alike. Wend taught them the word his people had wanted made flesh for a generation, the word the yards existed to prevent. Break-chain. It snapped any binding at a saying. The slaves struck off their own shackles with it, and some of them wept, and some of them laughed, and one old giantess just stood holding her two halves of link, one in each fist, weighing them, as if the scales of something long unbalanced had finally come level.

The Warden nearly spoiled all of it. Anchorwrath came off the drums at the shift-change — every drag-line in the yard welded into one thrashing anchor-serpent, hung with the bones of the crews forged into it — and it fought the only way a chain knows, by binding and hauling. It took six of the shield-line off their feet in one coil and ran them toward the quench-pits, and the pits were still boiling.

Nix got there first, because he was lightest, and had spent a war being lightest. He went up the moving chain itself, link over link — do not think about it, counting is better than thinking, four links, nine links, fifteen — and jammed Wend’s master-file crosswise into the living seam where link ground on link, and the serpent balked just long enough. Vega’s voice came down the yard like a rockfall, slow and complete as her people’s speech always was: “Break-chain. Unrivet. Now, all crews, and do not stop saying it.” The words walked down the serpent’s length in a wave, and Anchorwrath fell out of the air as dead links, a hundred fathoms of it, ringing on the forge-floor like the world’s largest purse being emptied. The drowned in it were freed to rest. The stair-lift’s curtain of unfinished chain ran slack and swung open.

And out of the wreck of the fight came Nix’s favorite arithmetic again, grown bigger. A quench knight — a steaming tower of pit-hardened plate that had held the pit-edge against them for half the battle — knelt at the end with its collar in frost-cracked pieces around its knees, and when it rose it looked down at Nix from twice Vega’s height and made a sound that its welded mouth had not been built for. It was trying to say a name. Its own, Nix judged, or someone’s it had lost.

“Take your time,” Nix said. “We’re going the same way. You can walk with me and say it when it’s ready.”

It walked with him. It never stopped walking with him, afterward — through the whole war above, a tireless quenched shadow at his shoulder — and by evening his squads were calling it the Kettle, and it seemed pleased, so far as plate can.

He did the count at the yard-gate. He had brought sixty up from the outwork, counting the twenty new. He marched out with a hundred and forty, and the difference was not recruits sent up from below. The difference had been issued to the enemy that morning.

The supply-sleds met them at the gate, hauled up the freed lifts from eighty floors of friendly country, and on the third sled were bread-crates stamped with Roothollow’s mark, the loaves still warm from Berrin Or’s own ovens at the bottom of the world — that good dense fair-day bread, sweet as a feast morning. Nix took his loaf and ate it slowly and thought, as he always thought, that whatever else the rising had, it had one man who never once forgot that an army was made of stomachs. He counted the loaves for the new-freed twice, to be sure there were enough.

There were. There were always enough. Berrin saw to it.

⁂

Floor eighty-three was a griddle, and the rising crossed it the way you cross a griddle: lightly, never stopping — three days and nights of it — and only where a man who knew the fire told you to put your feet.

The Furnace Roads ran on iron gratings over open furnace-pits, road-lattice slung across a lake of fire, glowing red from beneath so that the whole floor was lit like a forge from the wrong side. Cinder-swifts strafed the causeways. Slag dripped from the gantries. The road-crews who stoked it — soot-blind, half-cooked, losing one of their own through a weakened grate every shift — sent their boss to the gate to meet the rising, and Grint Stokegate looked the war-council up and down and said: “Whoever walks first walks where I say. The gratings lie. I know which ones.”

“That’s me,” Nix said. “I’m first. I’m always first, it’s because I’m littlest, they pretend it’s an honor.”

He had walked point before, on the Trapdoor Fields, twenty-nine floors and a lifetime ago, an army holding its breath behind him. He walked it again now, reading the glow with Grint at his shoulder — bright cherry holds, white lies, orange sings before it gives — and behind them the host came over the fire two by two, and behind the host Vega’s crews worked the floor’s own mercy. The word for it was Bank. Said right, with craft behind it, it damped any furnace to embers, and the freed road-crews said it like a prayer they had only just been allowed to finish. Stretch by stretch the griddle cooled to a road. Stretch by stretch the red light went out of the world below, and the dark that came up in its place was just dark — honest, cool, sleepable.

The Warden fought them at the road’s end, because it had to; that was the last cruelty of the collar on the Made’s own constructs, that even the tower’s furniture was driven. Cindergrate rose out of the under-grate ash wearing the road itself as armor — the fused slag and bone of a hundred fallen crews, a golem of everyone the floor had eaten — and it dropped the gratings out from under the vanguard’s feet, and it belched cinder, and for a hundred heartbeats it was the worst fight since the wall. They banked its fires out from under it. They broke it on the cooled plate. The burned crews in it were freed, and Grint shut the master damper with his own scalded hands, and the furnace-roads of the Made went cold for the first time since they were laid.

At the muster on the cooled road, under a gantry-sky with no fire in it, Ede’s officers took the count of the whole host, as they did at every lift. Nix stood in the lamplight and listened to the tally come in, company by company, tongue by tongue, and watched the general’s face do the sum.

They had come up into the Made three floors ago with the army the stolen world had given them. They stood now, at the foot of the fourth floor, larger — larger by every sapper walked out of a wall, every chain-slave struck loose, every road-crew off the griddle; larger by two hundred of the enemy’s own soldiery, sitting at the cook-fires relearning their names while the Kettle ladled for them. Every other army in the history of every world got smaller by fighting. The rising had finally, fully, become the thing the collar-breakers had promised at the Kingsfield: an army that grew by advancing, a line of battle that fed on its own victories, the tower’s design run backward.

“They should surrender now,” Nix said to the Kettle. “Save everyone the stairs. I hate the stairs. Have I mentioned the stairs?”

The Kettle rumbled. Overhead, at the road’s end, the stair-lift to floor eighty-four stood welded shut behind a seam of fresh iron — not barred, not chained. Welded. And down through it, riding the dead cold air of the banked furnaces, came a smell the whole muster fell silent under, one company at a time, as it reached them. Flux, and arc-smoke, and under those, unmistakable, scorched flesh.

Grint Stokegate spat on the cold grate and named the floor above them the way a man names a disease.

“The Welding Halls,” he said. “Where soldiers are made. Now you’ll see what the cages below were feeding, all these years.”

Nix stood a long time looking up at the welded seam, and did not count anything, and could not think of one funny thing to say.

Chapter 39 — The Welded

The seam split at dawn, if the Made had dawns, and Ede Stonhal led the rising into the Welding Halls, and after that nobody spoke above a murmur for a day and a night, and it was not an order. No order was needed. Some floors give their own orders.

The halls were lit blue-white, arc-light stuttering off black iron so that everything moved even when nothing did. The air tasted of flux. Under the flux was the other smell, the one that had come down through the welded seam and silenced the muster on the cooled roads below, and Ede made herself stop naming it, because naming it did not help and she would need her wits.

Rows of rigs ran away into the dark on either hand, arc-arms hanging slack. Between the rigs stood the racks.

She had been told what the racks held. Sef Seamside had come down through the sap-ways two nights before — a weld-captive, thin as a file, with the un-welding lore scratched on slate and lead in a bundle under one arm — and had told the war-council in a voice worn perfectly flat what the floor above them was for. This is where they take a captured soul and weld it into a soldier, seam by seam, iron over flesh, until what is left stands up and takes orders. This is where imps are made. This is where hellknights are made. Every welded thing you have fought since the First Outwork was made in these halls, and every one of them was somebody, dragged back up from a floor you freed.

The council had heard it in silence. There was nothing to ask. There was only the one thing to do, and the plan for doing it: take the Overseer’s Gantry, and run the rigs backward.

Now Ede walked the aisle between the racks with her people fanning quiet behind her, and the telling, which had been terrible, turned out to have been kind. The fresh-welded hung cooling in their rows, rank on rank of them, iron half-closed over faces, and some were still, and some were setting, and some — she did not let herself look away; a general who orders a thing looked at must look at it — some were awake inside the seams, and screaming, and no sound came out, because the mouths had been finished first.

Beside her, Sliver spoke against her palm, and for once the old splinter’s dryness had nothing in it but ash. I remember when this craft was for mending hulls, it said. I carried the smith who invented the seam. I am glad she is dead, and I have never once been glad of that before.

“Gantry,” Ede said quietly, and the word went back down the line, and the rising went to work.

⁂

The battle for the Welding Halls was short, and grim, and won almost gently, and Ede would remember all her life that the worst floor in the tower was the one that cost the fewest lives to take. The Made had not garrisoned it heavily. It had never needed to. Its horror was its garrison.

The seam-checker imps came at them down the rack-rows, probe-fisted, welding shut whatever they caught, and Nix’s collar-breakers met them between the rigs and freed eleven of thirteen, and the two that could not be reached in time died the deaths that should not have been needed, and were laid out properly, with their collars broken after, which was all anyone could still do for them and was done for every one.

The rejected lot came up out of the Scrap-Sink at the fighting’s loudest — botched welds, souls fused wrong and thrown away, melted into one shambling grief that dragged the living toward the racks — and there was no freeing there, no collar, nothing to reach; the mercy was the breaking. Vega’s crews broke it with long-axes and the Unrivet word, weeping while they worked, the way her people wept, silently and without stopping the work, and when it came apart the scrapped souls in it were freed to rest, and the halls were quieter by one sound Ede had not known she was hearing until it stopped.

Seamwright held the gantry. The master engine itself: a Warden grown out of the welding-rigs, arc-arms and clamp and cruel will, and it fought by making soldiers — reached into the reserve-racks and sent its own half-made against them, and reached for the living too, to weld them standing. It caught a Frosthall spear-thane by the arm and had the plate half-laid before three crews together drove it back off him with the new word, the word Sef had carried down the sap-ways in a bundle, the word the whole floor had been building toward since the first collar broke at the Red Warcamp.

Unseam.

They said it against the Warden until its own work ran backward, seams opening as fast as its arms could lay them, and then Ulfar Coalbrow put a forge-maul the length of a roof-beam through its gantry-heart, and the arc died all down the halls at once, every rig together, and the eviction took what had worn the engine and dragged it down out of the world, and the Welding Halls stopped. For the first time since the tower was raised, they simply stopped.

In the ringing dark, Sef Seamside climbed the Overseer’s Gantry alone, and stood at the master-board a moment with her hands flat on it, and then ran the rigs the other way.

⁂

The un-making took three days, and Ede stayed for all of it.

She had learned this about herself at the Red Warcamp cages and never since found reason to doubt it: there is a kind of work a commander cannot delegate, not because others would do it worse but because ordering it done and not watching is a coin the soul does not have. So she stood at the foot of the Cooling Racks while the crews and the craft brought the fresh-welded down one at a time, and Sef’s people found the seams, and the word was said, and the iron opened.

Not all of it opened. That was the first cruelty of the mending: Unseam split what had not set. What had set, stayed. A woman came off the racks with her hands her own again and her back iron forever. A boy — he had been taken young; the tower took what the press-gangs caught — walked free with one arm ending at a welded gauntlet no craft could unmake without unmaking the arm inside it. The healers and the smiths worked side by side at the racks’ foot, one trade now, and Vega moved among them saying the thing she said, that anything broken can be mended, and Ede listened to her say it and watched the seam-scarred file past and thought: mended is not unbroken. Nobody knew that better than Vega. She said it anyway. Perhaps that was what the word was for.

They came off the racks and they gave their names, and the clerks wrote them, and Ede stood with her muster-roll under her arm — the rebuilt roll, carried since the Cindermouth, the habit of it worn into her like a sword-hilt — and heard the names of six floors she had freed read back to her by people who had been dragged out of those floors after her army had marched on and up. Freed at the Frozen Sea, taken again on the Jarl’s Road. Freed at the Frosthall, taken at the feast. The sale’s ledger ran in both directions, and the rising had spent two years reading only one of them.

On the second day, they brought down the deep rows, the ones welded farthest back, and a soldier came off the rack who did not wait for the clerks.

He was iron from the jaw down. The seams on him had set years past — plate gone dull, rivets rusted at their crowns, a hellknight’s finished body — but the last weld, the one over the will, had been laid fresh; the tower re-welded its old stock before sending it up to the Herald’s muster, Sef said, the way a cooper re-hoops an old barrel. So the will-seam split. Everything below the jaw stayed. He came down off the rack on iron legs that knew how to march, and above the iron mouth were a man’s two eyes, and the eyes found Ede across the racks’ foot and stopped, and then his whole body did the thing his body remembered before his mind can have caught it up.

He came to attention. Heels together. Shoulders back. Shield-side arm crooked to carry what was not there.

Ede knew the stance before she knew the face. Third file, shield-side, the anchor of the push — you built a shield-wall around men who stood like that. And then the face, above the iron: older, ruined, the same. Wat Kello. Five years on the Stone of the Climb in Roothollow, the fifth name from the top of the thirty, cut there by her own hand with the chisel skipping because she had still been shaking in those years, and she had smoothed the skip with her thumb and left the name crooked. Wat Kello, who had held the third file at the Red Warcamp while the trap closed, whose body had never been found among the caged nor among the dead, and now she knew why, and the knowing was a whole war’s weight arriving at once. Not caged. Not killed. Sold on. Passed up the tower like goods, held in the dark for five years — the Silk Vaults, the Trophy tiers, whatever ledger-line he had been entered under — and fed at the last to these racks, to be finished into the enemy and sent down against his own.

The cages had been the sale’s first price. Here stood the full price, at attention, waiting on her order.

“Stand easy, Wat,” Ede said. Her voice held. She had spent two years learning to make it hold. “Stand easy. You’re stood down. You’ve been stood down five years, soldier. It’s done.”

The iron body eased, joint by joint, as if easing were a drill it was performing from memory. Somebody had run for the banner — it went by turns among the freed of Shieldwall since Dun Harrow fell, and it was Ede’s whole reserve of luck that it was near — and they brought it up the aisle, and Wat Kello looked at it a long moment, the old grey cloth, the wall-and-star gone thread-bare at the fly. He put out one gauntleted hand and took a fold of it between iron fingers with a delicacy that undid half the room, and the eyes above the iron mouth shone and spilled, and the mouth itself could do nothing; it was the tower’s mouth; the tower had made it and made it badly, for the tower had never needed its soldiers to do this.

Then he worked the mouth anyway. It moved like a rusted hinge, and the voice that came out of it was scraped hollow, a voice off an anvil, one word wide.

The word was not his name. It was not her name.

“Bread,” said Wat Kello.

The racks’ foot went still. Ede did not move at all.

“Again,” she said, gently.

The hinge worked. “Bread.” His free hand rose, shaking, and touched his own jaw, his nose — smell, the gesture said, the man who came smelled — and Ede stood in the blue-lit halls and was five years and eighty-four floors away, in a burnt hall on floor sixty hearing a freed prisoner rasp about a hooded courier at the Warcamp who smelled of Roothollow bread; on a trophy floor with her own shield on the wall and toll-marks on its rim; at a watch-fire telling a splinter of crystal the one thing she could tell no one alive. He was giving testimony. Five years welded shut, and the first act of his freed will — before his own name, before hers — was to report to his commander the last thing he had learned in the field. The man who sold us came to the cages to count us. I marked him. Bread.

“I know,” Ede said.

She said it very low, for him alone, and she watched the two human eyes above the iron read everything her face had spent a year hiding from an army. She let them read it. Five years he had carried his one word up through the dark, and he had earned the truth ahead of every living soul but one: that his commander knew the courier’s name, had known it since the Pale Court, and had climbed beside the man since, and eaten his bread at every muster, and said nothing, waiting on proof that would hold in open council — because the courier was beloved, and the accusation would shatter what the proof must carry whole.

“You’re the proof, Wat,” she said. “You and a serving-list and a ledger. Hold my secret three days more. Then I’ll ask you to say your word again, where it finishes this.”

The iron hand closed careful as a jeweler on the fold of the banner. Above the finished mouth the eyes changed, and it took her a heartbeat to understand what they were doing, because the mouth could not help them do it. He was smiling. Third file, shield-side. Give that man a wall to hold and he would hold it till the hills wore down.

The price of the sale, Sliver said against her palm, is standing in front of you, and it is still hers to spend. There is nothing in my long memory crueler than this floor. And nothing braver in it than that man.

⁂

That night Ede sat late with Mistress Calyne over a slate and a cipher-book, at a shielded lamp in a corner of the halls where the rigs stood dead, and between them, piece by piece, they laid the trap that had been two years in the gathering. The serving-list from a Herald’s boot. The toll-ledger’s payment, moved up the tower the week of the sale. The granary dates. And now a witness with an iron mouth and one word in it, worth all the rest together.

“Before the Herald’s Gate,” Calyne said. Her grey eyes were the same temperature they had been the day she arrived at the Broodthrone accusing everyone and trusted by no one, the coldest and least-loved and most constant hunter the rising had. “In open council, staged in sight of the gate, with every banner watching. It must be there. Do you see why?”

Ede saw. Five floors above them, past the warrens and the drill-lines and the iron chapels, Malgrim’s muster-gate stood at the top of the Made like a keystone, and behind its arch sat the owner of a certain willing collar. “Because there,” she said, “he has nowhere left to run but upward. Into the arms of the thing he serves. In front of everyone he fooled.”

“In front of everyone I could not convince,” Calyne said, without bitterness, which was the only way she said anything. She closed the cipher-book — her own hand, her life’s hunt, blood not yet on its margin — and looked at it a moment as one looks at a tool that has one use left in it. “Five floors, general. Keep him warm. Keep him baking. Let the whole rising love him for five more floors.”

Above them, in the dark of the racks, the freed of the Welding Halls slept their first sleep as themselves, and the halls that had made the King’s army lay silent, already being rebuilt by Vega’s crews to run backward forever. And up the long iron miles, five floors and a gate away, a quartermaster who smelled of warm bread was tallying loaves for an army he had been selling for twenty years, item by item, to the patient man behind the drums.

The rising would come to him presently. It was growing as it climbed.

Chapter 40 — The Vizier's Floor

Five floors lay between the dead welding-rigs and the Herald’s Gate, and the rising took them in nineteen days, and Rhovan of the Grey Rides — lately of nowhere, his Wake-token two years broken over his own knee — spent those nineteen days doing what he had always done best, which was watching everyone, and being wrong about the one that mattered.

He would think of it afterward in exactly those terms. An archer’s reckoning. Nineteen days of perfect watchfulness, every shaft in the quiver counted, and the whole time the target stood at his elbow handing him bread.

The Imp Warrens fell first, and fell strangely: not stormed but woken. Floor eighty-five was the barracks-slum of the Made — iron cell-blocks stacked in reeking, smoke-hung tiers where the tower’s soldiery denned between shifts — and Wend Sootside’s whisper-network had been working it from within for years before the rising ever reached the stair. The warrens ran out of sight in every direction, tier over tier under a soot sky of their own making, flue-fires guttering like low red stars, and the din of a hundred thousand soldiers at rest never once went quiet in the two days the army spent inside it. The Warden himself was the proof of what the smoke-net claimed: Fluegrim, the warren’s enforcer, a hellknight welded huge, whose collar was old and crazed with a hundred half-doubts. Nix went to him under a parley-rag, alone but for the Kettle, and talked for half a day — nobody ever learned what about, and Nix, asked, said “shift-rations, mostly, and being small in a big war” — and at the end of it Fluegrim reached up and broke his own collar with his own hands, which no one had ever seen, and his whole warren woke behind him. A barracks of the King’s army came over in a single evening, tier by tier, like a hillside letting go. The rising gained a floor, a spell for walking unchallenged in the tower’s own livery, and four thousand soldiers who knew every corridor of the Made from the inside.

Berrin Or fed them all by midnight. Rhovan stood in the ration-line dark and watched the old provisioner move down the cell-tiers with his tally-slate and his lamplit smile, learning the freed imps’ names as fast as they recovered them, doubling the portion of any soldier who looked at the food as if it might be taken back. Twenty years past, a man of the Grey Rides had taught Rhovan that you know a faction by what it does when nobody bids on the outcome. He remembered thinking, in the warrens: there stands the only one of us with nothing underneath. What Berrin did came from Berrin. It made a man restful just to watch.

He filed the observation where he filed everything, and the army climbed.

The Bastion Line tried to drill them to death. Floor eighty-six was discipline made a landscape, ramparts and parade-grounds running out of sight, ranked ballistae on the firing-steps and the tramp of the endless drill going through the ground like a pulse; it was the first floor to kill more of the rising than it lost, and for two days it was the war at its oldest and worst. The parade-ground had no far side that any scout found; the drill came out of one horizon and wheeled away into the other, and the slave-crews of Wallrest were still building the ramparts longer at both ends, as if the tower meant its army to drill to the rim of the world. Then Dara Wallrest’s stolen counter-signs reached the Command Bastion in a freed clerk’s hand, and the word Countermand went down the drill-lines like frost down a window, and whole columns wheeled on their own officers’ orders into confusion. Bastionheart’s collar broke at the drill’s dead center, and the drill stopped — the first silence on that floor in an age — and an army that had never once been permitted to halt stood at rest, and then, rank by waking rank, formed up again facing the other way.

The Siege Gardens they blighted in an afternoon and marched for three days after, Vega’s crews and the garden-thralls souring the iron-seed in its beds so that a season’s crop of rams and towers rotted unripe in the rows; the Warden flailed and fell apart into dead scrap, and Nael Ramside wept at the seed-vault door, and the rising marched out with engine-iron and iron-seed enough to grow siege-works of its own. Three days, because the gardens were a country: terraced beds stepping away to the horizon under trellised girder-vines, oozing black oil for sap, half-grown rams creaking in their rows like an orchard in wind — the Made’s mockery of every green floor it had been built by unmaking. The Iron Chantries were harder, because the enemy there was belief — the King’s black war-chapels, censer-smoke and iron liturgy working to make the bound mean the words — and Rhovan, who had spent a life being loyal in fractions, hated the floor with a completeness that surprised him. There was no light on all its miles but censer-glow, and no weather but the incense — a smoke that hung to the horizon and worked at the mind the way damp works at iron. They rang the Iron Belfry against the liturgy and the whole compelled congregation cracked awake; the counter- creed came out of the Reliquary and became a word, Recant, the art of breaking a faith that had been welded on; the freed furnace-priests stripped their censers and asked, as one body, to march with the van. Vega took the chapels’ brass — censer-brass, and later the great sounding-brass of floor eighty-nine’s silenced proclamation-horns — and began, in the night-camps, to found and hammer at something the size of a cottage that she would not yet name.

And on the Herald’s Road, under the last colossal horns, with the King’s titles choked to silence and Trumpetsteel’s freed voice blaring the King’s own decrees backward into the muster ahead, Rhovan stood at the road’s head and got his first sight of the Herald’s Gate: a fortress- square of mustering-yards under a gatehouse like a cliff, the drums beyond it beating men into soldiers, beating the tempo of the whole Made, never stopping. Five floors in nineteen days. Behind the rising, the warrens fed it, the bastion drilled it, the gardens grew its engines, the chantries sang for it. The tower’s last approach had been made the rising’s own arsenal.

That night Mistress Calyne found him at the picket-line and asked him, in the flat grey voice that twenty years of hating her had taught him to obey anyway, to call the full council for the morning. Every banner, every keeper, every captain of the freed. Staged on the road’s head, she said, in open sight of the gate.

“Whatever it is,” Rhovan said, “you might tell me first. Once. For the novelty.”

Calyne looked at him a long moment, the crows of the Grey Rides in her grey look, his handler once, the coldest constant in his sky. “I have been wrong in my method my whole life,” she said. “I worked hidden, and so the truth I found could always be hidden from. This one gets no shadow to die in. It gets noon, and witnesses, and a wall at its back. Call the council, Rhovan.”

Which was, he would understand within a day, the most she ever told anyone, and more than she had ever told him.

⁂

They staged the council on the Herald’s Road’s last rise, beneath the silenced Proclamation Arch, with the muster-gate filling the whole north sky. A hundred banners on the poles the King’s standards had hung from. The charter-table down the middle, the decade of it scarred and ringed; Ede at its head; the keepers and the captains and the freed Wardens in a horseshoe five ranks deep. Drums beyond the gate, always, like weather.

Rhovan stood at Ede’s shoulder with his bow unstrung, as protocol asked, and watched Calyne rise with a folio under her arm, and watched — because watching was his craft — the faces. So he alone, perhaps, saw the exact instant at which Berrin Or, laughing at something a Frosthall thane had said, counted the council’s exits.

It meant nothing to him. He filed it. The target handed him bread that very morning.

“Two years ago at the Frosthall,” Calyne began, without preamble, without warmth, without anything a listener could love, as ever, “the feast was struck by Heralds seeded through the serving-lines. I said that morning there was a collar inside the charter’s circle. I was not believed, being myself suspected. Here is the serving-list Nix took from a dead Herald’s boot.” She laid it on the charter-table. “The seating it follows was settled the night before, by four people. I am one. Two are dead at their doors. Attend, please, to the fourth.”

The council had gone very quiet. Rhovan had not moved, and could not afterward say when his heart had started to slam.

“Item,” Calyne said, in a voice from which she had removed everything, and Rhovan, who knew her ciphers better than any soul alive, understood that she was quoting the enemy’s own ledger-form back at him deliberately, a huntsman’s courtesy, a spymaster’s contempt. “The toll-ledger Rhovan took at floor eleven shows a payment moved up the tower the same week Shieldwall’s route was sold, drawn against Roothollow accounts. Item: the granary of Roothollow, burned that spring, was rebuilt that same summer, by unrecorded coin. I have the mason’s dates. Item: at the Pale Court hangs a Shieldwall shield sold upward through the toll-roads out of Roothollow that identical week. Item —” and here she paused, the only pause she permitted herself, and into it the drums beat twice — “three days ago, in the Welding Halls, a soldier of Shieldwall’s thirty, sold five years since, was unsealed. His witness is one word wide. He has carried it since the cages. He smelled the courier who came to count the merchandise.”

Ede said, quietly, “Say your word, Wat.”

He came forward through the horseshoe on iron legs, the banner-fold still in his fist, and the iron mouth worked, and the word fell into the council like a stone down a well.

“Bread.”

Rhovan did not understand for one whole breath. He was watching Berrin with everyone else and his mind was still running the numbers of every suspect he had ever kept books on — Calyne foremost, always Calyne — and the column would not sum, and then it summed.

The bread at the ration-lines. The bread at the cell-tiers, at midnight, learning the freed imps’ names. Roothollow bread, the freed prisoner had said at floor twenty, a hooded man who smelled of it. The granary. The tolls. The seating. The supply-trains that arrived, every single time, one march behind the enemy’s best-laid ambush — never before. The arrow Berrin took at the feast, in the shoulder, in the fleshy part, saving Ede in front of two hundred witnesses. Talking Ede out of hiding at the war’s very first hour, the oldest friend, launching the hunt — launching the hunt for himself, steering it, feeding it, twenty years of warm scenes and Rhovan had eaten from his hand that morning, and every one of them turned over at once like soil under a plow, and what was underneath had been underneath the whole time.

Berrin Or stood up slowly at the charter-table, sixty years old, flour on his cuff, the face the entire rising trusted above any face alive, and said, mildly, “Wat. Lad. You’ve been iron five years. Memory’s a cruel smith, and this cruel woman —”

“Open your shirt,” said Calyne.

The council did not breathe. Berrin looked around the horseshoe once, at all of them, keepers and captains and thanes, and Rhovan watched him find no exit in any face, and watched the warmth leave his eyes the way heat leaves a forge — not blown out; banked, hidden down under ash, where it had lived all along. That was the worst of it, Rhovan thought ever after. He had expected a stranger to surface. What surfaced was Berrin exactly, the same kind eyes, sums running behind them, and the sums had simply always been these sums.

“You were the best of us, Cal,” Berrin said, and his voice was the gentle, floury, evening voice that had fed a hundred floors, wholly unchanged, and that gentleness in that moment was the most obscene thing Rhovan had heard in a long life. “You always knew. Nobody would ever believe you. That’s why I never had to have you killed.”

He was old, and a provisioner, and cornered, and he was faster than all of it — faster than his years, faster than the two Frosthall thanes at his shoulders, faster by far than any reader of any warm scene would ever have dreamt. The knife came out of his sleeve where a tally-stylus should have been — a thin boning-knife, a kitchen thing, honed away to a whisper — and it went between Calyne’s ribs on the upstroke before her folio hit the table.

Even then, dying, she was working. Rhovan saw it and would see it the rest of his days: she did not clutch the wound, she clutched the man — both hands on his collar-band, hauling his shirt open to the whole council as her knees went — so that every banner of the rising saw with its own eyes, in noon light, the dull iron ring seated against the old man’s breastbone. Worn smooth. Grown into the flesh at its rim. A Herald’s collar, willing, chosen, twenty years polished by the skin of the rising’s beloved.

Proof laid, she let go, and fell against the charter-table, and was dead before Ede caught her.

Berrin ran.

He ran north, toward the gate, into the open killing-ground before it, and the council was a heartbeat behind him in shock and would be a hundred years behind him in understanding, and only two things in all that noon moved fast enough to matter. One was Rhovan’s hands, which had his bow strung and drawn while his mind still knelt by Calyne — a Grey Rides shot at a fleeing back at eighty paces, loosed clean, and it flew true and did not arrive: a gate-tender hulk, iron doors of shoulders, stepped out of the wall’s shadow and took the shaft in its plate the way a roof takes hail. The gate had been watching its investment. The gate had always been watching.

The other was the muster-gate itself, which opened.

Not wide. A hand’s width, a door held by a doorman for a man expected to dinner, and Berrin Or went through it without breaking stride, out of the rising, up into the Made, home to the owner of his collar. The last Rhovan ever saw of him on the free side of the wall was flour on a cuff and the glint of the iron at his throat, and then the gate swallowed him, and the drums beyond it did not so much as change their beat, and that was the most eloquent thing the tower had ever said. It had priced this day in long ago. A ledger entry does not retire.

⁂

They buried her at the road’s head that evening, under the arch her trap had needed and gotten, and the whole rising passed the grave in file, and the freed of eight floors who had cursed her name in cipher for two years laid iron and brass on the mound of the woman who had been hunting their enemy since before most of them were taken. The hateful ally, loyal all along. Rhovan spoke the Wake’s parting over her because there was no one left of her faction to do it and because he had been wrong about her the longest, which he said aloud, at the grave, in front of the army, in whole sentences, the first unfractioned truth he had told in twenty years.

Ede stood after the file had passed, alone with him, Calyne’s cipher-book in her hands. There was blood dried on its margin. She did not wipe it off, then or ever.

“You knew,” Rhovan said. It was not an accusation. He had spent the day recasting every warm scene of the age of the world; he had one recasting left in him. “Since the Pale Court. You knew and you climbed beside him.”

“I knew and I climbed beside him,” Ede said, “because knowing isn’t proving, and proving him quietly would have broken the rising in half — his half. She taught me that.” The drums beat on beyond the wall. She looked up at the gate, one hand flat on the dead woman’s book, and Rhovan watched the grief bank itself down under ash to where her sums were kept, and understood at last, coldly and completely, how alike the general and the traitor had always been, and why he had loved them both.

“He’s behind that gate,” she said. “With his owner, and his owner’s army, and every drum in the Made. And in the morning, so are we.”

The hundred banners snapped above the grave in a wind come down from nowhere, the first breath of a distant unseasonable storm, and beyond the wall the muster-drums beat men into soldiers, on and on, as they had beaten for an age, and had, though no drummer knew it, one morning left to beat.

Chapter 41 — The Battle of the Herald's Gate

Vega Coalbrow had built many things in a long life, and broken more, and she stood in the dark before the battle with her hand flat on the flank of the last thing she would ever need to build, and finished her sentence, because her people finished their sentences even when the sentence was a prayer.

“I have put into you the brass of the chapels that taught the bound to worship their chains, and the brass of the horns that shouted the thief’s glory, and the steel of the yards that quenched the shackles, and my own mark, which I once put into collar-steel and have spent this whole climb buying back. You are the apology of four floors and one smith. Ring true.”

The bell said nothing. Bells don’t, until asked properly. It hung from a gantry of grown engine-iron on a carriage the freed parade hulks had hauled up the Herald’s Road in the night, and it was the size of a cottage, and it had no name, because every name the council proposed had been too small. The freed just called it the bell.

Around it, in the last dark, the rising formed up for the greatest battle of the age, and Vega walked the line the way a smith walks a finished weld, looking for flaws and finding, floor by floor, the whole climb standing in ranks. Rivet-steel shields out of the First Outwork. Chain-flails and grapples of quench-steel from the yards. Grate-iron griddle-shields; seam-steel plate off the racks, reforged for the freed, not the bound; the drilled regulars of the Bastion Line standing at Bastionheart’s own parade-rest; Fluegrim’s warren-soldiers in the tower’s own livery; the garden-thralls’ grown rams and towers creaking at the rear on part-ripe limbs. Two years ago this army had been one disgraced captain, one lying archer, one guilty smith, and a goblin hiding behind a dropped standard. The tower had spent eighty-nine floors arming the thing that had come to unmake it.

In the night the line had given the ground its own names, as lines do, because a soldier will not die at a map-mark. The stretch of yard-iron where the shields would plant was the Anvil. The lane kept clear behind the center for the bell-carriage was the Bell-Road. The flat killing-ground under the gatehouse’s fixed lines the Bastion men called the Alley, with a drilled man’s flat humor, and nobody crossed it standing tall. And the yards themselves ran beyond any seeing — a fortress-square the size of a lowland shire, wall to far wall a hard day’s march, the King’s banners on the distant ramparts reading as weather, not cloth. Vega walked the front of all that and looked down the ranks, floor stacked upon freed floor in one iron morning, and thought the thought the whole muster was thinking, the one the Frosthall had signed and the climb had spent two years proving: not a tower. A stair.

And past the last shield stood the muster-yards of the Herald’s Gate, and Malgrim’s answer.

The King’s legions had been forming up since the gate swallowed Berrin Or — rank on iron rank filling the fortress-square from wall to wall, imps and hellknights and gate-tender hulks, ballista-lines on the gatehouse, banners of the King hanging like judgments, and over it all, on the high drum-dais they called the drum-throne, the Herald himself, beating the muster. The drums never stopped. They had beaten all night, and the beat did a thing to the blood that the chantry-priests said was kin to the liturgy: it told the bones you are a soldier, you have always been a soldier, there is nothing under the collar but the drum.

Ede came down the line in the iron morning, and stopped at the bell, and looked up at it a while.

“The whole book of the war is in that brass,” she said. “Every floor gave it a page. You’re certain of the ringing?”

“I am certain of the ringing,” Vega said slowly. “I am certain of the priests, and of the word, and of my seams. What I cannot promise is what no smith has ever promised: what the metal remembers. If it remembers the chapels, it will only frighten them. If it remembers what I asked of it —” she laid her hand on the cold flank once more — “then today the rising fights a battle mostly by declining to fight it, and I will have mended, before I die, one thing of the size of the thing I broke.”

Ede nodded, and did not offer comfort, which from her was a kindness, and went to her place under the hundred banners. Away down the ranks Vega could see Nix at the head of his collar-breakers — four hundred of them now, in threes and fives, the Kettle at his shoulder, the freed imps of six floors wearing Passfor like a borrowed coat, ready to walk into the enemy as the enemy — and she could see the chantry-priests, two thousand strong, forming their choir behind the bell-carriage in ranks deeper than any shield-wall, and she thought: no army in any history was ever drawn up like this one, with its heaviest engine a mercy, and its elite trained above all else in the touching of an enemy’s throat gently.

The sky, for what it was worth, was empty. The Storm Queen’s gales, promised by embassy and sworn by treaty-debt, were reported by the sky-skiffs to be two floors below and beating upward against their own contrary winds. Rhovan, hearing it, had said only: “Of course they are,” and gone back to fletching. No promised relief in any tale worth telling has ever arrived on time.

The drums quickened. The gates of the forming-yards swung. Vega Coalbrow spat on her hands, took up the maul she had forged for her great-grandmother’s forge and carried up a hundred floors, and the Battle of the Herald’s Gate began.

⁂

It began with steel, because it had to.

That was the bitter arithmetic the council had swallowed whole nights choking on: the bell could not ring at once. Nix’s threes and fives needed time to walk deep into the mustered ranks under Passfor’s coat; the priests could not carry the word past the drums’ own thunder unless the choir stood close, and the choir could not stand close until the enemy’s charge was spent against the shields; the whole mercy of the day hung on the rising’s line holding, unbroken and unhelped, through the worst hour the Made could hammer out of its yards. So the battle the histories would remember for its gentleness opened as ugly as anything since the Red Warcamp, and Vega fought that hour in the crush of the center hinge, where the gate-tender hulks came on like siege-towers walking.

The first charge was drummer-driven and total. Ballista-bolts came down the fixed lines off the gatehouse in flat black sleet; the shield-line staggered, planted, held; the drilled regulars of the Bastion Line took the shock at the hinge with a discipline the tower itself had spent an age beating into them, and gave it back with interest. A hulk broke the third rank’s corner and Vega met it maul to gate-arm, her feet furrowing the yard-iron, and it was strong the way engines are strong, and she was strong the way mountains’ daughters are strong, and she broke its knee and wept while she did it, because under the plate was some great captured soul that had hauled gates for a thief for fourscore years, and her maul could not tell it so.

That was the hour’s true wound, worse than the bolts. Every blow the rising struck before the ringing was a blow against the bound. The line chanted Break-collar where it could — a shield-man’s free hand on a fallen imp’s throat between one press and the next, the word said through gritted teeth — and collar by collar, even in the crush, the count that Nix had started at the First Outwork went on climbing. But the drums beat doubt back into whatever the word loosened; the freed staggered up only to be ridden under by the next driven rank; and the dead of the enemy, heaped where the shield-line met the charge, were climbing too, a heap of deaths that should not have been needed, each one of them somebody’s, each one of them the tower’s oldest crime committed one more time with the rising’s own hands.

Vega fought, and counted seams, and behind her, rank by deliberate rank, the choir came up the killing-ground with the bell.

She heard the fighting change before she saw why. Deep in the enemy’s mass — far beyond the line, out where no soldier of the rising could possibly be — collars were breaking. Faint, then not faint: that ice-off-a-rope crack, in fifties, in the enemy’s own rear ranks, and around each crack a knot of stillness spreading in the charge like calm spreading in a pot taken off the fire. The threes and fives had gone in under the borrowed coat, and the collar-breakers were doing their work in the heart of the King’s army, in the crush, in the dark of it, one gentle hand on one throat at a time. She could not see Nix. Nobody could see Nix; that had always been the point of Nix. But she could read his path across the enemy’s formation the way you read a mole’s run across a lawn — a raised seam of confusion, of ranks turning inward, of drums suddenly drumming at their own soldiers — and she found she was grinning in the middle of a battle-line with her maul running red, because the smallest soldier of the rising was carving the war’s whole meaning into its greatest battle, and the tower had no craft to answer him. How do you drum a soldier back into the collar you are standing on the wrong side of? How do you press a man who has, this very hour, been unpressed?

By mid-morning the field had settled into a shape, and the shape held long enough to be learned: the shield-line bowed across the yards like a drawn bow, anchored at the Anvil where its hinge took the weight; the choir and the bell coming up the Bell-Road behind the bow’s grip, slow as a tide-mark; the enemy’s mass pressed on the line’s whole outer face, deepest at the hinge, fed by the gatehouse down the Alley; and out in that mass, past any help, the threes and fives — a scatter of freed hands nobody could see and anybody could read, stillness spreading from them in rings, like rain beginning on a dusty road.

The Marshal answered. Malgrim’s second — a captured officer collared so long and so hard that his men said the collar had grown a man around it, rather than the reverse — wheeled the whole second echelon off the center and drove it not at the shield-line but at the bell.

He had seen what the day was, and he spent his legions the way you spend coin you despise: at the choir, at the carriage, at the gantry of grown iron, at the two thousand unarmored singers walking a bell into a battlefield at a hymn’s pace. Ballistae found the range. The carriage lost a wheel and the hulks bore the axle on their shoulders. Vega left the hinge — Ulfar’s Forge-Court legion closed the gap behind her, her brother’s voice roaring slow giantish oaths she had known since the cradle — and she got her shoulder under the low side of the axle, into the place a wheel had been, and walked.
