She would remember very little of that walk. A bolt took the priest walking at her elbow and the priest behind him stepped over the body and kept the line of the hymn unbroken. The wave of the Marshal’s echelon broke against the drilled square that Bastionheart had thrown around the carriage, drill against drill, the tower’s discipline fighting the tower’s discipline, and the square held, and the carriage rolled, and the yards of the Herald’s Gate came on and on beneath her boots as slowly as anything in her slow long life. Later she learned the walk had lasted from mid-morning to noon straight up. It had felt, she said once, and never said again, like carrying her own forge-mark the last mile back to the forge.

At noon the carriage braked. The square opened its north face. And in the sight of both armies, under the drum-throne of the Herald of the King, the choir of the freed chantries drew one breath and began the Recant, and Nix’s voice — Nix’s, of course, whose whole body was lighter than the striker’s counterweight, riding the gantry with a hammer-cord in both fists, an imp of the rising above the ranks of the imps of the King — cried the word that eighty-nine floors had waited for, and swung.

Break-collar.

The bell rang.

⁂

Vega Coalbrow stood under her own bell when it spoke for the first time, and what she felt was not sound. Sound was the least of it, the carrier, the cart. What rolled out of the brass and across the fortress-square at the pace of a slow tide was the word — the word as the priests had sung it into the casting for nine nights, the word as four hundred collar-breakers had said it one throat at a time up the whole height of the world, Break-collar and Break-chain and Unseam and Recant and Countermand gathered into a single spoken mercy the size of a battlefield — and it went through the King’s mustered legions the way thaw goes through an ice-field.

The Pressed ranks halted mid-charge.

Not as men balk. As frost breaks. All across the yards, in a wave front running outward from the bell faster than any charge, the collars snapped — thousandfold, that same small cracking she had first heard in a mine-dark cage on floor twenty, multiplied past counting into a sound like a frozen sea letting go of a fleet all at once — and the army of the Herald’s Gate sat down.

That was what the histories would never get right, Vega thought, standing in the ringing after. They would paint a rout, or a surrender, banners falling, weapons flung. What happened was smaller and enormous: freedom took the legs. Rank on iron rank, the imps and the knights and the hulks of the King’s last great muster sat down in the yards where they stood, weapons loose in their hands, and stared at their own fists as though the fists had just been handed back, and began — helmed heads bowing, iron mouths working — to say names. Their own. Their lost. A hundred thousand soldiers of the tower, remembering at one stroke that they were somebody, inside a battle that had been arranged specifically so that there would be someone standing by, in threes and fives, to tell each one of them: nobody drives you. Ever again. The enemy’s line of battle did not break. It woke. An army was freed inside an army; the rising’s ranks grew in the middle of its greatest battle by more than every muster it had ever counted, and the whole vast engine of the day simply stopped, because a battle requires two armies, and now there was only one, and it was everyone’s.

From the gatehouse wall, where the freed clerks of Lastmuster stood watching their whole world change owners, the wave itself could be seen, and their telling of it became the age’s: a ring of stillness widening out from the bell through a hundred thousand standing soldiers, the way a struck cymbal stills under a laid finger — and that ring, held one breath at its widest, every rank inside it seated and every rank beyond it still charging, was the picture the histories kept: the drawn line, moving outward, between the tower and whatever came after it.

Not all. Nothing in the tower was ever all. The Wrongmade fought on, having nothing in them to free — the living chains, the wall-flesh things off the gatehouse — and were broken and evicted by crews who worked now almost tenderly, the day’s rage gone out of everyone. A core of the deep-collared, too — souls like the Marshal’s, worn so long that the collar had rooted past any single word’s reach — reeled back toward the gatehouse in fighting order around the drum-throne, perhaps one man in twenty, the bell’s wave washing off them like water off old grease. The Recant reached some of those, choir-sung, one at a time, through the afternoon. The Marshal it reached last of all, kneeling, his blade planted, his eyes shut against it like a man refusing a sunrise; and when his collar went at last it went quietly, and he carried his sword to Ede across the yards laid flat on his two palms, and could not speak at all.

And through it all, over it all, the drums.

Malgrim beat the muster from the drum-throne through the whole of the breaking, and Vega, watching that far figure drum for ranks that were no longer his, understood down in her smith’s bones what the morrow’s parley would be, and grieved it in advance. The drum said you are a soldier, there is nothing under the collar but the drum, and ten thousand of the freed sat in the yards weeping with their helms off, proof it had always been a lie — and still he beat it, kind, reasonable, wrong, the one soul in the fortress-square the bell could not touch, because his was the one collar in the tower that had been put on from the inside. No one stormed the drum-throne. It would not have been needed. His army had been not defeated but dismissed, man by man, by a better word than his; the gate below him was already thick with the freed unbarring it from within. The Herald of the King beat the muster alone over an empty yard, to no one, for no one, and the sound of it — Vega would say this much, once, to her brother — was the loneliest made thing she ever heard, and she had heard the Yoke itself.

The drums were still beating when the sky arrived.

It came over the south wall at mid-afternoon with a sound like the world clearing its throat: the gales of the Tempest Court, treaty-sworn and two floors tardy, Zephyra’s whole unbottled cavalry riding down onto a battle that had been over since noon. The banners of the rising stood straight out from their poles. The wind swept the fortress- square from wall to wall exactly once, majestically, gathering up the last knots of the deep-collared rearguard still skirmishing at the gatehouse stair and depositing them, disarmed and upside down, in a quench-trough; it snuffed what fires the ballistae had started; and then it wheeled above the yards as if awaiting applause.

Rhovan put it best, as he put everything best, watching the gale- riders dress their glittering ranks over a field of the sitting freed. “One chapter late,” he said, to nobody, an elf’s reckoning of a debt, “and they’ll want a verse in the song regardless. Give them the verse. The wind can have the tidying. The noon belonged to the bell.”

⁂

They rang it once more at dusk — not for battle, for the dead.

The rising buried its own below the south wall, and buried the Pressed who had died before noon with the same rites in the same earth, because the difference between those two burying-grounds had been the entire meaning of the day, and there was to be no difference. Vega worked the graves with her crews, as smiths did, setting iron markers, and the count was cruel enough — the morning’s steel had cost the rising more than any day since the Frosthall, and cost the bound worse — and the count was also this, cried down the lines at the lamp-lighting by a goblin voice hoarse as a hinge: the rising had marched to the Herald’s Gate that dawn some sixty thousand strong, and camped that night, inside the taken yards, at more than twice it. The greatest battle of the age, and the rising had won it by refusing to fight nine parts in ten — had won an army, not off a field, but out of one. Every history of every war ran the other way. That was the whole crime of the tower, Vega thought, and the whole answer to it: it had believed, as every conqueror believed, that the sum of a battle must be subtraction.

She was banking the forge-wagon’s fire when Nix found her, the Kettle looming behind him with two bowls. The goblin looked entirely spent, and entirely at peace, and about nine years old.

“I counted,” he said, “you’ll want to know, I counted the whole way down the yards, I’ve been counting for two years and I want it said out loud to a person who builds things. Since the outworks. Since the first trench on eighty-one.” He held up his slate, and on it was a single number, and the number was larger than any muster-roll of the whole climb, and under it he had written, in his careful, taught-late, fair-day letters: joined, not lost.

“Say it to me tomorrow also,” Vega said slowly, “when we go up to the drum.”

The drums had stopped at moonrise. That was somehow worse; the whole camp heard the silence. Up on the gatehouse the freed had hung a hundred banners over the King’s iron, and behind the opened muster- gate the stair-lift to the throne-tiers stood in its arch, lit and waiting, one Warden from open — and on the drum-throne over the empty yard sat the Herald of the King, his sticks laid across his knees, watching the rising’s cook-fires the way a man watches weather coming, waiting, everyone understood, to talk. He had sent down, in a clerk’s flawless ledger-hand, a note of three lines. Ede had read it and passed it round the fire without a word.

Item: your bell. I heard it. Come up in the morning and tell me what you think it proves, and I will tell you what I know it costs. Bring the thirty. I have something of theirs to return — a ledger entry does not retire.

Somewhere behind the gate, in the iron dark, in the last unfreed rooms of a nearly freed world, a man who smelled of bread was learning what the tower paid its willing. In the morning the rising would climb to the drum-throne and parley with the best talker in the Made, and Ede Stonhal would look her dark mirror in the eye, and after that there would be a judgment to give that no bell could give for her.

The banners moved all night above the taken gate, a hundred of them, restless in the last of Zephyra’s punctual-at-last wind. Vega slept under the bell, as she would every night now until the top, and the cooling brass ticked over her like a great slow heart, remembering, seam by seam, everything it had been before it was melted, which was the tower’s story too, and the world’s, and would be the morning’s work, and the next floor’s, until the whole stolen country of the sky was rung back down to earth.

Chapter 42 — Malgrim

The muster-drums of the ninetieth floor had beaten for an age and a half, the captives said, and no one living had ever heard them stop. They stopped an hour before dawn, when the last drummer imp was freed of its collar and set down its sticks and sat looking at its own hands, and the silence that followed was so large that half the host woke where it slept among the wreckage, certain some new weapon had been loosed against them.

Ede walked the forming-yards in that silence. The iron squares were sown with broken collars, thousands of them, burst in the wave of the great Break-collar and lying open in the muster-moss like frost-litter after a thaw. Here and there a freed man sat among them, turning one over and over, not able yet to put it down. The banners of the King had been pulled off the gatehouse in the night and lay heaped for burning. High on the wrecked dais the drum-throne stood with its skins split. Zephyra’s gales, come too late to matter, had tidied the rout and gone, unthanked and knowing it.

Forty-one climbers, Sliver said against her palm, dry as a shelf of old paper. I have stood in a good many mornings-after. This is the first one that is quiet.

“It isn’t over,” Ede said.

No. He is waiting for you under the throne. He has been waiting since the drums stopped. He sent a clerk with his compliments.

She went up through the yards past the rows of the hurt, past the vault of the conscript-rolls where Dara Lastmuster stood at a lectern striking names — one stroke each, thousands of strokes, a work the muster-captain would give to no one else. Nix stopped at the dais stair without being told, and Rhovan took the other side of it, an arrow loose on the string.

Malgrim sat in the ruin of his throne like a man in his own parlor.

⁂

He was built like a man who had carried sacks and then carried orders. The weld-iron took him at the collarbones and ran up the neck in old silver seams, and above it his face was ordinary — lined, kind about the eyes, a provisioner’s face. One leg was crushed under a fallen drum and he had not troubled anyone to move it. Through his gauntleted fingers, link by link, ran his chain.

“Ede Stonhal,” he said. “Sit, if you like. The step is clean. I had a clerk sweep it.”

She stayed standing. “Malgrim.”

“Just so. Not Herald — you’ve broken my gate, the title’s in the wreck somewhere.” He said it without bitterness. “I watched you come up the yards. You walk like a woman going to a burying. That’s a compliment, general. Most conquerors walk like they’re going to a wedding, and those are the ones who burn the larders.”

“I’ve come to offer you what we offer everyone.”

“I know what you’ve come to offer.” He lifted the chain a little, let it pour from one hand into the other. “The word that breaks steel and the word that unsays oaths. A name on the rolls of the freed. Work, which is the part your people underprice when they make the speech — the work is the whole of it, I could have told them that. A place at a fire.” He smiled, and it reached his eyes, which was the terrible thing. “It is the best offer made in this tower in an age. I’ve read the reports of it going up floor by floor, and I never once found a lie in it. You mean every word.”

“Then take it.”

“No,” said Malgrim, gently, as if declining a second helping.

Ede sat down on the swept step after all.

“You were one of us,” she said. “You were taken in the Theft or born to the taken. Somebody freed you, once, on some floor far down.”

“The forty-third,” he said. “A climber with a grey beard and a splinter of crystal in his ear, and I never learned his name. He broke my collar in the morning and climbed on before noon, because the climb was always the thing, and I stood in the gate-town of a freed floor with my whole life in my two hands and nowhere to set it down.” The chain moved through his fingers. Link, link, link. “You want to know what freedom was, general, on the forty-third floor, thirty years gone? It was a door standing open onto a cold room. The keeper fed us a week. The strong went down. The clever went up. I was neither, and I had been a tallyman under the collar, and the freed did not want a tallyman — they wanted very much to burn the tallies, and I understood them, and I starved understanding them.” He looked up at her without any anger at all. “The tower took me back in the spring. And here is what I have told no soul before you: when the collar shut the second time, I wept with relief.”

“That’s not a choosing,” Ede said. “That’s a trap closing on a leg.”

“Is it. Who chose you?” He asked it kindly. “I know your story — I bought most of it, page by page, that’s my trade. Thirty names on a stone, two years dry, and then a shardmind chose you again, in front of the whole gate-queue, and the shards don’t re-choose.” He leaned forward as far as the fallen drum allowed, his voice never rising, never hurrying. “Something reached out of all the world and picked you, Ede Stonhal, you and no other, and everything you have done since — every floor, every banner, this whole gathered morning — grew out of being chosen. Well. The tower reached out of all the world and picked me. Out of every starved tallyman in the Made it put its hand on my shoulder and said: you, I will keep. Every other hand that ever held mine let go.” He sat back. The chain lay still. “Yours freed you. Mine fed me. You will forgive a poor man for not seeing the difference from inside.”

“The difference,” Ede said, “is standing in your yards learning to walk out of step. Sliver chose me and I am free to shame it, fail it, set it down. Set the chain down, Malgrim. That’s the whole test. What loves you lets you set it down.”

For three breaths she thought she had him. Something crossed the kind ordinary face like wind over a pond, some tallyman thirty years starved leaning toward a fire. Rhovan said afterward that his arrow-hand had eased. Then Malgrim shook his head slowly, the way a man refuses a kindness too large to owe.

“There’s my girl the general,” he said. “No — hear my offer instead, for you haven’t heard it and you should. I am the best herald in this tower. I know every roll, every road, every provisioner and every price from here to the throne, and the throne is going to fall — I have kept the ledgers, madam, you are going to win, and a rising needs a herald more than it knows. Take me up with you. I will serve you better than any free man on your rolls, for the reason you’ll refuse me: a free man wants, and wanting wavers. I have not wanted anything in thirty years. I am the most reliable instrument you will ever be offered.” He opened his hands, chain across both palms, an honest tradesman showing honest goods. “All I ask is that you leave me the collar. Not the King — the King is spent, I’ve kept his books, I know it to the copper. Just the collar. Just the being-kept. You may hold the other end yourself. You’d hold it lightly. I know you now; you would hold it so lightly.”

And it was reasonable. That was the horror of him — it was kind, and it was reasonable, and every word was wrong the way a well-kept ledger of stolen grain is wrong, wrong in the sum and true in every line. Ede looked at his open hands and saw the whole tower in little: the thing that makes the cage so warm and the door so cold that a man will hand you the key and beg you to lose it.

“No,” she said, and her voice broke on it, and she let it break. “We don’t keep anyone. Not even you. Not even asked.”

“Then we are at the bottom of the page,” Malgrim said, without rancor — with pride, even, as at an examination fairly passed. “Do the last mercy, general. Say your word over the steel. I should like to hear how it sounds.”

She said the Break-word. The collar at his throat parted with the sound of ice leaving an eave, and fell into two clean halves in his lap, and Malgrim, Herald of the King, picked the halves up in his gauntlets and held them the way another man holds a dead bird — and nothing in his eyes changed at all.

“There,” he said softly. “Now you have it whole, what I am. You cannot strike a roll a man keeps in his own hand.” He set the broken chain aside with great care and took up his mace from beneath the fallen drum, and got up onto his one good leg, tall against the morning. “I’d not have you carry a murder, girl. You’ve enough on the stone already. Let it be a battle. One more muster.”

“Malgrim —”

“Drums up,” he said, kindly, and swung.

It was short. He was crippled and spent and thirty years out of practice at wanting to win. She took the mace on her shield and it drove her to one knee, and Rhovan’s arrow stood in his shoulder-seam a heartbeat later, and Ede came up inside the second swing the way Hale had taught her when she was young and hit him once, true, where the weld met the man.

She caught him going down. She was not strong enough and it did not matter; she went down with him, and knelt in the muster-moss with his head against her arm while the kind ordinary face did the last ordinary thing a face does.

“Item,” Malgrim said. His voice was a ledger closing. “One general. Honest. Note to the King —” and whatever he had noted, he carried it up himself.

She freed the broken chain from where it had tangled and would have cast it away, and then did not, and folded his gauntlet around it instead, because it was his, because it was all that had ever been his. Then she sat with him while the yards went quiet around her, and grieved for the man from the forty-third floor as if he had died thirty years ago, which he had.

To Dara Lastmuster, afterward, she said: “Enter him. Not as Herald. A man of the forty-third, a tallyman, taken twice. Write that the second taking was the crime, and write it plain, so that whoever keeps rolls after us knows what the tower could do.”

⁂

They brought Berrin to her at noon.

Two of Malgrim’s clerks brought him — freed clerks now, quill-fisted and blinking in the light, but they had been given the errand before the battle, and a clerk finishes an errand as a river finds the sea. Berrin Or came bound at the wrists with muster-cord, walking small between them, and the year had eaten him: her oldest friend, the man whose bread had kept her alive through the drunk years, gray as ash and looking at nobody. The willing collar was gone from under his shirt — struck in the night when the great Break rolled through. He had not fought them, the clerks said. He had not anything.

The elder clerk held out a ledger-page, folded once. The last entry, in Malgrim’s fine hand:

Item: delivered to the woman who climbs, one quartermaster, used. He asked me a second time for extraction. I keep my answers: a ledger entry does not retire. Item, final: the account passes to you, general. See that it balances better in your keeping than it did in mine.

The yards went still, the freed and the host together, for everyone knew the story by now — Calyne dead proving it, the courier who smelled of Roothollow bread — and the whole climb watched Ede Stonhal stand before the friend who had sold her thirty and fed her after, and waited to see what the rising was.

Berrin looked up at her once. “You owe me rope,” he said. His voice was a ruin. “For the bread, if for nothing else. It’s cheap, Ede. I’d buy it myself but they’ve bound my hands.”

She had turned this over every night since the trophy wall of the Pale Court, and the thinking had taught her one thing: the judgment was not hers. She had known, and climbed beside him pretending; she had loved him; she had eaten his bread. A judge needs cleaner hands or a colder heart, and there was one company in the world with the right. She was certain of the rope, certain as a drawn sword; and she had learned — it had taken her a tower — to spend her certainty slowly.

“No,” she said. “Not mine to give or refuse. Shieldwall holds your trial.”

⁂

They held it at dusk in the lee of the broken gate, by firelight, and the freed muster-captives of Lastmuster stood back in a great quiet ring and let Shieldwall have the ground.

Twenty-two of the thirty were living. Dun Harrow was not, nor seven others, and the company called the full roll anyway, and for each dead name the line answered held — “Dun Harrow.” Held the north door. — until the thirty were all present, the quick and the dead together, and Marta Reed, who had been senior after Dun, turned to the prisoner.

There is no need to write out the whole of it. He confessed to all of it, in the flat voice of a man reading a delivery-bill: the route sold to Skarn’s buyers, the toll-payments, the seating-list of the Frosthall, the year and the price of each. Asked why, he said the collar had been put on him young, on the night of the Theft, and that this was not an excuse but an inventory, since they were counting. Asked what he sought, he said rope, and said it hoping.

Marta Reed said rope too, when the company spoke. So did five others. Then it came around the fire to Wat Kello, who had been welded, whose face was iron from the jaw down, whose first word in five years — spoken in the Welding Halls with his general’s name in his eyes — had been Bread. The speech-slit they had cut him at floor 84 made every word cost.

“Rope’s a wage,” Wat said. “Paid once. Done.” He worked the iron jaw. “He was a provisioner. Twenty years he fed floors to the tower.” A long breath, the fire ticking. “Let him feed them back.”

The word went around the fire once more, and around it turned. Not mercy — no one there would have called it mercy, least of all Berrin, who began, somewhere in the middle of it, silently to weep, in the manner of a man whose one remaining door is being bricked. What the thirty built between them, sentence by plain sentence, was this:

Berrin Or shall live. He shall walk down the freed tower, floor by floor, to every floor he sold, beginning at the twentieth, and he shall provision it — granary, seed-corn, salt, and roads — one year’s keeping for every soul his ledger cost it, at the reckoning of that floor’s keeper. When the list is done he shall begin it again, for the list of what he owes is longer than his life. He shall carry no blade, hold no office, and keep no books but the open kind. And he shall walk it under parole.

“Whose parole?” Marta Reed asked the fire.

Nix stood up. He was the smallest thing in the ring by three heads, and he had the Shieldwall standard on his shoulder that he had picked up at floor ten to hide behind, and he did not hide behind it now.

“Mine,” Nix said. “I held a cup for a king who was a joke, I can hold a leash for a baker who was a lie. I know floors. I count things, it’s what I’m for — twenty, twenty-one, the fairground that begged us to pass by, sixty. Sixty was the feast. I was in the kitchens; I counted the cooks that got out the south door and the ones that didn’t. Eleven and four, if you want the numbers. I always want the numbers.” He looked at Berrin, and all the fast talk went out of him, and what was left was the book’s oldest small voice. “You’ll walk in front. I’ll have the list. Every sack gets counted twice, once for the floor, once for the dead. You don’t get to die until it balances, and I count slow.”

Berrin Or bowed his head to a goblin, and the sentence stood.

⁂

It was Ede’s to speak at the end — the thirty had built it, but the rising’s seal was hers. She stood at the fire and understood, in the saying, that the price of what her company had done was to be paid by her.

Because she would have hanged him. She knew it as she spoke; she had known it since the Pale Court. Rope was clean. Rope ended the knowing. And more than that — worse than that — the thirty had taken the one thing Ede Stonhal had wanted for herself since the Red Warcamp, and they had given it to him: a life spent to the last hour making amends, every day a payment, every road a penance, no possibility of the ledger ever closing and no obligation to be anything but sorry. She had wanted to die making amends since the day she came down alone, and instead she would live and be followed, and Berrin — Berrin got the amends.

The rising’s justice was labor, not blood. She sealed it with a steady voice. It cost her more than Malgrim had.

“It’s done,” she told Sliver, in the dark after, on the gatehouse stair. “Ninety floors done. Tell me the rest is simple. Lie if you have to.”

I am a poor liar, Sliver said. It is a fault of crystal. Its light moved on her palm. Above this gate the throne-tiers begin. The first of them is a stair of black glass, polished to a mirror an age deep, and the glass is not idle. They say every climber goes up it beside themselves.

Ede looked at the small door in the great broken gate, and the dark going up.

“Then it can meet me,” she said. “I’ve been wanting a word.”

Chapter 43 — The Obsidian Stair

The stair was cut from a single sweep of black volcanic glass, and it was the only light on the ninety-first floor: a cold gleam going up into dark, polished by captive hands to a perfect mirror, so that every climber went up it beside a flawless dark copy of themselves.

Wend of Firststep met the vanguard at the bottom tread. She was old and bent from a life of polishing the thing that tormented her, and she gave the leadership the mirror-lore the way keepers had handed the rising their knacks on ninety floors below: plainly, holding nothing back.

“Don’t meet your own eye,” she said. “That’s the whole of it. The glass learns faces. Look at your boots, look at your neighbor, sing if you have to. Your reflection has been on this stair longer than you, and it has learned things. In the Silvered Gallery they step out whether you look or no. There’s a word for breaking them — my folk cut it into the Understep, where the copies are sourced. But the word only finishes it.” She looked up the long gleam and her mouth went hard. “You finish it. The word just sweeps up.”

The host would cross by companies, eyes down, in the days after. But the stair took the leadership first, one at a time; whatever else the black glass was, it was honest, and it knew some accounts cannot be settled in company.

Ede went first. She had promised it a word.

She climbed with her eyes level, against all the lore, having decided she would not spend the throne-tiers flinching, and her reflection climbed beside her in the black glass with her own tired stride for a hundred treads, and then, where the moss lay deep in the seams, it stopped matching. It straightened. It walked the way she used to walk before the Warcamp, shoulders squared to a beloved world, and at the first turning of the stair it stepped out of the glass and stood above her, and it was her: sober-eyed, unhaunted, rested. A general. The general.

“You hanged him,” Ede said. She could see it in the set of the mouth. Her mouth.

“At dawn, on the gate,” her reflection said, with her own voice, only certain. “Before the thirty could talk themselves into theater. It was kind — kinder than parading him down ninety floors behind a goblin with a list. And the host slept better for it. An army wants a hand that doesn’t shake, Ede. You gave them a shaking one and called it justice.” It came down a tread, moving the way Hale had tried to teach her to move. “I came back down from the Warcamp too. The difference is I never drank. I cut the thirty names into the Stone and then I went and cut the man who sold them, and I have not been tired a day since. Look at me.” She did. It was like looking at rest itself. “You could still be me. The glass keeps every road a face didn’t take. Step in, and the woman who climbs out sleeps sound, hangs what needs hanging, and her armies never see her weep over a Herald’s carcass like a widow —”

“There it is,” Ede said. “That’s the flaw in the casting.”

“You never wept for Malgrim,” Ede said. “You put him down and wiped the blade. And a general who can’t grieve the enemy’s dead can’t mourn a barrow-king’s precedence, and never wins floor thirty, and never learns what the sword means, and loses the whole war efficiently. I know your roads. I drink on some of them and I hang my friend on the rest, and they’re the same road walked at different speeds, and I have been declining it for two years, one morning at a time.” She climbed the last tread, eye to eye with the woman she had been building all her life in the dark, and did what Wend’s lore said no climber could survive: she met her own eye and held it, and did not look away, and let the glass learn her whole face — the shake, the grief, the mornings, all of it.

“Learn this too,” she told it, quietly. “I would rather he live.”

The reflection cracked from the eyes outward, like ice under a boot, and was glass, and was nothing. Ede stood alone on the treads, and then she climbed on, and where the black mirror caught her now it showed only a tired woman climbing, which was all she had ever needed it to show.

⁂

Rhovan crossed the Silvered Gallery at the mid-stair, where the mirror- walls crowded closest and the copies came out whether you met their eyes or no, and his was waiting mid-span, leaning on a balustrade of black glass with an elf’s patience, and he knew it at once by the token.

The Wake-token hung whole at its breast. His own lay in two pieces at the bottom of his pack, broken over his knee in front of the whole scattered army on a wind floor half a year gone, and he had carried the pieces the way you carry a tooth the farrier pulled — proof against the ache that it was truly out.

“You kept it,” Rhovan said.

“Kept,” the reflection agreed. It had his voice, light and unbothered, and it dealt its words the way he had dealt words for fourscore years: one card at a time, face down. “Calyne lives, where I am.”

“Say the rest of it.”

“There is no rest of it. She lives.” A fraction, dealt gently. “Her agent never defied her on the wind floors. Never burned her secrets to buy a spider’s goodwill. Never gave her cause to spring the trap alone —” it paused, an elf’s pause, letting him do the arithmetic he had done ten thousand times in the dark, “— so she did not spring it alone.”

“And Berrin?”

“Serves the Wake now, unknowing. We hold his collar’s ledger and we milk it. The rising climbs; the Wake steers; the tower will fall in a decade or three, when it falls to us.” It straightened off the balustrade, and its eyes were his eyes, and they were perfectly restful. That was the temptation, and it was real: not power. Rest. “Step in, brother. You were never made for the open. A handled man never has to be right — only accurate. He tells his fractions and sleeps. You told the whole of something once, on the wind floors, in front of everyone, and you have not slept since; I watch you at the fires, not sleeping. The glass keeps the road back. She lives, Rhovan.”

There it was, all of it, and the worst part was the accounting held. He had turned, and Calyne had died springing the trap alone, and the man in the glass had not, and she had not. A fraction of him — and he was made of fractions, he had been made of fractions all his long life — reached for the mirror.

He mastered it with the only weapon the copy could not have learned from him, because he had used it exactly once.

“The whole truth, then,” Rhovan said. “Since you’ve never told one, and since it’s the one move you don’t know. I blackmailed her at Roothollow on the first morning and loved her before the Broodthrone. I burned my faction because a spider’s contract was more honest than my handler’s. Calyne died proving Berrin because I was three floors away holding her faction to Ede’s war, and if the glass keeps every road, then keep this: given all of it again, the wind floors, the token, the trap, the grave —” his voice did not break; elves’ voices do not break; it merely told, for the second time in fourscore years, the entire truth at once, “— I would turn again. I am not sorry. I have never once been sorry. I grieve her and I am not sorry, and you cannot say that, and she knew it of me before I did, and that is why her cipher-book went to Ede and her last field-order went to me.”

The reflection opened its mouth to deal the next fraction, and found, visibly, that it held no cards at all — for it was the agent who never turned, and everything it was ended at the turning. It cracked along the token, and fell out of the world.

Rhovan stood a while in the gallery of black glass, one elf among a thousand faint climbing shadows, none of them now his. “Field-order received,” he said to no one, and went up.

⁂

The stair gave onto the ninety-second floor: the mirror-galleries, halls of tall silvered glass beyond lamp-reach, where the King hoarded the stolen faces of the taken. Quicksilver ran in the gutters. The mirrors were full. Vega Coalbrow, stooping under the arches, walked between likenesses of people she did not know — some familiar, all wrong, mouthing — and found Illa of Sheenrest by the Veiled Gallery, where the draped mirrors stood turned to the wall and the face-robbed hid from their own reflections.

“The Silvering Vault is below,” Illa said. “Where they peel them. Every face the tower ever took is shelved somewhere under our feet — the dead’s too. We can send them home when the master mirror breaks. But mind the deep galleries, smith. The glass down there doesn’t wear strangers.”

It wore Vega. She met it in the last hall before the Vault: a mirror tall as a forge-door, and in it herself — heavier, gilded, prosperous. Guild- chains lay across the reflection’s shoulders in soft gold loops, chains worn as honors, and her forge-mark was stamped bright on the steel collar it held up for inspection the way a smith holds her masterwork. The mark Vega had spent twenty floors learning to look at, burnished here with pride.

“Little sister,” the reflection said — it was what Ulfar called her, and the theft of it in her own mouth made her hands into fists. “You look starved. Come in and eat. I kept the forge, you know, after you ran.”

“I didn’t run. I climbed.”

“You quit,” the smith in the glass said, and its argument, when it came, was not cruel; it was the argument Vega had been losing to herself since the Adit, the one nobody else knew to make. “I stayed, and I gentled the steel. My collars ride a finger looser. My welds take at a lower heat — do you know what a lower heat spares a man at the welding? I could not unmake the tower, so I stayed at its shoulder and I filed its teeth, twenty years, ten thousand collars, each one kinder than the smith before me would have made it. And when you quit, Vega — this is just arithmetic, you were always the better mender but I was always the better clerk — when the kind smith quits, a rougher one is hired. Every collar since your climbing wears his iron, not mine. Add it up.” The reflection lowered the collar and its voice went gentle, and gentleness was the trap, had always been the trap, for here was her own mouth offering the mending. “Come inside and mend it from within. It’s what you’re for, mender. Nothing has to break.”

Vega stood a long time, the way giants do, and finished her thought before she spoke, the way her mother had taught both children at the trough.

“You are not broken,” she said at last. “That’s my answer. I have walked up ninety floors mending — locks I forged, cages I marked, my brother, half an army — and I have learned the one thing our mother’s craft never taught, because on her anvil it wasn’t true: some things are not broken. Some things are made wrong, whole and sound and wrong, and a made- wrong thing in the shape of a mender is the wrongest thing in the tower. You file its teeth so it can chew longer. You are not my shame, smith. You’re its masterpiece.” She unshipped the forge-hammer from her back, the old one, her mother’s, that had mended pack-ice fleets and cage- locks and never in this whole climb been swung to break; and the hammer, she found, remembered its other work. “This is an eviction.”

The mirror went the way mirrors go. Behind it, stacked in the dark on shelves of lead, lay stolen faces beyond counting, silvered and sleeping — and that, below, was mending, and she went down to it gladly, glass in her braids, to spend the evening giving the dead and the blank-faced back themselves.

⁂

Nix found his in the Grand Gallery itself, under the King’s own image, because of course the tower would spend its best glass on the smallest target. The whispering courtier had tried him first in the corridors — it had called him in Gnarl’s voice, cup-bearer, the King’s cup is dry, and Nix had answered without breaking stride, “The King’s dead, I was there, terrible service at that court anyway,” and left it whispering. But the great glass was ready for him. In it stood a goblin with a collar on, holding a full cup with both hands, safe.

Small. Warm. Alive. The cup-bearer who never dropped the cup.

“You’re still there,” Nix said. “In the throne-tent. Twenty years of floors and you’re still —”

“Still pouring,” his reflection agreed. It talked fast, like him; it counted, like him. “And count it out, go on, you love a count: kings dead since I started pouring, none of mine. Cup-bearers dead at the Kingsfield, none. Wars survived by standing at the elbow of whoever’s winning and topping him up: all of them, that’s the count, all of them. And your side of the sum?” Its eyes went to the standard on his back, the banner he had picked up at floor ten to hide behind, and its voice did the thing Gnarl’s used to do, the music-hall pity. “One banner, three feet of target. One parole, which is a leash with your name on the other end too, don’t think it isn’t. One south door at one feast where you counted the cooks — eleven and four, wasn’t it, you keep the fours, I know you keep the fours at night. Put it down, Nix. Come in and pour. Nobody mourns a cup-bearer because nobody has to; that’s the whole warm arithmetic of the cup.”

“That’s a real number,” Nix admitted. “The four. That’s a real number, I’m not going to stand here and say it isn’t.” He came closer to the glass, and the standard’s butt tapped the floor as he walked, tock, tock, like a slow drum. “But you got my count wrong, which is funny, because you’re supposed to be me and I never get the count wrong. You counted what I carried. You didn’t count what carried back. Freed kobolds who can say their own names because I sat with them at the census-wall: two hundred and six. Floors that surrendered to talking instead of dying, which is a thing a cup-bearer knows how to do, it turns out, it’s the same trade as pouring only both ways: one whole warren, first in the history of the war. Collar-breaker squads, floors eighty-one to eighty-four, collars: I stopped counting at four thousand because my slate ran out, my slate ran out. And one baker on a list, who is going to feed floor twenty for a year because a goblin with three feet of target on his back will be standing there counting the sacks.” He looked up — way up — at the King’s image hanging over the gallery, and then at the safe warm goblin in the glass, and his voice dropped out of the patter for exactly one sentence. “Gnarl died a king of nothing. I’d rather die a nothing of somebody’s.”

His reflection had no answer, so it did what the tower always did when talk failed: it offered. It held the cup out of the glass — truly out, the silver-lichen letting its small hands and the brimming cup through into the lamplight, the last warm door of the throne-tiers standing open.

Nix took the cup. He weighed it, one beat, the way he’d weighed it the day the banner-meadow burned.

Then he dropped it — the second time in his life, and this time on purpose, and this time it wasn’t fear — and when the cup broke on the gallery floor the whole glass broke with it, top to bottom, and the King’s image cracked across, and from the Silvering Vault below came up a sound like a thousand cage-doors opening at once as the stolen faces flew home.

⁂

They gathered at the stair-lift behind the fallen mirror-wall when the floor was freed: four climbers and no reflections, which was now a fact about them and not a trick of the light. Nobody said much. There is not much to say to people who have met you.

The lift-arch stood open on the dark going up, and out of it, faint, came a warmth underfoot, a hum in the black glass, steady, patient, old — the sound, Sliver said, of the thing every collar and weld and fever in the tower was fed from, one floor above.

The heart, it said. You have broken his mirrors. Now comes his fire.

“Then it’s Vega’s floor,” Ede said, and looked at her armorer, who was looking up the shaft with her mother’s hammer still in her hand, and her brother’s mathematics ahead of her in the dark.

Chapter 44 — Scram

Vega Coalbrow knew the ninety-third floor for what it was before the lift-gate finished opening: not a forge. Forges have color. The light that came through the gate-seams was colorless — not white, not pale, but absent, a glare like the idea of burning with everything burnable taken out — and it lay along the lead-vaulted galleries in long searing bars where the shielding had cracked.

This was the heart. Everything the climb had fought — the Yoke that maddened the floors, the fever that took the beasts, the aether that welded the Wardens, the warmth in every collar from Roothollow’s root to the gate below — burned out of the core at this floor’s center and went down the tower’s bones. Floor sixty-two had shown them the scar of an older heart, dead an age. This one was alive. It came up through her boot-soles like the breath of something sleeping.

Ferro of Coreside met them inside the gate. He was old the way the thralls of the deep Made were old — burned hollow, hairless, his skin gone the gray of his work — and he had waited a lifetime to say what he said now, so he said it without greeting:

“I keep the scram-lore. I know the shutting of the King’s heart, the safe shutting, and I have never told a living soul, because the tower reads souls down here and I would not spend it early.” His eyes, in all that gray, were terribly alive. “You are the rising. I am spent either way. Come to the Control Spire and I’ll say my lesson.”

Between the gate and the Spire lay the floor’s whole cruelty — two days’ march of gallery and sluice, dwindling ahead to a colorless vanishing point — and the host waded it. The forge-crews and the Forge-Court legion came through in core-lead — two days of stripped blast-shielding beaten into plate, the only armor that turned the glare — and behind the vanguard the collar-breakers went along the coolant sluices freeing the lead-crowned engineers one by one, and each freed engineer turned in the same motion and dampened the coil they had been feeding. No one instructed them. It was the first thing every one of them did with a free hand.

At the Fuel Pens, Vega stopped being an armorer for a while. The pens were cells off the core-floor where the spent were held — thralls whose bodies had failed past working, racked like cordwood to be fed to the engine as fuel. Her crews cut the pens open and carried out the living, one hundred and forty of them, weightless as bundled straw. Nix walked the line of litters counting them into the rolls, his slate scratching. Ulfar stood at the mouth of the pens looking in — at the racks, at the chute beyond them that ran down toward the coil-light — and Vega watched her brother’s face do a thing it had not done in all the floors since the Frozen Sea. The mathematics behind it moved. She saw a carried digit fall.

He said nothing. Giants finish their thoughts before they speak, and this one, she judged, was long.

The demon came at them in the last gallery, where the sluices met under the Control Spire and the shielding stood thickest. Halflight: the core’s own colorless flame given a body to guard its heart, taller than Vega, umbilicaled to the coils behind it by ropes of glare, burning at half a sun. Arrows went to ash a spear-length out. Core-lead blistered. It could not be killed, Ferro had told them, only starved — it cannot die while it feeds; nothing on this floor can — and so the battle for the heart of the tower was not a battle at all but a work-shift: shield- lines of lead holding the thing in the gallery mouth while the freed engineers choked the coolant sluices, six of them, one by one, driving the core up toward the red edge of its own law, where the scram could be thrown; and the rising paid the shift’s wages in burned crews and held.

“Now the Spire,” Ferro said, when the sixth sluice groaned shut. “It must be now. The heart is climbing toward its fury. We shut it from above or it shuts itself from below, and if it goes from below, this floor goes with it.”

⁂

The Control Spire was the one place on the floor the leak could not reach, and after the galleries its quiet was a blow. Shielded in lead an arm thick, windowed in leaded glass, it hung above the core-floor like a pulpit over a congregation of fire. Below, through the glass, the core itself: coil upon coil of the colorless burning, the stolen aether of a stolen country, breathing.

The controls were older than any craft Vega knew and simpler than she had feared. The tower’s makers had built for eternity, and eternity favors levers. Sluice-wheels ranked along one wall; the coil-governors in their banks; and alone on the high dais, under a hood of lead dusted by generations of thralls who never once touched it, the scram-bar: one great lever, counterweighted for a failing hand, that would drop the dampers between the coils and kill the broadcast in a breath. The Yoke, the fever, the welding-fire, all of it. One pull.

Ferro said his lesson. Wheel by wheel, turn by turn, the order of the safe shutting, and the freed engineers moved down the wall as he spoke and made the Spire ready, and the core’s hum rose slowly through the lead like a question, and at the end of it there was nothing left between the rising and the quiet but the bar.

“It should be a smith,” Ferro said simply. “It wants a strong arm and an honest one. I’ve neither left.” And he stood aside, and it was the Coalbrows who went up the dais, sister and brother, and Ulfar Coalbrow put his hand on the scram-bar and did not pull it.

“Vega,” he said, slowly. “Look down at the core and hear me out once, because you are the only soul in this tower who can check my figures.”

She looked at his hand on the bar. “Ulfar —”

“The survey was theory,” he said. “The light-table on the ice was theory. This room is not. From this Spire, sister, it can be done — everything I have argued since the Frozen Sea, done gently, done truly. Damp the coils to a tenth. A tenth is nothing — the Yoke at a tenth is a headache, not a madness; the fever at a tenth is a shiver. And with the tenth I keep the grav-engines fed, and I set the floors down.” His voice never rose. It never had. It was the terrible patient voice of the only person in her life who had never once lied to her, and every sentence arrived whole. “Sixty years, Vega. One generation of a lit core, and I lower a hundred floors onto the earth like a cup onto a table. No fall. No dying tower above a world of the freed, waiting an age to come down on their children’s heads. And at the end of it, the last coil damped, the Yoke out forever, and every floor a field — not a monument, not a grave, a country again. That is my whole crime, sister. I want to inherit the tower warm.” His hand flexed on the bar, and she saw it, the future, standing behind his eyes: the Forge-Court’s dream, the gentle mathematics, the tower not broken but kept —

— and the room made no sound, but the room was listening: Ferro gray and still, the freed engineers stopped along their wall of wheels, Ede at the stair-head with her hand nowhere near her sword, a sword being no answer here, and below the leaded glass the stolen heart of the world burning, burning, waiting to hear which Coalbrow was right.

Vega did not argue ethics. She had lost that argument on the ice, and she had watched him count the Fuel Pens, and she knew her brother to the grain: he could carry any weight except a figure that would not balance. So she said:

“Whose hands held yours in the quench-trough, the morning you lit your first fire?”

Ulfar was silent.

“Say it. It’s a plain question.”

“Mother’s,” he said.

“And what did Aud Coalbrow make you say, every dawn of your apprenticeship, before she would let you near the coals — because you were quick, she said, and quickness forgets? I was small. I stood on the bellows-crate to watch. Say it now, in this room, with your hand on that.”

And because he was a giant, and giants finish their sentences, he said it — the forge-oath of a smith-matron dead these forty years, in a lead pulpit over the burning heart of the stolen world:

“Light no fire I cannot quench.” His voice had gone down to the bedrock of itself. “Keep no fire past its work. And answer for every hour it burns.”

“Now do the sum you came up here to do,” Vega said. “Not the floors, not the engines — you were never wrong about the engines, and I will grieve your engines with you. The hours. Sixty years of a lit core is what — count it in collar-hours, count it in fever-nights, count it in the shifts they’d still be standing down there —” she pointed through the glass, at the sluices, at the pens, at the gray generations of Coreside, “— because somebody must tend a lit heart, Ulfar. Your survey has a crew-table in it. I have read it. Who did you write in the crew-table? Count the hours and answer for them, the way she’d stand there and make us. And if you can answer them all, pull the bar out of my reach and light your generation, and I’ll not stop you.” She stood eye to eye with him, two giants under a hood of lead, and finished it the way their mother finished everything, gently, with the hammer already falling. “But you cannot. Nobody in this tower has ever answered for the hours. That’s what the tower is.“

The hum of the core came up through the floor of the Spire, patient, enormous, sixty years long.

Her brother stood with his hand on the scram-bar for the time a lamp takes to gutter. She watched the mathematics behind his face to the end — the crew-table, the carried digit, the figure that would not balance — and she did not help him, because this was his fire, and their mother’s third clause had a meaning under its meaning that Aud never had to say aloud: whoever cannot answer, quenches.

“The work is done,” Ulfar said at last, to the core, in the tone of a smith closing his forge for the night. “It was done an age ago. It was never — ” and there the sentence failed, the only sentence Vega ever heard her brother leave unfinished, and Thane Ulfar Coalbrow of the Forge-Court pulled the scram with his own hand.

⁂

The bar went down. The dampers fell between the coils, all of them at once, with a sound like a mountain swallowing.

And the tower went quiet.

There is no way to write the quiet except by what it took. The hum went out from under everything — out of the floor, out of the lead, out of the small bones of Vega’s ears, a pressure she had carried since Roothollow and called silence, having never once stood in the true kind. Below the leaded glass the colorless glare folded into itself coil by coil, and the dark that came was not the dark of a lamp put out but of a voice stopping — the Yoke, dying mid-word on a hundred floors at once. On ninety freed floors and the six unfreed above, every collar went cold in the same breath; every welded thing lost its fire; the fever loosened its grip on every beast in the stolen world in one long unclenching, and no one on floor ninety-three heard any of it, because what they heard was nothing, enormous, everywhere nothing, the first nothing in an age and a half.

In the gallery mouth, Halflight guttered. The coil-fed demon starved standing, its glare going out of it like water out of cupped hands, and what remained was a cinder in the shape of a kneeling man. The freed engineers did not break it; they stood around it in a slow ring, the way folk look at a hanged tyrant and find him small.

Ferro of Coreside sat down on the dais steps, a man whose one lifelong purpose had just been spent, wholly, and wept without sound into his gray hands, and would let no one comfort him, and did not need them to.

Then Sliver spoke, and Vega learned what else the scram had cost.

They are going out, it said, from Ede’s palm, and there was a note in the dry old voice none of them had heard before. You will want to know what I am hearing. I will tell you. The Yoke rode the bones of my people — the dead network, the shard-roads. We told you this on the wind floors. I did not tell you that the riding kept the bones warm. A silence. The last of the network is going dark. Kel-of-the-sixtieth, gone. The three sleepers under the ice, gone. The one in the Wyrm’s hoard, who was rude to me for nine hundred years, gone; I had hoped — and the voice stopped itself, and started again, level. There. Quiet. I am lit, alone. I am the last light of the shardkind — and the first, in an age, that burns on nothing but its own choosing.

Ede closed her hand around the splinter and held it against her breastbone, and said nothing, correctly.

Vega stood in the dark Spire and ached, armorer’s ache, mender’s ache, to fix it — and let the ache pass through her and go, because the stair had taught her, one floor down, that not every grief is a broken thing, and this one asked only to be kept. She kept it: the freeing of the world, bought with the last hearth of the shardkind, and paid, like everything on this climb, by whoever had the least left.

Her brother stood apart, at the leaded glass, looking down at the dead coils. After a while he took the Forge-Court survey from its case — the whole gentle mathematics, the country lowered like a cup — and Vega braced to watch him burn it. He did not. He laid the scrolls on the dead controls, squared their edges, and left them there in the dark for whoever keeps the museums of lost futures, and came away, and his hands, she saw, had already begun their grieving; they were smith’s hands with no fire that was theirs to tend.

“There will be work,” she said to him. It was not comfort. Between Coalbrows it was scripture.

“There will be work,” he agreed, slowly, and finished his sentence, because he was himself again, or would be: “colder work, and better.”

They came down from the Spire into a floor full of freed thralls standing in the great new silence with their faces turned up, the way folk stand in the first snow. Above them the throne-tiers waited, six floors of the King’s last strength — deaf now, dark now, fire dead in its constructs, word dead on its roads, and somewhere at the top of all that quiet, the throne, and the last door before it.

It was Ferro who said it, at the stair-lift, keeper-fashion, knowing one thing more than he was asked.

“Ninety-nine has a door that never opened,” he said. “And a soldier in it older than the drums. The core fed everything in the Made, but not her; nothing feeds her. She just stands — the King’s champion, the first taken and the last kept.” He looked at Ede, and at the banner on Nix’s small shoulder, and the oldest rumor of the tower’s deep places surfaced at last. “Word among the thralls was always: don’t fear the door. Fear waking what stands in it — for she was somebody’s, once, before she was his. Some company’s, in the young world.”

Ede went very still. Behind her, on its pole, in the dead heart of the quiet tower, the Shieldwall banner stirred — though there was, of course, in that deep place, no wind at all.

Chapter 45 — The Stolen Years

The stair out of the reactor country climbed for half a day and came out nowhere.

Ede had the tower’s grammar by heart now, the way you learn a language you are marched through: a stair ends at a gate, a gate gives onto a country, a country has a sky. This stair ended on a landing of riveted iron broader than Roothollow’s whole market, and past the landing there was no sky and no country — only the dark going up and the dark going out, rib after colossal rib of anchor-iron dwindling toward a vanishing point the lanterns could not buy back. Not a floor. The space between two floors. They had climbed out of the world’s rooms and into its timbers.

Between the heart and the King’s waiting-rooms, Sliver said against her breastbone, low, as it had spoken ever since the scram. Every great house has such a space. The builders ran the binding-seams through here, and the counterweights, and the warm water off the core. You are inside the wall, general.

“How far across?”

Four days, at a host’s pace. There is a road; there has always been a road. A pause, dry as a shut book. Hood the lanterns when you find it. You will see why.

The quiet came with them. It had come with them since Ulfar’s hand on the bar — the great new silence of a tower with its heart stopped, no hum in the iron, no voice under the world, every collar cold — and in the galleries of the between it was so whole that the host made all the sound there was: boot-leather, cart-axles, the litters of the burned crews creaking, the hundred and forty saved out of the Fuel Pens breathing carefully in their wagons. An army is never quiet. This one came near it. No one ordered the hush; the place collected it, the way a church does, or a sickroom.

On the second watch of the first day, far down the run of the road, Ede saw green light.

Not the Yoke’s dead white. Not lamp-gold. Green — the low green of a summer evening under leaves, standing in one patch of the dark like a lit window in a dead town; and beyond it another light, smaller, grey-blue, the color of snow falling at dusk; and a third, warm as an open door. The vanguard stopped without an order being given. Ede walked out ahead of the column, and the smell reached her before the understanding did, across four hundred paces of dead iron.

Cut grass. Blossom. Rain on warm ground.

“Boss,” Nix said, very softly. “I can hear bees.”

⁂

The tenders called them the Years.

Their keeper came out to the road, as keepers had come out to roads the whole height of the world: a small dry woman with soil in the creases of her hands and a dead collar at her throat. She waited while a collar-breaker said the word over the cold iron; she caught it as it fell, looked at it the way you look at a tooth that has ached for years, put it in her apron, and got on with her office.

“Pell,” she said. “Of the Years. You’ll be the rising; we felt your work below.” She turned her face down the road, to the lights standing one after another in the dark, out to where the ribs of the world swallowed them. “Come and see what the tower kept.”

The road ran between garths. That was the tenders’ word, and it was the right one: garden-closes under long vaults of glass and iron, each a field or two across, each holding its own light, its own weather, its own hour — and inside the glass, growing, a piece of the old world’s last night.

“When he took the countries,” Pell said, walking, “he took them living, and living things are mid-task. You don’t steal a farm the way you steal a ring. There was hay half-cut, blossom half-set, dough proving in a hundred thousand crocks. The countries went up and were made floors, and the floors kept their weather — you’ll have marched through it; the sixtieth is always snow — but a floor’s weather is a wheel stopped. Nothing has finished up there in eighty years.” She set her hand on a rail of black iron before the first great garth. “The binders cut one close out of every country the night it rose. The year itself, mid-breath. They bedded them down here in the warm off the heart, under glass, and bound them to burn slow. Eighty garths; one for every stolen realm. My folk have tended them mother to daughter since the Theft. Fed them, trimmed them, kept them —” and here the dry voice found the word it had plainly used all its life, without ceremony — “unfinished.”

Beyond the rail stood an orchard in flower.

It was night everywhere in the between and would be night there forever now, but the orchard hung in its own early morning: a soft light with no lamp in it anywhere, dew standing grey on the grass, twelve apple trees in first blossom, and bees — real bees, old-world bees, an age old and one morning old — working the rows as they had worked them for eighty years, the same morning, over and over, never once reaching noon.

Ede stood at the rail a long time. Behind her she could hear the hush arriving as each rank came level with the glass, like a wave running backward.

“He never came,” she said at last. It was not quite a question. She had stood in the trophy-mind of this tower before.

“Never once.” Pell looked up at her, and the look had eighty years of mothers in it. “He kept them because they were his. He never needed to see a thing to own it.”

The Wyrm had said it of him, a hundred floors ago: he built the one tower tall enough to see the whole world from, and he has never once looked. Ede had not expected to find, one floor under his door, the garden of everything he never looked at.

“You should know what your lever did,” Pell said then, with no blame in it at all, which was worse and better at once. “The hold on the garths was the heart’s hold, and the heart is out. The beds are cooling. The years are off the leash, general, and they’re finishing. Eldest first.”

“Finishing,” Ede said.

“Running.” Pell spread her earth-dark hands, a woman laying down a burden and showing it to you at the same time. “A year that has stood still eighty years spends itself quick, like held breath let go. That orchard has a night or two left in its morning. Then it’ll run — noon, fruit, leaf-fall, all of it, a year in a night — and go dark, done.”

“Can they be kept?” Vega asked it from the road, low, and Ede heard in the asking all the mender in her.

“They were never kept, smith. They were stopped.” The old woman said it gently, keeper-fashion, one hard true thing at a time. “Eighty years my family kept the world’s last day from ever getting to its evening. Now it gets its evening. We’re not grieving down here. We’re sitting up with them. You’ll know the difference by the far gate.”

⁂

The crossing took the four days Sliver had promised, and the host made it in silence, and no one ordered that either.

The lanterns went hooded, because the garths were the light: eighty small skies standing in the dark of the between, each burning the hour of its own stolen night, so that the road ran from a midsummer evening into falling snow, and out of the snow into warm dark rain, and the army of the hundred banners walked it the way you walk the aisle of a sickhouse where every bed holds someone’s mother. Tenders moved among the glass with baskets and ladders, sitting up with the Years. The host watered its oxen at their cisterns and lent them its surgeons, and nothing else changed hands the whole width of that country except quiet.

Ede walked it as she had walked every floor, up and down her own column, and saw the garths take her army apart gently, one soldier at a time.

She saw the giants of the Frosthall’s muster stop before a close of deep midwinter — a steading corner, a sledge half-loaded, a doorway hung with juniper for a feast-night eighty years deferred — and stand in a row with their helms off, snow falling on the other side of the glass, not one flake of it ever reaching them.

She saw Rhovan stand a long while at a garth of night rain: one acre of sunken lane and hazel, warm rain falling straight down onto leaf-mold, eighty years of one shower that never wet the road twice. She did not ask an elf what he was doing in the rain. When he came away his face was the same as ever. His hair was not, and he let it dry as it liked.

She saw Nix at the first-taken’s garth, and that one she stopped for.

The goblin warrens had gone up before every realm in the world, the foundation-stones of the whole theft, and their garth was the eldest and nearest spent: a cellar-mouth and a mushroom bed and one tallow lamp over a stone table laid for a feast — the new-lamp night, Nix said, when the warrens light the year’s first wick off the year’s last. The lamp had burned eighty years on the last of the old year and never been let touch the new. It stood very low now. Nix leaned on the rail with the standard against his shoulder and looked at the low lamp for the time a lamp takes to gutter, and counted nothing at all; and Ede, who had learned from a whole climb what his counting was, understood the not-counting too, and stood beside him and let it be.

The rain-garth, Sliver said that night, unasked, in the dry old voice that had gone drier since its kin went dark. I knew that sky. I crossed under it twice before there was a tower, riding in a scholar’s ear. It rained like that the day I was cut. A silence. Every light down here is the last of its kind. I am in good company. I had wanted to say that word again before the end of things. Company.

Ede closed her hand around the splinter and walked on, correctly.

On the third night the orchard finished. Word came back down the column soft as word of a death, and the whole held morning let go at last and ran. Noon came to the orchard for the first time in eighty years, and the bees rose all together into the heat of the day they had been promised so long, and the blossom went over to fruit while a soldier’s supper cooked; by the middle watch the tenders were harvesting at a run, gently, weeping and working both; by false dawn the leaves had turned and the first apples of the stolen world were going down the column hand to hand to the litters, to the burned crews and the hundred and forty out of the Fuel Pens, who ate them slowly, in the dark, while twelve bare trees stood down into their first honest winter and the garth’s small sky went out. Done, Pell said. Not lost. Done.

⁂

The Fencerows garth stood last before the far gate, and it was the largest of the eighty, and it was barley.

A close of it, half-mown, cut off mid-swath on the night of the Theft: stooks half-built along the hedge, a whetstone lying in the stubble where a hand had set it down, and the standing rest leaning heavy- headed under a harvest evening’s light — that long gold hour when farm country races the dark, held at the racing, eighty years. Ede heard the murmur go down the column and come back, because half the low-floor musters had Fencerows folk in them, and they had walked four days through other countries’ held nights to find their own floor’s stolen evening standing ripe at the end of the road.

It was finishing. You could see it now that the orchard had taught them what to look for: the gold going long at the edges, the light leaning.

A Fencerows serjeant of the second muster came to Ede and asked what none of them would ask the tenders directly, and Ede took him to Pell, and Pell heard him out and looked a long time through the glass at the mowers’ scythes — still hanging in the hedge where the mowing had been meant to start again at first light, eighty years of first lights ago — and said, in the voice of a woman handing over a patient: “It wants finishing. Go in and finish it.”

They mowed the last field of the old world by its own last light.

Forty of the Fencerows’ freed went in with the dead mowers’ scythes, and the tenders showed them the rows, and the only sound the width of that whole dark country was the hush of blades laid into eighty-year barley while the garth’s gold hour finally spent itself over their shoulders. No one sang. Harvest is sung home in hedge country, the serjeant told her afterward, every year of the world — but you sing to make the work go, and this work went by itself, and besides, he said, and did not finish the sentence, so Ede finished it in her own head: you do not sing over a deathbed. You sit up.

She bound sheaves in the second row until her hands learned the work, which took a while, and nobody excused the general her crooked knots, which was its own kind of gift. Nix walked the stubble counting sheaves in a whisper, once for the floor and once for the dead, the way he counted everything now. The light went amber, then ember, then the color of a banked fire; the last swath fell as the first honest stars any of them had ever seen came out inside the glass, over one acre of the world, for the length of ten breaths; and then the Fencerows’ stolen year was over, and its garth stood dark and stubbled and entirely, quietly finished.

The grain went into sacks, and Pell chalked each one herself, in a plain hand, and what she chalked was not an inventory. SEED-CORN, the sacks said. FENCEROWS. BY THE FIRST GATE. Eighty years old and one evening old, the harvest never brought in, going home at last to be planting instead of bread.

“Nobody living has seen a floor’s second spring,” Pell said, watching the sacks tied off. “My grandmother held that the years below would run again when the garths were done, and my mother held it, and I hold it, the way you hold what you can’t stand at a rail and look at. Either way the seed goes home. And when the last garth ends, we’ll close the glass and come down after it. A freed world will be wanting almanac-keepers. Somebody has to teach a hundred floors their seasons back; they’ve had no practice.”

⁂

The far gate stood open on a rising stair, and above it, Sliver said, lay the King’s own waiting-rooms, the last patience of the Made.

The host came up out of the country of the Years in column, in the dark, in its own lantern-light again, and it did not smell of lead anymore, or of ash. It smelled of straw and cut barley and apples; it carried a dead world’s seed-corn in its baggage-train; and at the head of it, furled on Marn’s shoulder, the oldest banner in the world went up the stair trailing a last thread of harvest-dust from a field that no longer existed anywhere at all, and never would again, and had, at the very end, been brought in.

At the head of the stair Ede paused once and looked back down the road of small skies — fewer already than four days ago, going out one by one behind the host like lamps down a village street at bedtime — and drew one long breath of the green dark, and held it, the way you hold the last of anything.

Then she climbed toward the doors of the man who owned all of it and had never once come to see, and the rising came up the stair behind her, quiet as snow.

Chapter 46 — The Last Door

They took the antechambers in two days of walking and one blow.

Ede had feared the ninety-fourth floor the way she had learned to fear all the Made’s quiet, and it was quiet: a honeycomb of waiting-rooms running back bench by bench to the vanishing point, summons-bells ringing numbers that were recalled the same breath they were called. The waiting itself had a body there — a grey chamberlain grown out of a lifetime of tokens and unread petitions, seated at the last desk before the throne-approach — and it did not fight the host so much as file it. Nib of Doorward taught the vanguard the door-art called Precedence, and the rising walked the whole length of the queue the wrong way, and Vega’s hammer broke the desk like a bad promise. What Ede kept of that floor was not the eviction. It was an old woman of the taken standing up off a bench she had warmed for eighty years, looking at the brass token in her fist — her number, her whole life’s wait — and letting it fall. The sound it made on the marble was small. The sound of ten thousand more was not. Nib’s people brought the taken’s unread petitions up out of the Filing Crypt, the case against the King.

The trophy vaults took two days and cost more. The ninety-fifth floor was everything Vharuk had ever taken, shelved: crowns of drowned kingdoms, the standards of freed floors captured back, the arms of heroes labelled like seed-stock. Down one grim aisle, which Vess of Hoardside would not walk with her eyes raised, stood the gear of every climber who had come before and failed, each set named for its dead owner — and at the aisle’s end an empty plinth, dusted, patient, its label blank. Ede stood before the blank space a long while. Then she took the label off the plinth and put it in her coat, and Vess laughed, a rusted terrible sound, the first laugh that floor had heard in an age, and the curators began to empty the shelves. The rising armed itself out of the King’s own museum. Hero-steel with names on it went back into living hands; the failed climbers of five ages were named at last and laid to rest; the hoard-thing that rose to stop it was evicted with its own exhibits.

The night gardens Ede would not speak of afterward, and no one who crossed them spoke of them either. The ninety-sixth floor was beautiful. That was the whole crime of it. Black trees in flower, terraces, fountains running sweet — and in every pale bloom, if you looked, a face, because the King had grown his one garden out of the taken, planted living in the black beds and coaxed to flower. Elys of Bloomside walked the ranks of the rising with a waking-draught and a spade and taught them the un-rooting, and for a day and a night the army of the hundred banners knelt in flowerbeds and dug people out of the soil with their hands. Vega withered the great flower at the garden’s heart with the word Elys gave her, and the faces in its petals went still, and were freed, and some of them woke. Ede had not wept at the Warcamp cages. She had not wept at the Frosthall. She wept there, in the dirt, and was not the only general who did.

The Kingsway they took singing. Three days’ march of white marble climbed the last tiers, lined with the King’s own likeness a thousand times over, the Theft carved in stone as a triumph — a floor built to make a climber worship before ever seeing the throne. But the masons who cut it had hidden the truth in the plinths, low, where the King would never stoop, and Cord of Wayside read it out loud as the host came on: no royal line, no god, a usurper of nothing. Then the masons taught the rising which colossi stood hollow, and brought their own life’s work down across the avenue, statue after statue, with a cry and a crash and a cheer. The host walked the last mile past broken idols, unawed.

The court was worst, and shortest. On the ninety-eighth floor there were no collars to break, no fever to lift, no welds to cut. The courtiers of the gilded tiers had never been taken. They had chosen him, and fattened on him, and when Bly’s servants stilled the hall’s glamour with the truth-art called Silence, what stood exposed in the cold candle-light was only people — jewelled, willing, unrepentant people. The Grand Vizier laughed at the offer of mercy, because he had never been bound and would do it all again, and so the climb did the thing it had spent ninety-seven floors learning not to do, and judged. Ede gave the order herself and slept badly after. Below, everything they had fought was a prisoner wearing a monster. Here the monsters had worn people. Bly’s servants read their long ledger of the court’s crimes aloud over the ruin, and then folded it, and carried it up — so that when the King answered, everyone would hear.

Five floors in nine days. Behind the host every gate-town emptied upward, until the rising no longer swept the throne-tiers so much as rose through them, the way water rises through a house. Above was one floor of the Made, and then the summit, and between the two, the Door.

⁂

The ninety-ninth floor was one room.

Ede came up the last stair into a hall so vast the torchlight of the vanguard could not find its walls, and stopped, because everyone stopped; the whole climb narrowed to this point and every soldier felt it in the soles of their feet. There was no land here. No hedgerow, no ice, no gallery of gears. There was dim stone underfoot, and pale vigil-fungus shining in one long shadow, and at the hall’s far end, filling it from floor to unseen roof, the Door.

It was black. Not the black of night, which is only the absence of lamps, but the black of adamant and King-steel and welded sorrow, a wall raised to seal the throne away from all the world below. And the black was not smooth. Ede walked toward it, and the nearer she came the more the surface resolved, until she stood at the Weld-Face itself and understood what she was looking at, and took off her helm without deciding to.

The Door was made of the fallen. Liberators, welded into the metal where they fell, an age of them, their shapes raised faintly out of the black like figures under ice, their names still legible where the weld had taken them mid-stride, mid-swing, mid-word. Rhovan came and read beside her, and said nothing, which from Rhovan was a whole speech. Some of the names were in scripts the census-wall had taught her. Some were in no script anyone living knew.
