‘You’re reading them,’ said a voice below her elbow. ‘Good. Most climbers won’t. Most climbers look once and go grey and start talking about seams.’

The keeper of Lastward was a small bent man named Hollen, and his people were a handful — the last of the bound, kept in this hall to tend the Door that entombed their own greatest hero, kept, he said, as a living reproach. He led the captains along the Weld-Face, past the doorward imps that sealed its seams and the grey weld-wights that rose out of the metal to turn back the living as they had been turned back. And at the Door’s very centre, above the sill, flanked by a faceless sentinel of dead-black adamant and by one armored figure that stood guard the way only a living soldier stands, he stopped, and pointed up.

She was in the Door.

Taller than the figures around her, raised half out of the black metal like a ship’s figurehead, a woman in an old harness with her empty hands open at her sides and her face turned down toward the sill she had died — no. Not died. Ede looked at the open hands and the bent head and knew, the way she knew a sleeping sentry from a dead one, that the woman in the metal was alive.

‘The Champion,’ Hollen said. ‘The greatest liberator who ever climbed. She came up an age ago with the whole tower at her back, same as you, and she reached this hall, and at the last step they took her alive. He didn’t kill her. He made her the lock.’ The old man’s jaw worked. ‘Everyone says she’s gone. I’ve kept this floor my whole life on the belief that everyone is wrong.’

‘She fights?’ Ede asked.

‘She fights,’ Hollen said. ‘With everything she was. The binding drives her. But she doesn’t wish it — that’s the difference, that’s everything. Malgrim clutched his chain. The Vizier laughed at you. Her, they had to weld.’ He looked up at the bent head. ‘A captive who still wishes to be free always can be. If that’s not true, none of this was.’

Sliver was warm at Ede’s throat, and had been silent since the stair.

‘Her name,’ Ede said. ‘You keep it.’

‘I keep where it’s buried,’ said Hollen. ‘Under the sill. They put her name and her own arms in the ground when they welded her, so no one could call her back. Come and dig.’

⁂

The Threshold Undercroft was a vault beneath the Door’s own sill, and the things in it had been buried with hate and preserved with care, which told Ede more about the King than the Kingsway had. A harness of plain steel, old past reckoning, kept oiled. A soldier’s kit with a stoppered draught in it, saved and never spent. A sword — and Ede, lifting it, went still, because the sword was plain and notched and heavy in the way of a blade used all its life by someone who knew what it was for, and on the flat of its pommel was cut a device she had carried on her own shoulder for twenty years and behind her own banner for two: a grey wall, and a single star above it.

‘Sliver,’ she said quietly.

The splinter’s voice was a long time coming, and when it came it was drier than she had ever heard it, which for Sliver was grief.

‘Her name is Aldis Bright,’ Sliver said. ‘She founded the first banner of the Shieldwall, an age ago, in a village that is under your Roothollow now. I have carried forty-one climbers, Ede Stonhal. She was my fourth. She was the first of them stubborn enough to matter.’

Nix, who had come down uninvited because that was how Nix came everywhere, looked from the sword to Ede’s shoulder and back, said nothing at all for once in the whole climb, and went back up to fetch the banner.

⁂

They gave the fight everything, because the Door gave them no other terms.

The doorward imps sealed the seams and Nix’s collar-breakers freed them one by one, and at every collar that cracked, a seam gave. The weld-wights rose grey out of the metal, and the host broke them gently as it had learned to break the Wrongmade, and the freed of them drained into the adamant and rested. The sentinel of the sill came apart under Vega’s hammer to dead metal that rang for a hundred heartbeats after.

The Champion fought as Hollen had promised, with everything she was, and everything she was had nearly won this tower once. She fought from the Door and of the Door, half-freed of the metal and lashed back into it, and her open hands were empty no longer; the binding had given her a blade of black weld and it moved the way water moves. She killed no one. Ede saw it by the third exchange and shouted it down the line: the binding drove her sword and something under the binding was turning every stroke a half-hand wide. An age of nights in the metal, and the woman inside was still fighting the King with the only inch he’d left her.

The armored figure at her side was another matter, and nearly ended Rhovan, until Nix went in under its shield with the Break-collar word and the chantry-priests sang Recant behind him, and the Champion’s own lieutenant — taken with her at the last step an age ago, collared to fight at her side forever — came free mid-stroke, dropped guard, dropped blade, and turned to stand between his welded commander and her own descending sword. He took the flat of it and held.

‘Now,’ Ede said.

Hollen’s people had climbed the scaffold at the Lintel, the last handful of the bound, and they called her name into the Door. Not shouted it. Called it, the way you call someone home across fields at dusk. Aldis. Aldis Bright. The weld took the name and shivered. Vega laid both palms against the black seam where the woman met the metal, and spoke the capstone word, the one the whole Made had taught them syllable by syllable — Unrivet, Unseam, Mirrorbreak, gathered into Unmake — and the oldest weld in the world let go like frost off a morning line. The priests sang the Recant of the King’s hold. Ede stood on the sill with the plain notched sword held up hilt-first, and said, herself, last, quietly, into the opening metal:

‘Aldis Bright. You’re stood down. Come out.’

And the greatest hero of a dead age stepped down out of the Last Door alive, and put her hand on the offered hilt, and stood swaying on the sill in the torchlight of ten thousand people who had all, every one of them, stopped breathing.

⁂

She was grey where the songs said gold, and thin as a lath, and the weld had left its lines on her the way rivers leave lines on a country. She drank the draught her own kit had kept for her across an age, because Hollen put it in her hand and glared until she did. She asked for the date and was given it and took the answer like a blade going in, standing up. She said the lieutenant’s name — Tam — and Tam wept, and she did not, which seemed to cost more.

Then she saw the banner.

Marn of the thirty carried it now, the man freed at the Welding Halls, iron from the jaw down, who had carried it since the Herald’s Gate and let no one spell him. He had come to the sill unbidden, because Nix had fetched him, and the old cloth hung still on its pole: a grey wall, a single star.

Aldis Bright looked at it for ten heartbeats. Ede watched an age-old face do sums.

‘That is my banner,’ she said. Her voice was rust and courtesy. ‘I sewed the star crooked. It is still crooked.’ She looked at Marn, at the iron jaw, at the way he held the pole one-handed and would plainly die before dropping it, and something in her began, very slowly, to stand back up. ‘It never fell, then. All this time.’

‘It fell twice,’ Ede said, because the debt did not deserve a lie. ‘It fell at the Red Warcamp, when I lost the company that bore it. A man with one arm carried it out of the cages when we broke them, and bore it up forty floors, and died keeping the north door of a burning hall so that others could run. I got the company back. Thirty of them are in this hall.’

‘Show me,’ said Aldis Bright.

So Ede gave her the muster-roll — the rebuilt roll, the one she had written out of her own memory over a traitor’s protests, thumbed and rain-marked and carried against her heart from the Cindermouth to the Last Door. The Champion read it the way quartermasters read: fast, and twice. Ninety-odd banners. Thirty-one peoples and more since. Goblins mustered beside Men, keepers and Pressed and Tollmen and giants, column after column of the one thing the tower had been built so the world could never make.

She was a long time over it. When she looked up, it was not at the roll’s keeper but down the hall, at the host, at the light on all those faces going back farther than light could go.

‘Who commands?’ she asked.

And it was not Ede who answered, and not Rhovan, and not the keeper of the floor. It was the thirty — the freed of Shieldwall, the ones who had spent a winter half-believing their own captain had sold them, standing together down the sill the way they had stood in the cages, and they answered as one, the way a company answers at roll:

‘Stonhal.’

Aldis Bright, founder of the Shieldwall, first soldier of the oldest banner in the world, turned to the woman who had lost her company and rebuilt it into the gathered world, and brought the notched blade up before her face, edge outward, in a form of salute so old that no living company used it — and every soldier in that hall knew it anyway, and ten thousand blades and billhooks and hammers and spade-hafts came up in answer down the whole length of the ninety-ninth floor.

Ede returned it. She did not trust her voice, so she used the one that carried.

‘Shieldwall,’ she said. ‘Fall in.’

Behind them, unbarred, unguarded, open for the first time in an age, the Last Door stood on darkness and a rising stair. Somewhere at the top of it a man who had never once been made to wait was waiting.

The oldest soldier in the world fell in beside Ede Stonhal, and the host came on.

Chapter 47 — The Throne

There was no hundredth floor. There was a summit.

Ede came up the last stair of the world expecting a gate, a wall, a warden, one more of the Made’s patient horrors — and walked instead out under open sky. Black sky, starless, enormous, the lid of everything; and under it one hall with no roof but that blackness, iron-floored, so wide the eye gave up on it, crowning the hundred floors the way a cold ember crowns a heap of ash. This was where the Yoke had gone out from. This was where the fever had been sown and the collars drawn and the constructs poured. The highest and emptiest place in creation, built by a man who wanted to be alone above a world he had cut into a hundred pieces so that it could never rise against him.

And it was quiet.

That was the thing she had not been ready for, after two years of the tower’s endless voice — the drums, the bells, the broadcast hum under every floor like a tooth going bad. Ulfar’s scram had reached even here. The great hall of the summit was as quiet as the Fencerows at first lamp, and in the middle distance of that quiet, on a chair of cold iron raised above acres of empty floor, sat a single figure with its head cocked, listening.

Listening to nothing. That was the first thing Ede understood about Vharuk, King of the Ascent, architect of the Theft, source of every sorrow she had climbed through since the hedgerows: he was listening for his tower, and his tower no longer answered him. The Yoke was dark. The core was dark. The voice that had spoken into every floor at once for an age had been struck dumb seven floors below by a giant with his hand on a lever, and the man on the iron chair was learning what the taken had known from their first hour in the dark — what it is to call, and wait, and hear nothing come back.

He looked small. She had needed a hundred floors to earn that sight, and she stood a moment on the last stair and had it: one man, on one chair, alone.

‘Careful,’ Sliver said at her throat, very low. ‘A hoard is smallest just before it is spent.’

She came on. Aldis Bright walked at her right hand with the notched sword loose in its sheath, and at the sight of the iron chair the old soldier made a sound Ede chose not to hear, because it was an age of grief in one breath and none of it was for spending now. Vega walked at her left, carrying the blade she had forged in a single night from the broken metal of the Last Door — King-steel, black, plain, without device, the hardest metal in the world folded around its own history — and behind them came Rhovan, and Nix, and Tam, and Hollen, and the vanguard of the freed.

And behind the vanguard came the world.

⁂

Afterward, when the tale was told on a thousand hearths — and it was, and is — the tellers always spent their breath on the wrong marvel. They told the last blast and the black blade. But the marvel was the filling of the hall, and Ede stood in the middle of it and knew it for the victory itself, whatever happened after; because the filling of that hall was the one thing the whole tower had been raised, floor by floor and collar by collar, to make impossible forever.

Up out of the Last Door they came. The Lastward captives first, by right, Hollen leading them onto the floor their whole line had been kept below. Then the throne-tiers’ freed: Bly’s servants out of the walls of the court, carrying the long ledger of its crimes; Cord’s masons with their chisels and their true history; Elys’s gardeners, soil still on their knees from the beds they had emptied; Vess and her curators bearing the arms of five ages’ fallen heroes back up to the place those heroes had been climbing toward; Nib’s grey waiting-folk, each with a lifetime’s petitions in their arms and no token anywhere on them, walking — at last, at very long last — through the last door at the head of the queue.

And behind them, the floors. Ede had mustered armies and this was not that; no order of march survived the stair, and none was wanted. Giants of the Frosthall came up shoulder to shoulder with the barrow-keepers of Wakesend. The thread-cutters of Threadneedle came up beside freed thanes, beside sky-folk riggers, beside Voss’s Tollmen — who had, characteristic to the last, set up nothing on the stair and charged no one for it, Voss having grasped at the Herald’s Gate that there was no percentage left in the old world whatsoever. Dara Shieldwall came up out of the mine that had been her prison with her shield-folk armed in reforged warplate. The field-folk of the Fencerows came up with billhooks, and among them, eighty floors past the last fence he would ever have to mend alone, his hedge-count folded in his coat, came Hobb Fennick, who had told Ede once that the counts don’t match, and had climbed the whole tower to watch them made to.

Nix stood on an iron bollard by the door and counted the banners aloud as they came through, because he was Nix, and numbers were how his heart talked. Kobold banners and goblin banners. The wall-and-star. The freed hounds’ pale pennon that the kennel-folk had sewn as a joke and carried like a relic. He counted through the seventies as the hall filled, through the eighties as the peoples of the Made came on, and the count itself began to travel, repeated back down the stair, floor to floor, a number climbing a hundred floors of freed gates —

‘Ninety-nine,’ Nix called, as Mereth Wakesend’s grave-banner cleared the door.

And then nothing, for the space of three breaths. And then the last banner in the world came through the Last Door, carried one-handed by a man iron from the jaw down: grey wall, crooked star, oldest of them all and newest to the muster, and Nix’s voice broke clean in half calling the hundred.

The hall of the summit stood full. Men and Giants and Elves, the Deep and the Waste and the freed of the Made, the Pressed with their collars gone and the Natives’ keepers and the Wrongmade’s grieving kin, every people the King had dealt out across a hundred floors precisely so that no two of them could ever meet, and agree, and march — met, and agreed, and arrived. There was no sound in it you could call a war-sound. There was breath, and boot-leather, and somewhere down the ranks a baby crying, and Ede thought: that is what he is listening to. Not for his tower. His tower is dead. He is listening to the world, standing in his room.

Even filled, the hall was not full. The host stood in it as a town stands on a plain — ten thousand carried torches making one warm country of light, and the dark iron running out flat past its edges on every side, no wall in sight but the sky. The summit had never had weather; it had been built above all of it, one man’s cold room over the world. It had weather now. The breath of the gathered peoples went up into the black and hung there, a faint drifting mist above the banners, the first cloud the top of the tower had ever owned.

⁂

Vharuk rose.

He was not a devil. Two years of floors had taught Ede what he would be before ever she saw him, because she had read him in his works the way you read a smith in his welds: a sorcerer of the old world who had learned to steal aether from living souls and mistaken the theft for godhood. Tall, yes. Crowned, yes. Wrapped in a mail of borrowed light that moved when he moved, the last of the un-scrammed aether hoarded close about one body — all the stolen brightness of a hundred floors drawn in to warm a single man. He looked out at the gathered world with an expression she knew from ledgers, not from nightmares. He was counting.

‘You have done the arithmetic, then,’ he said. His voice was even, and it carried without effort, and it was neither kind nor cruel; it was the voice of a man to whom other voices had never quite been real. ‘A hundred floors. More peoples than that. They do not feed themselves, general. They never did. Grain-floors and forge-floors, water high and low — I portioned all of it. That is what the throne is. Not the chair; the portioning.’ He let the quiet hold a moment, and then he made the offer she had been promised by every tempter since the Storm Queen, the offer of the age, the deal at scale, and he made it the way the Wyrm had said he would — not as a lie. As the truth, from the source, in the source’s own voice.

‘So take it,’ said Vharuk. ‘Sit here. Rule them gently — gently is within your gift; it was never within mine. Lower the floors over a lifetime. Marry the peoples slowly, keep the engines warm, ration the light. You have seen what they are when no one holds them apart.’ The borrowed mail stirred about him. ‘Someone must.’

Ede said nothing at all.

She had thought about this hour for two years, and in the thinking there had always been words — for Dun, for Hale, for Calyne, for the thirty, for the faces in the flowerbeds. Standing in it, she found she needed none, because the answer was not hers to give and had already begun.

It began at the back of the hall, farthest from the throne, where a kobold banner-bearer grounded her staff on the iron floor. One knock, wood on iron. The freed hounds’ pennon grounded beside it. Then the grave-banner, then the wall-and-star, then ten at once, then a hundred, the sound rolling forward through the host like the sea coming in — every banner in the hall struck once against the floor of the summit and then held still, a forest a hundred banners deep, none dipped, none lowered, every staff planted — and the knock went out the Last Door and down the stair and became a thing no one in that hall could ever after describe without stopping a while: the same single stroke, taken up floor by freed floor by every gate-town and steading and hall the whole height of the tower, banner-staves and billhooks and hammer-hafts and crutches striking home on a hundred floors at once, descending, fading, going all the way down to the dark of the Fencerows, where — Hobb swore it ever after, and no one doubted him — somebody thumped a fence-post.

For the space of that answer, nothing in the hall moved at all. The tellers, who agree on nothing, agree on the stillness: a hundred banners upright as winter trees, ten thousand faces turned one way, one man standing before his chair with the mail of borrowed light stirring about him — the only moving thing in the world — and the sound going away downward under everyone’s feet like a stone dropped down a well a hundred countries deep.

And standing in that stillness Ede understood at last what was being refused, and it was not the chair. What he had built — floor on floor, collar on collar, portion by portion — was the wanting: the long-taught ache for a hand to hold the door and ration the light, the prison that outlives every stone it was learned in. Steel had never once touched it. The knock was the wanting breaking, a hundred floors at once, one more collar, the last one.

The world had answered before its general. Ede waited until the last knock died in the iron underfoot, and then she gave Vharuk, King of the Ascent, the whole of her half of the shortest conversation in the book of the war:

‘You’ve heard them.’

⁂

He spent the hoard.

There was no more where it had come from — Ulfar’s lever, thrown seven floors below, had seen to that; the core was dark and the light on his body was all the light he would ever own again — and he spent it anyway, all at once, the way misers die. He hurled the Made’s every art at the host in one breath: welded fire and walking stone, the court’s glamour and the garden’s perfume, bells and banners and a marble fist of the Kingsway’s rubble, and the hall of the summit became for forty heartbeats a place the tale-tellers would spend their whole craft on and never get right.

The vanguard had named the ground while it crossed it, quietly, in the way of soldiers who need a thing called something before they will die on it: the Last Field, for the open iron between the door and the dais; the Steps, which needed no more name than that. And the fight, for all its forty-heartbeat fury, had a shape simple as a hinge: the shield-arc planted across the Last Field’s head, the world massed behind it out to the torchlight’s edge, and one small moving knot — Ede, Aldis at her right, Vega at her left, Rhovan’s arrows going by them, Nix’s count coming after — walking up the middle of everything the King could do.

The host held. It held because every art he owned, some floor had already taught them to answer. The gardeners’ word withered his blooms mid-air; Silence stilled his glamour before it crossed the first rank; the masons toppled what he raised; the shields of reforged warplate locked over the front of the world and the world knelt behind them and was not moved. Ede went forward under the black blade with Aldis at her right and Vega’s hammer at her left, and Rhovan’s arrows went by her ear into the mail of light, one, two, three, finding the seams the Weld-Face had taught him welds must have.

Then Vharuk did the last thing, the cruelest thing, the thing he had built the tower’s whole height to be able to do, and reached for the Yoke.

Ede felt it come — everyone who had ever worn the fever or the collar felt it come, a hand closing on the back of the mind — the final blast, the master-word, every floor’s madness gathered into one command: kneel. And it broke. It broke on the chantry-priests’ Recant, rank on rank of them singing the hold apart; it broke on Bly’s Silence and the barrow- queen’s crown-unmaking; it broke on ten thousand collars that were no longer there — he was calling into sockets long since emptied, pulling on strings the Broodthrone’s cutters had severed, commanding an army of slaves that the climb had spent two years turning, one by one, floor by floor, mercy by mercy, into no one’s slaves at all. The Yoke’s last word went out into the gathered world and found not one bowed head.

Ede saw him understand it. That was the wound that finished him, before any steel: the arithmetic coming home. He had spent an age making certain he need never fight the world, and the world was in his room.

Aldis Bright reached the throne-steps first, an age late, and turned his guard the way she had once nearly turned it, and this time there was no one behind him to take her alive. Vega spoke Unmake into the mail of borrowed light and the light unraveled from him like thread off a spool. And Ede Stonhal came up the last three steps of the Ascent with the King-steel blade — the Door’s own metal, the fallen of five ages folded into its grain — and she offered him nothing. She had offered something to every warden since the tenth floor; a collar broken, a name called, a mound mourned, a mead of memory. For him there was nothing to offer. No collar to break: he had forged them. No fever to cure: he had sown it. No weld to undo: he was the welder. He was not the tower’s prisoner. He was the tower.

She ended him on his own throne-floor, above his own hall, in front of everyone.

⁂

What happened then, every survivor of the summit told differently, and all the tellings agree.

The light went home.

The hoarded aether did not die with him and did not scatter. It rose off the falling body in a slow bright tide, all the stolen brightness of an age — and it turned downward. It poured off the summit’s edges and down through the iron floor as if the floor were not there, gently, without haste, the way water finds its level, the way carts go at dusk toward a known barn. It fell through the hall in threads and sheets and slow rivers of pale gold, and where it passed among the host it did not burn; people stood in it with their hands open; Ede stood in it and felt nothing take and nothing pull — only passage, only a going-by, the largest homecoming there had ever been, moving through ten thousand people on its way down to everywhere.

And down on every floor at once, that hour — they pieced it together afterward, walking down, gathering the tellings like gleaners — the Theft let go. Every remaining collar in the tower fell open in the same breath, on floors the rising had freed and floors it had only passed through, in back-galleries and larders and forgotten pens, with a sound like frost leaving a line. The fever lifted out of every beast on every floor between one heartbeat and the next: fenland wolves went quiet mid-snarl and were only wolves; the last pack of the Fencerows lay down in the pale grass, and one of them, at a whistle from a hedge, turned its head. Every construct the King had ever poured drained where it stood to honest dead metal. In the Welding Halls the iron on the welded went inert — not flesh again, never flesh again, but no longer a hand on them, only a weight they wore — and Marn, standing under his banner on the summit with the light going by him, later told Nix it was the first hour in twenty years that the iron had weighed nothing at all.

The light fell past Sliver, and Sliver flared in its pouch like a struck lamp, and Ede’s hand went to it in plain fear.

‘Go if you’re going,’ she said, and her voice cracked on it. ‘You’ve carried enough.’

‘I was not stolen,’ Sliver said, and its dryness had never been so plainly a kind of love. ‘I chose. I have chosen nine times. Mind the step, general; you have been two years climbing and there are a great many stairs to go down.’

The last of the light sank through the deepest floors and was gone into the world’s old bones, and the hall of the summit stood in the plain lamplight of the torches the host itself had carried up — no other glow anywhere in it, nothing broadcast, nothing borrowed, every light in the room a light some person had trimmed and carried and owned. Under the black sky the gathered peoples looked at one another in it: at ten thousand faces of nine-and-thirty kinds, keepers and freed, giants and goblins, unportioned, unpartitioned, standing on the one floor in creation where no two of them were ever to have met.

It was Hollen who said it, at last — the oldest keeper of the highest floor, in the hush, to no one and to all of them:

‘He built a hundred floors so we would never stand in one room.’ The old man looked down the hall and began, helplessly, to laugh. ‘Look at us. Look behind you, look at us — every floor he ever cut apart is standing in this one now.’

The empty chair of cold iron stood over the hall a while longer, and then Vega Coalbrow, without any ceremony at all, took it apart with hammer and Unrivet for the metal — the rising being short of good iron, and the summit being, as Voss observed while weighing the scrap, no longer the sort of establishment that required a throne.

Ede Stonhal stood on the steps where it had been, with the black blade sheathed and the muster-roll against her heart, and did not sit down, and did not stay. Below her the whole world was in one room and already beginning, in forty languages, to argue about supper — alive, unruled, loud, and free — and down through the open Last Door lay a hundred freed floors and every one of them was on the way home.

She went down into her people, and the climb was over.

Chapter 48 — The Trials

There was no morning on the summit. There would never be one; the black sky over the highest hall in creation owed nobody a dawn, and the freed world camped beneath it woke instead to a bell.

Vega’s bell — rung once for a battle and once for the dead — spoke the third ringing of its life across the iron acres, and it was not the Break-word this time and not a mourning. It was a summons. The tower had rung bells at people for an age; here, Ede thought, under the gantry while the brass settled, was a bell that asked.

What it asked them to was the thing nobody had wanted to name the night before, while the light went home and the world argued about supper. The dead King had left the living behind. The courtiers of the gilded tiers taken alive when their floor fell. The under-heralds of Malgrim’s order, willing clerks of a willing trade, swept up alive at the Gate and after. The factors and lift-lords and levy-brokers of the freed floors, who had never worn a collar and had grown fat selling the collared their neighbors. And the collared-by-choice, the quiet willing of a hundred floors, whose iron had fallen dead off them in the same breath as everyone else’s, and who stood now in the crowds of the freed wearing the same bare necks and not the same hearts.

“Battle is a bad court, but it sits quick,” Ede had said at the council fire, the night the throne came down. “This is different. This is us, in the morning, with time to think. Whatever we do now is the peace, doing its first day’s work.”

She would not take the judgment. She had refused it at the ninetieth floor with her oldest friend bound in front of her, and she refused it now, for the same reason: she had climbed two years to keep the rising from becoming a thing with one neck and one hand. What she did instead, with the council, in the night, was build a court.

They built it out of what the climb already owned. The charter-table came up from the baggage — the long plank of the Frosthall, scarred and ringed by the whole war’s councils — and was set where the throne had stood until yesterday, on the bare patch Vega’s crews had left when they carried the King’s chair away as scrap. Dara Lastmuster brought the conscript-rolls and her striking-pen. Bly’s servants brought the long ledger of the gilded court’s crimes, read once over a ruin and carried up so the King would hear it; the King was past hearing, and the book remained, which was the way of books. Nib’s waiting-folk brought the petitions — eighty years of the taken’s unread grievances out of the Filing Crypt — and stacked them at the table’s end in bundles tied with grey cord: the case against the tower, written by its victims and filed by its own clerks. And Voss came with his scales and his day-book, because sentence in this court would be counted in grain and roads and years of keeping, and somebody had to price a year of keeping who could not be accused of mercy.

The jury was the freed. That was the whole invention, and it was not even new; it was the sentence of Berrin Or, grown up. For each of the willing, the wronged floor’s own people to hear and answer — not the general, not a bench of the grand. The ones who had paid were the ones with the right to set the price.

Evil kept good books, Sliver said from her breastbone, as the first bundle was untied. I told you at the Warcamp to learn the habit. I did not think you would improve on it.

⁂

The court’s first entry was the rising’s own.

Ede had settled that with herself in the night, and with nobody else. When the table was seated and the hall’s vast hush had gathered in, she went to Dara Lastmuster’s open book before any accused was called, and gave her name to the clerk as plainly as a recruit at a gate.

“Enter this. On the ninety-eighth floor, the court of the gilded tiers being taken and its lords refusing all terms, I ordered killing past the needs of the fight. The men and women who did it did it on my word, and their names do not belong in this line. Mine does.” The pen scratched. The hall did not breathe. “Write it plain, so that whoever keeps rolls after us knows the rising did it, and knows the rising wrote it down.”

Rhovan stood at her shoulder while it was written. On the third floor of the world, a lifetime ago, he had logged a kill of hers in silence, a surrendered kobold, one line in a book; neither of them had ever spoken of it, and he did not speak of it now. He only took the pen after her, as witness, and signed, and that was the whole conversation.

It was not absolution and was not meant to be. It was the ledger’s oldest law, the one the tower had taught them by obeying it: what is written down can be answered for. What is not written down is loose in the world, doing its work in the dark.

Then the willing were called.

⁂

The first called was a woman of Lampfall, Annis Cray, and her case taught the court its measure.

She was old, a miner’s widow, and the collar-scar at her throat was rubbed smooth with years. Nobody had informed on her; the willing signed nothing, that was the whole craft of the collar, and she could have stood among the freed forever. She came to the table herself, first of all the willing in the world, and said the iron had been her own asking — taken in the famine winter the tolls trebled, for a granary post and a double ration. She stood before her own floor’s people with her hands folded and said the ration had fed three grandchildren whose parents the tower had already taken; that she had weighed it at the time, her neck against their winter, and would weigh it the same again; and that they should do as they liked, since everyone she had made the bargain for was grown now, and safe, and here — and she nodded, once, at two young miners in the crowd with their caps in their hands.

The jury of Lampfall was out no time at all. An old digger stood up and said what the whole hall needed a first case to say: that the tower had built a world where a grandmother’s neck was the price of bread, and then had the gall to keep her receipt; that the crime was the famine, and the famine had an owner, and the owner was dead; and that Annis Cray’s name was to be struck from the registers of the willing and entered in the rolls of the wronged, where it had belonged for twenty years.

Ede sealed it and found her voice needed a moment. Somewhere behind her eyes a man on a broken drum-throne poured a chain from hand to hand and asked her who chose you? The line between the willing and the starved ran through every soul the tower had ever touched, and the court would misplace it a hundred times in the years ahead. But it had begun by knowing the line was there.

The second was Corven Dale, and he was another matter.

Levy-master of the Anvil Commons, a free man all his life: no collar, no scar, no famine at his back. For eleven years he had filled the tower’s labor-drafts under license, and his manifests — kept in his own neat hand, seized in his own strong-room — showed four hundred and six souls of his floor sold up the lifts, debtors and strangers and the awkward poor, priced by age and trade. He had grown mills on it. He stood before the jury of his own floor in a good coat and made the defense Ede had known was coming, because she had heard its shape before, from a kinder mouth.

“The drafts were the tower’s law,” Corven Dale said. “Refuse, and the press-gangs took at random — the old, the mothers, anyone. I chose. I chose carefully. Fewer went up under my hand than under any factor before me, and the Commons stood while harder floors burned. Read the numbers, if you can read anything. I was the gentlest thing the law allowed to exist.”

True in every line, Ede thought, and wrong in the sum. The tower’s arithmetic, still walking around in a good coat.

A woman of the Commons stood up in the jury with her hands shaking and asked for rope. Her name was Edda Trench; her two sons were in the manifests, one priced at fourteen, one at sixteen; neither had come back down with the freed, and Dara Lastmuster’s rolls had one of them struck at the Welding Halls, and of the other no line at all. She said rope, and said it again, and the hall’s great crowd made a sound under its breath like weather changing, because four hundred and six is a number, and a mother is not.

It was Marta Reed who answered her — senior of the thirty, who had stood in the ring at the ninetieth floor and voted rope for Berrin Or and been outvoted by her own company.

“I asked for rope once,” Marta said. “Ten floors down, for a worse man than this one. My company voted me down, and I have had the tiers to think about it, so I’ll say what the thinking came to.” She did not orate; Shieldwall had never had an orator; she said it the way you read a delivery-bill. “A man of ours said it at that fire: rope’s a wage. Paid once, and done, and light. That man —” she looked at Corven Dale the way you look at weather, “— owes four hundred and six years of other people’s lives, and I’m not minded to let him settle for one of his own. The sentence of Berrin Or stands at the ninetieth floor, written by the thirty and sealed by the general. I ask this court to cite it, and better it.”

So it was cited — read whole into the record by Dara Lastmuster, the first precedent of the freed world’s law: he shall live; he shall provision what he sold, one year’s keeping for every soul, at the reckoning of that floor’s keeper; no blade, no office, no books but the open kind; and he shall walk it under parole. And the jury of the Anvil Commons went out and came back and bettered it.

Corven Dale would live. His strong-rooms would open to Voss’s assessors, and his mills would grind under the floor’s own wardship — and he would do the accounting: name by name, he would trace his manifests through the tower’s registers, the welding-lists, the conscript-rolls, the grave-wards’ books, until every soul he had priced was found living and provisioned, or found dead and brought home, or written into the rolls of the lost with his name beside the entry as the man who owed the finding. One year’s keeping to every household, at the keeper’s reckoning. No blade, no office, no ledger but the open kind. Under parole.

“Whose parole?” Ede asked, as the form required, and Edda Trench stood up before Nix could, and said, in a voice with nothing left in it but the iron:

“Mine. He’ll start with my sons.”

Nix, who held one parole already and had come ready to hold more, took a fresh slate from his bag, wrote her name at the top, and handed it over, and showed her, leaning close, the way he ruled his columns: once for the floor, once for the dead.

⁂

They sat until the lamps were twice trimmed, and the shape of the peace came out of the cases the way a road comes out of walkers.

The under-heralds of Malgrim’s order, seven taken alive: the floors they had marked for the Herald-fire sentenced them to rebuild what the fire took, gate by gate, at the keepers’ reckoning — and then to a longer work. The petitions. Eighty years of the taken’s grievances stood bundled on that table, unread by the power they were addressed to, and the court sentenced the Herald’s clerks to read them — aloud, floor by floor, on the floors where they were written, to whoever came; every petition, to its end, however many years of evenings it took; because witness was half of every sentence this court gave, and because men who had spent their lives writing the tower’s answers could spend the rest of them delivering the world’s mail.

The courtiers of the gilded tiers, the willing who had yielded when their lords did not: sentenced each to the floors their fortunes had been farmed from, to work the reckonings their own stewards’ books laid out, under the parole of those floors’ keepers. Two refused — a silk- voiced pair spared the Vizier’s end, who declined, with perfect courtesy, to work at all. The court did not keep them. It kept nothing and no one; that law was older in the rising than the court was. Their refusals were written into the book, witnessed, and sealed, and the two were let walk out through the crowd of the freed with their names gone ahead of them down a hundred floors of a world that kept books now, and knew what they were owed, and owed them exactly that.

“They’ll starve on courtesy inside a year,” Rhovan said, watching them go.

“Then they’ll come to a reckoning hungry,” Ede said. “The table sits on every floor. That’s the sentence. They’re only choosing the door they come in by.”

Late in the day, Aldis Bright came to stand at Ede’s shoulder and watch the court work, the oldest soldier in the world reading the freed world’s first law the way quartermasters read, fast and twice.

“We never got this far,” she said at last. “My climb. We had the word for the collars and the steel for the Wardens and nothing at all for the day after. I used to think that was why we lost.” She watched Edda Trench bend over a slate with a goblin, ruling columns. “Write this part down twice, general. Any age can break a thing. This is the craft that doesn’t come back on its own.”

⁂

The bell rang the court down at dusk — the fourth ringing, and the first that meant only: done for the day, and more tomorrow, and tomorrow’s tomorrow, on the floors, going down. The court would travel with the wagons. It would sit at the Herald’s Gate and the Frosthall and the Fair and the Warcamp, wherever the willing had debts and the wronged had voices, for years, in gate-towns Ede would never see. The best of what they had built was that it did not need her.

She stayed at the table after, in the lamp-hush, with the seal cooling under her hand. It had cost her all day, the old cost: rope was clean, rope was quick, and every hour of that long sitting some tired soldier at the back of her skull had asked for the gate at dawn and the short answer — and she had paid the day out in the old slow coin instead, and the spending held, and holding it was work, and would be work all her life. Mercy was not the wage of the strong. It was the discipline of the tired, kept up daily, like drill.

Across the hall, in a ring of lamplight, an under-herald of the King sat on a wagon-board with a grey-corded bundle open on his knees, reading aloud to the waiting-folk the first petition anyone had ever answered: a request, eighty years old, in a hand gone brown, that the lamplighter of a drowned village be told his daughter was alive. The clerk read it badly, his voice going; the waiting-folk listened the way the freed listen, which is completely; and when he finished, an old woman at the ring’s edge stood and said the daughter’s name, and her own, and where she had died, and that she had died free — and the clerk wrote the answer on the petition’s back, the first line of a work of years.

Tomorrow the wagons. Tomorrow the long stair home.

Tonight the reading went on into the dark, petition by petition, name by name, and the freed world sat up listening under its black sky, and nobody anywhere was waiting for a number to be called.

Epilogue — The Descent

Going down took longer than going up, which nobody had thought to expect, and which made every kind of sense once you were in it. Climbing, you fight a floor. Descending, you meet it.

Nix counted the floors down the way he had counted everything since the Kingsfield — out loud, on his fingers, twice — and the count went slowly because every freed floor wanted its hour. The waiting-folk of the antechambers walked out of their hall for good and had to be shown, floor by floor, that doors elsewhere simply opened when you pushed them. The gardeners of Bloomside carried their woken planted down on litters, and every gate-town on the way turned out to feed them. The masons stopped a week on the Kingsway going down, not to topple anything — the toppling was done — but to carve, small and low on one standing plinth, in a plain hand, the names of everyone the avenue had ever lied about. Nix read them until his eyes gave out. Then he had them read to him.

Somebody asked him, on the long stair below the Kingsway, whether going down felt different from going up. Nix considered it for half a floor. “Turns out you climb a tower the same way you leave one,” he said. “One true step, then the next.” And went back to counting.

He walked, mostly, near the wagons. That was where his work was. The parole of Berrin Or was a single sheet, written by the thirty and signed by the thirty, and it said in effect that the man who had sold Shieldwall would spend the rest of his life provisioning every floor he had ever sold, forever, and that Nix held his rope. There was no rope. That was the point of it, Ede had said, and Nix had taken a while to understand and then had understood it better than anyone: the sentence was not that Berrin be dragged from floor to floor. The sentence was that he go.

So the traitor of the age drove a supply-wagon down the freed tower with a goblin sitting beside him counting sacks, and that was the justice of the rising, and there were people on every floor who thought it soft until they watched Berrin’s face for a while, and then stopped thinking that.

⁂

At the Frosthall they stayed nine days, because Jarl Hrimgar would not hear of fewer.

The hall had been building all the year of the upper war — the hall-folk of Hearthold had sworn, after the feast of the burning, that the rafters would go back up before the war came down, and they had kept it. New roof-trees, new benches, the old carvings re-cut where fire had eaten them; and the whole vast place smelling of sap and mead and snow through the open doors. The banners of the parliament hung again from the rafters, all hundred of them now, sewn fresh by anyone whose floor had one, and Nix stood under them a long time with his neck bent back, counting, which by then the whole host understood was how Nix wept.

There were two doors into the Frosthall, as there had always been, and the hall-folk had done a thing with them. Over the north door, cut deep in the new lintel-beam, it said DUN HARROW HELD HERE. Over the south, in the same hand, HALE HELD HERE. No titles on either, because Bruna Hearthold, who had ordered the cutting, judged that the one man had carried a banner and the other had carried a doctrine, and both had put down what they carried and kept a door, and a door is a door.

The feast was a true feast. Hrimgar gave the toast standing, in the old form, to the guest, the road, and the dead; the skalds sang Bruna’s true songs and then a new one, still rough at the joints, that Nix pretended very loudly not to recognize himself in. Giants danced. Rhovan was persuaded to sing, which he did the way elves do, one true verse and then silence for an hour while everyone thought about it.

And at the high bench Ede Stonhal sat at Hrimgar’s right hand and laughed, actually laughed, twice — Nix counted — and the second time she caught Nix counting and raised her cup half an inch to him across the hall, and Nix raised his, and neither of them said Dun’s name, and both of them drank to it.

⁂

At the fortieth floor the wagons halted at the mouth of the Broodthrone, and Rhovan of the Grey Rides straightened his coat like a man going to a hanging he approved of, and went in to pay his debt.

Nix went along, officially as parole-keeper of the wagons, in truth because nothing in the world would have kept him out. The great cavern was strung with silk still — silk was what she was, there was no freeing Vyx of Vyx — but the cocoons were long empty, the court long walked free, and the tribute-webs hung with honest trade-goods now: Threadneedle paid rates for websteel, and the rates were posted, and Voss himself had countersigned them, which Vyx appeared to regard as the only compliment the age had paid her.

The Matriarch received them on the throne-web, vast and grey-velvet and folded, a teacup of warmth steaming between her forehands in the old courtesy. The contract was recited — one true story, never told before, annually, in perpetuity — and the clauses examined, and found binding, and savored in the finding.

‘Well, hostage,’ said Vyx. ‘What have you brought me this year?’

‘The whole of it,’ Rhovan said.

Eight eyes considered him. ‘Define the whole.’

‘From the first page,’ said Rhovan. ‘A farm rising into the dark, and a child watching it go, whose name I will give you in its place in the telling. A sealed ledger-page. A woman who trained other people’s recruits. Everything the Wake did and everything I hid from you the year I sat your web and told you fractions. The feast. The shield on the trophy wall. What the wyrm smelled. Who it was. What we did with him. The Door, the throne, the light going home. All of it, in order, true, and never told whole to anyone — not even to her. It will take some nights.’

Vyx was still for a long moment, the way the web is still.

‘You are overpaying,’ she said at last, ‘by some years.’

‘I know my clauses,’ Rhovan said. ‘Call it interest.’

So the spider-queen of the Deep settled herself the way listeners have settled since fires were new, and the elf sat cross-legged on the silk where he had once sat as her hostage, and told her the book — this one, all of it — and Nix stayed for the first three nights, wedged in a warm gallery, hearing the war he had walked through come back at him as a story, until he got to the part where he was in it and left, embarrassed and gleaming, to see to his wagons.

He heard later that when the telling ended, days on, Vyx sat silent the better part of an hour and then said the contract was discharged for the year, and that the story wanted only one repair, which she would make herself: it had been told to her as the tale of a rising, and it was plainly, said the keeper of contracts, examining the thing from all sides with a broker’s eye, a tale about the keeping and breaking of words.

⁂

At the thirtieth floor the black moat lay quiet around the great mound, and the dead slept, and you crossed the water on a flat-bottomed ferry poled by a Tollman in Voss’s livery, and it cost a copper.

A posted board at the ferry-stage said so: ONE COPPER THE CROSSING. FREIGHT BY WEIGHT. KEEPERS, COLLAR-BREAKERS, AND THE THIRTY CROSS FREE. Nix read the last line four times. There were no chains anywhere on the stage — Nix looked, thoroughly, on principle — and when a drover’s family came up short at the fare-box, the Tollman waved them aboard with the weary air of a man under standing orders he had given up arguing with.

Voss was at the stage himself that day, weighing freight. He had grown no rounder and no kinder coming down; he had simply, as Ede put it, moved his whole ledger to the other side of the war and balanced it there. Ferry-fares on twenty floors, at a copper a head, honestly posted, honestly kept — ‘A toll is a wall with a slot in it,’ he told Nix, unasked, dropping the family’s uncollected coppers into the box from his own purse and marking the sum in a book titled GOODWILL, ‘whereas a fare is a road that pays for its own mending. I am in roads now. Roads,’ said the Tollmaster-General of the rising, with the contentment of a man who had finally found a commodity no one could liberate, ‘go both ways.’

Nix paid his copper. Voss gave him change. Nix kept the change all the way down to the first floor, in his cheek-pouch, like a pearl.

⁂

The twentieth floor still smelled of stone and old smoke, and would for a lifetime. But the drum-towers were down, and the pens were gone — torn out by the people they had held, the bars gone to Vega’s forges and come back as ploughshares and pit-props — and the mine was a mine again, and Shieldwall the gate-town was raising new roofs under the great gallery’s lamps.

The wagons came down the switchback road in the morning, eleven of them, grain and seed-grain, salt, iron stock, lamp-oil, and new bread. Nix rode the first wagon. Berrin Or drove it.

He was grey now, and thinner than the man who had kept a granary and everyone’s confidence in Roothollow, and he had driven the whole descent with the steadiness of a man who knows exactly how many floors his sentence has and does not mean to miscount them. At the gallery mouth, where the drums had been, Dara Shieldwall stood waiting with her tally-slate, and behind her, not gathered, only failing to be anywhere else, stood a fair number of the folk of the floor he had sold.

Nobody spoke. That was the shape the thing had found, delivery by delivery, floor by floor: nobody spoke, and nobody helped, and nobody stopped him. Berrin let down the tailboard and carried the sacks in himself, one at a time, past Dara, into the granary the orcs had once used for a gaol; and the smell of Roothollow bread went past the people of Shieldwall in the arms of the man whose smell it had once betrayed, and they watched it go by, and let it.

It took him until midday. Dara counted the sacks twice and signed the slate, and looked at him once, straight, the way you look at weather.

‘Same time next season,’ she said.

‘Yes,’ Berrin said.

That was all. That was always all; and Nix, who held the parole and had now watched twenty floors of it, understood better every time why the thirty had chosen it over a rope. A hanged man is finished. A provisioner is never finished. Berrin Or would spend the rest of his life doing, floor after floor, season after season, forever, the exact thing he had once pretended to be — and the difference between pretending and doing it was to be his whole punishment, and would be quite enough.

He climbed back onto the empty wagon and took up the reins and waited, eyes forward, for the word.

‘Nineteen to go,’ Nix said, and cracked his knuckles, and did not say it unkindly. Nix said very little unkindly anymore. He had counted what it cost.

⁂

They came down into the Fencerows at first lamp, if you could still call it that — the Yoke’s false daylight had died with the tower’s will, and Hobb Fennick’s people had strung honest lanterns along the hedgerow lines, so the valley went to a soft gold at the edges and the sky above it was plain dark, miles deep, restful.

The hedges were laid and trimmed. The Mother Ditch had a mended drove-bridge. There was a fair on Fairstone Green — an actual fair, pitches and bunting and a fiddler, the first in a lifetime — and a dog was working a flock along the water-meadow to a whistle, which stopped Nix in the road entirely for a while.

Hobb met the wagons at Lamplit Steading with his coat on and his tally in his hand, and he made them stand there, the whole train, while he did the thing he had been waiting the age of the tower to do. He counted them in. He walked the line with his stub of chalk, wagons, walkers, drovers, one goblin, and he checked it against the count sent down from the second floor, and he carried the two figures to the gate-post where his lifetime of wrong sums was notched, and he set them side by side, and looked at them a long time.

‘They match,’ said Hobb Fennick, to the gate-post, to the valley, to every climber he had ever counted into the hedges and never counted out. He said it the way other men say amen. ‘Everyone who went in has come out. The counts match.’

He chalked it on the post, and then, being Hobb, he checked it once more.

⁂

Roothollow had not grown so much as opened. The gate-queue was gone; the tower stood at the town’s back the way a cliff stands over a shore, filling the north of the world from horizon to horizon, its top beyond the air — a fact of the country now, its dark doorway propped wide and its first stair swept. People went up it to visit their kin on the near floors, and came down again for market-day. The Stone of the Climb stood where it had always stood, with its thirty names cut by Ede’s own hand in the bad years — and no one had scratched them out; instead someone had cut, beneath the column, in newer letters: CAME HOME.

The market square was loud the morning the last wagons came in. And at the head of the square, in the trampled ground between the aether-forge and the horse trough where the recruits had always drilled, the recruits were drilling.

There were more of them than there had ever been, and they were of more kinds than Roothollow had ever seen — farm boys and kobolds, a young giant, two of the waiting-folk’s grandchildren stood very straight — and the drill-mistress walking their line with her hands behind her back was Ede Stonhal, and the assistant drill-mistress arguing with her about footwork, to the open delight of the whole front rank, was Aldis Bright. The general of the rising had been offered the summit, the throne-hall, and the naming of the age. She had come home instead to train other people’s recruits — her old trade, made honest: she would climb with them now, whenever the climbing was wanted, the whole way, every time.

The banner went up at midday, without a speech. Ede had refused every proposal to plant it on the summit — the summit, she said, had had quite enough flags — and so it was done here, in the market, where the whole thing had started: a socket of good stone set in the trampled ground by Vega’s own hands, and the oldest banner in the world raised over the drill-yard by Marn’s one uncovered hand and Tam’s two, the grey wall and the crooked star hanging out full in the valley wind over recruits, and market-stalls, and the forge, and the trough, and the ordinary priceless racket of a town with nothing over it but sky.

Nix watched from the top of a grain-barrel, counting nothing at all for once. His parole-sheet was folded in his cheek-pouch beside Voss’s copper; the wagons were unloaded; the age was over; supper was cooking somewhere and he had been promised some of it.

The drill ended at dusk. The recruits racked their staves and went to eat, loudly, the way recruits have always gone to eat, and the square filled with lamplight and shout and the smell of bread that meant only bread now. And because a drill-yard is a working place and not a shrine, the last one off the ground — a farm girl with her hair in a soldier’s knot, born the year of the Warcamp, who would be tall enough for the wall in a season or two — lifted the banner out of its socket at the drill’s end as she’d been taught, to take it in for the night; and then supper called her too, the way it calls the young, and she stood the old pole down for just a moment, carefully, in the one place in the square where a thing may lean and not fall.

And that is where the story leaves it, if a story ever leaves anything: the hundredth banner and the first one, the grey wall and the crooked star, furled for the evening in a market square at the bottom of a mountain that used to be a prison — leaning against the horse trough.

APPENDICES

MATTER SAVED FROM THE FALL

compiled at Roothollow, in the first year of the ground

The Banners, and Who Carried Them

Compiled at Roothollow after the fall, from the rebuilt muster-roll, the census-wall, the tally-slates, the Herald’s ledger, and the mouths of the living. One line to a name; the clerks were told the paper was short and the grieving was not, and to let the line do both jobs.

The rising’s core

  • Ede Stonhal — of Men, of Roothollow. Cut thirty names on a stone and freed all thirty. Wanted to die making amends; sentenced by victory to live being followed. Drills recruits in the market square now, and climbs with them.
  • Rhovan of the Grey Rides — elf, archer, formerly of the Wake. Told the truth in fractions for forty years; the sum, audited at the end, came out whole. Overpays a spider annually and calls it interest.
  • Vega Coalbrow — giant, forge-matron. Put her mark into collar-steel young and spent a hundred floors buying it back; the receipt was a bell.
  • Nix — goblin. Picked up a fallen standard to hide behind and was never afterward found hiding. Counts things; weeps in numbers; holds the age’s one parole, and counts slow.
  • Sliver — a shardmind. Has carried forty-one climbers and chosen nine; last light of its kind; a poor liar, that being a fault of crystal.
  • Berrin Or — Roothollow’s provisioner. Fed the rising for two years and had sold it for twenty. Keeps books still, by sentence of the thirty — the open kind only, and a wagon, and a list longer than his life.
  • Mistress Calyne — spymaster of the Wake. Suspected to her face for three parts of a climb; right about everything, thanked for none of it, dead proving the case.
  • Gilder Voss — Tollmaster-General, then quartermaster-general at a fixed honest fee. Never became good; became invested. Is in roads now; roads, he will tell you unasked, go both ways.
  • Thane Ulfar Coalbrow — of the Forge-Court. Climbed to inherit the tower warm; his forty sheets of vellum were right about everything except the hours. Put out the world’s stolen fire with his own hand.

The Shieldwall

  • Aldis Bright — founder of the first banner; stood in the Last Door an age. Asked who commands, and, told, saluted.
  • Dun Harrow — standard-bearer, one-armed. The record shows no instance of his wavering. Held the north door; the lintel says so, without titles.
  • Marta Reed — senior after Dun. Called the roll until all thirty answered, the quick and the dead; voted rope; was outvoted; enforces the better sentence to the letter.
  • Sefwin — welded, iron from the jaw down, every word paid for. His first in twenty years was the courier’s name; his next four were the sentence: let him feed them back.
  • Marn — of the thirty, freed at the Welding Halls; carried the last banner in the world through the Last Door one-handed. The iron, for one hour, weighed nothing at all.
  • Tam — of the thirty. Wept when the Champion said his name; raised the banner at Roothollow with both hands, having kept them.
  • Dara Shieldwall — turned the keys of the twentieth floor’s cages. Counts the traitor’s sacks twice at the granary door, and says: same time next season.
  • Corran Vael — mourned by name at the black moat. Did more work dead than most swords manage living.

The keepers

  • Hobb Fennick — the Fencerows, floor one of the world. Kept counts that did not match for a lifetime, climbed a hundred floors to watch them made to, and checked once more.
  • Bruna Hearthold — hall-steward of the Frosthall. Hid the true songs eleven years and out-sang a skald in its own hall; ordered two lintels cut, without titles, a door being a door.
  • Mereth Wakesend — keeper of the funeral-forms. Taught an army to mourn in good order, which the enemy entered as a weapon.
  • Wend Threadneedle — thread-cutter of the fortieth floor. Nine years of cocoons, one strand at a time; both forearms say so.
  • Aline Bridlerest — last ride-master of the Grey Rides. Recalled a hundred-year hunt to its own supper.
  • Wend Latchlight — the Trapdoor Fields. Walked an army across a country that was one trap, saying, in the main, “slowly.”
  • Bruna Coldquay — the Frozen Sea. Taught the drivers Kindle and roaded the ice for a fleet’s worth of runners.
  • Ferro of Coreside — keeper of the scram-lore. Kept one lesson his whole life, said it once, to the right people, and wept without sound, and needed nobody.
  • Hollen — Lastward, floor ninety-nine. Kept where a name was buried, under the sill; laughed last, helplessly, and first.
  • Bly, Cord, Elys, Vess, and Nib — keepers of the throne-tiers: a Silence, a chisel, a garden, an armory, and eighty years of petitions, all walked up through the Last Door at the head of the queue.

The Wake

Founded after the Theft by three lamplighter houses in the only grove still lit; climbed to make the wood whole, whatever it let its hirelings believe. Its two great agents are entered above, and both were right.

The Broodthrone and the Warrens

  • Matriarch Vyx — freeholder of the fortieth floor by right of survival. Would not fight, that being arithmetic and not mercy; contracted, and kept every clause to its final letter. Regards a countersigned rate-board as the age’s one compliment to her.
  • Wend Silkstead — the Broodwarrens, which yielded whole to a goblin’s word and was betrayed by doctrine. Buried none of the Cullers on her floor. Her one condition.

The Cullers

  • Serjeant Hale — taught the general to fight and a generation to kill the surrendered. Broken by his own doctrine at the warrens; carried out his own dead; asked, once, how the goblin does it. Held the south door.

The Hunt

  • The Pale Huntsman — Warden of the eightieth. Never spoke, which was worse. Kept eighty years of fair-hand books and was walked in through one bad clause.
  • Nael Hornsend — horn-caller. Kept the calloff-lore against the day somebody would need a hunt unsounded, and lived to teach it.
  • The freed hounds — took no notice of the one man they should have; carry a pennon sewn as a joke and borne like a relic.

The Storm Court

  • Zephyra — Storm Queen, Warden of the seventieth. Offered the sky at the price of a throne; refused, scattered a third of the host. Paid her treaty-debt in full, one noon late, and wheeled above the finished field awaiting applause.
  • Her bottled court — kept in storm-glass as the tower’s sureties; freed unthanked. A queen schedules her own debts.

The tower’s own

  • Vharuk — King of the Ascent, architect of the Theft. Portioned a world and mistook the portioning for godhood; spent his last hour listening for a tower that no longer answered.
  • Malgrim — Herald of the King, floor ninety. Freed once on the forty-third and starved on it; chose the collar back and kept honest books for a dishonest house. Heard the Break-word said over his own steel and was not changed, which was the whole of him.
  • The Marshal — Malgrim’s second; the collar had grown a man around it, his men said. Reached last of all; carried his sword across the yards flat on two palms.
  • Gnarl — the Goblin King, floor ten. Crowned as a joke at his own people’s expense, and knew it; refused the Break-collar for the crown and died a king of nothing.
  • Warlord Skarn — the Red Orc, floor twenty. Priced his collar as a rank and defended it as one.
  • The Barrow King — a drowned king’s mound given voice, floor thirty. Could not be out-fought and was out-mourned; conceded the deeper grief and fell honestly.
  • Cindermaw — the Wyrm, floor fifty. Older than the Theft; the only being in the tower that pitied Vharuk. Smelled one climber twice, and said so.
  • Jarl Hrimgar — Warden of the sixtieth, welded at his own high seat, absent from none of the wrong feast’s eleven years. Freed, he set the crown down like a debt.
  • Halflight — the core’s demon; burned at half a sun and could not die while it fed. Starved standing; remains a cinder in the shape of a kneeling man.
  • The Heralds — the collared willing. Forty servers and twelve bows at one feast, spent to the last man and entered as worth it.
  • Dara Lastmuster — muster-captain of the freed, floor ninety. Struck thousands of names from the conscript-rolls one stroke each, would give the work to no one, and finished it.
  • Bastionheart and Fluegrim — a drill-master and a warren-captain of the Made; the tower’s discipline and the tower’s livery, both changed employers intact.
  • The Kettle — looms. Carries two bowls. Both facts are load-bearing.

A Gazetteer of the Hundred Floors

Compiled at Roothollow in the first year after the fall, from the reports of climbers and the letters of the keepers, this gazetteer sets down each floor of the Ascent as it was found and as it was left. Where the accounts disagree, the keeper’s word is kept.

Floors 1–20 · The Debt

1 · The Fencerows. Hedged farm country of Men, a whole valley of meadow taken mid-hay under bars of light that never set. The field-folk barred themselves in Lamplit Steading under Hobb Fennick, who counted the climbers into the hedges against the ones who came out. The warden Brackjaw, a wolf welded into plate, held the stair-meadow. When it fell, Hobb’s counts matched at last.

2 · The Rustwater Adit. A slab of the Giants’ mountainside cut off at the mine-mouth, still weeping orange iron-water, its ore-carts halted on rails that ended in open air. The miners of Lampfall held on under Bergda Ironkettle, pit-foreman, who read the mountain by the taste of its water. Rustmaw, a pit-hound with an ore-crusher’s jaw, hunted the flooded drift.

3 · The Drowned Pasture. Water-meadow taken mid-flood and never let drain; hay-ricks stood as islands and the weir went on working, keeping the wrong things wet. The trappers of Weirsend lived in punts under Old Nan Sedge, who had not been dry in a year. Sedgeback, a boar under weir-iron, wallowed the mill-pool. The old pasture tortoise outlasted it all, and was left in peace.

4 · The Lightless Glade. An elf-wood whose trees once lit themselves, put out in a night; past the first branches the dark was total. The lamplighters of Lanternroot hoarded their last wicks under Ithren of the Wicks, who carried an unlit lantern he refused to abandon. Palegleam, a pale stag crowned in a cage of false light, stalked the wood. One lamp in the Snuffed Hollow never went out.

5 · The Flooded Mine. The galleries below the Adit, sunk in black water with the great pumps drowned beside them. The pump-crews of Pumpstead rationed air in the dry levels under Durn Sumphand, master of pumps. Sumplock, an eel welded through with a pump-valve maw, ruled the flood. A steady knocking came from behind the Last Dry Gallery’s sealed door, and was answered in time.

6 · The Threshold Dark. The first true dark of the climb, a guano-floored vault where the floodlights failed and the under-world began. The delvers of Lastlight kept to the last lit stone under Sable Lastlamp, who knew the vault blind. Duskspin, a sentinel spider spinning steel silk, walled the stair. The Chimney held one grey smear of borrowed daylight.

7 · The Orchard Rows. Cider country taken a week before pressing, mile on mile of windfall fermenting under the lights, the air drunk with it. The pressers of Cider Cross kept the mother-tree’s true grafts alive under Cesta Pommet. Applewrath, a boar in press-iron and barrel-hoops, bedded in the deep windfall. The old press-house held a single paper nest the size of its rafters.

8 · The Ashline. Grey ash dunes the three realms called worthless, home to nomads who answered no crown. Kesh of the Grey Wells kept Greywell’s water by hard law. The warden Cinderhide was a Waste-born ogre with its collar fused to its skull — a conscript the tower made un-savable, and the grimmest early kill. A whole tent-caravan lay preserved dry under one dune.

9 · The Beacon Field. A signal-heath of humming pylons flashing false code, every light saying come here and none of them meaning safety. The keepers of Pylon Rest shuttered against the strobe under Wick Harlow, half-deaf from the hum. Glarefang, a wolf maned in strobing lamps, hunted inside its own flicker. A grounded sky-skiff lay in the heather where a false beacon brought it down.

10 · The Kingsfield. The muster-meadow where Men once raised their hosts, frozen mid-gathering under a hundred rotting standards. Serjeant Mella Rung of Bannerline kept a true color folded under her coat for years. The warden was Gnarl, the Goblin King — a goblin crowned by the tower to mock the field, welded to a throne of banner-poles, deadly and a punchline and aware of both. The color flew on the Standard-Elm at last.

11 · The Counting Halls. The Giants’ counting-houses, halls of ledger-shelves and coin-dust where gate-sized abacuses hung frozen mid-sum. The clerks of Ledgerstone kept doomed tallies under Tolla Brasspen, who would not stop balancing. Brassbone, a brass beetle that counted every blow it took and answered in kind, guarded the vault. When the theft was undone, the last ledger closed even.

12 · The Shearing Downs. Bare chalk sheep-country taken at the shearing, fleeces half-clipped, the wind never stopping. The shepherds of Woolgate counted a flock the wind had scattered under Ewan Woolgate, who knew the count was hopeless and kept it anyway. Ramsteel, a half-clipped ram helmed in flint and steel, charged the combes. The Long Barrow beacon brought the scattered in.

13 · The Eaves. The torn outer edge of the elf-wood, stranded a full tier from its own forest, humming with the dead signal of a lamp-network long snuffed. The edge-elves of Lastlamp kept a lonely watch under Nael Edgewatch. Duskhide, an edge-stag racked with dead antennae, threw the loop into the gloom. The Fallen Span still hung by one cable over the marsh below.

14 · The Gear Galleries. Machine-halls the size of towns, belts and flywheels turning on captured aether and carrying nothing. The machinists of Sprocket Row oiled them anyway under Grib Sprocket, who kept the great flywheel balanced by ear. Gearhide, a pit-fighter’s fury on a frame of meshing cogs, set the stair’s cadence wrong. The first silence, when the clutch was thrown, was worth the fight.

15 · The Cable Roots. A cavern strung floor to roof with cable so old it looked grown, live and spitting blue static. The cable-crews of Wiregate slept on dead lines under Fend Wiregate, one hand scarred white. Wirefang, a troll grafted with live bundles, re-wove the stair’s curtain faster than it could be cut. The Junction Spire wore a crown of blue fire.

16 · The Hollow Lanes. Droving country worn shoulder-deep into holloways, hedgebanks meeting overhead in green tunnels that closed behind a traveler. The drovers of Hedgeharbor were penned by their own hedges under Cobb Hedgeharbor, who no longer trusted his staff’s notches. Thornhide, a bull-boar hided in living holly, blocked the lane. A lane-map was scratched behind the Halfway Inn’s bar by a hundred hands.

17 · The Glass Flats. A plain of lightning-glass where every distance lied and the mirages had thickened to near-solid. The gleaners of Shardside rationed water and truth under Sira Shardside, who walked the flats blindfolded by choice. Glassjaw, a mirror-scaled salamander fighting from a dozen false positions, held the bluff. A merchant caravan stood fused whole in the black glass.

18 · The Millrace. Mill-country taken at full flow, every wheel turning and every stone grinding nothing, the water undercutting the ground it ran through. The millers of Wheelhouse held on under Pell Wheelhouse, who kept the great wheel true because a wheel let go crooked would tear the mill down. Milldrown, a boar made a wheel-engine, rode the head-race. Afterward the floor smelled of bread.

19 · The Fusion Vaults. The deep-forge’s fuel-store, racked with glowing aether-rods in a radiant dusk that sickened everything living in it. The vault-crews of Half-Light counted their years by exposure under Hesk Half-Light, grey-faced. Coreburn, a forge-remnant welded round a cracked rod, sealed the blast-door with its own heat. The Breach stayed shut: half a crew fused in the slag, a tomb no one reopened.

20 · The Red Warcamp. A seized mine-gallery pitched over with the tents and drum-towers of the Red Orc host, the floor’s own people caged beneath as labor and hostage. Dara Shieldwall passed word between the pens and waited. The warden was Warlord Skarn, himself first-taken, a conscript made a general — freed, not slaughtered, when the pens burst and the drums went quiet. Break-collar was learned on this floor, and the war changed its meaning.

Floors 21–40 · The Banner War

21 · The Burnt Common. A grazing heath caught by wildfire the season it was stolen, held forever at the moment after the burn, new green knee-high in the char. The commoners of Ashfold guarded the parish seed-store under Goodwife Ash Ashfold, who read the returning green like a calendar. Greycinder, a boar shelled in ember-iron, bedded in the warm ash. The seed had been hidden down a badger sett.

22 · The Whisperwood. An elf-wood that once carried the lamp-songs and now repeated everything else, giving a spoken word back armed hours later. The wood-elves of Hushfall spoke only in the Hush Hollow, under Sela Hushfall, who signed above ground. Hearsay, a quoted shade welded to the Sounding Oak, spoke with the whole wood’s voice. Silence was the only safety, until the true song was sung.

23 · The Anvil Commons. The giants’ shared forge-floor, a hundred anvils around one cold hearth, all ringing under no hand. The smiths of Hammerhome went deaf beside dead forges; old Dwald kept one banked ember alive in a clay pot, waiting for a hearth worth lighting. Anvilback, a bellows hound on an anvil frame, fought on the phantom chorus. Each quieted anvil held the tune of its dead smith.

24 · The Broodwarrens. Deep caverns packed floor to roof with silk, the under-town of Silkstead swallowed streets and all. The survivors barricaded the Sealed Vault under Wend Silkstead, a knife for silk and a lamp for the dark. Cradlesilk, a brood-midwife grown monstrous, re-wrapped the stair as fast as it was cut. One lamp burned in the only un-webbed room on the floor.

25 · The Gallows Fair. A travelling fair abandoned mid-season and still running itself — the carousel turning, the rigged games working the empty air. The fair-folk of Fairstead hid in darkened wagons under Pol Fairstead, who would not light the Big Wheel until it was safe to draw a crowd. Hookmaster, a fair-cat rigged with grabbing hooks, worked the ring. It was cheerful, and it was wrong.

26 · The Slag Barrens. The tipping-grounds of the deep forges, black glassy hills still ticking with old heat, picked by folk who made a living from what the realms threw away. Grint of Clinker Row knew which heaps would kill you by their color. Slagbone, a salamander hulked in crane-iron, slumped whole tips onto climbers. A vein of good metal, dumped in by mistake, lay buried in the largest heap.

27 · The Mirefields. Peat mire under a low sky, its safe paths marked by cairns and led wrong by drifting corpse-lights; what the peat took it kept, whole and brown. The peat-folk of Cairnside poled between firm islands under Cairn Cairnside, turf-mother, who trusted memory and never the lights. Mirebone, a boar shelled in bog-iron, wallowed the deep pool. An elder the peat had kept remembered where the old road ran.

28 · The Smelterworks. The smelting-floor, furnaces roaring on captured aether, molten runnels threading the stone, moulds casting tower-parts with no hand to shape them. The smelters of Crucible Gate poured for the tower they hated, under Bruna Tapfire, who held the master tap-plug against the day it was worth pulling. Smeltjaw, a runnel salamander with a furnace in its chest, fought on the tap-bridges. She pulled it.

29 · The Tithe Barrows. Grave-country of grassed mounds where Men laid their dead with a tithe, gone restless as the fever soaked the grave-gold. The wardens of Wakewater kept vigil under Sef Wakewater, who had not slept since the mounds began to stir. Tithegrim, a risen barrow-hound saddled with a rider of grave-gold, collected mound to mound. The vigil-book listed which barrows would open next.
