30 · The Kingsbarrow. One colossal mound ringed by a black moat, where the king-dead of Men were drowned to keep them sleeping. The tower woke the king wrong; what walked remembered only the crown. Mereth Wakesend, barrow-queen, held the causeway and refused the summons. The warden was the Barrow King, a dead sovereign commanding his drowned court — evicted rather than slain, the borrowed aether driven out and the true king laid at last to rest.

31 · The Grey Rides. The straight grey riding-roads of the elf-hunt, coursed forever by a spectral hunt after quarry freed long ago. The ride-keepers of Bridlerest walked the verges under Aline Bridlerest, sounding a recall no horn answered. Tallyhorn, a grey courser welded to its dead rider, compelled the living to run as quarry. The bridle-tally named every horse and rider the hunt had taken.

32 · The Deep Drifts. The giants’ deepest shafts, a country measured in fathoms down, winch-ropes creaking under loads that would never come up. The drift-crews of Ropewalk signaled into a dark that no longer signaled back, under Orla Ropewalk, who trusted neither rope nor echo anymore. Deepwinch, a blind digger welded into the headframe, dropped loads and swung cages. A chalked tally in the deepest bothy named the drift that broke through.

33 · The Troll Delvings. Galleries clawed out by trolls who shore up nothing — one slow cave-in held in place by luck. The miners of Delvers’ Rest propped their town as the floor fell around it, under Hune Delverest, who mapped the safe ground hour by hour. Mossmaul, a moss-hided troll with a millstone jaw, knitted its wounds as fast as they were dealt. The Elder Vault’s inner doors were barred from within.

34 · The Rig Graves. A drowned graveyard of the fen-farmers’ great harvest-rigs, hulls half-sunk, cores still ticking in the mud. The salvage-folk of Rustwake dived the wrecks under Weld Rustwake, who knew every hull by name. Rigrot, a rig-wight driving its flagship on rust-frozen legs, lured boats with drowned beacons. Before the host climbed, a salvaged rig walked the fen again — this time for the rising.

35 · The Vent Country. A cracked plain of steam-vents blowing on hidden clocks, sulfur-crusted and warm to the boot. The tenders of Steamgate timed every step under Sela Steamgate, who taught climbers to read the whistle, not the steam. Ventmaw, a salamander carrying its own fumarole, blew the vents in sequence to herd and scald. The floor was crossed by tempo or not at all.

36 · The Processional Way. A willow-lined burial causeway walked by a funeral a thousand years long, its passing-bells never still. The mourners of Mourngate tolled for a burial that could not end, under Cole Mourngate, who stopped his own ears against the bell. Mournhide, a coffin-bearer grown vast beneath the master bier, pressed the coffin onto any passer. The dead reached their grave at last.

37 · The Broken Doors. A tier of the giants’ great bulkheads, all blown off their hinges in a single season — proof that nothing the old world built kept the tower out. The doorwards of Last Lantern held the one surviving door under Bruna Lastlantern, shamed and unbending. Gatebone, a slab of blast-plate on hinge-legs, slammed shut on climbers in the choke-points. The unbreached bulkhead was the stair itself.

38 · The Peat Cuts. Industrial peat-country, house-deep trenches flooded ankle-deep and hazed with midge, where the fever taught the preserved dead to climb. The cutters of Turfside held the stack-field under Maddox Turfside, watching the Deep Cut. Peatlock, a boar under bog-oak plate that neither rotted nor tired, wallowed the bottoms. A peat-cured king was climbing his own cut when the wardens put him back down.

39 · The Echo Glades. Festival glades that caught the last revel the night of the Theft and replayed it forever; wander in and the glade danced you until you fell. The glade-elves of Fainthollow stopped their ears under Illa Fainthollow, who danced the edges to pull out the caught. Encore, the revel’s dead lead dancer, called encore on every ending. The true final note had been kept un-played in the Green Room.

40 · The Broodthrone. A cavern spun into a throne-hall of silk, hung with cocoons like lanterns, each a living captive kept as courtier or larder. Wend Threadneedle led the last free captives, cutting cocoons open one thread at a time. The warden was Matriarch Vyx, a spider-queen who puppeted her own court against the climb and wrapped to keep, not kill. When her master-lines were cut, a dozen floors’ worth of the taken walked free.

Floors 41–60 · The Gathering

41 · The Moraine Gates. A field of glacial rubble under a hard white sky, balanced boulders and a wind that cut to the bone — the first truly cold floor. The ice-folk of Coldquay hoarded warmth in the boulder-hollows under Bruna Coldquay, ice-reeve. Coldjaw, a moraine troll shelled in ice and erratic-stone, kicked avalanches down the ridges. Frozen scouts stood upright in the tarn shallows, and were counted.

42 · The Signal Deeps. Cavern-halls racked with the old world’s memory-lattices, still humming; whisper once and the deeps answered a hundred times, some of them true. The lore-keepers of Faintlight cross-checked everything thrice under Fenn Faintlight. Echobone, a signal husk welded to the Mainframe Spire, spoke with the whole archive’s false voice. The world’s own map of the tower lay in the Cold Aisle, and was read clean.

43 · The Bone Garths. The walled rendering-yards of Men, bone stacked house-high, the fever soaked into it until the heaps began to walk. The bone-wardens of Garthend laid them back down under Sef Garthend, who read a heap’s mood by its clatter. Garthbone, a hound in fused rendered bone, reformed itself from any stack it stood near. The Great Stack was fired in the old way, and the yards went quiet.

44 · The Ogre Steps. A hillside cut into hundreds of terraced kilns, all still firing on captured heat, fire-goats grazing the hot steps. The terrace-folk of Kilnrest tended a bake a thousand years done, under Grint Kilnrest, who knew every kiln’s temper by its glow. Kilnfist, an ogre welded into a kiln-oven body, vented scalding blasts across the terraces. Banked at last, the kilns fired armor for the rising.

45 · The Sunken Chantry. A pilgrimage chapel flooded to the waist, thousands of vigil-candles floating and burning still on the black water. The faithful of Bellmarsh kept the lights from the dry Bell-Loft under Mera Bellmarsh; the drowned congregation kept its pews, and wanted company. Bellrot, a drowned ringer welded to the cracked great bell, tolled a false vigil. The true bell laid the congregation to rest, at peace.

46 · The Frozen Sea. The giants’ coolant reservoir, frozen solid the night the forges died — pressure-ridges heaved up like walls, leads of black water opening without warning. The sealers of Floeside read the pack under Orla Floeside, who sounded every floe with her staff. Floeback, a troll shelled in pressure-ice, cracked leads beneath a climber’s feet. The Locked Fleet stood masts-up in the ice like a drowned town.

47 · The Doorstone Moor. A high heather moor of standing stones raised to keep something shut; one dolmen cracked open and breathed a cold wrong wind. The moor-folk of Stonewake kept the ring under Bran Stonewake, appalled that a seal had failed on his watch. Doorstone, the moor’s own stone sentinel fever-turned to hold every door open, barred the way. What was unsealed was never a beast — only a hunger — and it was shut in for good.

48 · The Black Meadows. The elves’ night-pastures, where dream-horses were bred on moonflower and sleep; the fever turned the gift, and the herds grazed the dreams of the living. The night-herders of Wickstead dared not sleep at all, under Nael Wickstead, who kept the true dream-bridle. Nightbridle, the herd’s lead stallion in a fever-bridle, trampled sleepers where they lay. Bridled true, the nightmares grazed kind again.

49 · The Rime Forest. A conifer forest flash-frozen standing, every needle a glass blade, the whole wood ringing like struck crystal; stags walked it with antlers of clear glass. The foresters of Firwatch moved in silence under Hune Firwatch, lest the canopy come down. Rimehide, a logger troll hided in regrowing rime-glass, felled the trees onto climbers. Rung true, the Watch-Fir chimed the wood into gentle thaw.

50 · The Cindermouth. The mouth of a living caldera, its rim lined with tribute-pyres kept burning a thousand years — the Waste-folk feeding their own to appease what dwelt below. Sira Lastwater tended the pyres she hated and hid the old free-rite. The warden was Cindermaw the Wyrm, older than the Ascent, maddened to hunger and cured, not killed. The pyres were doused, no life was given, and the freed wyrm remembered who freed her.

51 · The Hanging City. A city slung across a bottomless chasm on woven spider-silk, lamps hung in the void like low stars; its spinner-partners had turned, drifting the dark on balloon-silk and cutting the streets. Fenn Skeinside of Skeinside walked the cables checking every splice. Skeinback, a troll on silk-sails, dropped the bridges under climbers. The Undercatch net below held a fortune in fallen cargo — and the fallen who lived.

52 · The Cloudline. Ridge-pastures piercing a permanent cloud-sea, islands of sunlit turf over drops with no bottom in sight. The cliff-holders of Skyfoot herded the ledges under Mera Skyfoot, who read the thermals by the wind’s voice. Thermal, a harpy grown vast on welded wind-sails, stooped out of the cloud and rode back up out of reach. The ridge-lore was chalked on a shrine wall, and it held.

53 · The Ember Dunes. A marching desert run backward — sand cool through the sunless day, smouldering by night when the ember-moths came down. The nomads of Duneshade crossed by star-lore with the stars gone, under Sira Duneshade, dune-mother. Emberback, a sand-swimming salamander in an ember shell, struck from below the crests. Every so often the dunes moved off the Sunk Caravanserai and gave the road back.

54 · The Wreckfields. A high plain scattered with an entire sky-fleet, downed in one night — keels upturned like whale-ribs, drakes nesting in the holds. The salvagers of Hullside stripped the wrecks they had once flown, under Weld Hullside, who dived the deepest hulls alone. Keelhaul, a hull-borer wearing the flagship’s keel as a shell, dragged climbers into the splintered holds. One salved skiff flew again.

55 · The Troll Bridges. Snow-bridges arcing over crevasses with no visible bottom, built and held by the mountain-trolls, whom the fever had made jealous of their own spans. The keepers of Bridgeward sounded every crossing under Orla Bridgeward. Spanbreak, a bridge-troll with ice-block fists, collapsed the snow under crossers. In the Groaning Chasm’s blue ice a caravan hung frozen, still carrying its cable.

56 · The Rookeries. A sea-cliff miles high, every ledge packed with a numberless rookery stirred to permanent riot; the mob was what killed you. The keepers of Rookwall worked the ledges on rope under Cole Rookwall, who climbed where no one else would. Shrikewind, a matron harpy spurred like a shrike’s larder-thorn, drove the whole cliff down on climbers. The gatherers’ rope-town hung there still — a village with no ground.

57 · The Shade Walks. The lamplit boulevards of an elf wood-city, where the lamps failed one by one and shadows peeled loose from their casters, hungry. The lamp-keepers of Candlerow guarded the last flames under Illa Candlerow, a lantern always in hand. Palewick, a living dark welded to the city’s greatest snuffed lamp, pooled the black. When the Great Lamp was relit, every shadow on the Walks snapped home.

58 · The Giant Steadings. Freehold farm-country at a giant’s scale — barns like cathedrals, hay-ricks like hills, a climber wading a lawn to the knee. The freeholders of Thanesrest were penned by their own closing fences, under Dara Thanesrest, who meant to hold her steading free or not at all. Fencewright, a plough-bull walled in fence-stone and living hedge, penned climbers in closing lanes. The charter came out of the root-cellar with the seed-corn.

59 · The Trapdoor Fields. A flat grey cavern-plain, deceptively level and riddled edge to edge with the hinged lids of trapdoor-burrows; almost nothing was safe ground. The field-delvers of Latchlight re-marked the safe lines from the Watch-Mound — the one thing that was not a lid — under Wend Latchlight, who had scars for her wrong steps. Latchjaw, a spider whose shell was its own great lid, dropped the floor out from under the unwary.

60 · The Frosthall. The mead-hall of a giant jarldom, warm light spilling into the snow and an endless war-feast within, thanes dying and rising to drink again. Bruna Hearthold kept the Mead of Memory hidden and the true songs alive. The warden was Jarl Hrimgar — the best of them, his open hand frozen to a fist — freed, not slaughtered, when the Mead woke him. The feast became a welcome again.

Floors 61–80 · The Scattered Sky

61 · The Anchor Chains. A sky of vast mooring-chains slung tower to tower over cloud and a fall with no bottom — links walked like roads, the isles they once held long drifted away. The chain-keepers of Chainrest sounded every link by its hum, under Mera Chainrest. Shacklewise, a drake welded into the great windlass, snapped the spans to shake walkers off. One near isle was hauled home to its mooring.

62 · The Reactor Scar. A crater of fused colorless glass where one of the old world’s reactors melted down, burning still with a flame no eye could see — felt only as the sickness deepening. The survivors of Scarwatch lived at the rim on pale water, under Grint Scarwatch, who glowed faintly himself. Palescorch, a salamander welded round a shard of the broken core, fought invisible in the shimmer. The Glassed Town stood fused mid-street, clear as ice.

63 · The Updrafts. Wind-gorge passes where thermal columns boomed strong enough to lift a kite and its pilot; the crossings were flown, or not made. The kite-pilots of Kitegate read every column’s note under Cole Kitegate. Galecrest, a dueling harpy on wind-cutting vanes, owned the columns and cut lines mid-air. On the gorge-bottom lay the Wrecking-Floor, a drift of every kite that ever fell.

64 · The Ice Quarries. Open quarries sawn from a glacier of flawless clear ice, gantries swinging house-sized blocks that no one guided. The quarriers of Hewnstone worked under loads they had not locked themselves, under Hune Hewnstone, who read a block’s flaws by the light through it. Hewnheart, a troll welded into the great gantry-crane, crushed with its own cargo. The finest master-lens ever cut came up from the black sump.

65 · The Mast Forest. A sky-fleet that lost its lift and settled upright, hull-deep in the mountain — masts in ranks like a forest, rigging for a canopy. The mast-folk of Sparside lived aloft in the crowsnests under Weld Sparside and never trusted the wreck-choked floor. Mastwrack, a drake welded to the tallest main-mast, whipped the shroud-lines to snare and fling. One mast of the dead forest flew again.

66 · The Kennel Courts. The stone kennel-yards where the elf-realm bred and gentled every beast of its hunt; the fever ungentled all of it at once. The keepers of Stillhorn whispered the old names through the bars under Nael Stillhorn, hoping. Leashbone, a pack-matron harnessed by iron leashes to every run on the floor, hauled whole packs as a weapon. When the master horn sounded true, every beast heeled to its name.

67 · The Coolant Falls. The deep-forge’s outflow, a mile-high waterfall flash-frozen in the act of falling, milk-lit caverns behind the curtain. The falls-folk of Milkwater kept the source-shrine in the Cataract Cave under Bruna Milkwater, listening for the roar. Whitefall, a troll welded into the cataract itself, calved blocks of the curtain onto climbers. Pilgrims stood frozen mid-climb in the ice, and were thawed at last.

68 · The Silk Vaults. The Deep’s cold storerooms, aisle on aisle of neat white bundles; the fever turned the keeping cruel, and the vault-spiders wrapped the living for storage. The crews of Spoolgate cut free the newly wrapped under Wend Spoolgate, who knew which bundles held treasure and which held someone still breathing. Spoolhide, a keeper-spider welded into the master reel, spun binding faster than any blade could cut.

69 · The Lightning Fields. A plain bristling with copper poles under a storm-ceiling that never rained and never stopped flashing; the fever taught the sky to aim. The field-folk of Copperpole crossed by the safe lanes under Mera Copperpole, who read the next strike by the crawl on her skin. Groundstrike, a drake crowned in captured lightning atop the Master Pole, called the bolts down. The Glass Garden recorded every strike in fulgurite.

70 · The Tempest Court. A sky-palace on the highest crag, wreathed in a permanent roaring tempest of unbottled gales; a whole court clung to its perches, tied to the stone. Cole Galesend kept the bottling-lore alive among the captives. The warden was Zephyra, the Storm Queen — a good queen crowned into a tyrant of tempest, freed, not killed, when her storm-crown broke in the eye. The gales fell to a breeze.

71 · The Char Forest. A woodland burned so hot it petrified — trunks of black glassy charcoal, some still warm at the heart a thousand years on. The char-burners of Stumpwell worked the dead wood under Grint Stumpwell, who knew which snags held a live coal. Charspine, a salamander in a petrified husk glowing at every crack, reignited the trunks around it. The Great Snag was banked into the tier’s one safe hearth.

72 · The Drake Aeries. A cliff of tiered eyries and hoard-caves, every ledge heaped with drake-gold; Men had lived here by an old truce, and the fever broke it. The keepers of Scalewatch dodged the squabbling drakes under Weld Scalewatch, who climbed to the tyrant’s perch alone. Stoopfall, the old deposed tyrant-drake in a welded stoop-harness, beat climbers off the cliff. The truce was re-struck at the carved stone.

73 · The Wind Walls. Colossal ice-and-iron ramparts the giants raised to break the killing gales, their measuring shutters jammed open so the wind chose, and chose to kill. The wall-folk of Shuttergate braced in the casemates under Bruna Shuttergate, who read a coming gust by its note in the walls. Galebone, a troll welded into the master shutter, blasted climbers off the crown. The highest wall had been tuned by ages of gale into an organ.

74 · The Snare Woods. A hunting-grove seeded root to crown with wire, tripline, and deadfall — quiet, green, and wholly lethal. The snare-folk of Brambleside disarmed what they could under Illa Brambleside, who read a cocked wood by the tension in the air. Springtrap, a hound welded into a walking snare-engine, sprang the wood’s traps in cascades. In the Gibbet Glade the caught were cut down at last and given graves.

75 · The Jarl’s Road. The jarldom’s processional way, dead-straight across the uplands — and marched by a spectral column bound for a feast a thousand years fallen. The road-folk of Horncall pulled the caught out of the column under Hune Horncall, road-warden. Hallmarch, a lamplighter troll made a marching-engine, drove the ghost-muster on. The true horn, sounded from the Milestone Cairn, called the march home to its feast.

76 · The Quarry Runs. The elf-hunt’s coursing-runs, which remembered the chase; set foot on a lane and the whole wood decided you were quarry. The quarry-folk of Bayhollow learned to double back on the hunt under Nael Bayhollow. Foxglove, the runs’ apex quarry made their master hunter, drove the living to a killing-ground strewn with a thousand hunts’ antlers. Broken, the great fox-wolf was quarry once more.

77 · The Egg Galleries. The Deep’s hatcheries — pale caverns racked with glowing egg-sacs in their tens of thousands, the fever quickening the hatch to a flood. The watchers of Palewatch chilled sacs to slow it, under Wend Palewatch, who knew to the hour when a gallery would burst. Husklight, a sentinel spider welded round a cluster of mother-sacs, birthed broods as it fought. The Incubatory was stilled at the source.

78 · The Eye Wall. The dead-calm eye of the storm-queen’s tempest — soft gold light, suspended rain, and a peace that killed by kindness; those who sat to rest never rose. The eye-dwellers of Lullside stayed awake by the eye-wall’s roar, under Mera Lullside, who shook the drowsing awake. Stillmark, the killing calm given a body, welcomed climbers to lie down. In the Sitting Garden, folk seated eighty years stood up and walked.

79 · The Wyrmroad. An ancient migration-canyon drifted spear-deep in shed wyrm-scale, older than the tower and answering no crown. The road-folk of Moltgate sorted molt under Grint Moltgate, who knew which hollows held a sleeping wyrmling. Moltcrown, a young wyrm crowned in ages of welded molt, was cured rather than killed, and remembered it. The Bonefall — the skeleton of a wyrm dead before the tower rose — spanned the canyon.

80 · The Pale Court. The bone-white hall of the Master of the Hunt, hung with a thousand years of trophies and lit moon-pale; from it the Grey Rides, the Kennel Courts, and the Quarry Runs all took their law. Nael Hornsend, once the Master’s own horn-caller, kept the calloff-lore. The warden was the Pale Huntsman — the gentlest hand in the wood, hunted longest and furthest gone of all the freed lords. He woke, ended every hunt in the realm, and laid the horn down for good.

Floors 81–100 · The Made

81 · The First Outwork. The first floor of the Made — no soil, no green, only slag-fill and riveted plate, a siege-rampart the size of a border-country raised by slave-sappers whose bones went into the fill. Dara Sappersrest of the labor-camp kept the demolition-lore and the tally of the dead in the walls. Rivetgrim, a section of rampart risen and walking, dragged climbers into its plate. The outwork came down from below.

82 · The Chain Yards. Forging-floors slung over quench-pits, a country with a single product: chains — worked by the bound, who hammered their own shackles and hated every link. Wend Linkside filed at his shackle in the dark and kept the master-drain’s secret. Anchorwrath, the yard’s drag-lines welded into a thrashing anchor-serpent, hauled climbers to the pits. A hidden file cut collars cold, with no spell at all.

83 · The Furnace Roads. A lattice of iron-grating causeways over open furnace-pits, glowing red from beneath — a griddle-country crossed grate by grate. The road-crews of Stokegate lost one of their own through a grate every shift; Grint Stokegate read which bars would give by their glow. Cindergrate, the fused ash and bone of a hundred fallen crews, dropped the gratings under climbers. The master damper banked it all cold.

84 · The Welding Halls. The horror-factory of the Made, where captured souls were welded seam by seam into imps and hellknights; some on the cooling-racks still screamed inside their seams. Sef Seamside split free every half-made soldier the overseers left unwatched. Seamwright, the master welding-engine itself grown a will, welded climbers where they stood. Reversed, the halls ran backward — un-making soldiers into people.

85 · The Imp Warrens. A smoke-choked barracks-slum of iron cell-blocks where the tower’s soldiery denned between shifts, and press-gangs hauled the next batch through to the racks. Wend Sootside ran the whisper-network of the disaffected. The warden Fluegrim was no construct but a Pressed hellknight, his collar old and cracked; freed rather than broken, he turned his whole warren — the first great defection of the King’s own army.

86 · The Bastion Line. An endless drill-fortress — ramparts and parade-grounds out of sight, ranks wheeling and presenting forever to drum and horn, discipline made a landscape. Dara Wallrest kept the stolen drill-orders and counter-signs. Bastionheart, a drill-master welded into command-plate, was Pressed, not made; his collar broke, the endless drill halted for the first time in an age, and his army formed up behind the rising.

87 · The Siege Gardens. The King’s parody of the green world — terraced beds where siege-engines were grown from iron-seed, oozing black oil for sap, tended by thralls with pruning-torches. Nael Ramside kept the blight-lore, souring beds before they fruited into war. Rootram, a half-grown assault-ram fused to the garden’s root-cables, flailed on part-grown limbs. Blighted, the crop rotted in its rows and the seed-vault went cold.

88 · The Iron Chantries. War-chapels of black iron where the bound were made to chant the King’s creed until some half-believed — the floor that worked on the mind, not the body. Sef Chantside whispered the counter-creed to those slipping under. Vespergrim, the accreted false faith risen as a pillar of iron-smoke and scripture, hazed the naves with devotion. The belfry was rung against the liturgy, and the compulsion cracked.

89 · The Herald’s Road. The ceremonial approach to Malgrim’s gate — proclamation-horns the size of houses blaring the King’s titles without pause, a shout that was itself a weapon. Nael Clarionfall kept the knack of turning a horn against its own fanfare. Trumpetsteel, a Pressed honor-knight welded into a horn-bodied herald, was freed rather than felled — and blared the King’s decree backward, throwing the muster into confusion.

90 · The Herald’s Gate. The muster-gate of the Made, iron yards under a colossal gatehouse where drums beat men into soldiers and clerks tallied the conscript-rolls. Dara Lastmuster kept the knack of striking the rolls. The warden was Malgrim, Herald of the King — freed once far below, re-taken, and promoted until he loved the King who unmade him. Offered every mercy the climb had learned, he refused; he fell unfree, the hardest victory of the war.

91 · The Obsidian Stair. A grand staircase cut from one sweep of black mirror-glass, climbing through a lightless hall; the reflections did not always copy, and some stepped out. Wend of Firststep taught the held to look away before the glass learned their faces. Firstriser, the first reflection the stair ever freed, fought exactly as its climber did, only colder. It had been up there longer, and it had learned things.

92 · The Mirror Galleries. Endless silvered halls where the King hoarded the faces of the taken, worn and swapped by the things in the glass. Illa of Sheenrest returned stolen likenesses to the blank-featured one mirror at a time. Backglass, a hundred stolen faces welded into one figure, dueled behind borrowed masks with its victims’ own skill. The master mirror was shattered, and a thousand faces flew home.

93 · The Reactor Galleries. The tower’s living core — the colorless-flame engine from which the Yoke, the fever, and every collar and weld poured out. The reactor-thralls of Coreside fed the heart of their own bondage; Ferro kept the scram-lore, hoarded a lifetime. Halflight, the core’s own flame given a body, could not be killed while it fed, and was starved instead. The core was scrammed, and the whole tower went quiet at once.

94 · The Antechambers. A honeycomb of waiting-rooms before the throne, where the taken held numbered tokens for audiences that never came — bondage without a chain, refined to pure patience. Nib of Doorward kept the hard knack of standing up and walking out of the line. Anteroom, a grey chamberlain accreted from a lifetime of recalled tokens, kept everyone forever almost admitted. The queues simply walked out.

95 · The Trophy Vaults. The King’s museum of his own conquest — the crowns of drowned kingdoms, the arms of fallen liberators, and the gear of every climber who came before, labelled. Vess of Hoardside knew whose every relic was. The Curator, a construct of welded crowns and swords, turned the fallen heroes’ own famous weapons on the climb. On the Climbers’ Shelf a space had been kept blank at the end.

96 · The Night Gardens. The King’s one vanity of beauty — a walled pleasance of black-flowering trees, terraced and fountained, grown from the taken, who were planted in the beds and coaxed to flower. Every bloom had a face if you looked. Elys of Bloomside dug the planted free before they flowered past saving. Nightbloom, a vast night-flower whose every petal was a face, breathed a perfume that would have lulled an army into the beds.

97 · The Kingsway. A mile of white monumental marble climbing the last tiers — a thousand statues of Vharuk, arches, and reliefs retelling the Theft as glory. The captive masons of Wayside smuggled the true history into the plinths where the King never stooped to read. Waymarshal, a colossal marble Vharuk stepped off its plinth, guarded the approach with a god’s arrogance. The idols came down the whole length of the avenue.

98 · The Court. The gilded audience-tiers of the willing — courtiers never taken and never collared, who chose the King and prospered by him; the only captives were the servants in the walls. Bly of Courtside kept their long ledger of the court’s crimes. The warden was the Grand Vizier, a free man who laughed at mercy and was judged rather than freed. The ledger was read aloud over the gilded ruin.

99 · The Last Door. One immense threshold-hall and the Door itself — black adamant and welded sorrow, a mosaic of fallen liberators sealed where they fell. Hollen of Lastward kept the belief that the hero inside still lived. The warden was the Champion, the greatest liberator ever to climb, welded living into the Door as its lock. Given back her name and her sword, she stepped out of the metal alive, and climbed the last floor beside the host.

100 · The Throne. The summit — a single hall of cold iron and raw aether open to a black sky, built by a King who cut the world into a hundred pieces so it could never stand in one room. No town stood here, and no keeper; only Vharuk, the Demon King, a usurper of nothing, offered no mercy because he was the source. When he fell, the stolen aether went home, every collar opened, and the hundred floors stood together on his throne-floor — the one thing the tower was built to prevent. Then the host walked down.

The Charter of the Frosthall, as Signed

The original lies at Roothollow under glass. One corner is scorched, and the scorch is three nights younger than the ink. This is the surviving fair copy, collated against the original, marks and all. It is short. The Herald of the King, reading it that spring, observed that short laws are written to be kept.

The preamble

Being the agreement of the parliament of the banners, met at the Frosthall upon the sixtieth floor, in the deep winter of the second year of the rising; argued from first-frost to the signing; and agreed in the whole hall on this, if at first on nothing else:

That the world was one country before the Theft, and shall be one country after, and that no floor climbs alone.

The first article — of the engines

The engines of the tower, the grav-engines at its welds and the core that feeds them, shall be neither kept nor scrapped by any party to this charter, whatever its strength on the day. Their fate shall be decided by a freed world’s own council, met on the ground, after the throne; and until that council sits, nothing is kept, nothing is scrapped, and no hand rests on any lever it was not sent to pull.

The second article — of the gates

Every lift of a freed floor stands open to all, for nothing, forever. No toll, no chain, no ledger at any freed gate; and no man is turned back down a stair he is able to climb.

The third article — of the stores

Gilder Voss, called the Tollmaster-General, is made quartermaster- general of the rising: every store, train, granary, ferry, and toll- house of the freed tower under his hand, at a fixed honest fee, haggled for two hours as a point of honor and entered in the margin. His accounts stand open to the parliament and are audited twice in each year. The clerks minuted the general’s remark on the appointment: that there is no better warden for a lock than the man who has picked it.

The fourth article — of the Pressed

The freed Pressed — the goblins, the kobolds, the imps, and every folk the tower collared and the rising freed — hold a seat among the peoples of this parliament, in their own right, and in their own names; the names, where a name was lacking, being their own to choose, and no one’s to spell for them.

A fifth matter, the woods, was put to the hall and failed, and nothing failed is in a charter. It is minuted here because the mover of the failed clause gathered her papers and stayed, which the hall remembered later, and was ashamed.

The signatures

Jarl Hrimgar signed first, in the deep script, the pen a splinter in his fist. Vega and Ulfar Coalbrow signed together, brother and sister, one pen between them and the pen the worse for it. Mistress Calyne signed small and plain. Gilder Voss signed with a flourish that used more ink than his whole article.

Hobb Fennick, sent up from the first floor of the world with his counts under his arm, signed for the keepers in a farmer’s careful hand, checking the letters twice; and after him the keepers of forty floors, each in their own manner. Of those who could not write, the clerks described the marks, as instructed:

  • a tally-cord of the freed kobolds of the Adit, knotted once for each of them, tied through a slit cut in the margin of the page;
  • the character a kobold of the winter camps had lately been taught, set down backwards, its owner declining to have it recopied, the name being hers whichever way it faced;
  • the burned print of a smith’s glove, from a forge-hand of the Gear Galleries who owned a name but did not yet trust a pen with it;
  • and one paw-print, in hearth-black, which the hall-folk swore a cured hearth-wolf left unbidden, walking the table while the clerks supped. The clerks let it stand. Nothing in the four articles forbids it, and nobody wished to argue precedence with the dog.

Rhovan of the Grey Rides signed a name that may or may not have been his; the clerks were assured it would verify, and did not try. Nix drew the character he had settled on for himself, slowly, with his tongue between his teeth; a tally-cord was heard being counted under the seat, and is not minuted, and is minuted now. Dun Harrow signed one-handed, steadying the page with the butt of the standard.

Berrin Or signed last but one, in a quartermaster’s neat fair hand. The compilers observe that it is the same hand as the Frosthall seating- list, and let the two facts stand beside each other, as a charter would want.

Ede Stonhal signed last. Sixty-one banners hung in the rafters over the wet ink, and the draught from the smoke-hole moved them together, and the clerks, having nothing further to record, recorded that.

The Contract of Vyx, in Full

Taken down at the signing by the Matriarch’s scrivener in the silk- white book, under the hanging lanterns of the Broodthrone. This copy was furnished to the compilers by the Matriarch herself, at posted rates; her marginal annotations were charged for separately, and are worth it.

Memorandum of the parties

The party of the first part: Ede Stonhal, called the banner-general, for the host of the rising, its captains, its witnesses, its wagons and stragglers inclusive.

The party of the second part: the Matriarch Vyx, freeholder of the fortieth floor by right of survival.

Recital

The party of the second part declines to fight. The parties are to understand that this is not mercy but arithmetic: an army that mourns its dead by name would win, at the price of a tenth of its banners and the whole of a house nine hundred years in the furnishing, and ruin profits no signatory. Therefore: terms.

Clause the first — passage

The host of the rising crosses the floor of the second part unhindered, by the gallery road, van to rear-guard, wagons and stragglers inclusive; and the stair-lift behind the throne, kept wrapped these many years against exactly this negotiation, is unwrapped for its use. In perpetuity. Reinforcements, being wagons or stragglers within the meaning of this clause, pass also, for years, until there are no more. What is sold is the fortieth floor, open, without a single funeral.

Clause the second — warmth

Tribute, annual, part the first: once in each year, for one night, the cook-fires of the host are lit in the hall of the second part, and the brood of the second part sleeps in the warmth of a nation’s suppers. The first part provides the suppers. The first part provides the nation.

Clause the third — the story

Tribute, annual, part the second: one true story, never before told to any ear, delivered to the second part, at this court, in person, in each year, for so long as this contract runs, which is forever. Truth, novelty, and delivery are the coins of this clause and no other coin is accepted. The second part will know a false one. The first part is advised, in the instrument itself, not to try.

Annotation of the second part: they ripen. Everything I keep does.

Clause the fourth — the hostage

Tribute, part the third: one hostage of rank, to dwell in the court of the second part as surety while the host and its trains are upon her floor, and to be surrendered whole, unwrapped, and unpuppeted — the condition of the goods warranted by the second part — upon the rear- guard’s clearance of the tier-gate. Rank is of the essence. The second part does not warehouse spear-carriers; the pledge is only as dear as the head.

Clause the fifth — board

The hostage eats what the court eats. Annotation of the second part: the hostage’s correspondence, admitted under clause the ninth, rates the fish above the army’s. The court does not disagree.

Clause the sixth — liberty

The hostage is permitted any gallery whose silk bears his weight, and forbidden the Larder. The first is a courtesy and the second is a mercy, and the hostage is warranted intelligent enough to know the difference.

Clause the seventh — idleness

Boredom voids the warranty. The second part will accordingly supply instruction in cradles, the stakes being one true answer per crashed cradle, owed by the crasher. The parties acknowledge that the game cannot be won, only lost more slowly, and that this too is instruction.

Clause the eighth — the furniture

The court of the second part, wrapped and puppeted, is her property and her furniture and is not on the table. A party may dislike a clause and sign. It is done every day.

Clause the ninth — letters

The hostage may write. The court may read.

Clause the tenth — the instruments

The clauses of the second part hold whether a party is present or absent, paid or owing, living or otherwise; or her word is worth nothing, and the first part has wintered with a beast. The second part’s instruments have held nine hundred years. The first part’s attention is drawn to the distinction between the second part and the tower: there are always clauses.

Memorandum, entered at the first part’s insistence

This contract buys passage. It does not buy forgetting. The first part will come back for every hanging light.

Annotation of the second part: forgetting was not offered. The annotation is supplied at no charge, being her own.

Discharges, in the scrivener’s later hand

The first year’s story: paid at signature, and handsomely; the Wake’s declined correspondence of three centuries is regarded as answered.

The hostage-clause: discharged with the thaw; warranty honored; goods returned whole, and, the court notes, reluctant at the gallery’s edge, which the warranty does not cover and the second part did not cause, much.

The story-clause, in the year of the fall: paid in the whole of the war, from the first page to the light going home, told over some nights; ruled by the second part an overpayment by some years. The payer’s answer — that he knows his clauses, and calls it interest — is entered, and the account, uniquely in the books of the second part, runs on by the free choice of both signatories.

The wrapped court, not being on the table, was not bought, and was not forgotten. The cocoons stand empty; the rates for websteel are posted, and countersigned, and the Matriarch charges a copper to see them.

Malgrim's Ledger, Complete

Recovered from the writing-stand of the drum-throne on the day after the Battle of the Herald’s Gate, and carried down to Roothollow with the rest of what the fall left legible. The hand is fine, and never hurries, and never blots. The book ends mid-sentence; what was written after it ends is set down in a note at the close.

⁂

Item: the tenth floor is written off. The crown was issued as a joke at the goblins’ expense, and the joke has stopped being funny. The King of Nothing was offered the enemy’s word and refused it for the crown; enter the refusal to his credit, for a creature that dies keeping a worthless thing has at least understood keeping. One cup-bearer unaccounted. Cup-bearers are not a line.

Item: the Warcamp is spent. Write off the garrison, the drums, and the Warlord entire. Skarn priced his collar as a rank and defended it as one, and I enter the flaw so it is not repeated: never issue a collar to a creature that wants it. I am told there is one exception. I keep his file myself.

Item: the woman climbs.

Item: sixty-three units of aged inventory recovered by the enemy at the twentieth floor. I decline to enter a loss. Grief is a bond that matures; every cage she opens, she carries. Let her portage.

Item: the gate-toll investment performs. Three factions taught to hate one another at their own expense is the cheapest garrison on these books. Recommend no interference.

Item: a goblin observed at the head of parleys. Entered under sundries. There is no line in any ledger for what he is spending, and a currency we cannot mint we cannot counterfeit either. Watch him.

Note to the willing below: feed her the trail slowly. Small findings, honestly come by, at decent intervals; nothing that cannot bear her checking, for she will check. A hope led is a hope steered. And grieve well when the trail warms. You have always grieved beautifully. It is why I collared you.

⁂

Item: three casks of oak-gall ink, four gross of candles, one pen. The pen is for me. A herald’s hand must never be seen to tire.

Item: contrary to my minute of the spring, I ordered the Tollmaster’s houses burned at the thirtieth floor, to starve the line. The burning is a bad purchase: it has bought the enemy a quartermaster, for the Tollmaster walked into their camp with his strong-boxes on his back and his greed intact, and greed is the one recruit that never deserts. Enter the loss against my name. A book that spares its keeper is not a book.

⁂

Item: the Kingsbarrow account is closed at a loss. The drowned king was not carried by steel; he was carried by grief given precedence, and the mound, being honest, fell. They mourn well, and have learned to burn grief in good order. Recommendation to the throne: deny them occasions. They must not mourn at scale.

Item: the Broodthrone. The Matriarch kept her contract to its final clause and stood her court aside while the crown-iron broke. I decline to enter treason; a kept contract is the only piety the old powers have, and she was ours by welding, never by word. Write the fortieth relay off in full, and note for the archive: we held no paper on her. See that this is never again true.

Item: they have taken a census. Nine peoples, forty banners, one goblin. I confess the last line moved me; caution to my successor: it will move you also, and it is meant to. They have added themselves up, with chalk, on our own slate — the one arithmetic this house was built to prevent. Against their clerks, nothing; clerks can be replaced and martyrs cannot. Against their occasions, everything.

Item: our friend below requests extraction. Denied. His safety has never lain in appearing on lists but in keeping them; no man is so unseen as the one holding the pen. He asks what he is to us after so many years, and I pay him the wage of the truth: he is the best-kept entry in my books. A ledger entry does not retire.

Item, for my own account: when the fortieth’s crown-iron fell, the floor wept for relief. I remember the feeling. I chose against it once, and every year the choosing comes easier. Let the record show the collar is not heavy. What is heavy is choosing, and the collar ends the choosing. In this one respect I envy nobody climbing.

⁂

Item: the Wyrm has let them pass. It predates our books and has said so, to their faces. It pities the King. I have entered stranger assets, but none that kept.

Item: the woman now keeps a roll of everyone who has ever climbed before. Our friend keeps the roll. File under: economy.

⁂

Item: the feast bill comes due and is worth it. Forty servers and twelve bows, spent to the last man, as designed. Against that column: one standard-bearer, one serjeant of the old doctrine, a charter scorched, a parliament that now tastes its own soup, and a general who has stopped sleeping. I have bought worse for more.

Item: let no one below read cruelty in it. Cruelty is waste, and I keep clean books. We charged sixty-eight lives for a truth the tower usually prices in nations: a gathered world can be broken at one table. They will not learn it. Hope never audits.

Item: the woman doubles her guard and guards the wrong thing — the serving-lines, the galleries, the doors. Names were never our way in. Love is. No taster sips at that.

Item: our friend performs grief convincingly. He always did; I collared him for it. He wept for the standard-bearer and the weeping was, in the fullest sense, sincere. That is the whole art of a willing instrument: nothing in it is false but the sum.

Item: they climb for the chains. Advise the sky.

⁂

Item: the sky has answered, in her fashion. The Queen priced her gales at a throne we never promised her, was refused, and scattered a third of their host at her own expense. Enter under gifts. I would not have spent an army to buy a delay; she spent theirs.

Item: the Pale Court has fallen. The Huntsman kept better books than the Queen — every course entered, every quarry weighed, a thousand years in fair hand — and it availed him nothing, because his ledger carried one bad clause from before our time, and the woman found it and walked in through it, toward the horns. The stolen realms are a closed column. Assets that were liabilities dressed in crowns.

Item: they muster below the outworks, seventy banners, thirty-one peoples, one charter. I have read the charter. It is short, which is how I know it is dangerous. Long laws are written to be escaped.

Item: provision to the outworks doubled; the drums to beat without interval. What the tower owns, the tower may spend, and I intend to spend it by weight.

Item: our friend reports from their muster. The bread arrived before the army, as ever, and the hounds took no notice of him. The kennel-clerks swear a hound cannot be deceived about a man; either they flatter their stock, or my friend is by now so wholly the thing he pretends that there is nothing false left in him for a dog to find. On quiet evenings it unsettles even me.

Item: the siege begins when they touch the first rampart. Open a new page. Head it: expenditures.

⁂

EXPENDITURES

Item: the First Outwork. Their line of battle grows as it advances; every collar their small squads break deserts, not to them, to the future. There is no countermeasure in the manuals. The manuals never imagined an enemy whose sappers dig with mercy.

Item: the Welding Halls are lost, and among the aged inventory a unit recognized an officer of the enemy and said one word. The word is a name I know. For the first time in the keeping of this book I have considered a false page, and I enter the temptation instead of the lie, which is how I know the account is ending.

Item: the spymaster of the Wake is dead, proving what she was: right. Enter her with respect. Our friend is unmasked and climbing to me with nothing but the collar. I have opened the gate. What else is a keeper for.

Item: he asked, at the gate, what his service purchases at the last. I told him gently, because it is true and gentleness costs nothing: it purchases what he asked for on the night he knelt. To be necessary. He is. Until the account closes.

⁂

Item: the day. They have brought a bell.

Item: I know what it is for. The priests sang into the casting, and the smith’s mark on it is the mark from the collar-steel, bought back. There is nothing in the manuals, because no army before this one ever cast its siege-engine as a mercy.

Item: drums doubled. The drum says what it has always said, and this morning, for the first time in thirty years, I have written down what it says and looked at the sentence.

Item: in my own rear ranks, stillness is spreading, in fifties, in knots, like calm in a pot taken off the fire. My clerks look to me, and I beat the drum, which is my post.

Item: the carriage moves. The singers step over their dead and do not lose the line. Set it down fairly: they are the best troops I have ever seen, and none of them is armed.

Item: the wager of the day is now plain, and I find, setting it down in a fair hand while the yards decide it: their word against my drum. The drum says there is nothing under the collar but the drum. The bell proposes to ask each man singly what

⁂

Here the book ends. Every leaf after it is blank, save two writings never entered in the ledger proper. The first came down to the rising’s fires at moonrise:

Item: your bell. I heard it. Come up in the morning and tell me what you think it proves, and I will tell you what I know it costs. Bring the thirty. I have something of theirs to return — a ledger entry does not retire.

The second, a page folded once, was put into the general’s hands at noon the next day by two freed clerks, with the ledger’s last delivery walking bound between them:

Item: delivered to the woman who climbs, one quartermaster, used. He asked me a second time for extraction. I keep my answers: a ledger entry does not retire. Item, final: the account passes to you, general. See that it balances better in your keeping than it did in mine.

The muster-rolls of the freed record the keeper of this book, at the general’s own instruction: a man of the forty-third, a tallyman, taken twice; and that the second taking was the crime.

On the Yoke, the Shards, and the Break-Collar

Compiled at Roothollow after the fall, from the testimony of keepers, smiths, priests, freed clerks, one Thane, and one shardmind; with the Forge-Court’s survey and the Herald’s ledger open on the table. The compilers are not scholars. The scholars went up with everyone else, and this is what they sent down.

What the Yoke was

The Yoke was a voice. It went out from the core at the ninety-third floor and it never stopped, and everything cruel in the tower was one of its words. To the beasts it spoke as the fever, which maddens; to the Pressed it spoke through the collar, which holds; to the low floors it spoke as the false daylight, shadowless and lidded, which the Fencerows’ keepers can now confirm was never day at all, their valley having since been shown an honest dark. Under all of it ran the hum. Those who lived beneath it called it silence, never having stood in the true kind; a smith of the rising testifies that she carried the pressure from Roothollow to the Spire in the small bones of her ears and only learned its name when it stopped.

It must be understood that the Yoke was not sorcery in the fireside sense, a curse said once and left to work. It was administration. It was broadcast, continuously, at cost, from a burning heart that had to be fed, tended, and shielded by the gray generations of Coreside; and what is broadcast can be starved. The whole war, read rightly, was an argument about that one fact.

What the shards are

The shardminds are splinters of a single web of minds that thought across the whole country of countries before the Theft, and died in it. The network is gone; the splinters remain — dry, ancient, and loyal to whom they choose, and they do not re-choose, with one recorded exception, publicly made, at a gate-queue, which the reader already knows.

What no one knew until late, and the splinter in question confessed before it was forced to: the Yoke rode the dead network. The King strung his voice along its bones the way a man fences a field with the timbers of a burnt house, and every cruelty the broadcast carried, it carried on the bones of the shardkind. The riding kept the bones warm. When the core was scrammed, the last of the old network went dark in a breath — the sixtieth’s, the three sleepers under the ice, the one in the Wyrm’s hoard that was rude for nine hundred years — and one splinter now burns alone, on nothing but its own choosing. It declines to be cited in this appendix. It is cited anyway, with thanks.

What the Break-collar did

The collar is a socket of the Yoke: steel around a throat, warmed from a hundred floors away. The Break-collar is the word that parts it, and the word’s whole craft is that it must be given — said to one throat at a time, gently, by someone standing close enough to be killed. The collar-breakers of the rising were trained above all else in the touching of an enemy’s throat gently, and their count, kept by a goblin on a slate, ended larger than any muster-roll of the climb, under the words: joined, not lost.

At the Herald’s Gate the word was rung instead of said. A bell the size of a cottage, cast from the brass of the chapels that taught the bound to worship their chains and the horns that shouted the thief’s glory, with the word sung into the casting by the freed chantries for nine nights and a forge-mark on it that its smith had spent the whole climb buying back — Break-collar, Break-chain, Unseam, Recant, and Countermand gathered into one spoken mercy the size of a battlefield. A clerk of the freed at Lastmuster records that the King’s army did not break at the ringing; it sat down, and began to say names. The limits are also on record: the deep-collared, worn past any single word’s reach, wanted the Recant sung one at a time through the afternoon; the Wrongmade have nothing in them to free; and the one collar the bell could never touch was the collar put on from the inside. The Herald heard the bell plainly, and asked, the next morning, to hear the word said over his own steel, and heard it, and was not changed. His ledger is the fifth appendix, and the compilers recommend it be read standing.

Why power, and not stone

The reader who has never left the low floors must now be told what every climber learned by walking: a floor of the Ascent is not a room. It is a country, the breadth of a whole stolen realm, under kilometers of its own sky and its own weather; the rim is a journey, not a glance, and the true ceiling shows, on the clearest days, as a faint dark grain. No brick that was ever fired could hold one country above another. The tower does not stand by masonry and never did.

It stands by power. The Forge-Court’s survey — forty sheets of vellum, three generations in the drafting, laid at last on the dead controls and since carried down — shows a grav-engine at every weld, the thief’s own tackle, still rigged: the engines that lifted the floors, mid-harvest, mid-flood, without spilling a hay-rick, because the thief wanted his takings whole. Binding-spells seam the joints; the black iron everywhere is not the strength of the thing but the anchor the power is bolted to. And every engine in the column answers to the core and to nothing else. The Thane of the Forge-Court, questioned by the compilers, answered slowly and finished his sentences: the throne is the winch-house; hold the core, and the whole Ascent answers you like a bellows-crew; and this, precisely this, is why it could only ever be held by one hand, and why one hand was always too many.

What the breakings did

The decade-Wardens were relays. The Herald’s own ledger writes off “the fortieth relay” with the Broodthrone’s fall, and the keepers’ testimony agrees from below: when a relay fell, the whole span breathed — people wept on floors that had not heard the news, and did not know why. Each Warden broken was a span of the voice struck dumb. By the time the rising stood in the Made, the Yoke was a king shouting down a stair with the landings knocked out.

The scram ended it. The keeper of Coreside kept the safe shutting his whole life and said it once; a Thane pulled the bar with his own hand; and the broadcast died mid-word on a hundred floors at once — collars cold in the same breath, the fever unclenching from every beast in the stolen world, the core’s demon starving where it stood. The price is entered above, in the matter of the shards.

But the scram did not bring the tower down, and could not. It put out the fire; the engines’ ears are stopped, the welds are dead iron, and dead iron holds — for a century, the Thane says, or four; forever is not a figure either. The last of the power, the mail of hoarded light the King kept about his own body, outlived the core by two floors and died with him, and every survivor of the summit agrees on what it did: it went home. Down through the iron as if the iron were not there, gently, into the world’s old bones, and the last collars everywhere fell open, and the false daylight went out of the low floors at last.

So the Ascent still stands at Roothollow’s back, filling the north of the world from horizon to horizon, top beyond the air: a hundred countries on dead welds, held now by nothing but what iron remembers. What is to be done with the engines is not the compilers’ question. It is reserved, by the first article of the Charter, to a freed world’s own council, met on the ground; and the ground, for the first time in an age, is where everyone is.
